A character class in a token or layout regex denotes a set of characters, so any character may be a member or a range endpoint, in a negated class as in any other: krust accepts `r"[^é]"`, `r"[à-ÿ]"` and `r"[^a-zà-ÿ]"`.
A range denotes the characters from its start to its end; one across the surrogate code points, which are no characters, such as U+D7FF-U+E000, holds the characters on either side of them and nothing else.
Both scanners krust builds match a class against whole characters: the Flex scanner as the UTF-8 encodings of the characters the class denotes (see "Generated parser tokens are characters"); the in-process scanner reads a positive class as written, and a negated class as the class of the characters outside it, computed as a set of scalar values, since the `regex` crate's own negation of a class whose members lie on either side of the surrogate gap (`[^\x{D7FF}\x{E000}]`) also matches those members.
This diverges from the pinned K, which rejects a non-ASCII range endpoint with "Unsupported non-ASCII characters found in character class range" and a non-ASCII member of a negated class with "Unsupported non-ASCII characters found in negated character class".
(`a_class_may_hold_any_character_as_a_member_or_a_range_endpoint` in [k-rust-regex's tests](../crates/k-rust-regex/tests/regex.rs), `kast_gen_parser_scanner_matches_a_class_range_of_non_ascii_characters` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), `flex_printer_classes_match_whole_characters_as_the_in_process_pattern_does`, `a_class_range_across_the_surrogate_gap_holds_exactly_its_characters` and `flex_printer_classes_denote_the_characters_of_the_class` in [definition_regex.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/definition_regex.rs).)

## Generated parser token literals

A generated parser prints each token as a domain value whose KORE string literal denotes the code points krust's frontend reads from the token's text, the same term the in-process route converts the token to (see [Strings and their literals](#strings-and-their-literals)): a token of a sort hooked `STRING.String` denotes the characters its K escapes write, one of a sort hooked `BYTES.Bytes` the bytes after its leading `b`, each as the code point of its value, and any other token its characters.
A token subsort (`syntax MyStr ::= String [token]`) reads the text of its phrase as a token of its own sort, so its literal is that text read by its own sort's hook, not the literal of the phrase's sort: with `MyStr` unhooked, `"a\nb\x41"` is a `MyStr` token whose literal is its ten characters as written, where the same text read as a `String` denotes `a`, a newline, `b` and `A`.
The text of the phrase is the input it spans, from the first character of its first token to the last of its last, with the layout between them, whether or not a token production read it: with `syntax Pair ::= Name "." Name` and `syntax MyTok ::= Pair [token]`, the input `  ab .\n cd  ` is the `MyTok` token `ab .\n cd` on both routes.
A `[token]` production of several items is one token whose text is its whole phrase in the same way: with `syntax Paren ::= "(" Name ")" [token]`, `( ab )` is the `Paren` token `( ab )` on both routes; the pinned K's generated parsers read the text of the production's first item only, the `Paren` token `(`.
A phrase whose first or last part is empty (an empty list, or the empty end every list has) runs from its first token to its last all the same, on both routes: with `syntax Names ::= List{Name, ","}` and `syntax MyToks ::= Names [token]`, `( a , b  )` is the `MyToks` token `a , b`, which a `[locations]` `MyToks` locates from `a` to `b`.
The text of a token or of a phrase is every character of the input it spans, U+0000 as well as any other: with `syntax Name ::= r"~[^ \\n]+" [token]`, the input `~a`, U+0000, `b` is one `Name` token of four characters on both routes, written `"~a\x00b"`, and a `String` token holding a raw U+0000 between its quotes is a `String` literal holding that character.
The pinned K's generated parsers copy a token's text as a C string, which ends at its first NUL byte, so that `Name` token is `~a` there and that `String` token ends before its closing quote.
The literal is written as krust's KORE writers write one: printable ASCII as itself, `\" \\ \n \r \t \f`, and any other code point by the shortest of `\xhh`, `\uhhhh` and `\Uhhhhhhhh` that holds it, so the output is printable ASCII as `kore-syntax.md` requires.
The parser runtime reads and writes these escapes from tables krust prints from the constants its own K and KORE literal codecs use (`krust_literals.h`, from `bison::literals`), so the two routes cannot read a different set of escapes.
A `String` or `Bytes` token whose text is not such a literal (an unknown escape, a hexadecimal escape whose digits are not all hexadecimal, a surrogate or a value above U+10FFFF, an octal `Bytes` escape above 255) denotes no term on either route: the generated parser reports a syntax error located at the token (`invalid String token: unsupported escape`) and exits with a failure status.
The pinned K's generated parsers copy a `String` token's text into the KORE output unchanged, so a raw non-ASCII or control character, or an octal escape, reaches the KORE reader as written; they copy a `Bytes` token's text after the `b` unchanged, so `b"é"` denotes the one code point U+00E9 instead of the two bytes of its encoding; and they write every other token byte by byte, so a token `é` becomes the two code points U+00C3 U+00A9.
krust's frontend also reads a hexadecimal escape only from hexadecimal digits, so `"\x+1"` is no `String` literal; the pinned K reads the digits as a signed number there. K's `String` token regex admits only hexadecimal digits after `\x`, `\u` and `\U`, so only a definition that declares its own token regex for a hooked sort can write such a token.
(`kast_gen_parser_writes_each_token_as_the_kore_literal_of_its_code_points`, `kast_gen_parser_reads_a_token_holding_u0000_as_the_in_process_parser_does`, `kast_gen_parser_reads_a_token_production_of_several_items_as_its_whole_phrase` and `a_token_subsort_reads_its_text_as_a_token_of_its_own_sort` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)

## Generated parser list recursion

A generated parser recognizes every user list by left recursion: each element follows the elements before it, so an LR parser reduces as it reads and a list's length does not grow its stack, and a list far longer than `--bison-stack-max-depth` is read.
How the parser recognizes a list is not what the list is: the list's constructor keeps its signature (element, list), `syntaxDefinition.kore`, the rules, `krun`'s in-process program parser and every other artifact do not depend on it, and each program gets the term the in-process parser gives it.
The elements are collected in reading order, and when the list ends its term is built from the last element back: the constructor applied to each element and the list after it, from the terminator, each application written and located as the constructor's own production writes and locates it, so a list `e1, ..., en` is `cons(e1, cons(..., cons(en, nil)))`.
A list whose separator is empty (`NeList{E, ""}`) reads each element right after the one before it.
A possibly-empty list (`List`) is read as its terminator or as a non-empty list.
A GLR parser that reads the elements two ways merges (`Lblamb`) the two readings of the elements read so far, and prints every reading of the list.
There is no option to choose the rendering: `kcompile --bison-lists`, which before chose this rendering over right recursion (each element followed by the rest of the list, whose LR parser holds every element of a list on its stack until the list ends), is removed, and passing it is a command-line error.
This diverges from the pinned K in two ways: its generated parsers recognize lists by right recursion unless `--bison-lists` is given, so a long list can exceed its stack bound where krust's parser reads it; and its `--bison-lists` changes the definition: the list's constructor is declared over (list, element), its generated parser builds `cons(cons(nil, e1), e2)`, a rule written over the constructor means something else with the flag than without it, and a `List` is rejected (`Cannot use List with --bison-lists`).
A generated parser's grammar (`parser.y`) for a definition with user lists therefore differs from the one krust wrote before: a list `L` of `E` is the productions `Ne#L ::= E | Ne#L sep E` and `L ::= Ne#L L#Terminator` (and `L ::= L#Terminator` for a `List`), in place of `Ne#L ::= E sep Ne#L | E L#Terminator` and `L ::= Ne#L`.
(`generated_and_in_process_parsers_give_each_list_one_term` and `generated_parsers_read_long_lists_in_a_bounded_stack_and_every_reading_of_an_ambiguous_list` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)

## Generated parser disambiguation

A generated parser and in-process parsing exclude the same edges: which child node may not stand at which operand of which node.
Both read one definition of the exclusions (`inner::Exclusions` and `inner::NodeShape`): which production builds a node and which passes its child through, which operands of a node are judged (those `applyPriority` names, else the first and the last item where they are nonterminals, and none below a production with one nonterminal and no terminal, which writes nothing around its child), the side of each, and the scope, cast, priority and associativity exclusions.
In-process parsing judges each tree with them; the exporter writes each judged operand as a copy of its sort without the excluded nodes, through the productions that pass their child through, so the generated parser never builds an excluded edge.
So a program's acceptance under priority and associativity does not depend on which of the two parsers reads it.
In grammars that hold K's own rewrite, sequence or `#let` productions, both exclude such a node at an end operand of a constructor outside its scope as they exclude a node of lesser priority; in-process parsing then chooses, among the readings of the whole input that break no relation, at every position those rooted there at a rewrite, else a sequence, else a `#let` ([Rule bodies over user syntax that reuses a rewrite token](#rule-bodies-over-user-syntax-that-reuses-a-rewrite-token)), while the generated parser has no such choice and gives every reading.
This diverges from the pinned K, whose generated parser judges the operand of a labelled production with one nonterminal and no terminal: with `syntax Top ::= Exp [symbol(wrap)]` and `syntax priority wrap > plus`, it rejects `x + x * x` as a `Top`, which its own `kast` reads as `wrap(plus(x, mul(x, x)))`; krust's generated parser and in-process parsing both read it so.
(`generated_and_in_process_parsers_give_each_program_one_term` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs); `exported_operands_are_restricted_by_the_in_process_exclusions` in [grammar.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/bison/grammar.rs).)

## Rule bodies over user syntax that reuses a rewrite token

In a rule body, `=>`, `~>` and `#let ... #in` are the rule's own structure, not operators of the user's language, and each means something only where its parent gives it a scope; a reading that puts one elsewhere (`(a => b) + a`, a rewrite below `+`) denotes no term and is dropped like any reading that breaks a priority, cast or associativity relation.
When user syntax reuses one of their tokens (`syntax KItem ::= A "=>" A [symbol(arrow)]`, a match arm), a body can also be read through that syntax.
Among the readings of the whole body that break no relation and whose applications resolve, krust keeps, at every position, those whose subtree there is rooted at a rewrite, else at a sequence, else at a `#let`, and otherwise every reading: at the body's root (the term before `requires` or `ensures`) as in a cell, an argument or brackets, wherever the scope of these constructs lets one stand.
A position is a child slot of a node of a reading, and a reading is compared there only with the readings identical to it outside that slot, so the choice is made per tree: it depends neither on where readings share subterms, nor on how the forest packs them, nor on readings that denote nothing. `rule a => a`, `rule a => a + a` and `rule a + a => a` are rewrites, where before the choice `a => a` was an ambiguity and `a => a + a` a rewrite only because the invalid reading `(a => a) + a` existed beside it; `rule <k> a => a ... </k>` rewrites the cell's first item and `rule f(a => a)` rewrites the argument, instead of reporting the `arrow` reading beside the rewrite.
A construct whose own sort cannot stand at a place is not what the rule means there and is not preferred there: a sequence has sort `K`, so as the argument of `syntax A ::= h(KItem)` the user's `syntax KItem ::= A "~>" A [symbol(seqish)]` reads `rule h(a ~> a) => a`.
Readings that differ above a position are not compared at it. Two readings rooted at a rewrite that split the text at different `=>` share no position below the root and stay an ambiguity: with `A "~>" A` also reused, `rule a => a ~> a => a` is `(arrow(a, a) ~> a) => a` or `a => (a ~> arrow(a, a))`, and both are reported. A rewrite's sort is not part of its reading: it is a parameter that inference chooses, at most the sort of the place the rewrite stands at, so the rewrite is preferred at a place of any sort (with `syntax Foo ::= Bar "=>" Bar`, `rule h(bar => bar)` rewrites the argument of `h(Foo)`) and its sides stand at that place. With `A "~>" A` reused, the readings of `rule a => a ~> a` agree at the root and differ at the rewrite's right side, where the sequence is chosen: `a => (a ~> a)`; as the argument of `g(K)` it reads the same, while as the argument of `f(KItem)` a sequence cannot stand, and `rule f(a => a ~> a)` is `f(a => seqish(a, a))`. A rewrite's sort is also at least each of its sides' sorts, so where a sort written on a side (a production's, a token's, or one written on a variable) cannot stand at the place, the rewrite cannot either and is not preferred there: with a match arm `syntax Arm ::= Pat "=>" Exp`, `rule match(V, p => e) => e` reads the arm, as do `match(V, 1 => B)` and `match(V, P:Pat => B)`.
Below a rewrite (in a side of one, at any depth) a rewrite ranks after every other reading: a side of a rewrite is a term, not a change, so no rule has a rewrite there, and where the position has another reading the rule means that one. With the match arm above and `match` a function, `rule match(_, _ => B) => B` reads the arm (choosing the rewrite there would leave a rewrite inside the rule's left side, and `B` both below `Arm` and at `Exp`); with `syntax KItem ::= A "=>" A [symbol(arrow)]`, `rule f(a => a) => .K` is `f(arrow(a, a)) => .K` and `rule f(a) => g(a => a)` is `f(a) => g(arrow(a, a))`. Where a position below a rewrite has only rewrite readings, the rewrite stays and is reported as nested (`rule f(a => a) => .K` without the user's `=>`).
A side condition is a term too, a `Bool` the rule tests rather than a change, so in a `requires` or `ensures` clause a rewrite also ranks after every other reading: with the match arm and `syntax Bool ::= isArm(Arm)`, `rule f(a) => .K requires isArm(P => B)` reads the arm in the condition, and with the user's `arrow` and `syntax Bool ::= ok(KItem)`, `requires ok(a => a)` reads `ok(arrow(a, a))`.
The choice is made before sort inference, from the sorts written in a reading, so it does not read a variable's sort from the variable's other occurrences and can keep only readings that inference rejects. When it does, sorts decide among every reading that breaks no relation and puts no rewrite inside a side of another or in a side condition where the position has another reading (such a reading is no rule whatever its sorts): under `f(Foo)`, with the user's `Foo ::= Bar "=>" Bar` and `g(Bar)`, `rule <k> f(X => Y) ... </k> <c> g(X) => .K ... </c>` prefers the rewrite at `f`'s argument, which needs `X` both a `Foo` and a `Bar`, and reads the user's `=>` there, its one well-sorted reading (as `X:Bar` reads it without that step). The rule the choice stands for is the per-position choice among the well-sorted readings, which compares a reading at a position only with the well-sorted readings identical to it outside that position; it is not a choice per position of the forest, since a variable's sort ties distant positions, so it is approached in two steps that never give a parse it would not keep. First, the preferred readings, when inference accepts some of them: inference decides among them as among any readings. That step weighs only the preferred readings, so it can decide where the per-position choice would leave readings to inference: under overloads `syntax KItem ::= k(Foo) | k(Bar)`, `rule <k> k(X => X) ... </k> <c> g(X) => .K ... </c>` reads `k(Bar)`'s rewrite, while the per-position choice also keeps `k(Foo)`'s user `=>` (the readings differ first at `k`, a different production in each, and `k(Foo)`'s rewrite is not well sorted), which inference would report beside it as an ambiguity. It can also give another term than that choice: with the user's `=>` over `syntax Foo ::= Baz "=>" Baz`, `syntax Baz ::= Foo | Qux`, `syntax Qux ::= Bar` and `g(Qux)`, the same rule reads `k(Bar)`'s rewrite, with `X` a `Bar`, while among the readings the per-position choice keeps inference takes `k(Foo)`'s user `=>`, whose typing lets `X` be a `Qux`. Otherwise, every position at which readings differ in rank is asked which ranks well-sorted readings have there. Where they have one rank at every such position, no well-sorted reading ranks lower than another where they first differ, the per-position choice keeps every well-sorted reading, and inference decides among them. Where they have two ranks at one position, the parse is reported as an ambiguity between a well-sorted reading of each, where that choice might take the lesser: with `D` a `Bar`, `rule <k> f(A => B) ~> f(C => D) ... </k> <c> g(D) => .K ... </c>` can only read `f(C => D)` through the user's `=>`, and reports `f(A => B)` as a rewrite or the user's `=>`. When no reading is well sorted, the error is the preferred readings' own. A body whose preferred readings are well sorted pays nothing for the second step.
This diverges from the pinned K in two ways. First:
Where no reading of the whole body that denotes a term is rooted at a rewrite, a reading through the user's syntax is the body: with `syntax S ::= A "=>" A "=>" A [symbol(two)]`, `rule a => (a => b) + a => a` is `two(a, (a => b) + a, a)`.
Each reading is judged by itself: a reading rooted at the rule's first or second unparenthesized `=>` puts the other directly below it, which the rewrite's associativity forbids, and every other reading that is not `two` puts a rewrite directly below `+`; none of them denotes a term. `two(a, (a => b) + a, a)` puts its one rewrite under parentheses, where a rewrite may stand, so it is the one term the text denotes and the rule means it; a reading that denotes nothing cannot make the text mean something else, or nothing.
The pinned K rejects the rule (`Rewrite is not allowed to be an immediate child of plus`), reporting a reading that denotes nothing.
Second, the pinned K rejects `rule match(V, P => B) => B` with the match arm (`Rewrites are not allowed to be nested`) and, with `match` a function, `rule match(_, _ => B) => B` (`Sort of variable B inferred as greatest lower bound of ...`), reporting readings that are not rules where one reading is.
When no reading of the whole body denotes a term, krust reports a violation of the part of the body none of whose readings passes, and a scope error if some reading of that part puts a rewrite, sequence or `#let` where it has no meaning (a misplaced construct in a part that has a valid reading, as `(a => a) + a` beside `a => (a + a)`, is not what fails): `rule a => a ~> a => a` (meant `(a => a) ~> (a => a)`) reports `#KRewrite is not allowed to be an immediate child of #KSequence`, the construct parentheses would fix, rather than the right child of a rewrite below another, which only a reading that moves a rewrite out of the place it was written breaks. Where its readings break different relations and none breaks a scope, the reported violation is the least of theirs by kind and by the labels and side it names: an associativity violation before a priority violation, of an operator or of a cast's operand, which only a reading grouping operators against their declared precedence meets; each is true of some derivation of the input, and choosing by what it says keeps the report independent of how the grammar numbers its productions, which writing an operand through a pass-through sort changes without changing a tree.
(`a_rule_body_whose_arrow_user_syntax_reuses_reads_as_the_rewrite`, `a_rewrite_below_the_body_root_wins_over_user_syntax_reusing_its_token`, `a_rewrite_in_a_cell_wins_over_user_syntax_reusing_its_token`, `a_choice_is_made_at_each_position_of_the_readings_kept_above_it`, `a_construct_whose_sort_cannot_stand_at_a_place_is_not_preferred_there`, `readings_that_differ_above_a_position_are_not_compared_at_it`, `a_rewrite_is_preferred_at_a_place_of_any_sort_and_its_sides_stand_there`, `a_rewrite_whose_sides_cannot_stand_at_a_place_is_not_preferred_there`, `a_rewrite_inside_a_side_of_another_does_not_win_over_user_syntax`, `a_rewrite_in_a_side_condition_does_not_win_over_user_syntax`, `sorts_decide_among_every_reading_when_no_preferred_one_is_well_sorted`, `sorts_deciding_among_every_reading_report_readings_of_different_ranks_as_ambiguous`, `a_body_whose_rewrite_readings_all_break_a_relation_reads_through_user_syntax`, `a_body_with_no_reading_reports_a_misplaced_rewrite_before_another_violation` and `a_body_with_no_misplaced_construct_reports_its_own_violation` in [inner_rules.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/inner_rules.rs); `a_reported_violation_does_not_depend_on_a_pass_through_renumbering_productions`, `a_reported_scope_error_does_not_depend_on_a_pass_through_below_a_rewrite` and `a_rewrite_reading_below_the_root_does_not_depend_on_a_pass_through_above_it` in [prediction.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/inner/parser/prediction.rs); `a_rule_body_parse_is_one_the_choice_among_well_sorted_readings_keeps` in [rules.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/inner/rules.rs).)

## Line marker productions

A production of sort `#LineMarker` (`syntax #LineMarker ::= r"#line [0-9]+\\n"`) declares which text is a line marker: text that is not a token and that relocates the text after it.
The production fixes only that much: a token regex has no groups, so it does not say where the line number or a file name is in the marker, whether the number names the marker's own line or the next one, whether columns restart, or what `#location` and diagnostics then report.
Any meaning krust gave it would be invented, and the two parsers krust builds must give each program one term, so krust rejects every `#LineMarker` production when it loads the definition, in the module that declares it or in any module the parsed one imports, with `UnsupportedLineMarker` located at the production; `kast`, `kast --gen-parser`, `kcompile --gen-bison-parser` and `krun` refuse the definition alike.
To skip marker lines without renumbering, declare their text as `#Layout`.
The pinned K accepts the production: its generated scanner calls a C function `line_marker` that the user links in (`--bison-file`); krust has no such option.
(`a_line_marker_production_means_one_thing_to_both_parsers` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)

## Token precede and follow restrictions

A regex terminal in a KAST JSON document (`KRegexTerminal`) may carry `precedeRegex` and `followRegex` fields.
The value `"#"` restricts nothing, and krust reads it as no field.
Any other value makes the token's language depend on the text around it: a precede regex rejects a token that starts where a match of it ends, and a follow regex rejects a token that a match of it follows.
No K source syntax writes such a restriction, krust's grammar has no place for one, and neither parser krust builds scans a token by its neighbours, so the KAST JSON reader refuses a document whose regex terminal states one, with a message that names the module, the production (sort, items, label, source and location) and each restriction.
Reading the regex alone would accept a larger token language than the document states and say nothing: with an identifier regex restricted not to follow `[A-Za-z0-9_]`, `12abc` is an error, while the unrestricted regex scans it as `12` and `abc`.
krust writes neither field.
The Rust library's `ProductionItem::RegexTerminal` holds only its regex, and its former `precede_regex` and `follow_regex` fields are removed, since a field nothing honours would drop a restriction without a word: a library consumer that builds regex terminals from a grammar of its own that carries such restrictions refuses one that restricts something, as the KAST JSON reader does, instead of dropping it.
The pinned K's Python reader (`pyk`'s `KRegexTerminal`) keeps only the regex and reads such a document as unrestricted, where krust refuses it.
(`kast_json_token_restrictions_are_refused_naming_the_production` in [definition_json.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/definition_json.rs).)

## Configuration value transport

Every configuration-map value is transported as `#KConfigValue(K):KItem`, an ordinary private constructor generated with the partial function `#unboxKConfigValue(KItem):K` and its sole equation `#unboxKConfigValue(#KConfigValue(KS:K)) => KS`.
The generated module and these labels are reserved.
A nullary cell initializer is declared `total` only when every generated rule has evidently defined configuration content and a trivial `ensures`; initializer dependencies are judged by a least fixed point, so cycles remain unproven.
At least one unconditional equation must cover its only argument tuple.
Configuration content may concatenate hooked map or set elements: domains.md makes a map concatenation undefined exactly when its operands share a key, and a set concatenation when they share an element, so a concatenation tree whose leaves are element applications with pairwise distinct literal keys of one sort (integers compared by value, Booleans, strings without escapes) and defined values counts as evidently defined (`issue-946`'s `1 |-> ... 5 |-> ... 8 |-> ...`).
A repeated or differently spelled key (`1`, `+1`), keys of different sorts, or a computed key leave the concatenation undecided, and the initializer without the claim.
Generated cell constructors and optional-cell units use the same equation totality judgement as written labels; hooked collection units count as defined only when their own production is configuration-generated and they head no non-simplification rule, including ordinary, anywhere and macro equations.
Before cell closing, the evidence also checks every child unit that closing could insert, conservatively withholding parent totality even when a child with a partial absence unit is already present.
Local-function resolution precedes this judgement and records which generated lambdas cover every argument tuple; a lambda that does not cover its parameter sort is `\bottom` on every argument its pattern does not match ([Refuted local-function patterns](#refuted-local-function-patterns)).
Both early and deferred lambda totality require a variable pattern whose every semantic cast and recorded inferred sort equal the generated parameter sort; a nested cast or inferred sort narrower than the parameter sort loses `total`, even when its body is defined.
A generated lambda's parameter sort is the least upper bound of the binder's sort and the argument's sort, the least sort that holds both every argument the call passes and every value the equation matches.
When the two sorts have no least upper bound (two incomparable common supersorts, or a sort the definition does not relate), the parameter is `K` if either sort is `K` and `KItem` otherwise, a common supersort; the binder is then covering only if its own sort is that parameter sort.
The binder's sort is the same claim however it is written: a semantic cast, the variable's own sort, or the sort its metadata records (which sort injection gives an unsorted variable); so a sorted variable binder `X:S` compiles like its cast spelling `#SemanticCastToS(X:S)`, and is covering, keeping `total` when its body is defined, exactly when `S` contains the argument's sort.
An unsorted binder matches every term and takes `K`. Only structured input can supply one, since source inference casts every binder; its lambda differs from the source compile of the same rule by the parameter sort, with the argument placed in a one-element `K` sequence, and stays covering.
(`a_sorted_variable_binder_takes_the_least_upper_bound_parameter`, `a_sorted_variable_binder_compiles_like_its_cast_spelling`, `a_metadata_sorted_binder_is_not_covering_over_k` and `a_sorted_let_binder_declares_the_same_lambda_from_source_and_structured_input` in [kompile_passes.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/kompile_passes.rs).)
A lambda body that applies a partial written function counts as defined where that function's only rule is an unconditional equation `f(X1, ..., Xn) => R` whose argument variables, and the cell content variable of a configuration context `[[ .. ]] <c> V </c>`, each match every value of their position, and `R` is defined.
There too a variable's sort is its own, else the sort its metadata records, which is the sort the emitted equation's premise gives it; so a variable sorted only in metadata, narrower than its position, matches only that sort's values, and the function counts as partial.
This judgement only decides lambda bodies: it adds no claim to the written function's own declaration.
(`a_metadata_sorted_argument_does_not_make_a_written_function_total`, `a_lambda_over_a_metadata_partial_function_is_not_total` and `a_metadata_sorted_cell_variable_does_not_cover_its_cell` in [kompile_passes.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/kompile_passes.rs).)
The deferred judgement admits those lambdas and nullary initializers in one least fixed point, so a lambda can use a proven initializer while cycles cannot prove themselves; lambdas already proved total retain their declarations.
An initializer reads `project:S(#unboxKConfigValue(Map:lookup(Init,key)))`, retaining its declared projection, including `project:K` for a K variable and `project:KItem` for an uncast variable.
An actual K sequence, empty K or fixed K-returning function remains the whole K payload; a KItem or a value with a licensed embedding into KItem becomes a singleton K sequence.
The outer placement preserves the instance and macro choice made at the existing KItem preference for a K read.
KItem-to-K placement uses `kseq(item,dotk)`, never an injection.
So `==K` and `=/=K` compare whole `K` values: `dotk` and `kseq` are free constructors, and two constructor-like `K` terms are equal exactly when they are the same term, whether they are the empty sequence, a sequence of several items or items of sort `KItem` itself (`.K ==K .K` is `true`, `(a() ~> b()) ==K a()` is `false`).
A function hooked `KEQUAL.eq` or `KEQUAL.ne` compares two operands of one sort other than `K` as values of that sort in the same way; operands of two different sorts (`Foo` and `Bar`, `KItem` and `K`) can spell one value differently, so such an application is left unevaluated.
The pinned reference's LLVM backend answers such a function over `Int` by representation: with `syntax Bool ::= Int "eqi" Int [function, total, hook(KEQUAL.eq)]`, `01 eqi 1` is `false` there and `1 eqi 1` ends without a result; `01` and `1` are one `Int` value, so the KORE gives `true` for both, which is what k-rust answers (`a_user_symbol_hooked_kequal_is_decided_on_its_own_sort` in [execution_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/execution_semantics.rs)).

For a binding declared at K, the configuration parser prefers a successful KItem reading and tries its selected module's existing K entry only when there is no item reading or no KItem start sort.
The selected module's K entry has sequence syntax only when the module's own grammar declares it; importing `KSEQ` provides it but also `K-TOP-SORT`'s `KItem` path for list sorts, which makes every element of a user list ambiguous with its one-element list; cell contents (ground K values) are parsed with `ProgramParser::for_ground_values`, the module's program grammar plus exactly the K sequence constructors.
Ambiguity, inference, validation and macro errors are retained.
Explicit program start-sort parsing and grammar visibility remain unchanged.
Compiled metadata now records a K-declared variable as K, while a generated `$PGM` parser still starts at KItem for a K declaration.
Supplied values are still parsed, expanded and checked when no initializer reads the map, then dropped because the top initializer is nullary.

Serialized maps and depth-zero initializer patterns expose the box, which ordinary simplification removes from initialized cells.
Already compiled definitions must be recompiled to use this transport format.
The pinned reference emits an unlicensed K-to-KItem injection for whole K values; k-rust's transport preserves their sequence tails and uses only licensed injections for map keys and item embeddings.
N37 removes only this private protocol from the k-rust side of compile comparisons and translates initializer reads back to the reference's item representation.
A translated initializer's body-derived identifier is aligned with the reference only after the complete translated sentence matches, excluding that identifier and the comparison's existing source metadata.
The parsed-definition comparison removes only the exact private module and its imports and translates the same initializer reads before injection.
It counts all rewrites, rejects a reference declaring or using either private transport symbol, and retains unrelated equations, lookups, projections and no-junk alternatives.

## Refuted local-function patterns

`#let P = E #in B` and `#fun(P => B)(E)` denote `B` under the binding that matches `E` against `P`, and nothing when `E` is no instance of `P`: in matching logic, `\exists vars(P). (E = P) /\ B`, which is `\bottom` when `E` cannot equal any instance of `P`.
This is k-rust's definition of the constructs; their builtin declarations (`kast.md`, module `K`) state syntax only.
Local-function resolution lowers each to a generated function `#lambda` applied to `E`, with the equation `#lambda(P, C1, ..., Cn) = B`, where `C1, ..., Cn` are the variables of the enclosing rule that `B` reads.
When `P` covers the parameter sort (a variable whose every cast and inferred sort equal it, [Configuration value transport](#configuration-value-transport)), that equation gives a value on every argument.
Otherwise the Rust target adds the `owise` equation `#lambda(X, C1, ..., Cn) = \bottom`.
Without it the definition would leave the lambda's value off its pattern unconstrained: under the result sort's no-junk axiom that application could be any value of its sort, so a rule could still match it by narrowing (`swap(E) => #let pair(X, Y) = E #in pair(Y, X)` on `swap(3)` narrows `#lambda(3)` against `swap(E)` again at every step), and a stuck `#lambda(3)` would not say that the pattern was refuted.
With it, a step whose result applies a lambda to a value outside its pattern has an undefined result and ends the run: `krun` prints `\bottom` and warns `execution ended with no successor at depth N: rule … applied with an undefined result`.
A function in the initial configuration that does so (`scale(pair(1, 2))` with `scale(E) => #let M:Int = E #in M *Int 2`) makes the initial configuration `\bottom`, and `krun` warns `the initial configuration simplified to \bottom before any rewrite step; check the configuration variables and the functions it applies` ([Undefined instances of the initial configuration](#undefined-instances-of-the-initial-configuration)).
A constructor pattern (`pair(X, Y)` on an `Exp`) and a variable pattern narrower than the parameter sort (`M:Int` on an `Exp`) are refuted alike.
The equation holds only where the lambda's own equation does not apply, and the backend applies it only where that equation is decided not to apply: a symbolic argument, or an application no equation evaluates (`#let pair(X, Y) = h(1) #in …` with `h` defined only at `0`), keeps the lambda application with its definedness open, so no claim is proven vacuously through it; a symbolic configuration holding it can still narrow, and a variable-free one halts as [Concrete rewrite instantiation](#concrete-rewrite-instantiation) states.
A lambda whose body is `#Top` (`#let pair(_X, _Y) = E #in #Top`) gets an equation whose right-hand side is `#Top`, which gives the application no term; the definition is refused when it is loaded ([Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules), on equations with a `#Top` right-hand side), as for a written equation of that shape.
A refuted `#let` in a `requires` clause makes the clause undefined, so the rule does not apply: with `rule r(E) => done(1) requires #let pair(X:Int, _) = E #in X >Int 0`, `r(3)` takes no step, and a function whose conditional equation is refuted so falls to its `owise` equation (`g(3)` is `0` beside `g(_) => 0 [owise]`).
A covering lambda gets no such equation, since no argument reaches it, and keeps its totality judgement; the match predicates `:=K` and `:/=K` already have their `owise` `false` equation.

`#if`, `andThenBool` and `orElseBool` are functions of every operand, so an undefined operand makes the conditional undefined whichever operand the condition selects: with `fst(E) => #if isPair(E) #then (#let pair(X, _) = E #in X) #else 0 #fi`, `fst(3)` is `\bottom`.
Guard a refutable `#let` with `requires` instead: `g(E) => #let pair(X, _) = E #in X requires isPair(E)` and `g(_) => 0 [owise]` give `g(3) = 0`, since the refuted equation is not applied.
Both pinned reference backends give `0` for `fst(3)`; this is a divergence.

The LLVM target (`--backend llvm`) does not emit the `\bottom` equation: an LLVM interpreter already ends a run at an application no equation matches, which realises the same `\bottom`, and the pinned `llvm-kompile` 7.1.337 fails on an `owise` `\bottom` equation.
The semantic KORE comparison drops the equation from the port's side (N49 in [Comparison contract](#comparison-contract)).
(`krun_a_refuted_constructor_let_ends_undefined`, `krun_a_refuted_variable_let_ends_undefined`, `krun_a_refuted_let_in_an_untaken_if_operand_is_undefined`, `krun_a_refuted_let_in_requires_is_not_satisfied` and `kprove_does_not_refute_a_let_whose_bound_value_is_undecided` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs); `a_lambda_whose_pattern_does_not_cover_its_parameter_is_bottom_off_it` in [local_function_syntax.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/local_function_syntax.rs); `only_the_rust_target_states_the_refutation_equation_of_a_local_function` in [kompile_passes.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/kompile_passes.rs).)

## Cells written inside another cell

A rule mentions only the cells it needs, and cell completion places each one where the configuration declares it, filling in the cells the rule omits.
A cell `C` written directly inside a cell `P` that the configuration does not place it under states something no configuration satisfies (`C` inside `P`), so completion must decide what the rule means.
When the configured parent `Q` of `C` is a strict ancestor of `P`, Rust places `C` as a child of the instance of `Q` that holds `P`: every cell that contains `C` in the compiled rule also contains `P`, so the compiled rule keeps every containment fact the rule writes except the impossible one, and adds none.
If that instance already has a cell of `C`'s sort that is not `multiplicity="*"`, the rule is rejected with a located error.
In a `type="List"` collection the order of the instances is part of what the rule matches, and a rule states that order only among the items written side by side in one cell body and the rest of the list that the cell's `...` stands for (the items, and the variables written beside them, are concatenated in their written order, and the rest is placed where the `...` is written: after them when it follows them, before them when it precedes them).
A term placed in a collection by completion therefore has a stated position only when nothing else may be part of that collection.
The frame `...` stands for the instances no item written beside it is, and in a list those are the instances on the side of the items where it is written: `<rs> <r> R </r> ... </rs>` states the instance first, and `<rs> ... <r> R </r> </rs>` states it last, so a rule written with a leading `...` reads, removes (`<rs> ... (<r> R </r> => .Bag) </rs>`) and adds (`<rs> ... (.Bag => <r> R </r>) </rs>`) an instance at the back. This is the reading the K manual gives these rules, and the one a leading `...` has in a cell holding a `List` value (`<out> ... .List => ListItem(I) </out>` appends), so a `...` written beside the items of a list states the same side in both kinds of list cell. A cell completion builds has no written `...`; its frame follows the cells it holds, so an instance written without its parent (`<r> <rid> I </rid> ... </r>` beside `<k>`) is read at the front. Written on both sides of items of the list (`<rs> ... <r> R </r> ... </rs>`), the `...` would stand for the instances on either side of them, two parts of one list that the rule does not divide, and the item would be matched anywhere in it; such a rule is rejected with a located error naming the cell, which says that a rule matches an instance of a list at one of its ends. A `...` on both sides of a cell that writes no item of its list is the whole list, and is accepted.
The pinned K frontend places the rest of a cell collection's list after the written items wherever the `...` is written, so it reads, removes and adds at the front for a leading `...` as well, and it accepts a `...` on both sides of items, reading it as a trailing one (checked with `kompile --backend haskell` and `krun` on rules that write the instance after a leading `...`, and on both sides of it). Both are recorded divergences: the written position of the `...` is the only thing in such a rule that states which end of the list the instance is at, and a rule that ignored it would match the instance at the front whatever the author wrote, while a `...` on both sides states no end at all. The execution case `list-cell-positions` in `scripts/reference-differential.toml` records the three programs whose results differ (`last`, `drop-last`, `mk-last-get`) as oracle exceptions; `a_list_cell_frame_stands_for_the_rest_of_the_list_where_it_is_written` and `a_list_cell_frame_written_on_both_sides_of_its_items_is_rejected` in `crates/k-rust/tests/execution_semantics.rs` run and search those rules and check the rejection.
A variable written beside the items of a list in a cell body (`<rs> B:Bag <r> R </r> </rs>`, of `Bag` or of no sort; a variable of the instance's cell sort stands for one instance instead, as described below) stands, like the frame, for instances no item written there is, and states their place the same way: they are the part of the list between the items written before it and those written after it. `<rs> B:Bag <r> R </r> </rs>` reads the back instance, `<rs> <r> R </r> B:Bag </rs>` the front one, `<rs> B:Bag (.Bag => <r> R </r>) </rs>` adds at the back, and `<rs> <r> R </r> B:Bag <r> S </r> </rs>` reads the front and the back. In a cell that has other children the variable may stand for children of several sorts (it is `Bag`), and its part of the list is placed where it is written just the same.
A rule matches a list by the items at its two ends around at most one part that a term stands for: the items before that part are the first instances of the list, those after it the last, a split at each end. Two such parts in the list a rule matches (two variables, or a variable and the frame, on whichever sides of the items, or with no item) divide the instances between them in a way the rule does not state, and an item between them could be any instance; such a rule is rejected with a located error naming the cell and both terms, which says that at most one variable or `...` may be written in a list. A list built on the right side of a rule's rewrite is not matched: it is the concatenation of its parts, and any number of them may be variables. The right side of a claim is accepted on the same terms, though it is the pattern the final states are checked against: there two parts state that the list has the items somewhere between them (`<rs> (L:Bag => ?A:Bag <r> R </r> ?C:Bag) </rs>`, an instance anywhere in the list), and the prover decides that meaning. A list value is a finite sequence and concatenation is associative, so such a pattern equals a final state's list exactly when its items are elements of that list in their written order and each run of parts between two items (or an end) is the elements between them: the implication check tries each placement of the items at the positions of the state list's items, a disjunct whose equations bind the parts to cuts of the state's list, and a placement that holds proves the claim (`?A <r> 1 </r> ?C` against `<r> 1 </r> <r> 0 </r> L` binds `?A` to `.List` and `?C` to `<r> 0 </r> L`, whatever `L` is). Where the state's list has no part of its own, each of its elements is at a known position, so no placement means that no instance is in the destination, and the claim is disproved; where it has one, an item may lie inside it, or an adjacency hold where it is empty, which no placement states, so the equation itself stays one more disjunct, and the claim is left unproven where no placement holds. The pinned `kprove` proves neither such a claim nor the one-part claim `<rs> (<r> <rid> 0 </rid> </r> L:Bag => <r> <rid> 1 </rid> </r> ?C:Bag) </rs>` after `mk(1)` adds `<r> <rid> 1 </rid> </r>` at the front, which krust proves by the split at the list's front: it reports both stuck, "The configuration's term doesn't unify with the destination's term" (checked with `kompile --backend haskell` and `kprove`). Each final state those claims reach is in their destination by the reading above, so proving them is a recorded divergence (`a_claim_finds_a_collection_cell_instance_between_two_parts_at_any_position` in `crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs`; the placements in `crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/matching.rs`).
The pinned K frontend places such a variable before the items wherever it is written: `<es> (<e> I </e> => .Bag) B:Bag </es>` removes the back instance, as `<es> B:Bag (<e> I </e> => .Bag) </es>` does, and `<es> <e> I </e> B:Bag <e> J </e> </es>` compiles to a list pattern with the variable first, on which its Haskell backend stops with an error (checked with `kompile --backend haskell` and `krun`). It rejects two such variables in one cell, and such a variable beside the cell's `...` (`<es> B:Bag <e> I </e> ... </es>`, `<es> ... <e> I </e> B:Bag </es>`, `<es> B:Bag ... </es>`), with `AC matching of multiple cell variables not yet supported`, so krust's rejection of two parts, above, is not a divergence, and it does not parse a variable of the collection sort in a cell body (`B:ECellList`). Placing the variable where it is written is a recorded divergence: its written position is the only thing in such a rule that states on which side of it the items are, and a reading that ignored it would match `<rs> <r> R </r> B:Bag </rs>` and `<rs> B:Bag <r> R </r> </rs>`, which write the instance at opposite ends, at the same end. The rejection of two parts loses no rule that ran: a list pattern with two rests, which such a rule would compile to, stops a run with an undecided remainder whenever the rule is tried. `a_list_cell_variable_stands_for_the_part_of_the_list_where_it_is_written` and `a_list_cell_variable_beside_another_part_of_the_list_is_rejected` in `crates/k-rust/tests/execution_semantics.rs` run and search such rules and check the rejection.
In a `type="Set"` or `type="Map"` collection, and in any set or map value, the right side of a claim may likewise write several parts beside the items (`<rs> (<r> <rid> 1 </rid> </r> => ?A:Bag <r> <rid> 2 </rid> </r> ?C:Bag) </rs>`, or `?A:Set SetItem(2) ?C:Set`). Set and map concatenation is associative and commutative with the empty collection as unit, so existential parts that the destination names nowhere else (in no item, no other part, no other cell and no `ensures`) stand together for their union, and the destination states nothing of them but that union: it holds exactly when the destination with one part in their place does, with any split of that part's value as the witness. The prover merges such parts into the first of them and binds the others to the empty collection (`merge_free_parts` in `crates/k-rust-backend/src/matching/collections.rs`, applied to the collection pairs of an implication's match remainder), so with 0 to 3 items beside two or three such parts the claim is proven where its items are instances of the final state, in any written order (every part empty where every instance is an item), and left unproven where an item is not. A part the destination also names elsewhere is constrained there, so no one split decides the destination: it is not merged, the equation stays an obligation, and a claim that needs another split than the one the merge would take is left unproven, not disproved (`?A:Set SetItem(2) ?C:Set` with `ensures 1 in ?C`, or with another cell `<c> 1 => size(?C) </c>`), while one whose other occurrence no split satisfies is not proven (`?C` also the value `SetItem(9)` of another cell). The pinned `kprove` proves none of these claims with two or more parts, with or without items, though it proves the one-part claim `<rs> (<r> <rid> 1 </rid> </r> => <r> <rid> 2 </rid> </r> ?C:Bag) </rs>` after `mk(2) ; mk(3)`: it reports each stuck, "The configuration's term unifies with the destination's term, but the implication check between the conditions has failed" (checked with `kompile --backend haskell` and `kprove` on `type="Set"` and `type="Map"` collections and on plain `Set` and `Map` cells). Each final state those claims reach is in their destination by the reading above, so proving them is a recorded divergence (`existential_parts_beside_set_or_map_items_are_one_part` and `a_part_named_elsewhere_in_the_destination_is_not_merged` in `crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs`; the merge in `crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/matching.rs`).

A destination set or map with items, against a final state's set or map whose items are all known (it has no part of its own), is decided by placing the destination's items on the state's items. The state is defined on each of the antecedent's instances, so the items of its set (the keys of its map) are pairwise distinct there, and the destination equals that collection on an instance exactly when each destination item equals a distinct state item (for a map, key and value) and the destination's part holds the state's items no destination item took, or, where the destination has no part, none is left: such a placement makes the destination's items distinct and disjoint from its part, so the destination is defined and equal to the state's collection, and conversely every item of an equal destination is a state item. The implication check therefore tries each placement, one disjunct of item equations and the part's binding (`item_subject_expansion` in `crates/k-rust-backend/src/matching/collections.rs`); a placement on an item that cannot match has no disjunct, and an item identical to a state item is placed on it alone. A state item whose identifier is symbolic is thus decided by its equation: after `mk(N)` with `N` in (4, 6), `<rs> (<r> <rid> 1 </rid> </r> => <r> <rid> 5 </rid> </r> ?C:Bag) </rs>` holds because `N` is 5 on every instance, `?C` being the instance 1, and so does `?A <r> <rid> 5 </rid> </r> ?C` with the two parts read as one (the merge above is kept when the collection solve is left undecided, since it is an equivalence by itself); after `mk(N) ; mk(M)` with `M` in (6, 8), the destination's `<r> <rid> 7 </rid> </r>` is `N`'s instance or `M`'s, and the second placement holds; the same holds of `SetItem(5) ?C` and `5 |-> 5 ?C` in plain `Set` and `Map` cells after an `add(N)` that adds `N`. Where no placement holds on some instance (`N` in (4, 7), which may be 6; a map value other than `N`), the claim is not proven (over plain cells it is disproved), and neither is a destination with no part that leaves a state item out. The pinned `kprove` proves only the one-part claim over a `type="Set"` or `type="Map"` collection with one symbolic instance: it reports the two-part claims, the claims with two symbolic instances and the claims over plain cells stuck, with "The configuration's term unifies with the destination's term, but the implication check between the conditions has failed" or "The configuration's term doesn't unify with the destination's term" (checked with `kompile --backend haskell` and `kprove`), and reports the claims krust disproves stuck the same way. Each final state the proven claims reach is in their destination by the reading above, so proving them is a recorded divergence (`a_set_or_map_item_with_a_symbolic_identifier_is_found_by_its_equations` and `a_plain_set_or_map_item_with_a_symbolic_value_is_found_by_its_equations` in `crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs`; the placements in `crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/matching.rs`).

A claim whose destination is a disjunction holds where, on each instance, one of the disjuncts does, and each disjunct of a set or map is decided as that destination alone would be: its free parts beside items read as one part and its items placed on a final collection of items alone, as above. So `<s> .Set => (?A:Set SetItem(2) ?C:Set) #Or (SetItem(9) ?D:Set) </s>` after `add(1) ; add(2) ; add(3)` holds by its first disjunct, and `(?A:Set SetItem(5) ?C:Set) #Or (?B:Set SetItem(6) ?D:Set)` after `add(N)` with `N` in (4, 7) holds by its first disjunct where `N` is 5 and by its second where `N` is 6, though neither disjunct holds alone; the same for maps. A claim where some instance is in no disjunct is disproved (`N` in (4, 8), which may be 7), as is one whose part named in another cell no split satisfies. The pinned `kprove` reports these disjunctive claims stuck with one of the two messages above, and proves only those whose disjunct holds with a single part beside concrete items (checked with `kompile --backend haskell` and `kprove` on plain `Set` and `Map` cells); proving the others is part of the same recorded divergence (`a_disjunctive_set_or_map_destination_is_decided_disjunct_by_disjunct` in `crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs`).

A rule condition that tests a symbolic item's membership in a set whose elements are all known, such as `notBool I in S` once `S` is `SetItem(N)`, splits a state into the instances where it holds and those where it does not, and each part keeps its side of the condition. Such a set holds exactly the values of its elements, so `Set:in(e, S)` is `true` exactly where `e` and `S` are defined and `e` equals one of them, and `false` exactly where both are defined and it equals none; the simplifier states the membership as those equations (`set_membership_equations` in `crates/k-rust-backend/src/simplify.rs`), which the solver reads as equalities of their sort (of integers here) where the membership itself would be an opaque Boolean to it. A part whose condition contradicts the other constraints then has an unsatisfiable condition, denotes no state, and is pruned: after `add(N)` with `N` in (4, 6), `add(M)` with `M` in (6, 8) is stuck only where `M` is `N`, which the ranges exclude, so `add(N) ; add(M)` reaches `.K` on every instance, and the claims that the final set has the item 7, or an item above 3 when `M` is in (0, 2), hold; where the ranges overlap, the instance `M = N` is stuck and the claim is not proven. Path conditions and residuals state such a membership as its equations (`M = N`, `M = L \/ M = N`) rather than as the membership. The pinned `kprove` leaves the part where `M` is in `SetItem(N)` stuck beside the disjoint ranges, "The configuration's term doesn't unify with the destination's term" (checked with `kompile --backend haskell` and `kprove`, on the one-step claim from `SetItem(N)` and on the two-step claims, where it is already stuck at `add(N)` on `N in .Set`), and it leaves stuck in the same way a step whose rule requires `I in S`, where the ranges make `I` equal an element of `SetItem(N) SetItem(L)`. Each instance of the claims krust proves reaches their destination, so proving them is a recorded divergence (`a_guarded_add_has_no_stuck_branch_where_the_ranges_keep_its_item_out_of_the_set` in `crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs`; the equations in `membership_in_a_set_of_written_elements_is_equality_with_one_of_them` in `crates/k-rust-backend/src/simplify.rs`).

A destination set or map with items, against a final collection that is an update of a map or a union of a set with a set of items rather than a collection of items, is decided by writing that collection as the items it holds beside the rest. `M[K <- V]` binds `K` to `V` and every other key as `M` does, so it equals `K |-> V` beside `M[K <- undef]`, which does not bind `K`; along `M[K1 <- V1][K2 <- V2]` the outer update wins for a key both write, and the chain is read as items only while its keys are identical or constructor-like and different, so the items have distinct keys. Every key, value and element is read at the collection's own sorts, as the update stores it: an update declared with a key or value operand of a strict subsort of the map's key or value sort (`Map "[" Val "<-V" KItem "]"` on a map of `KItem` keys) writes its injection, so `v()` written by it and `inj{Val, KItem}(v())` written by `_[_<-_]` are one key, bound to the outer update's value, and the rest is the map without that key, removed with the `MAP.remove` declared on the map's sort and key sort; an update whose key or value is of no subsort of those sorts, or whose map operand is of another map sort, ends the chain as an opaque part of the rest, and without such a `MAP.remove` no chain is read. `A |Set B` with `B` a set of items equals `B` beside `A -Set B`, and a chain of such unions likewise, while `B` is a set of the collection's own definition, the other operand is of its sort, and its `SET.difference` is declared on two sets of its sort; a union with a set of another definition ends the chain (`a_chain_of_updates_of_other_sorts_is_read_at_the_collection_sorts` in `crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs`). The equation holds wherever the final collection is defined, which it is on each of the antecedent's instances, so the claim is then decided as against that collection (`subject_collection_form` in `crates/k-rust-backend/src/implication.rs`): after `add(2)` from an arbitrary map `M`, `<m> M => 2 |-> 2 ?C:Map </m>` holds with `?C` the map `M[2 <- undef]`, and so do `?A:Map 2 |-> 2 ?C:Map`, the claim that also requires `notBool 2 in_keys(M)`, `2 |-> 2 3 |-> 3 ?C:Map` after `add(2) ; add(3)`, and `SetItem(2) ?C:Set` of `S |Set SetItem(2)`, and a destination of parts alone, `?A:Map ?C:Map`, read as one part; a destination item with another value or key, the inner key of `N[K <- 1][L <- 2]` with `K` and `L` symbolic (which `L` overrides where they are equal), or both items of `(S |Set SetItem(K)) |Set SetItem(L)` with `K` and `L` symbolic (one item where they are equal), is not proven, and an update whose key a destination item equals only under the path condition (`M[N <- N]` against `5 |-> 5 ?C` with `N` 5) is not decided either. The pinned `kprove` leaves each of these claims stuck, "The configuration's term unifies with the destination's term", the implication check between the conditions failing (checked with `kompile --backend haskell` and `kprove`, including on the claims krust does not prove). Each instance of the claims krust proves reaches their destination, so proving them is a recorded divergence (`a_destination_item_written_by_a_final_update_or_union_is_found_beside_the_rest` in `crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs`).

A term *may be or hold an instance* of a cell sort `S` when it is a cell of sort `S`; a cell of a sort above `S` that is a variable, a complete cell or a function result, or that is written with an open `...` (which stands for its unwritten children) or with an item that may; a rewrite with an item on either side that may, or an `#as` whose pattern or alias may; or a term whose cell sort is unknown (a variable of `Bag`, of a cell collection sort or of no cell sort, a function of `Bag` sort, a matching-logic connective) that may stand for an instance of `S` or of a sort above it.
An empty bag may not, and neither may a cell of any other sort, whatever its body: after completion it holds only the cells the configuration places in it (a misplaced cell has been lifted out of it), and none of those is `S` or above it.
A term of unknown cell sort written in a body of a cell `P` stands for children of that instance of `P` that no item written there is, on its side of the rule (an item outside a rewrite is on both sides; a `multiplicity="*"` child is never used up, since the instance holds any number), so in a body of the cell that is to hold the placed cells it may be or hold an instance of `S` only through such a free child of sort `S` or above it: a `Bag` variable written beside `<x>`, `<z>` and `<p>` in an `<a>` that has only those children stands for nothing, and cannot hold the `<fr>` cells below `<p>`.
The decision follows the term item by item: a rewrite of `<sp>` cells whose sides also hold a `Bag` variable may hold `<fr>` cells, though its cells are all `<sp>`, when a child at or above `<fr>` is free there.
A lifted cell that may be or hold an instance of a `type="List"` child of the instance that holds it is placed only when no other item there may, or every other item there that may was lifted out of the same written parent (they keep their written order); the rule is rejected otherwise.
A cell `C` written beside a cell of one of its configured ancestors is placed inside the closest such written cell `H` when the rule writes exactly one cell of `H`'s sort there and no cell on the configuration's path from the enclosing cell down to `H` is `multiplicity="*"`: the enclosing cell then holds one instance of each cell on that path, so `H` is the only cell that can hold `C`.
If that written cell is closed (no `...`) or is not a cell body (a rewrite, a variable, a complete cell), the rule is rejected with a located error.
The cells moved into `H` from one body form one run in their written order. When an item of the run may be or hold an instance of a `type="List"` cell below `H` (a cell above that cell brings its own list and does not count), the run is placed only when nothing in `H` may be part of the same list, and the rule is rejected otherwise. To decide it, the written cells on the path that the run goes into (cell bodies of sorts above the list's cell that are not `multiplicity="*"`, hence the only ones of their sort) are looked into without their `...`, which is the rest of the list, placed beside the run where it is written, as beside a written run; every other item of `H` is decided as above.
An item of the run is decided the same way, with the reading of a term of unknown cell sort above: a term of unknown cell sort in it is a sibling of the item's cells, so it stands for free children of an instance of their configured parent `P`, on its side of the rule. When `P` is `H`, that instance is `H`, whose items are its body and the run, so the term may be part of the list only through a child of `H` at or above the list that none of them is on that side (when the written `D`, the child of `H` on the path to the list, is not `multiplicity="*"`, it is the only one, and the term cannot stand for another). When `P` is below `H` and `H` holds `D` on that side, the item goes into the written `D`, or into a written cell below it, and is decided again there; otherwise the rule is rejected: `D` cannot take the item (it is closed or not a cell body), or the item stays where it is written and completion would need a second `D` (as when an ordered `multiplicity="*"` cell lies between `D` and the item). When `H` does not hold `D` on that side, the instance of `P` holds only what the item writes.
If `multiplicity="*"` cells lie on the path below `H`, the rule writes `C` in some instance of the topmost one, `R`, inside `H`, and completing `C` there builds a fresh instance of `R` in the one instance of `R`'s parent that `H` holds: beside `H`'s `...` when `R` is a child of `H`; when written or fresh cells lie between `H` and `R`, inside the lowest of them, beside that cell's `...` (a written cell on the path is the one `C` goes into, and completion builds the missing ones, as it builds every omitted cell).
`C` is moved into `H` whatever lies on the path below `H`: written beside the only cell that can hold it, `C` states nothing a spelling with `C` written inside `H` does not, so the two spellings are one rule, and both are completed inside `H` by the same reading of which instance of `R` holds `C` (in particular, when `R` is `type="List"`, the fresh instance gets the position the convention below gives it in either spelling). Whether that reading is a fresh instance or a rejection is decided inside `H`, as for the written-inside spelling, and the same rule results in both cases.
Whenever a `multiplicity="*"` cell lies on the path above `H`, the rule does not state which instance holds `C`; `C` is then completed where it is written, in fresh instances of its missing ancestors, and the rule is rejected when that needs a second instance of a cell the configuration allows only once there; otherwise `C` is in a fresh instance of the `multiplicity="*"` cell when no written item can be the one that holds it, and the rule is rejected when one can (below): `H` may be in the instance that holds `C`, or in another.
Completion builds a fresh instance of a `multiplicity="*"` cell around the cells a rule writes without it, as it builds every omitted cell (`<w>` written in `<stack>` is in a fresh `<fr>`).
A fresh instance stands for an instance that no item written beside it is, so it is the rule's reading only when no written item can be the one that holds those cells.
Let `P` be the cell completion builds around a group of cells written without it, and `R` the closest `multiplicity="*"` cell from `P` up (`P` itself, or a cell above it below the enclosing cell; without one, the enclosing cell holds one `P`, and a written `P` has taken the cells in already).
A written item of `P`'s sort beside the group may be the `P` that holds it when, on each side of the rule (the built `P` is on both), it is a cell of `P` that writes none of the group's children of `P` that are not `multiplicity="*"` there and has an open `...` or a term of unknown cell sort to stand for them, or it is a term that stands for one whole `P` (a variable of `P`'s cell sort, a function result, an alias of one cell decided as its cell).
Such an item may also be written inside a written cell above `P` (a rewrite of a cell above `P` replaces that one cell, so the `P` it holds is on both of its sides when one side writes it and each side writes it or leaves it unwritten, in a written cell with an open `...` or a term of unknown cell sort or in a term that stands for a whole cell above it (a variable of that cell's sort); unwritten, it has none of its children written there, in the frame on the left and built with their initial values on the right, so the group's cells may be in it: `(<r> <rid> 1 </rid> <a2> <y> 1 </y> ... </a2> ... </r> => <r> <rid> 2 </rid> ... </r>)` beside `<d> 5 => 6 </d>`; a `P` removed or added, with no cell of its rewritten cell's sort on the other side, does not count): it is then a written `P` of the rule all the same, and the group's cells may be in it.
Then the rule writes the group in either and does not state which, and it is rejected with a message naming the group, `R` and the written item: with `<thread multiplicity="*">` holding `<id>` and `<env>`, `<thread> <id> 1 </id> ... </thread> <env> E => 0 </env>` (the `<env>` can be thread 1's or another thread's); with `<r multiplicity="*">` holding `<a2>`, which holds `<y>` and `<d>`, `<a2> <y> 1 </y> ... </a2> <d> 5 => 6 </d>` (the `<d>` can be in the `<r>` of the written `<a2>` or in another), and likewise `<r> <rid> 1 </rid> <a2> <y> 1 </y> ... </a2> ... </r> <d> 5 => 6 </d>`, where the written `<a2>` is in a written `<r>`.
A written cell that already holds such a child, one that is closed with no term of unknown cell sort in it, a complete cell, and a cell the rule removes or adds (it is on one side only) cannot be that `P`; a fresh instance is then the only reading and the rule compiles with it, as `lambda.k`'s `<k>` and `<tenv>` written beside the `<task>` cells the rule adds.
Nor can an item that denotes one value on both sides of the rule (a variable, or a function result with no rewrite in it, written outside every rewrite, or one named variable written on both sides of a rewrite) when the group writes, in a child of `P` that is not `multiplicity="*"`, values on the two sides that are apart: that `P` would hold both values in its one cell, so the reading with the group in it has no instance. With `<r multiplicity="*">` holding `<p>`, which holds `<c>`, `V:PCell <c> 5 => 6 </c>` compiles with a fresh `<r>`, and `V:PCell <c> E => 6 </c>` is rejected (`E` may be `6`).
Values are *apart* when that is decided without a solver: two tokens of one sort whose values differ (different strings of an unhooked sort; integers of different value under `INT.Int`; `true` and `false` under `BOOL.Bool`); applications of two free constructors of one sort (a label declared at one sort other than `K` that is unhooked or is `KItem`, none of whose productions is a function, a token, a bracket, a hook, a macro, an overload or an `assoc`, `comm`, `idem` or `unit` operation, and that no visible rule matches; `KItem`'s hook declares the sort of single sequence items and relates none of its values); one free constructor applied to arguments of which a pair is apart; and `~>` sequences whose items, read from the front while each is a token or a free constructor application, have a pair apart, or, when both are written to their end, differ in length. A cell with children is apart from the same cell on the other side when one of its children that is not `multiplicity="*"` is written once in each and is apart; a leaf cell's content is compared as a sequence, up to the shorter one when either is open on the right, and not at all when either is open on the left. Anything else (a variable, a function, tokens of another hooked sort, terms of different sorts, a cell written on one side only) may be equal, and the item may still hold the group.
A term of unknown cell sort (a `Bag` variable, the frame `...`) is not such an item: it stands for any number of instances, and the fresh instance beside it already denotes the one among them that holds the group.
This rejection is a recorded divergence from the pinned K frontend, which compiles such a rule with a fresh instance: that reading drops the one in which the written item holds the cells, the instance the author most likely meant, so the compiled rule never applies to a configuration with one instance. A rule means one reading by writing the cells inside the instance that holds them, or by writing their own instance.
Cells a rule writes without their parent, side by side in one body, are completed in instances of that parent as follows.
A rewrite of cells of several sorts in one bag, `(L => R)`, is completed as one rewrite `(L_s => R_s)` per cell sort `s`, `L_s` and `R_s` being its cells of sort `s` in written order (the empty bag when there are none): a rule means its two projections, and within a bag the place of a rewrite is immaterial, so the two spellings are one rule, whether the parent is omitted, written closed or written open.
Likewise a removal `(X => .Bag)` and an addition `(.Bag => Y)` of one child of an instance that is not `multiplicity="*"`, when nothing else in the instance writes that child, are the one rewrite `(X => Y)` of it; completed apart, the instance would hold the child twice.
Two items *can share* an instance of their parent `P` when they write no common child of `P` that is not `multiplicity="*"` on the same side of the rule (an item outside a rewrite is on both sides). This never reads the direction of a rewrite, so swapping the sides of every rewrite of a rule swaps them in the compiled rule and changes nothing else.
Items that can share are in one instance, a convention of completion: the instances are the classes of "can share" when that relation is an equivalence, in the order of their first items. A class is never placed in a written instance: when one may hold it, the rule is rejected, as above. With `<th multiplicity="*">` holding an optional `<o>`, `(<o> 1 </o> => .Bag) <o> 2 </o> (.Bag => <o> 3 </o>)` compiles to two `<th>`, one rewriting `<o> 1 </o>` to `<o> 3 </o>` and one holding `<o> 2 </o>`, as the spelling with those two `<th>` written does.
When an item can share an instance with two items that cannot share one, the rule does not state which instance holds it, and it is rejected with a message naming `P` and the items: with `<r multiplicity="*">` holding `<s>` and `<a>`, the `<s>` of `<s> 1 </s> <a> <e> 1 </e> ... </a> <a> <d> 0 </d> ... </a>` can be in the `<r>` of either `<a>`.
A term of unknown cell sort written in a cell body stands only for children of that cell that no written item is, on its side of the rule; the body's frame `...` is such a term.
When two of them on one side can both stand for one child that is not `multiplicity="*"`, the rule means the union of its readings over the ways of giving that child to one of them.
When each of them is *carried*, that is, it is the frame, or a variable written once on each side of the rule, in that body, and nowhere else, every reading gives the same relation (what a carried term stands for is matched and kept unchanged, and read nowhere), so completion gives the child to the first such variable in written order, and to the frame only when no variable can take it: `<a> <y> Y </y> B:Bag ... </a>` compiles as `<a> <y> Y </y> ... </a>`.
Otherwise a variable that can take the child is not carried (it is written on one side only, twice, in a condition, inside a leaf), its readings differ (it is that child, or it is empty), nothing in the rule chooses one, and the rule is rejected with a message naming the variable and the child: `<a> <y> Y </y> (B => .Bag) ... </a>`, where `B` and the frame can both stand for `<z>`.
The compiled rule keeps every written variable it can: a variable written on both sides of the rule is on both sides of the compiled rule or on neither, and only a variable that can stand for nothing but cells completion writes in its place leaves it (a carried variable that is given no child, or a variable that stands for several children, which the compiled rule names one by one).
An alias `P #as V` written in a cell body denotes `P` and binds `V` to it, so when `P` is one cell it takes the child `P` takes, and the rule compiles as the rule without the alias, with `V` bound: `<a> (<y> Y </y> #as YC) ... </a>`, and an alias of one `multiplicity="*"` instance in its collection, which is the collection item holding the instance (keyed by the instance's key cell in a `Map`) with `V` bound to the cell inside it; where `V` stands on the right side, the item is rebuilt from `V` and, in a `Map`, keyed by the key cell `P` matched (an alias inside it standing for its variable), which is `V`'s key, so the key is not computed from `V` again at each application. `V` then names one cell, so it is of that cell's sort (`YC:YCell`) wherever it occurs in the rule (in another cell, in a condition), whatever sort it took from the cell body it is written in (`Bag`, the sort of any number of cells). A variable written as the alias of cells of two sorts is rejected, naming it and both cells: its one value would be both cells, and cells of two sorts have two labels, so the rule never applies.
An alias written inside the pattern of an instance, such as in its key cell (`(<r> (<rid> I </rid> #as K) <v> V </v> </r> #as R)`), is read the same way: `K` names the cell its pattern matches, and in a `Map`, whose items are keyed by that key cell, the item's key is matched as the pattern the alias aliases, with `K` bound to the key found. The rule then applies exactly where the rule without the alias applies, with the same results, in a `Map`, a `Set` and a `List` (`an_alias_inside_a_collection_cell_instance_matches_what_its_pattern_matches` in [execution_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/execution_semantics.rs)).
The pinned K frontend rejects every alias written in a cell body while it concretizes the configuration, whether at the top of the rule, in an open cell or inside an instance (`Unexpected kind of term found in cell. Expected variable, apply, or rewrite; found KAs`, or, in a cell whose other children the rule writes, `Closed parent cell missing required children`). Accepting these rules is a recorded divergence: the alias is the conjunction of its pattern with a variable, which matches what the pattern matches and binds the variable to it, so such a rule has the one meaning of the rule without the alias with that variable bound, and accepting it changes the meaning of no rule the frontend accepts.
A variable of a `multiplicity="*"` cell's sort written in that cell's collection (`<rs> (R:RCell => .Bag) ... </rs>`) stands for one instance, so it is the collection item holding that instance, placed where a cell written there is: on the left side the pattern of one item, keyed in a `Map` by a fresh variable, which the map's keying of every item by its instance's key cell ties to the instance `R` matches, and on the right side the item built from `R`'s value, keyed in a `Map` by the key read from it. The rule then applies exactly where the rule that writes the instance's cells in its place applies, with the same results, in a `Map`, a `Set` and a `List` (`a_left_side_variable_of_a_collection_cell_sort_matches_one_instance` in [execution_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/execution_semantics.rs)). The pinned K frontend compiles such a left-side variable as an injection of the cell sort into the collection sort, which is no collection item, and its execution of such a rule fails; this is a recorded divergence. A search pattern (`krun --search --pattern '<rs> R:RCell ... </rs>'`) is matched against states as a left side is, so such a variable written in it is read the same way, with the minted key anonymous (its binding is not reported): the pattern matches where the pattern that writes the instance's cells matches, with `R` bound to that instance (`a_search_pattern_variable_of_a_collection_cell_sort_matches_one_instance`). The pinned K frontend compiles it as the same injection, and its search reports no match (`#Bottom`) on one instance and on three, in a `Map`, a `Set` and a `List`, where the written cells match; this is the same recorded divergence.
