Wherever it is written, the alias of one cell is placed as that cell is: wrapped in the cell's missing parents when written above them (`(<thread> <id> 1 </id> ... </thread> #as TC)` at the top level), lifted to the cell's configured parent when written in a cell below it, and completed with the cells written beside it that belong inside it, when it is the only cell of its sort there (`(<a> <y> Y </y> ... </a> #as A) <z> Z </z>` puts `<z>` in the aliased `<a>`). `V` then names the cell the alias's pattern is in the completed rule, the one the alias names. A cell placed inside the alias's pattern this way that holds a rewrite (`(<u> <ux> 1 </ux> ... </u> #as U) <wx> 3 => 4 </wx>`, with `<wx>` inside the one `<u>`) is rejected with a message naming the cell and the alias: the pattern is matched and `V` stands where it is on the right side, so that rewrite has no right side, as a rewrite written inside an `#as` pattern has none.
An alias of several cells (`<a> ((<y> Y </y> <z> Z </z>) #as F) ... </a>`) would name a part of the instance that is not one of its children, and has no term of its own there; such a rule is rejected with a message naming the alias.
These two rejections are recorded divergences from the pinned K frontend, which accepts such rules: a variable that is not carried and competes for a child gives the rule more than one meaning, and an alias of several cells names no term of the compiled rule; a rule that means one reading must write the cells it means.
In a `type="List"` collection the fresh instance is placed after the instances and the variables written in the same cell body, with the rest of the list where the cell's `...` is written (after them all, unless the `...` is written before the items); several fresh instances in one list are in the order completion builds them.
When the fresh instance is the only item of its list it gets the position of an instance written alone there; otherwise its position is not stated by the rule, which writes cells of an instance and so states only that some instance holds them.
This placement is a convention of completion, kept because rejecting such rules would reject accepted definitions: `pl-tutorial/1_k/5_types/lesson_6/lambda.k` writes `<k>` and `<tenv>` of the current `<task>` beside new `<task>` cells, and compiles to new tasks placed before the current one.
A rule that needs another position must write the instance.
A cell moved into the one written cell that can hold it is completed there as a cell written in that cell's body, so the convention places its fresh instance alike in both spellings: with `<r multiplicity="*" type="List">` holding `<d>` in `<a>`, `<a> <x> X </x> ... </a> <d> 5 => 6 </d>` compiles to the rule `<a> <x> X </x> <d> 5 => 6 </d> ... </a>` compiles to, a fresh `<r>` holding `<d>` before the rest of the list.
A lifted or moved cell is never placed in a list beside a fresh instance: the fresh instance is an item that may be part of that list, so the rule is rejected.
Every other misplaced cell is rejected with a located error: one whose configured parent is not an ancestor of `P` (placing it would put it in another branch the rule never wrote), one written inside a cell of its own sort or inside one of its descendants, and one whose move would cross a rewrite arrow, an `#as` pattern, a K sequence, any other symbol, or the outermost cell of the rule.
Rust never discards a written cell.
The cell and the update it states are part of the rule's meaning: a compiled rule without them matches configurations whose `C` has any content and leaves `C` unchanged, which is a different rewrite relation from the one the rule writes.

This is a recorded divergence from the pinned K frontend, which discards such a cell and compiles the rule as if it did not mention it.
The pinned KMIR semantics has two such rules, `setupCalleeClosure` and `setupCalleeClosure2` (`kmir.md:558-626`), which write `<stack>` inside `<currentFrame>` while the configuration places `<stack>` beside `<currentFrame>` under `<kmir>`.
Rust compiles their `<stack>` under `<kmir>`: the rules apply only when a caller frame is on the stack, and mark its `TUPLE` and `CLOSURE` locals `Moved`.
In the reference's axioms the stack's place holds the variable its completion generates for an unmentioned cell, on both sides of the rewrite.
N45 ([Comparison contract](#comparison-contract)) compares those two axioms with exactly that cell application in the port replaced by the reference's variable.

## Written cell productions

A cell written in a configuration is the declaration of that cell, and declaring it generates the cell sort's one `cell` production, label `<name>`, whose attributes (`cellName`, `maincell`, the multiplicity and collection type, the format) come from the declaration and drive initializing, completing and wrapping the configuration.
The configuration grammar therefore holds no `cell` production: a cell written there always reads as a declaration, never as a term of a `cell` production written beside the configuration.
The sort such a production declares stays a sort of the configuration grammar, so a configuration variable or cast of that sort parses as before.
Expansion then rejects every `cell` production of a declared cell's sort other than the one the configuration of an imported module generated, at that production, naming the production, its cell sort and the configuration that declares the cell.
This includes a written production that repeats the cell's tags, label and items (`syntax TCell ::= "<t>" Int "</t>" [cell, symbol(<t>)]` beside a configuration declaring `<t> 0 </t>`), whatever attributes it writes, `cellName` included: it would stand in for the production the declaration generates, without the declaration's attributes, so the compiled definition would no longer be the one the configuration describes.
A production written under the label an imported module's configuration generated for the cell is a second production of that label, and the definition checks reject it at the written production as a duplicate symbol.
Expansion also rejects, at the production, a written `cell` production of another sort whose tags are a declared cell's (`syntax FooCell ::= "<t>" Int "</t>" [cell]` beside a configuration declaring `<t> 0 </t>`): a cell's tags are its syntax, so the production would be a second syntax of the declared cell, which no rule or configuration writing `<t> ... </t>` could tell from the cell's own.
The pinned K frontend accepts a written `cell` production under the label of the cell's generated production (`symbol(<t>)` above), for an ordinary cell and for a `multiplicity="*"` cell, and rejects one under another label, as Rust does.
It accepts a `cell` production of another sort with a declared cell's tags, and rejects each rule that writes the cell as ambiguous between the two productions.

## Domain values

A literal `#token("s", "S")`, emitted as `\dv{S}("s")`, denotes the element of `S` that `s` names in `S`'s literal domain, and the emitted definition declares that domain only by the attribute `hasDomainValues` on the declaration of `S`'s head.
A sort admits domain values exactly when its head has a token declaration (a `token` production or a `token` sort declaration; for a parametric one, the instances of the declared sort), or it is `Bool` or `KConfigVar`.
`Bool` and `KConfigVar` admit them in every module, even a structured definition without their token productions, because the compiler writes their literals itself: a `requires` clause becomes an equation against `true`, and configuration-map keys are `KConfigVar` literals.
A hook alone does not admit domain values: it fixes how the backend computes on a sort's values, not a literal syntax for them, and `MAP.Map` has a hook and no literals.
A literal at any other sort is rejected by the definition checks with `InvalidDomainValue`, and one a compiler pass writes after them is rejected by term conversion with `InvalidToken`.
The emitted declaration of a head carries `hasDomainValues` exactly when that head admits domain values, so `Bool` and `KConfigVar` carry it even without token productions, and every domain value of the emitted definition is at a sort declared with it.
The backend reads every domain value it holds by the hook its sort is declared with in the loaded definition, not by the sort's name, on every route that builds one: definition load, `kore-exec`, `kore-rpc` and `kore-simplify` requests, search patterns and claims, a function hooked `STRING.string2token`, the hooks' results, the renaming of a `KVar` token, and the solver's models.
A sort carrying a builtin sort hook (`INT.Int`, `BOOL.Bool`, `MINT.MInt`, `FLOAT.Float`, `STRING.String`, `BYTES.Bytes`) has its values read as the builtin's values by the hook, and it stays a sort of its own: its values are not values of the builtin sort, and incomparable sorts keep disjoint carriers.
So the literals of a sort hooked `INT.Int` under another name (`syntax MyInt [hook(INT.Int)]`) are integers, and `07` and `7` are one value whether the definition writes them or `String2MyInt("07")` builds one at run time; a sort named `SortFloat` or `SortMInt` declared without a hook is a token sort whose text is its value on every route.
Every route validates a text for its sort's kind and stores the kind's one canonical text, so equal elements of a sort are one term and no text outside its sort's carrier is held: a text that names no element is refused at load with `InvalidDomainValue`, and a `string2token` function given one has no value the definition gives ([Hook values at the edges of their domains](#hook-values-at-the-edges-of-their-domains)).
A builtin hook declared at a sort whose hook gives its values another kind than the builtin's (a `FLOAT` operation at a sort hooked `FLOAT.Float` under another name, a `STRING` result at a token sort) is not evaluated there: the value the hook computes is a value of its builtin's own sort, not of the application's.
A hook declared at a strict supersort of its value's sort gives that value's injection, the one term of the declared sort that denotes it, since KORE has no implicit subsorting: with `syntax Val ::= Int`, `addV(Int, Int) : Val [hook(INT.add)]` gives `inj{Int, Val}(5)` for `addV(2, 3)`, and so do `KEQUAL.ite` over `Int` branches and the collection hooks that give an `Int` or a `Bool` ([A collection hook's value is a value of its declared result sort](#a-collection-hooks-value-is-a-value-of-its-declared-result-sort)). The hook's value comes first, before any equation of the definition for the symbol, as at the value's own sort.
(`a_hook_value_of_a_strict_subsort_of_the_result_sort_is_its_injection` and `a_subsort_hook_value_takes_precedence_over_equations` in the `hook_result_sorts` [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), `krun_injects_a_hook_value_of_a_subsort_of_the_declared_sort` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)
(`tests::domain_values` in the [backend](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/domain_values.rs), whose tests cover each route, `every_domain_value_is_built_by_a_listed_route` in [term/domain.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/term/domain.rs), and `kprove_proves_nothing_through_a_hooked_int_token_built_from_a_string` and `krun_reads_a_string_to_token_value_by_the_hook_of_its_sort` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)
The sorts named `SortInt` and `SortBool` are the one exception: they are the integers and the Booleans whether or not they carry the hook, because the frontend's generated equations (a `requires` clause, a sort predicate), the backend's SMT translation and its INT and BOOL hooks read these names as the builtin sorts, and a definition compiled with `--no-prelude` declares them without their hooks. So `07` at an unhooked `SortInt` is `7`, and a text that is no decimal integer at `SortInt`, or neither `true` nor `false` at `SortBool`, is refused at load with `InvalidDomainValue` (`unhooked_builtin_names_keep_their_builtin_kind` in [definition.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/definition.rs), `kore_simplify_reads_an_unhooked_sort_int_as_the_integers` and `a_definition_compiled_without_the_prelude_loads_its_unhooked_booleans` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).

A machine-integer literal of `MInt{W}`, `[+-]?[0-9]+[pP][0-9]+` (`domains.md`, `MINT.literal`), names an integer and a width: the digits after `p` or `P` name `W` numerically, and the value is the signed decimal integer before it reduced modulo `2^W`. The carrier of `MInt{W}` is the `2^W` bit patterns of width `W`, and the only element a literal can denote is its integer's two's-complement truncation, the map `Int2MInt` computes. So `256p8`, `0p8` and `0p08` are one element, `-1p8`, `511p8` and `255p8` are one element, and `0377p8` is `121p8` (the digits are decimal).
The backend stores every machine-integer domain value in its canonical text: the residue in `[0, 2^W)` in decimal, a lowercase `p`, and the digits of the sort's width argument as declared (`Sort8` gives `p8`, and the `Sort08` the pinned frontend declares gives `p08`). Literals with equal residues are therefore one term on every in-process route (definition load, `kore-exec`, `kore-rpc` and `kore-simplify` requests, search patterns, claims), so matching, `==K` and the proof engine compare elements, and results print the canonical text.
Load refuses a domain value of a sort hooked `MINT.MInt` with `InvalidDomainValue` when its text is not that literal syntax (a token from a user `token` production or from `#token("abcp8", "MInt{8}")` included), when its suffix names another width than its sort's (`\dv{SortMInt{Sort8{}}}("1p16")`), and when the sort names no width: an argument that is no numeral (`MInt{Foo}`), or a sort hooked `MINT.MInt` under another name (`syntax Word [hook(MINT.MInt)]`), which cannot be parametric. A negative literal of a very wide sort is refused by a resource bound ([Resource bounds](#resource-bounds)).
(`mint_domain_values_are_canonical_at_construction` in [term.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/term.rs), `machine_integer_literals_are_read_as_their_residue_or_refused` and `domain_value_kind_comes_from_the_sort_hook` in [definition.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/definition.rs), and `krun_reads_mint_literals_as_their_residue`, `kprove_proves_nothing_through_two_spellings_of_one_mint_element` and `kprove_proves_nothing_through_two_spellings_of_one_hooked_int_value` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)
A `Float` literal names a rational number, its mantissa times ten to its exponent, rounded to nearest, ties to even, in the format its suffix names (`domains.md`): no suffix, `d` or `D` is binary64 (`p53x11`), `f` or `F` is binary32 (`p24x8`), and `pNxM` has `N` bits of precision, the implicit bit included, and `M` exponent bits, read numerically (`p053x011` is binary64), represented like an IEEE 754 format: `emax = 2^(M-1) - 1`, `emin = 1 - emax`, with subnormals, and overflow to the infinity of the sign. The format is part of the value (`0.5f` and `0.5` are two values), `0.0` and `-0.0` are two values, `Infinity` and `-Infinity` are the format's infinities, and the literal syntax names one `NaN` per format. So `2.0`, `2.0d`, `0.2e1`, `2.0p53x11`, `2.0p053x011` and `2.00000000000000001` are one binary64 value, and `1.0p11x5` and `1.0001p11x5` one p11x5 value.
The backend stores every domain value of a sort hooked `FLOAT.Float` in the canonical text of its value, in every format: for binary32 and binary64 the text the `FLOAT` hooks write (`2.0000000000000000e+00p53x11`, `-0e+00p53x11`, `Infinityp53x11`, `NaNp24x8`), and for every other format the exact decimal `[-]De<Q>p<N>x<M>` of the rounded value `D * 10^Q`, `D` without trailing zeros (`1e0p11x5`; the zeros are `0e0p<N>x<M>` and `-0e0p<N>x<M>`). Every binary value has a finite decimal, so that text is exact; it grows with the value's exponent (the least subnormal of p113x15 writes 11,529 digits). Spellings of one value are therefore one term on every route, so matching, `==K`, map keys and set elements, and the proof engine compare values, and results print the canonical text.
Load refuses with `InvalidDomainValue` a text outside `FLOAT-SYNTAX` and a suffix with a precision or exponent-bits field of 0, which names no format. A format whose field is 1 has no IEEE-like parameters (no trailing significand bit, or `emin > emax`); k-rust does not compute it, and load refuses its literals with the resource refusal, as it does a literal whose canonical text is beyond a bound ([Resource bounds](#resource-bounds)); a `string2token` function given either leaves its application unevaluated, never undefined. The fold leaves unfolded a `Float` result whose canonical text the backend would refuse.
A sort named `SortFloat` without the hook (`syntax Float ::= r"[0-9]+[.][0-9]+" [token]` without `FLOAT-SYNTAX`) is a token sort whose tokens keep their text: `1.0` and `1.00` are two values there, and no `FLOAT` hook reads them, whatever its result sort (`1.0 ==Float 1.00` is not evaluated).
(`float_domain_values_are_canonical_at_construction` in [term.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/term.rs), the [canonicalizer's tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/float/canonical/tests.rs), which check it against the exact rational rounding of the hook models and Rust's binary32 and binary64 parse, `the_backend_canonical_float_text_agrees_with_mpfr` in the backend's [MPFR oracle](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/float/canonical/mpfr_oracle.rs) (test feature `mpfr-oracle`, run in CI), `float_literals_load_as_the_canonical_text_of_their_value`, `an_unhooked_sort_named_sort_float_keeps_its_tokens_text`, `float_literals_that_name_no_element_or_exceed_a_bound_are_refused_at_load`, `string_to_float_builds_the_canonical_text_of_its_value`, `string_to_float_beyond_a_bound_is_too_large_whatever_its_totality`, `a_float_hook_result_is_checked_against_the_kind_of_its_sort` and `float_hooks_read_no_token_of_an_unhooked_sort_float` in the [backend's domain-value tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/domain_values.rs), and `kprove_proves_nothing_through_two_spellings_of_one_float_value`, `kprove_proves_nothing_through_two_spellings_of_one_wide_float_value`, `kprove_does_not_count_one_wide_float_key_twice`, `kprove_keeps_the_tokens_of_an_unhooked_float_sort_distinct`, `krun_compares_float_values_not_spellings` and `krun_reports_a_float_format_it_does_not_compute_as_an_unsupported_hook` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)
[Token admissibility tests](../crates/k-rust/tests/token_admissibility.rs) pin the admitted sorts and the rejection of a hooked sort's literal.

## Token productions over a phrase

An unlabelled `token` production whose only item is a nonterminal, `syntax Toks ::= Names [token]`, spells each phrase of `Names` as a domain value of `Toks` whose text is the input the phrase spans; it builds no term of `Names`.
So no `Names` value is a `Toks` value, and the production states no subsort: it is in the syntactic subsort relation (a `Names` phrase stands where `Toks` is written) and not in the semantic one, the compiled definition has no `subsort{SortNames{}, SortToks{}}` axiom and no `inj{SortNames{}, SortToks{}}`, and the no-junk axiom of `Toks` is that of a sort of domain values.
A sort of domain values may have no subsort in a loaded definition (the backend's verification rejects one), and here none is stated: the pinned reference emits the subsort axiom, and its backend then rejects the compiled definition, where k-rust compiles and runs it.
In a rule or a configuration, such a phrase is read as program text only: each nonterminal of the module's own `token` productions derives there only the module's own syntax, and none of what the rule or configuration grammar adds to it (a variable, a configuration variable, a `#token` literal, a cast, a rewrite, a generated bracket, sort predicate or projection, a record pattern, a cell, or a user list's written terminator `.Names`, which a program writes as no text at all).
A token's value is its text, and every `Toks` value a program produces is the text of a phrase of the module's own syntax; a reading through one of those constructs would make a token of the construct's spelling, which no program produces, and would lose the construct itself (a variable to bind, a configuration variable to read).
So `T:Toks` in a rule is the variable `T`, `$PGM:Pgm` with `Pgm ::= Toks` is the configuration variable, whose program krun places, `#token("1 . 2", "Toks")` is that literal and nothing else, and `run 1 . X` has no reading, while `run 1 . 2` is still the `Toks` token `1 . 2`.
The parse error of such a rule says so when the phrase would read the construct at the declared sort of its nonterminal and go on: at `X` in `run 1 . X`, the phrase expects a `Name` that only a rule's variable begins, and with `Tree ::= Tree "<+>" Tree [token]`, after the bracket `(done)` in `(done) <+> done`, only the phrase would go on with `<+>`.
It adds that a token's text is fixed, so a rule spells the token as program text or as a whole as a `#token` literal of the phrase's sort.
When the token before the failure could also be read as another lexeme that goes on with the failing token, that note is given as well: one token does not tell which reading the rule means. Under `"stop" Name ";" [token]`, `stop (N:Name) ;` fits the phrase but for the cast, while with a symbol `stop` the application `stop(N:Name)` is the whole rule, which the scanner's reading of `stop` as the literal rather than as a `#LowerId` label prevents.
The pinned reference reads all of these through the phrase as well: it compiles `T:Toks` to the token `T`, and reports `run #token("ab . cd", "Toks")` ambiguous between the literal and the token `#token("ab . cd", "Toks")` (checked with `kompile --backend haskell`).
So a configuration variable a configuration writes is always read: a `$X` the scanner reads with the `KConfigVar` lexeme (not text inside a string or another token) is a configuration-variable token, the phrase of a module's own `token` production reads program text only and so holds none, and the other `token` productions over a nonterminal, those of `#KVariable`, `KLabel` and `#CellName`, derive identifiers, which do not begin with `$`.
(`a_token_subsort_over_a_list_compiles_without_a_subsort_and_runs` and `a_configuration_variable_whose_sort_reaches_a_phrase_token_places_the_program` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), `a_token_subsort_is_only_a_syntactic_subsort` in [definition_relations.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/definition_relations.rs), `a_configuration_variable_whose_sort_reaches_a_phrase_token_is_read_as_the_variable` and `a_phrase_of_program_text_in_a_configuration_is_a_token_of_its_text` in [inner_config.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/inner_config.rs), `a_variable_at_a_phrase_token_sort_is_a_variable_and_no_token_of_its_name`, `a_token_literal_at_a_phrase_token_sort_is_its_only_reading`, `a_phrase_of_program_text_in_a_rule_is_a_token_of_its_text`, `a_phrase_holding_a_rule_construct_is_no_token`, `a_rule_construct_in_a_phrase_is_explained_by_the_phrase_reading_program_text`, `a_bracket_that_opens_a_phrase_is_explained_by_the_phrase_reading_program_text` and `a_phrase_reading_program_text_and_a_shadowed_label_are_both_noted` in [inner_rules.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/inner_rules.rs).)

## Strings and their literals

A KORE string literal is a sequence of code points (`kore-syntax.md`): printable ASCII stands for itself, and each escape names one code point, `\xhh` the code point U+00hh.
A K `String` is a sequence of code points too (`domains.md`: K's escapes each "indicate a code point"), and a `Bytes` value is a sequence of bytes, whose literal writes each byte as the code point of its value.
k-rust stores a domain value's code points in one form only (`string::LiteralEncoding` in the KORE crate): one byte per code point for the sort `SortBytes`, the sort hooked to `BYTES.Bytes`, and their UTF-8 for every other sort and for string patterns; so equal values have equal payloads, and string comparison, length and indexing are by code point.
Every reader keeps that form: the KORE text and JSON readers decode a literal's code points (in a `Bytes` literal a code point above U+00FF names no byte and is an error, and at a sort hooked `BYTES.Bytes` under another name, whose payload holds the code points' UTF-8, load refuses such a literal with `InvalidDomainValue`), binary KORE reads a non-`Bytes` payload that is not UTF-8 as text (each byte that begins no UTF-8 sequence the code point of its value), and the frontend converts a K `String` token by its code points.
The form is chosen by the sort's KORE name, not its hook, because a pattern outside a definition (a program, an RPC request) carries only the name: the frontend reads a token's code points by its sort's hook (a `BYTES.Bytes` token's bytes are the code points of their values) and stores them in the form of the sort's name, so a sort hooked `BYTES.Bytes` under another name than `Bytes` holds its values as UTF-8 code points, which its KORE text writes and reads back unchanged (`stores_each_token_by_the_encoding_of_its_kore_sort` in [term_to_kore.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/term_to_kore.rs)). The backend's `BYTES` hooks evaluate values of `SortBytes` only.
The writers write printable ASCII as itself, `\" \\ \n \r \t \f`, a code point up to U+00FF as `\xhh`, up to U+FFFF as `\uhhhh`, and any other as `\Uhhhhhhhh`; KORE JSON writes the code points themselves.
Console input and output are bytes: K's console hooks are POSIX operations on characters, and `#getc` and `#putc` read and write one character as one byte, its value; so `#read` and `$STDIN` read each byte as the code point of its value, and `#write` and a captured `stdout` buffer write each character as the byte of its code point, which reproduces exactly the bytes a program read. A code point above U+00FF is no byte: `#write` of one is the unsupported-hook outcome `#putc` gives a value above 255 (`domains.md` supports only the first 256 code points in strings), and a captured `stdout` buffer holding one is an error that names it (`captured_output_names_a_code_point_above_255` in [execution.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/backend/execution.rs)). A string from the definition's text is therefore written one byte per character, so `#write(1, "é")` writes the byte `0xe9`. The pinned reference's LLVM backend, its backend with console I/O, writes the same bytes for `"café ÿ!"` (`63 61 66 e9 20 ff 21`), but writes `"caf\u00e9"`, the same string spelled with an escape, as `63 61 66 c3 a9`: its console bytes depend on how a literal is spelled, while k-rust's are a function of the string, so k-rust writes `63 61 66 e9` for both spellings (`krun_writes_each_non_ascii_character_to_the_console_as_the_byte_of_its_code_point` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), against the harvested [reference fixture](../crates/k-rust/tests/fixtures/reference/cli/non-ascii-stdout)).
(`krun_reads_and_writes_strings_as_code_points`, `krun_io_on_preserves_descriptor_order_and_arbitrary_bytes` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), `console_write_and_putc_preserve_hook_order_and_exact_bytes`, `console_write_of_a_code_point_above_255_is_unsupported` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), the string tests of the [KORE codec](../crates/k-rust/tests/string.rs).)

## Constant folding

Before emitting KORE, the frontend replaces applications of pure `BOOL`, `INT`, `STRING` and `FLOAT` hooks to literals, on rule right-hand sides and in conditions, by the literal of their value, in every build.
This is an optimisation: it replaces a term only by a token that denotes the same value, so it never changes what a rule means and never changes whether a definition compiles.
The fold computes nothing itself.
It calls the function the backend's evaluation entry applies to every hooked application of a loaded definition (`k_rust_backend::builtin::evaluate_declared`), in the evaluation mode outside an execution, on the arguments the emitted definition holds, and over the declarations the emitted definition states: which sorts have domain values, the hooks of their declarations, and the subsort axioms.
Each literal is read as the loaded definition reads it, by the hook of its sort, and injected where its sort is a strict subsort of the argument's declared sort.
The fold writes a token exactly where that function gives a value of exactly the application's declared sort, and the token is that value's canonical text.
So a literal application and the same application on run-time arguments have one value wherever the backend computes one, and the fold writes no value where it computes none:
- an undefined application such as `1 /Int 0` (its hook's value is `#Bottom`), and an application of a `total` hook whose value the definition does not determine, such as `substrString` outside its valid indices ([Hook values at the edges of their domains](#hook-values-at-the-edges-of-their-domains));
- an application of a hook without a backend evaluator: the deprecated `categoryChar` and `directionalityChar`, whose meaning `domains.md` does not give, and `findChar`, `rfindChar`, `replace` and `replaceFirst`, whose meaning is their module's equations, which execution applies;
- a builtin hook declared at a sort that is not its builtin's: a token sort, or a sort hooked to the builtin under another name (`syntax MyInt [hook(INT.Int)]`, whose operands the `INT` hooks do not read); at a supersort of the builtin's sort the backend computes the value's injection, which no token writes, so the fold leaves that application as written too;
- a `String2Token` of a text that names no element of its result sort, such as `"abc"` at a sort hooked `INT.Int`, where `"07"` is the integer 7 and folds to `7`;
- a value beyond a bound of the backend, and a value larger than the fold's own bound below;
- an application of a `macro`, `macro-rec`, `alias` or `alias-rec` production, which means its expansion, not the hook's value: `myAdd(1, 2)` with `myAdd` hooked `INT.add` and the macro rule `myAdd(X, Y) => X -Int Y` is `1 -Int 2`, which macro expansion, after the fold, writes.

`MINT` applications are not folded: the backend evaluates them on literals ([Hook values at the edges of their domains](#hook-values-at-the-edges-of-their-domains)).
Shift amounts, exponents, and bit indices and lengths are natural numbers of any size, so the fold writes every such result that stays within its bound (`0 >>Int 2147483648` is `0`, `-1 ^Int (2^64 + 1)` is `-1`, `bitRangeInt(5, 0, 2^64)` is `5`), and a negative one is undefined.
A folded value is written into the emitted definition and read on every load, so the fold writes none larger than 2^20 bits (an `Int`'s bit length, eight times the byte length of any other value's literal, such as a `String`'s UTF-8 or a `Float`'s canonical text), a bound of its own that is never above the floor of the backend's bound on `^Int` and `<<Int` results ([Resource bounds](#resource-bounds)), and leaves a larger value for execution (`leaves_results_above_the_result_bound_unfolded`).
The fold passes that bound to the backend's evaluation, and every evaluator whose result can outgrow its arguments (`^Int`, `<<Int`, `bitRangeInt`, `replaceAll`, the `Bytes` padding and `Int2Bytes`, the `MINT` results) refuses a larger result before computing it, and `^%Int` does not evaluate an application whose exponent's bit length times the square of its modulus' exceeds a sixteenth of the bound squared, which bounds the products and reductions of its square-and-multiply loop (an application just under that limit, such as a 65,535-bit exponent with a 1,001-bit modulus, adds well under a second to a release compile): `2 ^Int 16777215`, a `replaceAll` whose result would be quadratic in its folded arguments and a `^%Int` with a 131,072-bit exponent and modulus stay as written at once (`leaves_a_constant_larger_than_the_fold_bound_unfolded_without_computing_it`, `leaves_a_costly_powmod_unfolded`). A result the fold does not write may still be built and discarded when its size is within about twice its arguments' (the backend's own bounds on `^Int`, `<<Int` and `bitRangeInt` admit that much), so a compile spends on it memory of the order of its folded arguments and time polynomial in their size.
Execution has no such bound beyond the backend's own, so what a run computes is unchanged.
A definition whose rule holds such an undefined application therefore compiles, where the reference rejects it while folding; the conformance expectations record that recipe as `undefined-constant-unfolded`.
The `FLOAT` hooks are folded exactly where the backend computes them outside an execution: `+Float`, `-Float`, `*Float`, `/Float`, `minFloat`, `maxFloat`, `--Float`, `absFloat`, `floorFloat`, `ceilFloat`, `truncFloat`, `rootFloat`, the comparisons (`<Float`, `<=Float`, `>Float`, `>=Float`, `==Float`), `roundFloat`, `Int2Float`, `Float2Int` and `maxValueFloat` in binary32 and binary64, and `precisionFloat`, `exponentBitsFloat`, `signFloat` and `isNaN` in every format. They are left as written where execution leaves them: an operation over a binary32 and a binary64 operand, whose result format only an execution chooses, `signFloat` of a NaN, whose sign only an execution chooses, `Float2Int` of a NaN or an infinity, whose value the definition does not determine, any of these in another format, and `expFloat`, `logFloat`, `sinFloat`, `cosFloat`, `tanFloat`, `asinFloat`, `acosFloat`, `atanFloat`, `atan2Float`, `^Float`, `%Float`, `exponentFloat` and `minValueFloat` in every format, which the backend does not evaluate. The `STRING` conversions to and from `Float` have no backend evaluator and are not folded either. So the compiled definition is the same in every build of k-rust (`folds_binary64_float_constants_in_every_build`, `leaves_float_hooks_the_backend_does_not_evaluate_unfolded` and `folded_and_evaluated_applications_agree`).
The tests `folds_exactly_what_the_backend_computes`, `leaves_an_undefined_constant_operation_unfolded`, `leaves_hooks_without_a_fold_implementation_unfolded` and `folds_right_shifts_by_any_natural_amount` in [kompile_passes.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/kompile_passes.rs), `evaluate_declared_agrees_with_the_entry` in the [backend's domain-value tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/domain_values.rs), and `folded_and_evaluated_applications_agree` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), which runs every hook the fold evaluates on literals and on program arguments, cover this.

## Resource bounds

Simplification budgets and term nesting depth are k-rust's own resource contracts, specified in [backend-port.md](backend-port.md#simplification-iteration-budgets) and [backend-port.md](backend-port.md#json-depth-policy); this section records how they differ from the reference and why.

The simplification budget bounds the rewrite rounds of one fixed-point lineage: sibling subterms each receive a copy of the current budget, and the terms a rewrite produces inherit that rewrite's reduced budget.
Booster instead counts passes of its whole-term equation loop against `--equation-max-iterations` (`booster/library/Booster/Pattern/ApplyEquations.hs`, `iterateEquations`).
The bounded surfaces of both default to 100 (k-rust's `kore-simplify` command and RPC `simplify` method are unbounded), but equal numbers do not denote the same cut, and k-rust's `--max-simplification-iterations` option and RPC `max-simplification-iterations` parameter are not translations of the Booster option.
The reason is the budget's purpose.
An equation replaces a term with one equal to it, so the budget never decides what a pattern denotes; it guards only against a chain of rewrites that does not terminate.
Non-termination is a property of one chain of rewrites of one subterm, so the count belongs to that chain; a whole-term pass count measures a chain's length only through the evaluation schedule, that is, through how far one pass advances each chain, which does not bear on whether the chain terminates.
Inheriting the reduced budget keeps an expanding equation from resetting its own cap.
Where the budget is exhausted, the retained configuration is still equal to the one being simplified, but it can be less simplified, and a step that needs the missing value may not be taken; the outcome on each surface, and the `SimplificationBudgetExhausted` diagnostic that marks this incompleteness, are specified in backend-port.md.

`^Int` and `<<Int` compute a result of at most `max(2^24, 2 * a)` bits, where `a` is the total bit length of their arguments (`MAX_INT_RESULT_BITS` and `result_size_limit` in the backend's `builtin.rs`); a larger result is defined but not computed (reason `ResultTooLarge`), and its size is decided before the value is computed (a `^Int` power is computed and then measured only when its estimated length lies within `max(10^-6, bound * 2^-40)` bits of the bound).
Term simplification leaves such an application unevaluated with the `UnsupportedHookUnevaluated` diagnostic and no error, whatever its arguments: arguments are simplified before it is known whether their values are used (except the operands `#if`, `andThenBool` and `orElseBool` discard, [Conditionals evaluate only the operand they select](#conditionals-evaluate-only-the-operand-they-select)), and a subterm the simplification discards must neither fail nor cost anything (`false andBool B` is `false` for every `B`, and the `\ceil` of the dropped application holds, since a result is refused only where the hook has a value).
A run never decides a condition over one and never reports a stuck or final state holding one, though: a rule attempt whose conditions would carry it undecided (a match condition, a `requires` the solver did not decide, the right-hand side's carried obligations or `ensures`) halts the step on its subject with the unsupported-hook error; a search-pattern match whose reported condition would hold one undecided is that error instead of a solution, and the state's search entry is incomplete (the constraints that hold one are first put to the solver under the rest of the condition and the state's facts: proven, they are dropped from the reported condition; refuted, there is no match; and a solution may still bind a variable to one, which decides nothing); and a state that no rule rewrites halts with that error, instead of being reported stuck, when it holds one; in execution, search and proofs alike (`a_search_match_is_not_reported_under_a_condition_on_a_result_above_the_size_bound` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), `krun_search_matches_no_condition_on_a_result_above_the_size_bound` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).
A run cut short by a depth bound, a cut point or a terminal rule reports the state at the cut as it is: a snapshot of the configuration, not a verdict on it.
Implication and model queries need no such check: a residual condition that holds an application the solver reads as uninterpreted has no certified model, so they answer unknown, never invalid or satisfiable.
The value is determined but not computed, so a rule condition over it that the solver cannot decide (`<<Int` has no interpreted SMT encoding) would let rules fire whose conditions are false, a branch the semantics does not have, and a final configuration holding it would read as the semantics' answer although the backend did not finish it.
A state may carry one through steps that do not depend on its value (a rule that drops it), a condition the solver decides is not carried (a `^Int` result above the bound in a `requires` that the solver proves or refutes does not stop the run), and simplification requests return the unevaluated application, which equals the input.
The bound keeps the cost of one hook application polynomial in the size of its arguments, so it refuses only a result that is large relative to them: these two hooks and `bitRangeInt` are the only `Int` hooks whose result can be exponential in that size, since the exponent, shift amount or length is written in logarithmically many digits. Within twice the arguments' size a result is at most what one `*Int` makes from the same values (`Y *Int Y`), and `*Int` is not bounded (a product has the size of its factors together, so a program that grows a value by multiplication pays in each step for what it already built); so `Y <<Int 1`, `Y ^Int 1` and `Y ^Int 2` are computed whatever the size of `Y`, as `Y *Int 2` and `Y *Int Y` are (`results_are_bounded_relative_to_their_arguments` in [builtin.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin.rs)).
The floor of 2^24 bits bounds time, not memory: it bounds the cost of converting an `Int` between decimal text and binary. A term holds an `Int` as its decimal domain value, and every hook application on it parses that text again, so a value is paid for once per use, not once per run. Parsing decimal is quadratic in the length (num-bigint 0.4.8, release build), four times the work for each doubling: measured as user instructions, 2.2·10^10 at 2^22 bits, 8.7·10^10 at 2^23, 3.5·10^11 at 2^24 and 1.4·10^12 at 2^25 (1.2 s, 5.2 s, 23 s and 75 s on a loaded host), so one read of a 2^32-bit value would take days; writing the decimal text of a result grows more slowly (3 s at 2^24 bits). The floor is where a refused result could not be used in reasonable time anyway: a run can afford a few reads of a 2^23-bit value, not of a 2^25-bit one. That is a judgment about this cost, not a property of the semantics, and it is fixed rather than read from the host, since a faster host divides the times by a constant and leaves their growth unchanged. Memory does not enter into it: a 2^24-bit value is 2 MiB, 5,050,446 bytes of decimal text, far below what a process may use, and the bound would have no reason to exist if `Int` values were held in binary, after which only the memory a result needs, as for `Bytes` and `List` below, could justify refusing one.
Results that stay small are computed whatever the argument: `0 <<Int N`, `1 ^Int N`, `0 ^Int N` and `-1 ^Int N` for any natural `N`, and `>>Int` by any amount.
`bitRangeInt(I, IDX, LEN)` is bounded the same way where `I >>Int IDX` is negative, since its value is then `LEN` bits long and refused above `max(2^24, 2 * a)` bits, `a` the total bit length of its three arguments (`bitRangeInt(-1, 0, 2^30)` is refused); otherwise it is at most `I >>Int IDX`, and `signExtendBitRangeInt(I, IDX, LEN)` is never larger in magnitude than `I >>Int IDX`, so both are computed whatever the length (`bitRangeInt(5, 0, 2^30)` is 5, `signExtendBitRangeInt(-1, 0, 2^30)` is -1). The bound applies to their result, not to the `1 <<Int LEN` of their `domains.md` equations ([Hook values at the edges of their domains](#hook-values-at-the-edges-of-their-domains)); where module `INT` is imported, a refused application may still be rewritten by those equations, and the refusal may then name the `<<Int` they build, with the same outcome (`bit_ranges_are_bounded_by_their_result_not_by_two_to_the_length` in [builtin.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin.rs)).
`padRightBytes`, `padLeftBytes` and `Int2Bytes` build a `Bytes` result exactly when one application of the hook fits the memory the process is allowed: when `k * R <= L`, `R` being the result's length in bytes, `L` the process's allowance and `k` the hook's peak factor (`ResultCap` and `BYTES_RESULT_PEAK_FACTOR` in the backend's `builtin.rs`); a longer result is defined (`domains.md` defines padding for every nonnegative length, and `Int2Bytes` is `total`), but it is refused before anything is allocated, with the same `ResultTooLarge` outcome as above, and the refusal names the cap, the allowance and the limit that sets it, for example `a Bytes result of length a 41-bit integer is longer than its cap of 8589934592 bytes (8 GiB): half of the 16 GiB this process may use (cgroup memory.max of /user.slice/run-1.scope), since one application holds 2 bytes per byte of its result`. Padding to a length the input already reaches is never refused: its result is the input, which the run already holds, so the application allocates nothing.
The allowance is read once per process (`allowance.rs` in the backend): the smallest of the cgroup v2 `memory.max` of the process's cgroup and of each of its ancestors, the soft `RLIMIT_AS` and `RLIMIT_DATA` limits, the physical memory and the target's address space, each a line past which an allocation fails or the process is killed. It is not reduced by the memory already in use, so whether a result is refused depends only on the input and those limits, not on what the run allocated before or on the timing of parallel branches; a result within the cap that does not fit in what is left fails as any other allocation does, and so can a later use of an admitted value that needs more than its application did (rendering a `Bytes` value as KORE JSON takes up to six bytes per byte). The factor is measured, not chosen: the backend's tests record, with a counting allocator, the most memory one application holds at two result lengths, and take its growth per byte of result, which leaves out the few bytes every application holds: 2 for each `Bytes` hook, the result, built in one buffer of exactly its length, and its copy into the term that holds it, whatever the length of a padded input (empty, one byte, a quarter, half or all but one byte of the result). Reading the arguments is not counted: it holds memory in proportion to values the run already holds, whatever the result's length. These values cost time linear in their length (a padding of gigabytes takes seconds), so memory, not time, decides: within the cap a result is computed however large, and the step timeout bounds the time. There is no floor and no term relative to the size of the arguments, and so no fixed size below which a result is always computed: the same input is computed on a host allowed more memory and refused on one allowed less, never given a different value. 4 GiB of value, the most a 32-bit address space holds, is therefore computed wherever the process may use 8 GiB, so a semantics that models such a memory (a WebAssembly memory has at most 65536 pages of 64 KiB) has no memory operation refused there. A host whose address space is itself 32 bits (`k-rust-wasm` on wasm32) cannot hold that: Rust refuses one allocation above `isize::MAX`, 2^31 - 1 bytes, by panicking, and the whole memory is at most 4 GiB. The `Bytes` bound is at most a sixteenth, `(isize::MAX + 1) / 16` bytes (`TARGET_BYTES_RESULT_LEN`, 128 MiB on a 32-bit host), because a `Bytes` value must also be rendered when a run reports it: KORE has no escape shorter than `\xhh` for a byte outside printable ASCII, so its KORE text takes up to four bytes per byte and its KORE JSON (`\u00hh`) up to six, and the library's JSON response holds about sixteen bytes per byte at its peak (the value, its externalized copy, the JSON string of its characters, the KORE JSON and the response that embeds it), which then stays within half of the address space, with the largest single allocation below `isize::MAX`. This term binds there before the allowance, which is at most the 4 GiB address space; on a 64-bit host it is 2^59 bytes and never binds. A per-application bound cannot make every configuration renderable: a `List`'s rendering depends on its elements, and a shared subterm is rendered once per occurrence, so on a 32-bit host a final configuration whose rendering exceeds the address space still aborts the instance when it is rendered (`bytes_results_on_a_32_bit_target_can_be_rendered`, `bytes_lengths_are_capped_by_the_allowance`, `padding_to_a_length_the_input_reaches_is_not_capped`, `bytes_lengths_are_bounded_by_what_the_target_can_allocate` and `bytes_hooks_hold_twice_their_result` in [bytes.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/bytes.rs)).
`makeList(N, V)` is computed for every `N` a list's tree counts, any natural number up to `usize::MAX` (2^64 - 1 on a 64-bit host, 2^32 - 1 on a 32-bit one), and refused above it with the same `ResultTooLarge` outcome, the refusal naming the length and that count; a later operation whose result would hold more items than that count (one more item after `makeList(usize::MAX, V)`) stops the run with an error (a panic of the backend) rather than give the list a length its items do not have. Its value is `N` copies of `V`, and the backend holds them as the tree it holds any list of `N` items in, with one node standing for every subtree that holds the same items in the same shape: copies of one value differ only in their positions, so each level of the tree holds a few distinct nodes (at most `3h + 4` at height `h`, four in every length tested), `O(log^2 N)` nodes in all, and the application holds memory that grows with the tree's levels, not with `N` (about 5 KiB more at 2^24 copies than at 2^12). So no allowance bounds it: the result fits wherever the run does. Later operations keep that cost where they read or change a few positions: `size`, a lookup or an update at an index, a concatenation, `range`, and a match of the list's ends cost `O(log N)` each and share every node they do not change, so `makeList` serves as a large array that a run fills by updates (`ensureOffsetList` in `domains.md` extends a list by `makeList` to the index it writes). An operation that reads every item costs time linear in `N` (rendering the list, `LIST.in` of a value it does not hold, comparing it with a list that shares no node with it; and, when `V` has variables or a definedness obligation, substituting into the list and computing its definedness, which visit each copy at its position), as for any list of `N` items, bounded by the step timeout, and rendering a configuration holding a long one takes memory linear in its text, so, as for any large value, a final configuration whose rendering does not fit the process still fails when it is rendered (`make_length_above_usize_is_unsupported`, `make_computes_every_length_a_list_counts` and `make_holds_memory_logarithmic_in_its_length` in [list.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/list.rs), `repeating_an_item_builds_the_tree_of_its_copies`, `repeating_an_item_shares_its_nodes` and `a_sequence_longer_than_usize_max_is_not_counted` in [chunked/tests.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/term/chunked/tests.rs)).

A negative literal of a machine-integer sort `MInt{W}` whose width exceeds `MAX_INT_RESULT_BITS` (2^24), or does not fit in 64 bits, is refused when the definition or request holding it is loaded (`DomainValueTooLarge`), and a function hooked `STRING.string2token` given one leaves its application unevaluated (`ResultTooLarge`, whether the function is `total` or partial): its canonical text, the residue modulo `2^W` ([Domain values](#domain-values)), is a decimal of about `W` bits, which the same bound declines to compute. The literal names an element of its sort; the refusal is this bound, not a judgment that the literal is malformed. A nonnegative literal is reduced within its own length and is never refused. Below the bound a negative literal is computed, and its cost grows with the width, not with the literal's length: `-1p16777216`, about 25 bytes of KORE, takes about 3.3 s and becomes a canonical text of about 5 MB each time it is loaded (0.3 s at width 2^22, 0.03 s at 2^20; release build).
A `Float` literal of a format other than binary32 and binary64 is stored as the exact decimal of its value ([Domain values](#domain-values)), whose significand `D` has about `N + 2.32 |e|` bits for a value `m * 2^e` with `e < 0`: a bound on the precision alone does not bound it (`1e-100000p53x20` writes about 230,000 digits). The backend bounds `D`, and the exact arithmetic that rounds the literal and writes `D`, by `MAX_FLOAT_CANONICAL_BITS` (2^20 bits, in the backend's `builtin/float/canonical.rs`), estimated from the literal's digit count, its decimal exponent and the format before any arithmetic of that size: overflow to infinity and underflow to zero are decided first from exponent bounds (so `1e99999999999999999999p53x30` is `Infinityp53x30` and `1.0p53x99999999999` is computed, its range never materialized), a value exactly representable with a short binary significand is written whatever the precision (`1.0p99999999999x8` is `1e0p99999999999x8`), though one whose check itself exceeds the bound is refused (`1e500000p99999999999x64`, whose binary significand `5^500000` has about 1.16 million bits, although the value's exact decimal is the literal itself), a decimal exponent of more than twenty digits is decided from its length and sign without being read (an infinity or a zero where the exponent field is at most 64 bits, refused otherwise), and a literal whose estimate exceeds the bound (`0.1p99999999999x8`, `1e-1000000p53x32`) is refused when the definition or request holding it is loaded (`DomainValueTooLarge`), and leaves a `string2token` application unevaluated (`ResultTooLarge`, whether the function is `total` or partial). The literal names a value of its format; the refusal is this bound. The bound is closed under reading back: a canonical text is refused only when its own significand exceeds the bound, so the canonical text of every literal load accepts is accepted too (`every_accepted_literal_has_an_accepted_canonical_text_at_the_bound`). A format whose precision or exponent-bits field is 1 is refused the same way (`a_huge_precision_is_computed_only_for_values_it_represents_exactly` and `a_remote_exponent_is_decided_from_its_length` in the [canonicalizer's tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/float/canonical/tests.rs)).
The `MINT` hooks share the bound with `^Int` and `<<Int`: a result that wraps through `2^W` (`Int2MInt` of a negative `Int`, `-MInt`, `--MInt`, `~MInt`) at a width `W` above `max(2^24, 2 * a)`, `a` the total bit length of its arguments, and a `<<MInt` result that keeps a set bit beyond that bound, are defined but not computed (`ResultTooLarge`), decided before any value of about `W` bits is built; every other `MINT` result is no larger than its arguments' size class and is computed (`a_wrapped_result_wider_than_the_result_bound_is_too_large`, `a_left_shift_wider_than_the_result_bound_is_too_large` and `a_left_shift_at_a_width_beyond_u64_keeps_the_bits_below_the_width` in [hook_models/mint.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/hook_models/mint.rs)).

The diagnostic of a failed inner parse is limited in work ([backend-port.md](backend-port.md#json-depth-policy), `DiagnosticWork`). Within the limit it is the context-free grammar's: a priority, associativity or scope relation that a derivation of the input breaks, or `NoParse` where the grammar's longest viable prefix ends. Past it, it is `NoParse` at the first position past which no reading that respects the relations can be completed with the rest of the input, marked with the limit (`work_limit`) and saying that whether a reading that breaks a relation exists was not decided; an input that only breaks a relation is then reported as an unexpected token at the operator the relation forbids, and an unclosed parenthesis as an unexpected token after its first operand (the first attempt also leaves out readings whose tokens the rest of the input lacks, so no reading continues past the operand that would need the missing `)`), where the exact diagnostic reports the end of the input. The reason is that the context-free diagnostic is general context-free recognition, cubic in the length of an associativity-disambiguated chain whose parse failed in linear work (a stray `+` after 400 operands took 6.7 s to report), while a diagnostic decides nothing: what is accepted, and what a rejection rejects, do not depend on it, so a bound changes only how some rejections of long inputs are described. The limit is the larger of 16 times the first attempt's chart work and a floor of 2^20 chart steps, so a short input always gets the exact diagnostic; `KRUST_PARSE_DIAGNOSTIC_WORK` sets the floor, or `unlimited` restores the exact diagnostic whatever its cost; `krust` refuses any other value before it parses anything. The one place where the kind of a rejection selects what is accepted, a `K` configuration value that is read at `K` only when it has no `KItem` reading, decides an attempt that reached the limit again without it.

An ambiguous inner parse is reported at the smallest subterm outside which every reading agrees, the readings being the distinct terms its parses lower to. When the readings there have several root symbols (a head with its arity), they are listed one entry per root symbol, each the least of its readings in term order, marked when other readings share its root, and naming the production that reading's root comes from; where overloads of one label build that same reading, it names the one of least text. Otherwise they differ at several subterms of it, and each such subterm is reported the same way, one entry per root symbol of its readings; a subterm whose readings share one root symbol is not itself reported, but the subterms below it at which they differ are. In `a(X, Y, t(...))` read through two productions `a`, the readings are listed as the two applications of `a`, each with the least reading of `t(...)` when that has several. Each reported subterm carries its source location: its line and column in the definition in rule and configuration diagnostics, and in the program text, whose first character is line 1, column 1, when a program or a configuration value is parsed. Equal readings parsed from different text are one reading that covers each text, so a subterm's location covers the text of every parse that reads it there, however the forest packs those parses. The report is a function of the set of readings, so it does not depend on how a parse forest packs them, nor on which sort-inference engine decided the forest, and it is computed in time polynomial in the size of the tree that sort inference passes to it, the parse forest with every shared subtree copied: the readings, whose number can be exponential in that tree, are never listed or counted (`0 + 1 + ... + 10 - 11 - ... - 21`, with no relation between `+` and `-`, has 352,716 readings; it is reported in two entries). That tree is built before the report, and for some rejected rules it is itself exponential in the input, as it was before this report. Two things a report could say about the readings are deliberately left out: their number, and which combinations of the reported subterms' readings are readings. `f(a, c)` and `f(b, d)` are therefore reported as their four combinations would be, by the subterms `a` or `b` and `c` or `d`, and a rule whose readings differ on both sides is reported by the subterms of each side where they differ, not as its whole readings. Neither can be computed in time polynomial in that tree for every tree unless P = NP: with one symbol declared by k productions over n arguments, each argument sort being `Z`, `O` or a sort with both as subsorts, and `Z ::= "t"` and `O ::= "t"` two readings of one token, the rule body `f(t, ..., t)` has a forest of O(kn) nodes (O(kn) also with its shared subtrees copied) whose readings are the satisfying assignments of a k-clause formula in disjunctive normal form over n variables, so counting them is #P-complete and deciding whether they are every combination of their arguments is coNP-complete. The report can name a larger subterm than one that stops at the first ambiguity met inside a forest: in `rule (T[])[int, Ts:Types] => T[Ts]`, where `T` is a `Type` or an `Id` under a `class(Id)` constructor without `prefer` or `avoid`, the readings of the rule differ on both sides, and the two `T` are reported, with two and three entries.

Term readers and writers impose no nesting-depth cap: depth is not part of what a term means, so a fixed cap would reject well-formed input.
KORE readers, writers, and host and RPC request ingress are bounded by process memory; on a native host the backend runs its recursive passes over a pattern or definition on a stack it sizes for the input's depth, refusing with an ordinary error only input deeper than the largest stack it provides; the simplifier's recursion, whose depth the input does not determine, takes up to 1 GiB of stack beyond its thread's (4 GiB for all threads of a process) and then stops with `StackExhausted` ([backend-port.md](backend-port.md#simplification-stack-depth)); and the stages still bounded by the host thread's stack are listed in [backend-port.md](backend-port.md#stack-bounds).
Those accepted recursions are not deviations from the reference: a thread's stack bounds the depth of a recursion in any implementation, and none of them changes what a term means, so each is listed there with its measured threshold and its reason rather than here.

## Formal parameter preference

A formal sort parameter of a parametric production is not a variable of the sentence (K user manual, "Parametric productions and `bracket` attributes").
Sort inference therefore orders the readings of a sentence only by well-sortedness, by maximality over the sorts of its variables, and then by `prefer`/`avoid`; a sentence with several readings left after these steps is an ambiguous parse error (K user manual, "Variable Sort Inference" and "Symbol priority and associativity").
The `K`/`KItem`/`Bag` preference on formal parameters only chooses how a kept reading is instantiated: it compares parameter vectors that keep the same set of well-sorted readings and never removes a reading that some vector types.
A parameter that the kept readings leave free is instantiated at `K` by that preference, also where the module's order leaves `K`, `KItem` and `Bag` unrelated, as a program grammar does: each of the three then meets one preference, and the preference ranks `K` first, then `KItem`, then `Bag`, so a free parameter is one parse, not an ambiguity between its instances.
A parameter is instantiated only at sorts of the module.
A parameter that occurs inside a parametric sort (the `Width` of `MInt{Width}`) ranges over the module's declared instances of that sort, so a width the kept readings leave free takes them: one fitting instance is chosen, several are an ambiguous parse error, and none is a rejection naming the production.
For example, `rule foo(X) => g` over `syntax {W} Foo ::= foo(MInt{W})` is accepted at `MInt{8}` when `MInt{8}` is the only instance.
The pinned K frontend does not terminate on that rule, and picks one of two declared widths where Rust reports the ambiguity.
Alternatives that are equal once bracket nodes are erased are one reading, not several: `rule #Ceil(X:W) => (#Top) [simplification]` has one parse, `#Top{K}`, exactly as without the brackets.
Of the instances of one bracket that read one text, the parse keeps the instance at the sort its position gives (`K` where the position bounds no parameter), since that instance expects its child exactly where the text without the brackets stands: in `foo((0 #And 1))` with `syntax Exp ::= Int | foo(Exp)`, `#And` is instantiated at `Exp`, as in `foo(0 #And 1)`, and not at `Int` through the bracket's `Int` instance.
Both inference engines keep that instance, so checked inference compares one parse.
The concrete instances of one parametric production are one production of the parsed term, so they never form separate readings either.
The instance a kept reading was parsed at is not part of the loaded sentence: rule, claim, context, context-alias and configuration bodies are loaded with no label sort parameter, since source text cannot write one, and sort injection instantiates every parametric label from its arguments and position ([Sorts of compiled terms](#sorts-of-compiled-terms)).
A program, configuration value or search-pattern root is macro-expanded and instantiated at one position: the configuration position of its declared or start sort (`KItem` for `K`), or `GeneratedTopCell` and `Bool` for a pattern's body and condition.
Expansion discards the parser's parametric instances and reads each application's instance from the typing of the current value at that position, which is the instance sort injection emits.
Macro expansion runs before sort injection, so where a macro rule's head is parametric it reads instances from the typing view (`SentenceTyper`, the typing compilation gives each position): a head's instance from the typing of its macro rule, and an application's from the typing of the sentence it stands in or of the ground value at its position, taken after its arguments are expanded and after every earlier rewrite of that sentence or value.
The instance is the label's own, as injection instantiates it.
A macro rule applies only to an application whose instance agrees with its head's on every parameter both fix; a parameter the typing leaves open agrees with every value.
Where the sentence or ground value does not type yet, the instance is unknown and only a head that fixes no parameter applies; one still unknown at the fixpoint is left for injection to reject.
Every application to which no rule applied from a rule set with a parametric head fixing a parameter is retried after later rewrites until nothing changes, since a rewrite elsewhere can change even a known instance.
Every other comparison of a rule's label with a production or term label (the macro table, `anywhere`, and rule-by-label lookups) is by the label's name.

The pinned K frontend also uses the parameter preference to choose between distinct readings, by sending a parameter to `K` where that makes another reading ill-sorted.
Rust does not.
Where this makes the reference pick the non-`prefer` reading, Rust takes the `prefer` reading (`#fun2` over `#fun3` in `#fun(P => B)(A)` when both are well-sorted).
Where no `prefer` or `avoid` separates the readings, Rust reports the ambiguity and the conformance expectations record the step as `port-reports-ambiguity`.
In `issue-2287-simpl-rules-in-kprovex`, the claim `<k> c => 2 #And n +Int n </k>` of `a5-spec.k` reads as `(2 #And n) +Int n` and as `2 #And (n +Int n)`: both are well-sorted, the claim has no variables, and no priority, `prefer`, or `avoid` relates `_+Int_` and `#And`, so the claim is ambiguous.
The two readings have different KORE and different proof behavior; a specification that means one of them states it with brackets.

## Grammar scope of token precedence

A regular-expression terminal is one lexeme, and the `prec(N)` attribute orders lexemes when the scanner must choose between tokens of equal length (K user manual, "`prec` attribute"); two token productions with the same regular expression and different precedences therefore give one lexeme two contradictory orders, and Rust rejects that grammar ("Inconsistent token precedence detected.").
Rust judges each module's grammar in the view its own imports give it: a sentence denotes in the syntax its own module sees, and the main module's grammar is checked even when the module has no rules.
The imports compilation adds for configuration support are imports like any other: a main module that sees no configuration imports `DEFAULT-CONFIGURATION`, a module that declares a configuration imports `MAP`, and a module sees what these export to it, no more.
`MAP` imports `INT-SYNTAX` privately, so these imports give a view the sort `Int` but not `Int`'s lexeme.
The cells compilation adds to the configuration (the counter cell `<generatedCounter>`, see "Compiler-resolved fresh constants") are in exactly the views that hold a cell of the configuration: a module that sees no configuration can name none of its cells, the added ones included.
A module that imports `INT-SYNTAX` and declares `syntax Foo ::= r"[0-9]+" [token]` is rejected; a module that declares the same production and imports nothing is accepted, since `Int`'s `[0-9]+` (with `prec(2)`) is not in its view, and a program or pattern written in it reads `12` as `Foo`.
The pinned K frontend rejects the second definition as well (`checks/invalidPrec.k`), naming `Int`'s token from domains.md, which reaches the main module only through modules compilation adds to it.
In Rust no scanner holds both `Foo`'s and `Int`'s `[0-9]+`, so the definition gives no lexeme two orders; the conformance expectations record that recipe as `compiler-added-grammar`.

A literal terminal is a lexeme as well.
The manual's default precedence, 0, is the default of regular-expression tokens; a literal without `prec` has the largest precedence, so that a keyword wins over a regular-expression token of the same length (`if` over an identifier), which every grammar with keywords needs.
A production's `prec(N)` gives its literals precedence `N` as it gives its regular expressions, in the in-process scanner and in generated parsers alike: with `syntax Foo ::= "ab" [token, prec(-1)]` and `syntax Bar ::= r"[a-z]+" [token]`, the text `ab` is a `Bar`.
Every production that mentions a literal declares its precedence, explicitly or by default, so a literal that one production gives `prec(-1)` and another mentions without `prec` is rejected, whichever comes first, as a regular expression with two precedences is.

The scanner reads one token at each position, by these rules alone, and does not ask what the parse expects there: the token stream of a text is a lexical fact that every reading of it shares.
A rule or configuration grammar holds K's own lexemes beside the module's: `#LowerId` and `#UpperId` (`prec(2)`), the `KLabel` and `#KVariable` spellings and, in a configuration, `#CellName` (`prec(1)`).
They outrank a module's regular expression of the default precedence, so a cell name, a label and a variable stay writable whatever identifier regex the module declares; a module whose `syntax Name ::= r"[a-z]+" [token, prec(3)]` outranks them can no longer write `<k>`.
So with `syntax Name ::= r"[a-z]+" [token]`, a rule's `run ab` reads `ab` as a `#LowerId`, and has no reading where a `Name` is expected, as `run ab . cd` has none for a phrase token over `Name` (the pinned reference rejects `run ab` as well).
The parse error then names the token, the lexeme that read it and the expected lexeme that matches the same text, and spells the token as a literal, `#token("ab", "Name")`; inside a phrase, where a literal is no program text, it says to write the whole phrase as one, `#token("ab . cd", "Toks")`.
A literal is a term, so it is advised only where the place the token fills, followed out of the productions of one item, is one a `#token` literal fills too; where the expected lexeme is read as a `KLabel`, as the label of an application, the note spells the token as the backquoted label instead: with a literal `"stop"` in the module, `stop(N:Name)` is written `` `stop`(N:Name) ``, and `#token("stop", "#LowerId")(N:Name)` does not parse.
The advice follows each place: a token the parse expects both as a label and in the phrase of a token production (`syntax Name ::= #LowerId [token]`) is advised as the label and as the phrase, and a token that already has the backquoted spelling has no other label spelling to advise.
A module that wants such names written as program text in its rules says so: `syntax Name ::= #LowerId [token]` (importing `BUILTIN-ID-TOKENS`) makes the `#LowerId` token `ab` a `Name` token of the same text, and so `run ab . cd` the `Toks` token `ab . cd`; this also adds `#LowerId` to the module's program syntax.
The note is given for the failing token, and for the token before it when the lexeme that lost it could go on with the failing token, so a keyword that is read correctly is not reported.
(`a_no_parse_names_an_expected_lexeme_that_lost_the_text_of_a_token_by_precedence` in [inner_parser.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/inner_parser.rs), `reference_scan_b_lowercase_identifier_is_not_a_user_token_in_rules`, `a_lowercase_name_in_a_phrase_is_read_by_the_rule_grammar_lexeme_that_outranks_it`, `a_shadowed_label_is_advised_in_its_backquoted_spelling`, `a_shadowed_lexeme_that_is_a_label_and_a_phrase_is_advised_for_both` and `a_shadowed_backquoted_label_is_not_advised_in_its_own_spelling` in [inner_rules.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/inner_rules.rs).)

## Token regex line anchors

A token regex `^R`, `R$` or `^R$` denotes the tokens of `R` under a condition on where the token is in the scanned text, not on what it contains. `^` holds when the token starts the text or follows a line end, and `$` holds when a line end follows the token. The line end is not part of the token and does not count when candidate tokens are compared by length, and the end of the text is not a line end.

A line end is one of the eight line terminators that locations count (see "Generated parser locations"): `\n`, `\r`, `\r\n`, vertical tab, form feed, U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029, with `\r\n` one terminator, so no line starts or ends between its `\r` and its `\n`.
A token regex's anchors read these lines too.
A location says where lines start, and `^` and `$` say where a token is relative to lines; with one notion of line, a token that `^` accepts is located at column 1, and one that `$` accepts is the last on its line, whatever terminator the program uses.
krust holds the terminators in one table (`crates/k-rust/src/lines.rs`), which the locations, both scanners' anchor checks and the C runtime of generated parsers read.
This diverges from the pinned K's generated parsers, whose Flex `^` and `$` read only `\n` as a line end: there `r"^b"` rejects a `b` right after `\r`, U+2028 or another terminator other than `\n`, and `r"a$"` rejects an `a` right before one, where krust accepts both.

The in-process scanner and generated parsers give anchors this meaning alike (`tests/support/line_anchors.rs`, and the CLI test `regex_line_anchors_hold_at_every_line_end_a_location_counts`).

The pinned K's in-process `kast` reads `r"^b"` as also requiring a newline after the token and reads `r"a$"` as `r"a"`: it accepts `a` with no newline after it and rejects `b` at the end of the input; krust does neither.
The kast manifest case `regex-anchor-local` parses [anchor.k](../crates/k-rust/tests/fixtures/reference/inner/regex/anchor.k), which declares those two tokens, with both and records the two as divergences: `regex-anchor-local:end-anchor-at-the-end-of-the-text` as `port-rejects` (krust rejects `a`, the reference accepts it) and `regex-anchor-local:start-anchor-at-the-end-of-the-text` as `port-accepts` (krust accepts `b`, the reference rejects it with `Scanner error: unexpected character sequence 'b'`).
Its other entries pin where the two agree: `a` and `b` each followed by a newline are accepted, and `b` after a space is rejected.
The kast gate runs both sides on every entry and fails when either moves.

No reference-corpus definition uses an anchor. Another reference differential case that does needs a declared normalisation, exclusion or recorded divergence.

## Default layout line ends

The default layout, module `DEFAULT-LAYOUT` of [kast.md](../crates/k-rust/builtin/kast.md), which programs of a definition that declares no `#Layout` production and the rule and configuration grammars use, reads lines as locations and token-regex anchors do: a line end is one of the eight line terminators (see "Token regex line anchors").
A `//` comment ends at the first line end, and every line end is whitespace: a line end separates lines, so text after one is on the next line, never inside a comment of the line before.
So `a // c` U+2028 `b` is the tokens `a` and `b`, as `a // c` `\n` `b` is, and `a` U+2028 `b` is the same two tokens.
The comment's class excludes `\n` through `\r` (line feed, vertical tab, form feed and carriage return) and U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029, and the whitespace class holds the same characters with space and tab; `crates/k-rust/src/inner/parser/scanner.rs` holds the layout both scanners use, which a test keeps equal to the module.
This diverges from the pinned K's default layout, `//[^\n\r]*` and `[\ \n\r\t]`: there the comment in `a // c` U+2028 `b` runs on to the next `\n` or `\r` and takes `b` with it, and a vertical tab, form feed, U+0085, U+2028 or U+2029 outside a comment is a scanner error.
(`the_default_line_comment_ends_at_every_line_end_a_location_counts` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), which runs both scanners; `the_default_layout_is_the_default_layout_module_of_the_prelude`, `the_default_line_comment_ends_at_every_line_terminator` and `every_line_terminator_is_default_layout` in [scanner.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/inner/parser/scanner.rs).)

## Definition text layout

A definition is one text: its module, import, syntax and `requires` sentences and attribute lists surround the bubbles of its rules, claims, contexts and configurations, which are parsed with the default layout (see "Default layout line ends").
krust reads the text around the bubbles with that same layout, so a character is layout everywhere in a definition or nowhere: whitespace is space, tab and every line terminator, a `//` comment ends at the first line end, and a `/* */` comment at the first `*/`.
A bubble ends before a sentence keyword that this whitespace separates from it, so U+2028 between the end of a rule and the next `rule` ends the first rule as a newline does, and a character that is no layout, such as U+00A0, separates no outer tokens, as it separates no tokens in a rule.
A `//` comment on the last line of the text ends with the text, as the default layout's comment `//[^…]*` does.
The positions of the outer syntax (sentence and bubble locations, the outer parser's errors) count lines as every other location does, so a location in a file whose lines end in `\r` is on its line.
An unquoted attribute value, such as `a b` in `[foo(a b)]`, ends on its line: any line end in it is an error, as a `\n` is.

This diverges from the pinned K's outer lexer, which ends `//` only at `\n` or `\r`, rejects a final `//` comment without a line end, and rejects a vertical tab, form feed, U+0085, U+2028 or U+2029 between outer tokens; in a bubble, a vertical tab or form feed before the next sentence ends its compilation with an internal `NegativeArraySizeException`, and the other three are a scanner error of the rule.
krust before this change read the text around bubbles with every Unicode `White_Space` character as whitespace (U+00A0 and U+2003 between `module` and its name were accepted) while splitting bubbles at ASCII space, tab, `\r` and `\n` only, and counted outer lines at `\n` only: in a file whose lines end in `\r`, an undefined sort on line 3 was reported at `1:51` where the pinned K reports `3:18`, which krust now reports too.
(`outer_whitespace_is_the_default_layouts_whitespace` and `outer_line_comments_end_where_the_default_layouts_do` in [parser.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/outer/parser.rs); `line_comments_end_at_every_line_end`, `outer_tokens_and_bubbles_split_at_the_same_whitespace`, `outer_positions_count_every_line_terminator` and `unquoted_attribute_values_end_on_their_line` in [outer_parser.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/outer_parser.rs).)

A comment is layout wherever a bubble word may begin: at the start of a bubble, or after whitespace or another comment in it, a complete comment is skipped as whitespace is, whatever text touches its end, and that text begins the next word.
So `rule /*c*/ [l]: a => b`, `rule /*c*/[l]: a => b` and `rule /* a longer comment */ [l]: a => b` each label the rule `l`, and in `rule [l]: a => b /*c*/rule [m]: b => a` the second `rule` begins a sentence, as it does after `/*c*/` and a space.
Within a word a comment is part of the word: the outer syntax reads a bubble as words its whitespace separates, since it does not read the tokens of the grammar the bubble is parsed with, so `b//syntax` and `b/*c*/rule` are one word each and end no bubble.
