The tags of `syntax priority` and of `syntax left`, `right` and `non-assoc` are words read the same way, so `syntax priority times /*c*/ > plus`, `syntax priority times /*c*/> plus` and `syntax priority /*c*/times > plus` each put `times` above `plus`, and `syntax left /*c*/plus` declares `plus` left-associative.
This diverges from the pinned K's outer lexer, which reads a comment at the start of a bubble word only when it is at least as long as the word's whitespace-free text: it reads `/*c*/[l]:` as one bubble word and rejects `rule /*c*/[l]: a => b` at the `:` (the label stays in the rule's text), and reads `/*c*/rule` as one word, so the two rules above are one sentence there, rejected at the `:` after `[m]`.
krust before this change read a comment as trivia only when it was longer than that text, so it also rejected `rule /*c*/ [l]: a => b`, whose comment is exactly as long as its word, which the pinned K accepts with the label `l`.
In tag lists likewise: the pinned K accepts `syntax priority times /*c*/ > plus` and rejects the other three declarations above, naming `/*c*/>`, `/*c*/times` and `/*c*/plus` as tags no production has; krust before this change rejected all four, reading the comment as a tag or part of one (`/*c*/` alone before a space).
(`bubble_comments_are_layout_wherever_a_word_may_begin` and `tag_list_comments_are_layout_wherever_a_tag_may_begin` in [outer_parser.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/outer_parser.rs).)

The text of a Markdown definition is its selected `k` blocks: prose and unselected blocks are not part of the definition, so they contribute only layout, and they keep their place in the file.
A prose character that is whitespace above (the default layout's) stays itself, and every other prose character, such as a letter, a fence marker, U+00A0 or U+2003, becomes one space for each byte of its UTF-8 encoding: prose never makes an outer-syntax error, and every character of the definition text is at its byte offset in the file.
This diverges from the pinned K, which keeps a prose character it reads as whitespace (U+2003, U+2028) and then rejects it as an outer lexical error, so a Markdown definition with such a character in its prose compiles with krust and not with the pinned K; U+00A0 in prose both accept.
krust before this change kept every Unicode `White_Space` character of the prose; with the outer syntax reading only the default layout's whitespace (above), U+00A0 or U+2003 in prose was an outer-syntax error.
(`markdown_prose_contributes_only_layout` in [outer_parser.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/outer_parser.rs).)

So the definition text has the file's line ends, each where the file has it, and a location in a block has the file's line and column whichever characters the prose and the fences around it hold.
A line end is a property of the characters on both sides of it (`\r` then `\n` is one line end, `\r` then anything else is two), so text that removes the characters between a `\r` and a later `\n` changes the file's lines, while text that replaces each of them with a space, which ends no line, keeps them.
So in `Prose\rbefore.\n` before a block, in a prose line ending in `\r` before an opening fence line ending in `\n`, and in a block whose last line ends in `\r` before a closing fence line ending in `\n`, the `\r` and the `\n` are two line ends, and a rule failure later in the file is reported on its line of the file. A position on a prose line (only the end of the text, when the last line is prose) counts each multi-byte prose character before it on that line once per byte of its UTF-8 encoding, so `éé tail` ending the file puts the end at column 10 where the line has 7 characters; the pinned K reports such an end at column 1.
This diverges from the pinned K, which reports each of those failures one line early (its own excerpt of the file shows the line above the rule); a Markdown file whose line ends are all `\n`, all `\r\n` or all `\r` gets the same locations from both.
krust before this change dropped the prose characters other than whitespace and reported the same early line as the pinned K.
(`markdown_prose_keeps_every_line_end_of_the_file` in [outer_markdown.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/outer_markdown.rs).)

A Markdown definition's lines are the lines of the definition text: they end at every line terminator, so a fence opens and closes on a line that `\r`, a vertical tab, form feed, U+0085, U+2028 or U+2029 ends as it does on one that `\n` ends, and `\r\n` is one line end.
So a Markdown definition whose lines end in `\r` alone compiles, and its locations count those lines, as the pinned K's do: a rule failure on line 11 is reported on line 11 whichever of `\n`, `\r\n` and `\r` ends the lines.
This diverges from the pinned K for the other terminators, where it rejects the definition: with U+2028, U+2029, a vertical tab or a form feed as the line end, an outer lexical error at the first one it keeps, and with U+0085 no module is found; krust reads their fences and compiles it.
krust before this change split a Markdown file at `\n` only, so in a file whose lines end in `\r` alone no fence opened and no module was found.
(`markdown_lines_end_at_every_line_terminator` in [outer_markdown.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/outer_markdown.rs).)

A string of the definition text (a terminal, a token regex `r"…"`, a user-list separator, a `requires` path, a quoted attribute value) is a K `String` literal as `domains.md` defines it: any character but `"`, `\`, newline and carriage return stands for itself, and `\"`, `\\`, `\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\f`, `\xHH`, `\uHHHH` and `\UHHHHHHHH` each name one code point.
So a definition's terminal `"\u2028"` is the one-character string a program's `"\u2028"` is, and a regex's string is decoded before it is read as a regex: `r"[\u2028]"` is the class of U+2028.
A string holds text, so an escape that names no Unicode scalar value (a surrogate, or a number above U+10FFFF) is an error.
This diverges from the pinned K, which rejects `\f`, `\x`, `\u` and `\U` in each of these strings, so krust accepts definitions the pinned K rejects; and the pinned K accepts a carriage return written verbatim in each of them, which krust accepts in none.
krust before this change rejected the four escapes too, and the verbatim carriage return in attribute strings only.
A line terminator other than `\n` and `\r` may stand for itself in a string, and is then a line end of the definition's text, which locations after it count. The pinned K counts no line at such a character: `syntax Foo ::= "a` U+2028 `b"` on line 2 is located at `Location(2,18,2,23)` there and at `Location(2,18,3,3)` by krust, and everything after it one line lower.
So [kast.md](../crates/k-rust/builtin/kast.md) writes U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029 in the `DEFAULT-LAYOUT` regexes as `\u0085`, `\u2028` and `\u2029`: the regexes are the same, and the module's lines are its lines as written.
(`outer_strings_are_k_string_literals` in [outer_parser.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/outer_parser.rs).)

## Compiler-resolved fresh constants

Within one rule or context, each distinct `!` variable receives a distinct consecutive offset from the generated counter and every occurrence of the same full variable name reuses that offset.
Rust assigns offsets in lexicographic order of the full names and advances the counter by the number of distinct names.
This deterministic association is Rust's stable compiler policy; portable programs must not depend on a particular association between names and offsets.
The policy must not depend on source paths or emulate the iteration order of Java's `HashSet`.

Distinct offsets, consistent reuse, and counter advancement are portable frontend properties.
Generated-value freshness additionally relies on the selected sort's `freshGenerator` contract.
Pinned K assigns names to offsets in unspecified `HashSet` iteration order, so its name-to-offset permutation is not a compiler oracle.
Each backend executes the permutation in its compiled definition; differing concrete values in ordered result positions must remain visible and must not be normalized as alpha-equivalent.
The `fresh-variables` compilation differential checks a case where the permutations happen to agree, while the focused Rust pass regression pins lexicographic allocation and repeated-name reuse.

Only a rewrite step of the configuration reads and advances the counter cell, so a `!` variable is accepted only in a rule applied as such a step.
It is rejected with `InvalidFreshConstant` at the sentence in a function, simplification, anywhere, macro or alias rule, each an equation applied to the term its left-hand side matches without the configuration, and in a claim, which states which configurations a path reaches and takes no step of its own.
Within a rewrite rule the value exists only once the step applies, so a `!` variable may occur only where the rule builds its result: on the right-hand side of a rewrite and in `ensures`.
It is rejected where the rule matches (the left-hand side of a rewrite, or outside every rewrite), which would compare the subject with a value nothing in it holds yet, and in `requires`, which decides whether the step applies before it allocates anything.
It is also rejected anywhere in a context: the heating step builds the context's term and the cooling step matches it again, and a value one step allocates is not one the other can match.
A local function (`#fun`, `#let`) on the right-hand side of a rewrite rule may mention a `!` variable: its equation receives the value from the enclosing rule as an argument.

The counter cell `<generatedCounter>` holds an `Int` that starts at the domain value `0`, so the configuration has it only when the main module sees a sort `Int` with domain values (token syntax).
A rule or claim may name the counter cell in any module whose view holds a cell of the configuration, as it may name the configuration's other cells there; in a module that sees no configuration, `<generatedCounter>` is not syntax and the sentence is a parse error.
The pinned K frontend parses the counter cell in every module and rejects it outside the main module after parsing ("Found klabel <generatedCounter> not defined in any production"); Rust accepts it in a module that sees the configuration and is not the main module, where the cell is a cell of the configuration the rule rewrites.
A `--no-prelude` definition without such an `Int` gets a `<generatedTop>` around its own top cell alone, and its claims get no counter cell; nothing in it can draw a fresh value, since a `freshGenerator` takes an `Int`.
A `!` variable in such a definition is rejected with `InvalidFreshConstant` at its sentence (with no `INT` module, the report is that the counter increment needs `INT`).

## Anywhere rules

Rust accepts and executes anywhere rules.
This differs from K's Haskell frontend, which rejects them (`checkAnywhereRules` in `k-frontend/src/main/java/org/kframework/kompile/Kompile.java`) or, under `--allow-anywhere-haskell`, removes them (`removeAnywhereRules` in `k-frontend/src/main/java/org/kframework/backend/kore/KoreBackend.java`).
The reason is the language specification: the [`anywhere` attribute](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/blob/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/docs/user_manual.md?plain=1#L1322-L1340) instructs "the backends" to apply the rule wherever it matches in the entire configuration, and it names no backend for which the attribute is unavailable.
A definition that uses the attribute is therefore a K definition whose meaning is specified, and a backend implementing that meaning must not reject or silently drop its rules.
Rust emits each anywhere rule as a KORE equation whose left-hand-side symbol carries the `anywhere` attribute, and the backend evaluates it during simplification like a function equation, as the manual's "simplified similarly to a `function`" describes.
Because that equation sees only its left-hand side, an anywhere rule that needs the configuration around it is rejected at the rule: a fresh variable `!X` draws its value from the counter cell, which the equation can neither read nor advance ([Compiler-resolved fresh constants](#compiler-resolved-fresh-constants)), and a left-hand side of several sibling cells is a fragment of their parent's children with no head of its own (the parent cell written around them is a term, and such a rule compiles).
A symbol that carries `anywhere` or a macro-like attribute and neither `constructor`, `functional`/`total` nor `function` is a partial function evaluated by its equations, and its applications carry definedness obligations.
When the frontend withholds `functional` from a value production, it emits the declaration attribute `value-production{}()` alongside its equation marker.
The Rust/Haskell target also emits `function{}()` so that KORE verifiers admit equations headed by the symbol.
`value-production` states that an application no equation reduces is a value of its sort; it asserts neither totality nor freeness.
The LLVM target emits neither `function{}()` nor `functional{}()` for such a production.
The external LLVM backend reads `function` to mean that an irreducible application is a computed application rather than a value, and does not read `value-production` to restore the value reading.
Withholding `function` therefore preserves the value production's irreducible applications while its anywhere equations can still reduce other applications.
Rust's backend sets `declared_function` only for `function` without `value-production`, so an anywhere head (the head of an anywhere rule, or an overload-greater) stays an `EquationHead` and a macro-like head stays `Residual`.
Both load as `Function(Partial)` and retain definedness obligations.
The backend reads a definition with anywhere equations by its normal-form model `M_NF`: each sort is interpreted by the normal forms other than `\bottom` of innermost evaluation, and each symbol by normalizing its application.
A step rewrites an application only when its arguments are normal forms: an equation binds each argument of its head by membership in its argument pattern and its variables range over values, so it states nothing at an undefined binding, and which equation applies is decided on the values of the arguments.
Among the equations offered at the application, the highest priority group with an applicable equation is used (an `owise` equation applies only where no higher one does); an equation applies where its left-hand side matches modulo injection composition, the overload axioms and the collection axioms with some substitution and its `requires` normalizes to `true` under it, and where its `ensures` normalizes to `false` the application is `\bottom`.
Matching modulo the map and set axioms is not unitary: `pick(K |-> V M)` matches `{1 |-> b, 2 |-> b}` with `K = 1` and with `K = 2`, and any matching may be taken, so the one equation applied under two matchings is a pair of steps from one term like two equations are.
A hooked symbol is evaluated by its hook, a declared partial function that no equation rewrites is `\bottom`, and an application of any other symbol that no equation rewrites (an equation head, a symbol declared `total` or `functional`, a partial symbol that is not a declared function) is a normal form.
`M_NF` is the model in which the backend's value claims below hold, not a reading the backend applies to partial functions: the backend does not decide an application of a declared partial function that no equation rewrites `\bottom`; its definedness is not decided by evaluation (no equation discharges or refutes its `\ceil`; only an assumption that the state is defined discharges it), and a rewrite rule that meets it applies, narrows or halts as [Concrete rewrite instantiation](#concrete-rewrite-instantiation) states.
`M_NF` is a model of the definition when evaluation terminates, which every evaluation assumes, and the critical pairs of the executable equations join, which the backend checks.

The backend makes two claims about the applications of an equation head (an `anywhere` production that is not a declared function) and about collections: *free*, every instance is a normal form distinct from every other normal form (an instance-normal token, the simplifier's certificate, and the apartness decisions and rigid-head readings made from them), and *defined*, an irreducible application of a `value-production` head is defined wherever its arguments are, so its `\ceil` is that of its arguments.
Both hold in `M_NF` once every term has one normal form.
They are minted from failed unification or matching only: an application one of whose equations matches it gets neither, also where that equation's `requires` is false (`\ceil(wrap(1))` under `wrap(X) = mk(X /Int 0) requires X ==Int 0` stays an obligation), which loses precision and never claims.
Equation matching reads a ground equation-head application written in a left-hand side by the same evidence as an equal subject: a ground term is its own only instance, so when no equation reaches it, it denotes exactly the normal form it writes, distinct from every other normal form whichever side of the match holds it.
An application whose variables only name ground subterms through `\and` (`h(1 #as X)`, which a left-hand side writes to reuse `1` on its right-hand side) denotes that one ground term too.
After the check the load records the instance-normal token of each such ground term under an equation-head application below the root of an executable left-hand side or in a term of a predicate simplification lemma's left-hand side, in rounds until one records nothing, since the scan of one term may match a left-hand side that holds another.
Equation matching then refutes a recorded application against a subject whose root is a constructor, a domain value, an injection or a collection, or against a concrete equation-head normal form of another symbol, as it refutes a rigid pattern root against a concrete equation-head subject: both sides are normal forms the backend certifies, and distinct normal forms are distinct values.
A recorded application is left undecided against a function application or an equation-head application without evidence, neither of which is known to be a normal form, and against a symbolic equation-head application of another symbol, as an equation-head subject is refuted by its root only when it is concrete.
So under `rule h(0) => k [anywhere]`, `rule f(h(1)) => 1` and `rule f(_) => 2 [owise]`, `f(k)` is `2`, as `f(h(1))` would be under `rule f(k) => 1`.
A ground term that nests the head of an anywhere rule whose left-hand side nests that head too is recorded the same way when the scan below shows it a normal form: under `rule (S1 ; a) ; S3 => S1 ; S3 [anywhere]`, `rule f(b ; a) => 1` and `rule f(_) => 2 [owise]`, `b ; a` is recorded, so `f(c)` and `f(b ; c)` are `2` and `f(b ; a)` is `1`, each with no `\ceil` left (`an_owise_equation_applies_past_a_ground_nested_anywhere_pattern` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).
In equation matching and implication, a ground left-hand-side application that an equation reaches (`h(0)` there), an application with a variable that names no ground subterm, and an implication pattern are still read up to equations, as any value of their sort.

The instance-normal scan, which decides that no equation reaches any instance of an application, reads a left-hand side differently: an equation-head application written there, with variables or without, is read by its root wherever the check leaves that head's claims in place and the head is not an overloaded production.
A step of `M_NF` matches a left-hand side modulo injection composition, the overload axioms and the collection axioms, not modulo the equations, so it matches a pattern `g(p)` against a normal form only through the root `g`, and reading the pattern by that root decides whether `M_NF` reaches an instance.
The equation's membership axiom also holds of values that only reduce to an instance of `g(p)`; whether `M_NF` satisfies the equation at those values is what the pairs at the heads in equation arguments decide ([Anywhere heads in equation arguments](#anywhere-heads-in-equation-arguments)), and a pair there that does not join withdraws `g`, whose applications the scan then reads up to equations, leaving their roots open.
The head's own claims are asked for, not only those of the head the scanned equation is rooted at, because the scan also reads `symbolic` equations and simplification lemmas, which the closure does not: a lemma rooted at another head that nests a withdrawn `g` keeps `g` open even where that other head keeps its claims.
A lemma or `symbolic` equation rooted at `g` itself is paired by no check and does not withdraw `g`; that the values it equates with `g(p)` are values `M_NF` gives `g(p)` rests only on the assumption, stated below, that such an equation is a theorem of the executable equations.
An overloaded production stays read up to equations: its overload axioms equate `g(inj(x))` with `inj(l(x))`, a value with another root.
So under `rule (S1 ; a) ; S3 => S1 ; S3 [anywhere]` alone, whose pairs all join, `seq(b, a)` is a normal form (an instance of the rule's inner `S1 ; a` is a sequence, never the constructor `b`): `(b ; a) ; d` is `b ; d` and `wrap((b ; a) ; d)` is `wrap(b ; d)`, with no `\ceil` left, and a rewrite rule `go => (b ; a) ; d` ends at `b ; d` (`an_anywhere_rule_that_nests_its_own_head_rewrites_where_its_pairs_join` and `a_rewrite_rule_is_not_applied_under_an_equality_with_a_scanned_normal_form` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).
Where the head is withdrawn, as `seq` is under the unrestricted reassociation `rule (S1 ; S2) ; S3 => S1 ; S2 ; S3 [anywhere]`, whose pair with itself the check does not show to join, `(b ; c) ; d` stays as written under `\ceil(seq(b, c))` (`a_reassociation_whose_head_is_withdrawn_keeps_the_nested_application_open`).

When a definition is loaded the backend checks its critical pairs once (`k_rust_backend::confluence`).
A root pair is two executable equations of one index and priority group, or one equation with itself when its left-hand side may match one subject in several ways (a map or set pattern with a key or element that the rest of the left-hand side does not fix: a key fixed by another argument, `lookup(K |-> V M, K)`, is matched in one way only); an inner pair is an equation, a non-variable position strictly below the root of its left-hand side, and an equation that could rewrite the subterm there (those offered at its index, at the other productions of its overload family, and at a bare variable).
Each pair is unified over normal-form instances modulo injection composition and, for the overload families whose own overload equations join, the overload axioms.
Each overlap is then separated by priority, refuted by its conditions (both `requires`, simplified with every claim withheld and read for complementary literals; the simplification of each condition is bounded by a budget of polls for a stop, and one that exceeds it, whether or not its evaluation ends, decides nothing, so the verdict is a function of the definition except where a simplification of the check ran out of native stack ([`docs/backend-port.md`](backend-port.md), the critical-pair check); the check ignores the loading request's cancellation and deadlines, so the verdict cached with the definition does not depend on the request that loaded it), or joined by innermost steps valid on every instance: a step may erase or bind a subterm it cannot show defined only when that subterm is defined on every instance, and a residual is split into the cases the equations offered at it distinguish.
An equation paired with itself joins when its right-hand side and `ensures` read only variables every matching binds alike (`nonEmpty(K |-> V M) = true`); otherwise it is not shown to join, under the rule that an overlap involving a map or set pattern is not joined unless proven, and is reported with the shape "a map or set pattern that matches one collection in several ways".
Each matching of a map or set pattern is an instance of the equation, `f(t) = rhs σ requires R σ`, and each instance holds by itself; so the equation applies to a term when one of its instances does, and the priority groups below it are reached only when none does.
The evaluator tries the instances in a fixed order and applies the first whose `requires` holds. It passes to a lower priority group only when every instance is refuted (a failed match, or its `requires` refuted, whether or not a `concrete` or `symbolic` attribute set its binding aside); when one is undecided and none holds, the application is kept, as for an equation that matches in one way. An instance set aside by a `concrete` or `symbolic` attribute whose `requires` holds or is undecided keeps the application too: the attribute chooses when the evaluator uses the equation, not what it states, so the equation holds or may hold of the application, and the premise of every lower-priority equation of the function (an `owise` equation's, or the priority reading's) excludes it. The set-aside instance's `requires` is decided only where it can change the outcome, when a lower-priority group of the function follows; elsewhere the application is kept either way. This loses precision where a true result relied on falling through while that `requires` is undecided: under `rule w(X) => mk(0) requires X >Int 10 [anywhere, concrete]` and `rule w(_) => mk(0) [anywhere, owise]`, `<k> w(N) => mk(0) </k>` holds for every `N`, but for a symbolic `N` the first equation's `requires` is undecided, `w(N)` is kept, and the claim is not proven; with `requires N <Int 5` on the claim that `requires` is refuted and the claim is proven. Ceil equations and simplification lemmas are tried the same way, so a lemma whose map or set pattern matches a term in several ways applies when one of its instances does; for simplification lemmas, which state nothing about the lemmas after them, a set-aside instance only lets the next lemma be tried.
A left-hand side that reads a variable it binds again in a map key or set element matches a subject whose collection holds that item under another symbolic term exactly where the two terms are equal: `rule [[ total(WHO) => B ]] <account> <id> WHO </id> <balance> B </balance> </account>` matches `total(AID)` over an account `<id> BID </id>` only where `AID` equals `BID`, as `lookup(K, (K |-> V) _)` does `lookup(A, B |-> 7)`. Such an instance is refuted where the path condition refutes those equalities together with the instance's `requires`, and is decided, like a set-aside instance, only where a lower-priority group of the function follows; otherwise it keeps the application. So with `rule total(_) => 0 [owise]` beside it, `<k> total(AID) => 0 </k>` over the one account `BID` is proven under `requires AID =/=K BID` and not proven without it; over two accounts it needs a disequality for each key; and over accounts with a frame it is not proven, since the frame may hold `AID`. A key matched through an application of a function with algebraic axioms (`assoc`, `comm`, `idem`, `unit`) only suffices to bind the subject's variables, so such an instance is never refuted this way; nor is any matching that rests on such bindings, since another assignment of the function's arguments may match the same subject: with `cat` commutative, `lookcv((cat(K, J) |-> val(J, V)) _)` beside an `owise` 0 keeps `lookcv((cat(1, 2) |-> val(1, 7)) (cat(3, 4) |-> val(9, 9)))`, whose value is 7 with `K = 2`, and `reqcv(cat(K, J)) = 1 requires K <Int J` keeps `reqcv(cat(2, 1))`, rather than reaching the `owise` equation through a value or a `requires` that fails only under the arguments as written (`tentative_bindings.rs` in [the backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/tentative_bindings.rs)). A K definition reaches the same reading through a concatenation of collection variables below an equation's root, whose concatenation is such a function: `rule m2(M1:Map M2:Map) => 1 requires size(M1) ==Int 1` beside `rule m2(_) => 0 [owise]` matches `m2(A B)` as written with `M1 := A`, so under `size(A) ==Int 2 andBool size(B) ==Int 1` the claim `m2(A B) => 0` is not proven, since `M1 := B` satisfies the `requires`; likewise for a `Set` and a `List` (`an_owise_equation_does_not_apply_where_only_the_written_split_of_a_concatenation_fails` in [proof_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs)). Where the matcher decides some matchings of a map or set pattern but leaves others undecided (an entry whose value repeats its symbolic key under a function, `B |-> val(B +Int 1, 8)` against `(K |-> val(K, V)) _`, or an entry of a map with a frame, which may hold another matching entry), each decided matching is still an instance and is applied when its `requires` holds, whatever the undecided ones turn out to be: `f((1 |-> val(1, 7)) (B |-> val(B +Int 1, 8)))` is 7. The undecided matchings keep the application from being refuted, so the lower-priority groups are reached only when every matching is decided and refuted.
So under `rule f((K |-> _) _) => mk(1) requires K ==Int 2` beside `rule f(_) => mk(2) [owise]`, `krun` gives `f(1 |-> 0 2 |-> 0)` the value `mk(1)` whichever key the first matching binds, and `f(1 |-> 0 3 |-> 0)` the value `mk(2)`.
When several instances hold with different results, the definition equates those results (or has no model); that is the equation's pair with itself, which the check reports unless the right-hand side and `ensures` read only variables every matching binds alike, in which case every instance gives the same result and the order does not matter.
The cost is one `requires` per matching, tried until one holds, for an equation whose pattern matches in several ways; an equation that matches in one way evaluates one.
A lemma whose right-hand side is `#Top` gives its subject no term to be rewritten to. Where the subject is an operand of a term-level conjunction, the lemma drops it (`T #And X` is `X` when a `#Top` lemma applies to `T`, under the definedness of the terms the instance binds; when lemmas apply to both operands, one operand is kept); anywhere else the lemma is not used: the subject is left to the other equations of its symbol and kept as it is when none applies, so the simplification does not fail.
An executable function or anywhere equation must give a term (or `#Bottom`) as its right-hand side. One whose right-hand side is `#Top`, with or without an `ensures`, states that the application is every value of its sort, while an application of a function or anywhere head has at most one value: the definition then has a model only if that sort has at most one element, and on such a sort the equation says no more than the value it equals. A right-hand side that is only a predicate (`rule c(X) => #Ceil(X)`) denotes every value or none, and is refused for the same reason. `krun`, `kprove`, `kore-exec` and the RPC server refuse the definition when they load it ("the function equation ... of ... gives no term as its right-hand side, only \top or predicates"), as they refuse a rewrite rule whose right-hand side is `#Top`; the message names the equation by its identifier, its written position when it has one, and the symbol it defines, so a generated equation without a position, such as a `#let` lambda's, is named by its identifier and its `#lambda` symbol. `kcompile` still succeeds: it loads the backend definition only for a definition with an `anywhere` rule, to report value-claim warnings, and discards a load error there. A `[non-executable]` such equation is not loaded and is not refused. `#Top` remains a right-hand side of simplification lemmas, as above, and of ceil axioms (`#Ceil(t) => #Top`).
An executable equation rooted at a free constructor or an injection, indexed at a collection or a bare variable, or an overload equation of another shape than `g(inj(x1), ..., inj(xn)) = inj(h(x1, ..., xn))`, violates a precondition of the claims and is reported; the first two kinds also withdraw claims, as a pair that does not join does: the check does not classify their overlaps, so each such equation seeds the closure below. A `Variable`-indexed equation is loaded, and since its variable ranges over its whole sort, it withdraws every equation head a value of that sort can hold, which for a `List` or `KItem` variable is nearly every head.
A pair not shown to join (the reducts differ, a condition is neither refuted nor decided, the two `ensures` differ, the unifier meets a shape it does not decide, a left-hand side matches in several ways with different results, a step must be refused, or the join runs out of its budget) and that withdraws a claim is named at load by `krun`, `kprove` and the RPC server, and at compile time by `kompile` (warning category `ValueClaims`), with both equations' written positions, the overlapped term, the reason, and the equation heads and overloaded symbols whose claims it withdraws (below), each of which loses claims the author may rely on. A pair at an argument of an equation's left-hand side whose second equation is rooted at an anywhere head is named whether or not it withdraws a claim, as an equation not shown consistent ([Anywhere heads in equation arguments](#anywhere-heads-in-equation-arguments)).

A pair that does not join is an axiom `M_NF` may violate: a model of the definition may then identify terms `M_NF` keeps apart, or make undefined terms `M_NF` calls defined.
Its claims are withdrawn through a closure: both equations of the pair are tainted; an equation whose left-hand side mentions a tainted symbol is tainted; a tainted equation taints every symbol it mentions that is neither a free constructor nor a hooked symbol other than an equation head (a hooked function is interpreted by its hook in every model, while an equation head's applications denote what its equations make of them, hooked or not); and it taints every equation head whose applications can occur inside a value of the sort of one of its variables.
The models meant here may have empty sorts: an axiom's variables range over values, so `g(X:A) = c1` beside `g(X:A) = c2` leaves only models in which `A` is empty, and with it every value production that builds `A`'s values, whose applications are then undefined.
A value of a sort holds the values of its subsorts, of its collection elements, and of the arguments of every symbol of the sort whose application to a value the model below reads as a new value: a constructor, an equation head, a total `injective` symbol, a partial symbol that is not a declared function, and a total function or `functional` symbol whose sort has no value built from literals, constructors and total symbols alone.
A total function or `functional` symbol that is not injective and has no algebraic axiom, whose sort has such a value, holds nothing: the model reads its unrewritten applications to new values as one fixed value that every model has.
A total `injective` symbol into a hooked sort, whose values are fixed literals, cannot be read either way, so the equation heads its arguments can hold are withdrawn and the symbol is reported.
No tainted index gets a token or a certificate, and so no `value-production` discharge.
With this closure, whenever the definition has a model, one model makes every claim on an untainted term true at once: it keeps the values of any model of the definition, adds the untainted equations' normal forms that hold an untainted equation-head application as new values, reads each symbol on new values as stated above, and no tainted equation can see a new value through its symbols or through its variables.
The load classifies only the pairs that can change a claim.
The claims are asked about the applications of equation heads (tokens and certificates), of overloaded symbols (certificates) and about collections (tokens); collections are withdrawn only by the precondition rows above.
An equation is *relevant* when the closure seeded by that equation alone taints an equation head or an overloaded symbol, and a pair other than an overload family's own (whose verdicts the unifier needs) is classified only when one of its two equations is relevant.
Each claim the load makes is sound on its own classification: each rule of the closure has one premise, so the closure of the pairs that do not join is the union of the closures of each pair's equations, and a pair with no relevant equation adds to that union no symbol a claim is asked about, whether it joins or not.
When the check of every pair classifies each of the load's pairs as the load does, every token, certificate, `\ceil` discharge and warning is the one it gives; what the load does not compute is the pairs that withdraw no claim (WASM has 61 of them, functions over `Int`, `String`, `Bytes` and lists), and the function symbols only those pairs taint.
That equality is tested, not derived: the variables a pair is classified under are renamed apart with counters that the pairs classified before it advance, and a condition's simplification has a time limit, so the two checks could in principle classify one pair differently, and each would still be sound.
`BackendDefinition::value_claims_in_scope(CheckScope::All)` runs the check of every pair; the backend's randomized oracle compares the two verdicts on every definition it generates, and unit tests compare them on the pairs where the filter's decision matters, which are those at a hooked symbol, a collection or a bare variable (a left-hand side the collection laws normalize to one), since at any other index the two equations of a pair are both relevant or neither is; no executable equation is headed by an injection (the load rejects one), so the injection index has no pair.
A reported pair is spelled with the variables the check introduced numbered from 0 in order of occurrence, so its warning is the same in both checks.
A definition that declares no equation head, and no overloaded symbol that can be withdrawn (each is a free constructor or hooked, or there is none), has no pair checked: no pair can change a claim. Its equations the evaluator never applies are still reported, as stated below.
Every equation head can be withdrawn, a hooked one (a hooked production declared `anywhere`) included: the closure withdraws equation heads through a variable's sort, through a pinned symbol, through an equation that mentions them and through an equation whose left-hand side mentions them, whether or not they are hooked, since a hooked head may be partial. A K definition declares no hooked equation head: the frontend emits no `hook` attribute for a non-function production and refuses `anywhere` on a production, so one comes only from KORE written by another producer.
Within one check a condition that is an earlier one with its free variables renamed is not simplified again: the earlier answer, renamed back, is an answer for it, because every simplification step is an axiom instance, which holds under any injective renaming, and the definition, the options and the solver of the check's simplifications are fixed. A condition with a quantifier is reused only as written, and an answer is reused only when renaming it back can neither capture a variable under a quantifier nor share one the simplifier introduced. Whether an answer can be renamed back depends only on the renamed condition, so when it cannot, the memo records that, and every later condition that renames to it is simplified as written without simplifying the renamed one again. The verdicts, and the work counts other than the memo's own (its hits, and the conditions simplified renamed or as written), are the same without the reuse (`ConditionMemo::Off`); the randomized oracle and the corpus gate compare them on every definition.
The backend's randomized tests check this without the closure, by the axioms the claims rest on: they instantiate every executable equation with the elements the backend says are defined and, where its `requires` holds and no higher priority group applies, fail when the two sides evaluate to two different claimed values, or one to a claimed value and the other to `\bottom`, unless the contradiction involves no claimed symbol (the definition then has no model, and the check must name a pair). Their definitions include maps and sets, hooked equations and the FUN list and prelude fixtures. The judge is the backend's evaluation, which reads an equation from left to right only, so a claim refuted only by reasoning in both directions is tested by dedicated probes; the self pairs are also tested against a brute-force enumeration of matchings over every map with keys in `{0, 1, 2}` and values in `{0, 1}` and every set over `{0, 1, 2}`, and the closure's collection edges (map keys and values, list and set elements) by one probe each.
The enumeration covers flat patterns over one map or set of `Int`: up to two entries or elements, each key, value or element a literal or a variable (a key may also be bound by another argument), and an optional rest, with a result that reads literals, entry variables or the rest, an optional `requires`, and either an optional `ensures` or a disjunction of two results. Patterns outside it (nested collections, keys or elements built by a symbol, collections of other sorts) rest on the fixpoint's proof alone.
Under `h(3) = h(1)`, `w(h(3)) = mk(1 /Int 0)` makes `w(h(1))` undefined in every model; the inner pair of the two equations at `h(3)` has the sides `\bottom` and `w(h(1))`, which do not join, so `h` and `w` are withdrawn and `\ceil(w(h(1)))` stays an obligation.
FUN, SIMPLE, KOOL, WASM and IELE report no pair that withdraws a claim, and FUN's `\ceil(k -> 7)` is `\top`.

The contract states, and does not check, that evaluation terminates; that a symbol declared `total` is defined on defined arguments (an author axiom; a contradicted one is named, [A contradicted `total` attribute is named, not repaired](#a-contradicted-total-attribute-is-named-not-repaired)); that a rewrite axiom is a transition, not a constraint on its left-hand side; that simplification lemmas and `concrete` or `symbolic` equations are theorems of the executable equations; and that a hooked symbol's equations agree with its hook wherever the hook is defined.
A definition that breaks one of these, or whose axioms have no model at all, gets the warnings and no further guarantee.
An equation instance carries the definedness of what its left-hand side consumes.
An equation binds each argument of its head by membership in its argument pattern, and an undefined term is a member of no pattern, so its instance at `f(t1, ..., tn)` holds only where every `ti` is defined: the step takes `\ceil(ti)` of every argument a variable does not bind whole (an element variable's binding carries the same obligation, and a set variable ranges over the undefined pattern too, so its argument carries none), minus what the instantiated `requires` states, and decides it with that `requires`: a refuted obligation makes the equation not apply, and an open one is carried with the result.
This covers a function application in an argument pattern (`fa(X, pf(X)) = one`, with `pf` defined at `c` only, gives `fa(d, pf(d))` the value `one` only under `\ceil(pf(d))`, and `pf(pf(Y)) = m(0)` likewise on `pf(pf(d))`), an equation head whose claims are withdrawn (under `h(m(I), Y) = Y requires I ==Int 0` and `g(h(m(0), Y)) = m(1 /Int 0)`, `g` is undefined on every value, and `k(g(c)) = d` gives `k(g(c))` the value `d` only under `\ceil(g(c))`), and the distinctness of a map or set the pattern splits (`f((c |-> V) _M) = V` gives `f({c |-> 1, Y |-> 2})` the value `1` only under the distinctness of `c` and `Y`).
An argument whose stored `ceil_free` bit is set (a concrete map or set of distinct rigid keys among them) is defined outright and costs one read of that bit.
A simplification lemma is read the same way: it states its equality without memberships, but an application is undefined wherever one of its arguments is, so its instance where an argument it consumes is undefined would equate an undefined term with its right-hand side and make that side empty there; the lemma says nothing at such an instance and carries the same obligations.
A predicate lemma consumes the term operands its pattern does not bind to a variable; their obligations are used, when open or refuted, only where the predicate is strict in the operand (`\equals(sf(t), "c")` is `\bottom` where `sf(t)` is, `\equals(sf(t), sg(t))` is not), as a bound variable's are, and an obligation of an operand under a quantifier leaves the lemma unused.
The positions an equation consumes are matched as written, a computed application or a ground subterm not in normal form included (`fg(pf(c)) = one` applies neither to `fg(c)` nor to `fg(pf(c))`, which evaluates to `fg(c)` first), as [Function applications on a left-hand side](#function-applications-on-a-left-hand-side) states; a rewrite rule reads a computed position by value instead, and the step that matches it evaluates the application, so a configuration where the application is undefined is not matched.
(`a_step_keeps_or_carries_every_consumed_subterm_it_cannot_show_defined`, `an_equation_matches_a_computed_pattern_position_as_written`, `an_equation_matches_a_ground_pattern_subterm_as_written` and `a_computed_pattern_position_of_a_rewrite_rule_matches_the_value_it_computes` in [definedness_classes.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/definedness_classes.rs), `a_predicate_lemma_carries_the_definedness_of_the_operands_it_consumes` in [simplify.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/simplify.rs).)
A ceil equation `\ceil(l) = q` is used through the same instances wherever the backend states a term's definedness: at a `\ceil` the simplifier meets, and in the obligations it states for a term it binds, consumes or carries, where it takes the condition of the first usable instance of an unconditional ceil equation and keeps the opaque `\ceil` when there is none. An instance a `concrete` or `symbolic` attribute rejects, or that binds an element variable to a pattern with a set variable, is used by neither.
Nor is an instance that binds an element variable to a pattern containing the subject: `rule #Ceil(X:W) => #Top [simplification]` holds of every element `X`, but its instance at a partial application `f(t)` is `\ceil(f(t)) = \ceil(f(t)) /\ \top`, which says nothing of whether `f(t)` is defined, so under it `\ceil(f(t))` stays an obligation. This differs from reading the lemma as "every term of sort `W` is defined", which is false wherever a partial function of sort `W` is undefined; a definition that relies on that reading to discharge such obligations keeps them. A lemma over a set variable, `rule #Ceil(@X:W) => ...`, binds the subject without an obligation and is used.
(`the_static_analysis_uses_only_the_instances_a_concreteness_attribute_admits`, `the_static_analysis_does_not_bind_an_element_variable_to_a_set_pattern` and `an_instance_whose_binding_is_the_subject_gives_its_ceil_no_condition` in [definedness.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/definedness.rs).)
A rewrite step carries the definedness of a collection its left-hand side consumes. A step is a transition, so its successor may denote only states its predecessor reaches, and a map or set is defined only where its keys (elements) are distinct: `f((c |-> _) M) => rest(M)` matches `f({c |-> 1, Y |-> 2})` only where `Y` is not `c`, while the bindings `M = Y |-> 2` and `_ = 1` are defined everywhere. The step therefore takes `\ceil` of the subject's collection at every position where the left-hand side has a map, set or list pattern rather than a variable (a list's concatenation is total, so a list carries only its elements' obligations), beside the `\ceil` of every non-variable binding: a refuted obligation makes the step vacuous, and one the path does not establish (with, on the proof route, the assumed definedness of the initial state) is carried into the successor's constraint, here `\not(c = Y)` beside `rest(Y |-> 2)`, as binding the whole map to a variable (`g(M) => rest(M)`) already carried it. Like a binding's obligation, it is a condition of the step; the instances of the subject where it fails are those where the subject itself is undefined, which denote no configuration: on the initial configuration no remainder, lower priority or stuck result is given them ([Undefined instances of the initial configuration](#undefined-instances-of-the-initial-configuration), `updating_a_map_value_beside_an_opaque_rest_keeps_the_absence_obligation` in [rewrite.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/rewrite.rs)), and a later state's own step carried the obligations that exclude them. A collection whose stored `ceil_free` bit is set costs one read of it (`a_rewrite_step_keeps_the_distinctness_of_a_collection_it_splits` in [definedness_classes.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/definedness_classes.rs)).
The load reports an equation that holds a declared function's application below its left-hand side's root as never applied to the value that application computes (it matches that position as written, so it applies only where the subject spells the same application, and then under that application's `\ceil`), and likewise an equation whose right-hand side or `ensures` has a variable its left-hand side does not bind; each is an axiom the evaluator does not apply to every subject it describes (the first not where the argument holds the value the application computes, the second not at all), whether or not the definition makes value claims, so the report is made for every definition, by one walk of each equation's left-hand side, and names the claims the equation withdraws only where it withdraws some (`an_equation_never_applied_is_reported_without_any_value_claim` in [execution.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/backend/execution.rs)).
The marker is emitted from the production kind and totality judgement, not read from a K source attribute.
A source function carries no such marker: an application its equations leave unevaluated remains a computed application.
Any other symbol without one of those claims is rejected (`Invalid type for symbol`).
A non-function value production carries compiler-added `functional` and its functional axiom exactly when it heads no anywhere, macro-like or overload equation, or every such equation has a trivially true `ensures` and a right-hand side evidently defined by declared-total symbols and heads admitted earlier.
A label heads a rule equation only through a rule that is not a simplification lemma, so a simplification lemma or a macro label that heads no rule makes no production an equation head.
On a value production, the frontend discards a deprecated source `functional` before emitting the judgement's claims in both the semantic and syntax definitions.
It restores `functional` only when the judgement establishes totality, keeping the declaration and its functional axiom consistent with the definedness reading used for generated lambdas.
To state totality as an author axiom instead, use `function, total`; that declaration remains trusted.
A deprecated `functional` on a `function` production states the same claim and is read exactly as `function, total`: the declaration carries `functional` and its functional axiom.
An equation equates the head with its right-hand side wherever its condition holds, so an undefined right-hand side contradicts an unconditional totality claim; conditions and left-hand-side patterns restrict applicability but do not establish definedness.
The judgement uses a least fixed point: neither a head nor a dependency cycle may establish its own totality.
A stage that still sees the self-referential equations `foo(a => b)` (whose right-hand side is `foo(b)`) or `(5 => 6) : _` cannot use the head itself as totality evidence.
The judgement reads each stage's terms: macro expansion can remove a circular dependency before emission, while constructor-only overload chains and macros with defined expansions retain `functional`.
Normalization N36 drops the reference declaration's `functional` and its functional axiom only when the port carries the same equation-head attribute and withholds both claims.
For a matching value-production declaration it also drops the port's `value-production{}()` and any extra `function{}()`, counting each removal separately.
It accepts both the Rust target's marked function and the LLVM target's marked value declaration.
It preserves a source function's `function{}()` and every unrelated attribute and axiom.
For the kore/llvm pairing, normalization N38 adds `function{}()` only to marked value-production declarations in a separate copy used for reference-tool verification, and prints the number changed.
It refuses a marked LLVM declaration that already carries `function` or `functional`.
The verifier requires a function or functional equation head, so this copy lets it check the remaining KORE without asserting evidence-free totality or changing the definition the LLVM backend executes.
The Rust/Haskell pairing verifies the original definition without N38.
Rust's kompile does not add `injective` to the symbol declaration of such a production, but emits a user-written `injective` attribute as written. That attribute is an axiom (equal applications have equal arguments), and an anywhere equation may identify applications with different arguments: `wrap(s(X)) = wrap(X)` gives `wrap(s(z)) = wrap(z)`, and with `s(z) != z` the declared theory would be inconsistent. The same holds for the head of a macro-like rule, whose emitted axiom is an equation of the same kind.
For the same reason Rust's kompile adds neither `constructor` nor `injective` to a production that heads a macro-like rule and emits no no-confusion axioms for it, even when the macro-like attribute sits on the rule and not on the production: the rule `plain(X) => done(X) [macro]` is the equation `plain(X) = done(X)`, which contradicts `plain(X) != done(Y)`. The same holds for a production whose label another, macro-like production also has, since every rule headed by that label is then a macro-like rule. Such a declaration carries no `macro` attribute either, since a rule whose left side does not cover every argument leaves the other applications unexpanded. The definition checks admit macro-like attributes only on productions, so this case reaches emission only through the library functions that emit an unchecked definition (`module_to_kore` and its `_from_resolved` variants).
An overload-greater production keeps `injective` exactly when every production below it in its overload family is a non-function, non-`assoc`/`comm`/`idem` production that heads no anywhere or macro-like rule: its overload equations send each application to the injected normal form of the most specific lesser production that accepts the arguments, or leave it normal, so two argument tuples meet only through the same production at the same arguments. `simplification` rules are lemmas and do not count. This differs from the reference frontend, which declares every such production `injective`; no backend decision in Rust reads the attribute of an anywhere symbol.
An overload-greater production `g` never carries `constructor`, because its overload equations evaluate it; it has the injectivity axiom and the no-confusion axioms (`\not(g(X) ∧ c(Y))` against every other symbol `c` of its sort that has them) exactly when it heads no anywhere or macro-like rule itself and two conditions hold, and otherwise has neither. Condition (a) is the one above: every production below `g` is locally injective. Condition (b): no other production related to `g` in the overload order, whether above it, below it, or sharing a lesser production with it, has `g`'s result sort head. The overload order is transitive, so both conditions cover chains of lowerings. Under (b) every value an application of `g` reaches is either that application in normal form or an injection `inj{T,S}(v)` from a strictly smaller sort, which differs from every application of another no-confusion symbol of `S`; under (a) two such values coincide only through the same production at the same arguments. Without (a) the injectivity axiom is false: with a lesser `abs(Int) : Int [function]`, `g(inj(1)) = inj(abs(1)) = inj(abs(-1)) = g(inj(-1))`. Without (b) a disjointness axiom is false: for `asInt(Int) : Value` below `asNumber(Number) : Value`, the overload equation `asNumber(inj(X)) = asInt(X)` instantiates `\not(asInt(X) ∧ asNumber(Y))` at `Y := inj(X)` to `\not(asInt(X))`, which denies a functional application, so the theory has no model; two greaters of one sort that share a lesser `l` both equal `inj(l(x))` in the same way. The withheld axioms are therefore false in every model of the emitted equations, not merely unproved. A greater that satisfies (a) but not (b) keeps the `injective` attribute, and one that fails (a) carries neither the attribute nor any of the axioms. Normalization N34 drops the corresponding axioms from the reference side of the compile comparison.

The `supported-superset` exclusion applies to expectations that demand frontend rejection or removal of anywhere rules.
It does not exempt their execution from verification or equation tests.
The differential manifest includes anywhere inference fixtures; compiling the reference with its `kore` frontend policy allows the emitted equations to be evaluated with `kore-exec`.
An LLVM execution result may still differ because LLVM matches a normalized anywhere symbol syntactically while Kore can match it through a simplified function equality; the corresponding conformance case records that separate limitation.

After equation normalization reaches a fixed point, the backend treats a term as concrete when every application head is either a constructor or an anywhere-attributed production without the `function` attribute.
This concrete-after-normalization classification is shared by equation matching, overload lowering, structural predicate simplification, and rewrite instantiation; on execution and search routes rewrite instantiation also reads a variable-free configuration as one state ([Concrete rewrite instantiation](#concrete-rewrite-instantiation)).
The simplifier may cache a closed normalized anywhere or overloaded application as evaluated only after a fresh scan has found every compatible equation inapplicable independently of the current path condition.
A symbolic application, a scan with an indeterminate equation, or an equation refuted under the current path condition must remain unevaluated.
An equality from the path condition that can replace any part of a cached term must be applied before the cache can short-circuit simplification.
Rewrite matching decomposes equal rigid heads and rejects a different rigid head, while matches against variables and ordinary function heads remain symbolic.
A subject application of an anywhere production without the `function` attribute is a rigid head, in rewrite, equation and implication matching alike, only when it is normal on every instance (defined below); otherwise the pair stays symbolic, since an anywhere equation such as `wrap(s(z)) = wrap(z)` gives `wrap(s(X))` the value of `wrap(z)` at `X = z` and a rule for `wrap(z)` must not be refuted on it, nor `wrap(X)` decomposed against `wrap(z)` into `X = z` alone.
A ground application that the simplifier has certified is such a normal form without the scan. The certificate is set only when every equation offered the application fails to match it and the load-time check leaves its head's claims in place, as described below for structural equality.
The exception is the root of equation matching: an equation is tried on the application it rewrites as written, and only that application's arguments are compared as the values they denote.
The left-hand side of a rule or equation is matched as written.
The rule index keys a subject's overloaded anywhere head apart from unrelated rigid heads only under the same condition, and keys it as a wildcard otherwise.
While the load-time check withdraws a head's claims ([Anywhere heads in equation arguments](#anywhere-heads-in-equation-arguments)), no application of that head is normal on every instance, so a rewrite rule whose left-hand side has another constructor where such an application stands does not decide that pair.
On a configuration with variables, or on a proof route, the rule narrows.
On a variable-free configuration on execution and search routes, a rule that leaves a left-hand-side variable unbound halts the step ([Concrete rewrite instantiation](#concrete-rewrite-instantiation)): with `rule <k> go(X) => (X ; c) ; d </k>`, `krun` of `b ; c` halts at depth 0 with `leaves Rule-hVarX uninstantiated on a variable-free configuration`.
A rule that binds every variable, such as one with a ground left-hand side, applies under an equality between the two, kept as a condition of the branch beside a branch under its negation.
Such a rule whose result is again such an application then applies again on every state it builds, and the run does not terminate, also on a program that no rule rewrites in `M_NF`.
Beside the withdrawn reassociation `rule (S1 ; S2) ; S3 => S1 ; S2 ; S3 [anywhere]` and `rule a ; S => S [anywhere]`, with `go` a further constructor of `Stmt`, the rule `<k> go => (b ; c) ; d </k>` makes `krun` of `b ; c` and of `go` run without end, and `--depth 2` on `b ; c` gives three leaves: `b ; c` under `\not(\equals(go, seq(b, c)))`, and `(b ; c) ; d` twice under `\ceil(seq(b, c)) /\ \equals(go, seq(b, c))`, once with `\equals(go, seq(seq(b, c), d))` and once with its negation, and `--depth 6` gives the same three leaves, since the run revisits these states (`a_withdrawn_anywhere_head_makes_a_rebuilding_rule_branch_on_equalities` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), which also pins the halt of `go(X) => (X ; c) ; d`).
The halt of [Concrete rewrite instantiation](#concrete-rewrite-instantiation) does not end such a run: the rule's left-hand side is ground, so it leaves no variable unbound and keeps its match as a branch condition.
This is a known limit of concrete runs over a withdrawn head; a definition avoids it by a form whose head keeps its claims, such as the reassociation restricted to an atomic first leaf ([Anywhere heads in equation arguments](#anywhere-heads-in-equation-arguments)).
Equation matching lowers a concrete overloaded application through `symbol-overload` relations when every argument can lower to the corresponding lesser sort.
The most specific successful lowering supplies sort membership; a concrete application for which every compatible lowering fails refutes membership.
After compatible lowering, equation matching treats different productions in an overload family as distinct rigid heads when the subject is concrete after normalization and its head is rigid in the sense above.
An ambiguous lowering, a variable, or an ordinary function argument remains symbolic.
Structural equality (predicate simplification, the syntactic truth check, and the pairwise disequalities of map keys and set elements) rejects two concrete terms only through heads known to be normal forms: a domain value, a constructor or injection, or an anywhere application the simplifier has certified. The simplifier caches an application as evaluated when every equation offered it fails to match it, and certifies it when, in addition, the load-time check leaves its head's claims in place; the cache alone only spares the next scan. An equation that matches but is set aside because of a `concrete` or `symbolic` attribute still holds, so the application is not cached, and no lower-priority equation of its function applies unless the set-aside equation's `requires` is refuted. Two such heads that differ, an equal such head with structurally distinct arguments, or such an application against a sort injection refute the equality; the `injective` attribute plays no part. Where a definition is at hand (predicate simplification and the collection disequalities), two injections from different source sorts are also separated when the sort graph shows that no value of one injects to a value of the other, and an injection from a subsort is compared with the other side injected to its sort; neither step needs the injected terms to be normal forms. A ground anywhere application without that cache decides nothing, because being concrete after normalization is a shape: `wrap(s(z))` has it and equals `wrap(z)` under the anywhere equation `wrap(s(X)) = wrap(X)`.
Unification replaces an equation between two applications of the same anywhere head by equations between their arguments only when both applications are normal on every instance: the load-time check leaves the head's claims in place, every argument is normal on every instance, and every equation that equation selection offers the head, whatever its priority and `requires`, either does not syntactically unify with the application or is refuted on it by equation matching, whose refutation holds for every instance. The scan also runs on ground applications: being concrete after normalization is a shape, not evidence that no equation applies, and the unifier builds terms by substitution that no normalization has seen.
Otherwise the equation stays a residual constraint, because an anywhere equation such as `wrap(s(z)) = wrap(z)` makes `wrap(s(X)) = wrap(z)` true for `X = z`; an overload-greater `f(X) = f(Y)` stays a constraint for the same reason, which is incomplete but never refutes a satisfiable equation.
These rules follow from what the manual makes of the two symbol families.
An anywhere rule leaves its symbol ["still a constructor, even though it is simplified similarly to a `function`"](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/blob/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/docs/user_manual.md?plain=1#L1338-L1340): once no anywhere equation applies to a normalized application, the application is a constructor value, so it is concrete when its arguments are, and two such values with different heads are different.
A symbol that also carries the `function` attribute is excluded because a function application denotes the value of its equations, not a value of its own.
An [`overload(_)` family](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/blob/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/docs/user_manual.md?plain=1#L352-L388) groups constructors in which a more specific production is a restriction of a less specific one; after lowering has placed a concrete application at its most specific successful production, different productions of the family are different constructors.

## Anywhere heads in equation arguments

A function rule whose left-hand side contains an anywhere production is emitted as a function equation whose argument pattern contains that production's symbol, declared `anywhere` and, when its anywhere equations can identify applications with different arguments, neither `constructor` nor `injective` ([Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules)).
Such a sentence is a valid KORE axiom: the validity conditions of the KORE language ([`docs/kore-syntax.md`, "Validity"](https://github.com/runtimeverification/haskell-backend/blob/ad54c7a55085b726c4d3c2728242a7e0695b0439/docs/kore-syntax.md#validity)) concern declarations, scoping and sort agreement, and none restricts the symbols that may occur in an axiom's argument pattern.
The pinned reference `kore-parser --verify` additionally checks the shape of equations and rejects such a definition with `Found invalid subterm in argument of function equation`: it admits in an equation argument only applications of `constructor` symbols, sort injections, symbols declared both `anywhere` and `injective`, and the concatenation, element and unit symbols of the builtin collections.
No declaration Rust could emit meets that check and stays true. Semantic attributes must correspond to axioms of the definition ([`docs/kore-syntax.md`, "Attributes"](https://github.com/runtimeverification/haskell-backend/blob/ad54c7a55085b726c4d3c2728242a7e0695b0439/docs/kore-syntax.md#attributes)), and `constructor` and `injective` both state that equal applications have equal arguments, which the anywhere equations below falsify; the symbol is not a sort injection, and it is not a collection symbol.
In FUN (`pl-tutorial/2_languages/3_fun/1_untyped/1_environment`) the anywhere rule `F P = E => F = fun P -> E` equates `(f x) = e` with `f = fun x -> e`, and `[E1,E2,Es|T] => [E1|[E2,Es|T]]` equates two `[_|_]` applications with different arguments, so the equations of `names`, `exps` (argument `X:Name = E and Bs`) and `getMatching` (argument `[H | T]`) cannot be accepted by the check under any true declaration.

Such an equation is still a sentence of a consistent theory, and Rust evaluates it as the rule specifies.
An equation `f(p(X)) = r(X)` holds in a model when every value that is an instance of `p(X)` gets the value of `r` at that instance; it can contradict the rest of the theory only if two instances that denote the same value give different right sides.
In the intended model every value is the value of a normal form and distinct normal forms are distinct values.
When the argument pattern is normal on every instance, meaning no anywhere equation of a head in it applies at any instance of its variables, two instances denote the same value only when they are equal, so the equation assigns each argument value at most one result.
The three FUN patterns are normal on every instance: the `_=_` equation applies only when its first argument is an application `F P`, and an injected `Name` is never one (an `F P` value is an application of that production or an injected `Val` constructor application); the `[_|_]` equation needs at least two elements before `|`, and `[H | T]` has one.
A value that equals an instance only through an anywhere equation, such as the binding `(f x) = e` for the `exps` equation, is still matched: arguments are normalized before the enclosing equation is tried, giving `f = fun x -> e`, and matching decides an anywhere subject application only when it is normal on every instance, so an `owise` equation such as `getMatching(_, _) => matchFailure` never applies to an argument an earlier equation matches modulo the anywhere equations.

The load checks the condition, through the critical pairs at the anywhere heads of each equation's arguments.
For an equation `e1` and an equation `e2` rooted at an anywhere head (or an overloaded production) at a position in one of `e1`'s arguments, a common instance is an instance of `e1`'s argument pattern that `e2` rewrites, and the two axioms make `e1`'s application there equal both `e1`'s right-hand side and the application with the rewritten argument.
An argument pattern normal on every instance has no such instance; one that has some is still consistent when, at each of them, the two terms are shown to reach one term.
A joined pair at `e2`'s head is also what lets the instance-normal scan read an application of that head written in `e1`'s argument by its root ([Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules)): the values that only reduce to an instance of the argument pattern are the common instances the pair decides.
Where they are not shown to, `kcompile` reports `UnjoinedCriticalPair` at `e1`'s rule ("the equation `e1` is not shown consistent with the equation `e2`, which rewrites some instances of an argument of the first's left-hand side"), and `krun`, `kprove` and the RPC server print the same warning, whether or not the definition makes value claims.
Beside `rule h(s(Y)) => h(Y) [anywhere]`, the equation `f(h(X)) => X` is reported: at `X = s(Y)` it makes `f(h(s(Y)))` equal both `s(Y)` and `f(h(Y))`, that is `Y`, which the constructors keep apart; `g(h(z)) => z` and `k(h(X:Zero)) => X`, with `z` the only `Zero`, are normal on every instance and are not (`kcompile_names_an_equation_whose_argument_an_anywhere_rule_rewrites` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).
The check shows consistency, not its absence, so it also names an equation whose two terms are equal but not shown to be: `f(h(_X)) => z` is named, because the check does not apply `f`'s equation to `f(h(Y))`, whose argument is not normal on every instance.
K's reference toolchain accepts such a definition without a warning.

A reassociation of an anywhere head's own applications is such a pair.
`rule (S1 ; S2) ; S3 => S1 ; S2 ; S3 [anywhere]` is named as not shown consistent with itself at `seq(seq(seq(S1, S2), S3), S4)`, and its mirror `rule S1 ; (S2 ; S3) => (S1 ; S2) ; S3 [anywhere]` at `seq(S1, seq(S2, seq(S3, S4)))`, each with the reason that the two reducts reach different terms.
In `M_NF` the two reducts reach one term: with `S1` to `S4` normal forms, both normalize to the same fully reassociated sequence (right-nested for the first rule, left-nested for its mirror).
Showing it for the first rule needs induction on how deeply the value of `S2` is itself a sequence: the join rewrites innermost and splits a residual into the cases the equations offered at it distinguish, and on `seq(S2, S3)` the case `S2 = seq(B1, B2)` asks the same question of `seq(B2, S3)`, so the splits stop at their depth bound without a common term; closing the pair needs the reassociation at the smaller instances as an induction hypothesis, which the join does not use.
This is a limit of the check, not of the definition: the pair it names holds in `M_NF`.
Its effect is that of every pair not shown to join: `seq` loses its claims, no application of `seq` is shown a normal form, so equation matching does not decompose `seq(b, c)` against the reassociation's inner `S1 ; S2`, and `(b ; c) ; d` stays as written under `\ceil(seq(b, c))` (`a_reassociation_whose_head_is_withdrawn_keeps_the_nested_application_open` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).
No ground pattern of `seq` is recorded either, so an `owise` equation stays blocked past one: beside `rule f(b ; c) => 1` and `rule f(_) => 2 [owise]`, `f(d)` and `f(b ; d)` stay unevaluated, where beside an admitted head they take the `owise` equation ([Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules), `an_owise_equation_applies_past_a_ground_nested_anywhere_pattern` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)); and a rewrite rule that rebuilds an application of `seq` can run without end ([Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules)).
A reassociation whose first leaf is a variable of a sort that holds no sequence, `rule (A:Atom ; S2) ; S3 => A ; S2 ; S3 [anywhere]`, has no overlap with itself (an `Atom` value is never a sequence) and keeps `seq`'s claims; since innermost evaluation right-nests an inner sequence before the outer one is tried, it reaches the right-nested sequence of any nesting whose leaves are `Atom`s, `((b ; c) ; d) ; e` giving `b ; c ; d ; e` with no condition (`a_reassociation_restricted_to_an_atom_first_leaf_reaches_the_right_nested_sequence` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).

The conformance driver therefore verifies a copy of the definition (N47 in [reference-normalisations.toml](../scripts/reference-normalisations.toml)) in which every symbol declared `anywhere` without `injective` also carries `injective{}()`; the compared and executed definition is unchanged.
The added attribute may be false of such a symbol, which is why the emitted definition withholds it, but nothing reads the copy except the verifier, and it changes only which applications the verifier admits in an equation argument: whether those equations are consistent is the question the critical-pair check above decides at load and compile time, and its warnings remain.
Every other sentence and every other argument of the copy is verified as emitted, so a different rejection of the same definition is reported; FUN's copy verifies completely.
A rejection of a `kore-verify` step is never excluded ([Driver scope](#driver-scope)): the verifier reports one rejected sentence at a time, so an exclusion of one diagnostic would hide every later one.
The Rust verifier (`k-rust-backend` `verify.rs`) does not make this check and loads the definition.

## Function applications on a left-hand side

A function application on a rule's left-hand side is read in one of two ways, decided by the kind of rule and the same at every position (`definition/checks/functions.rs`).

An equation (a function rule, whose left-hand side is rooted at a `function` production; an `anywhere` rule; a macro-like rule) matches its argument patterns as written: the evaluator compares them with the arguments of the application it rewrites, and does not evaluate them.
An application of a computed symbol below an equation's root would therefore match only a subject that spells the same application, never the value it computes, which is the subject an evaluated argument holds there.
Below an equation's root only the collection constructors are admitted (the unit, element and concatenation of a `LIST`, `SET`, `MAP` or `BAG` sort, which build the collection their arguments describe), and their arguments are checked like every other argument; any other function application there is rejected with `IllegalFunctionOnLhs`, at every position alike: a map key, a map value, a set element, a list element, and a list update `L[I <- V]` (a function of a list, not a way to write one).
A definition that needs the value of a computed position binds the position to a variable and states the value in `requires` (`f(X, (K |-> V) _M) => V requires K ==K pf(X)`), which says what it means and which the evaluator decides.
A simplification lemma is not checked: it is a theorem of the definition, read as written.

A rewrite rule (or a context) reads a function application inside a key, value or element of a collection, or an operand of a collection concatenation, by value: it matches the entry whose key, value or element equals the value the application computes once the variables in it are bound elsewhere in the rule (`#ContextLookup(IDS, TFIDX) |-> ADDR` matches the binding of the address the lookup computes).
The reading is the same at every argument of a collection constructor and at every depth below it, and it is how the rewrite step matches such a position: it instantiates the bound variables, evaluates the application and matches the result.
Elsewhere on a rewrite rule's left-hand side a function application is rejected, with one shape of list update admitted.
A list `S` is an update `L [ I <- V ]` of some list `L` exactly when `I` indexes `S` and `S` holds `V` there, so a rewrite rule decides that match by value, with `S` itself as the updated list, as long as nothing reads which list was updated: the list argument is a variable that occurs nowhere else in the rule, such as `_` in `<list> _ [ I <- J ] </list>`. A list argument the rule reads elsewhere (`<list> L [ I <- J ] </list>` with `L` on the right-hand side or in `requires`) would be bound to one of the many lists that differ from `S` at `I`, and is rejected, as is any other list argument; a variable at the position and `requires` state such a match.
Both readings evaluate part of the pattern, so the rule must bind each variable of that part elsewhere on its left-hand side, outside every application read by value: every variable of an application read by value, and every variable of a matched update's index.
