A match chooses an entry or element by what is written there, and cannot invert a function to find the arguments that compute a given value, so `<k> go => done(X) </k> <m> 1 |-> pf(X) </m>` and `<k> go => done(I) </k> <list> _ [ I <- 1 ] </list>` are rejected with `IllegalFunctionOnLhs`, while `<k> go => done(Y) </k> <n> X </n> <m> pf(X) |-> Y </m>` is admitted (`X` may also be bound by another entry of the same map). An update's value is the element `S` holds at the index, so it binds its own variables.

K's reference toolchain admits a function application at a map key, a set element and a list-update index on every left-hand side, and nowhere else below the root; krust rejects these three positions in equations (where the evaluator never applies such an equation to a value, so a definition loses no evaluation) and admits a map value and a list element in rewrite rules, so that one reading holds at every argument of a collection constructor. The reference toolchain also admits a list update with any list argument on a rewrite rule's left-hand side; krust admits there only the update of a list the rule does not read, for the reason above. The reference toolchain admits an application at a map key or a set element, and a list-update index, also when the rule binds its variables nowhere else; krust rejects those, since no match can find values for them.
(`an_equation_admits_only_collection_constructors_below_its_root` and `a_rewrite_rule_reads_a_function_application_by_value_at_every_collection_argument` in [definition_checks.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/definition_checks.rs), `a_function_application_is_admitted_alike_at_every_collection_hook_argument` and `a_rewrite_rule_reads_a_computed_collection_position_by_value` in [definedness_classes.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/definedness_classes.rs), `a_rewrite_rule_matches_a_list_update_by_value_only_when_it_reads_no_updated_list` and `a_rewrite_rule_reads_an_application_by_value_only_when_it_binds_its_variables_elsewhere` in [definition_checks.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/definition_checks.rs), `krun_recovers_evaluable_collection_function_patterns` and `krun_rejects_a_list_update_whose_updated_list_the_rule_reads` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)

## Concrete rewrite instantiation

A configuration that denotes one state is read as one state: a rewrite step must not invent states for it.
The backend reads a configuration so on every route when its whole normalized term is concrete (below), and on execution and search routes (`krun`, `kore-exec`, `Backend::execute`, RPC `execute`, search, and the library, napi and wasm entries that reach them) also when its term has no variable.
The public single-step functions (`rewrite_step`, `rewrite_step_with_solver`, `rewrite_step_sequential_with_solver`) keep the concreteness boundary alone, as proofs do: a library caller that drives its own loop with them still narrows a variable-free configuration, and can still run without end on `swap(3)`; driving execution through `execute*` or search gives the halt.
On such a configuration, applying a rewrite rule requires a substitution covering every free variable on its left-hand side.
Unification and `requires` simplification may supply those bindings; an unresolved variable must produce an explicit instantiation failure before applying the right-hand side or `ensures`.
An impossible match or false `requires` remains non-applicable.
Rust reports this unsupported instantiation as typed indeterminacy (`IndeterminateReason::Instantiation`, printed `rule … leaves … uninstantiated on a variable-free configuration`, and `Unable to instantiate semantic rule` over RPC), so execution and search retain an incomplete outcome instead of inventing existential successors or treating the rule as non-applicable: `krun` prints no configuration and exits 1, an execution or RPC `execute` reports an indeterminate leaf, and a search reports itself incomplete and keeps the states it reached.

A term with no variable denotes at most one element, so wherever a rule applies to it, that element fixes each of the rule's left-hand-side variables.
The match can still leave one unbound: the configuration may hold an application no equation evaluates (`swap(E) => lam(E)` on `swap(3)`, where `lam`'s only equation reads a pair), or an operation whose `assoc`, `comm` or `unit` axioms the matcher does not apply (`sum(I:Int & B)` on `sum(1 & 2 & 3)`, which parses as `(1 & 2) & 3`).
An existential minted for that variable adds no successor the configuration does not determine; it restates the undecided part of the configuration as a fresh unknown, and the successor carries it, so the next step meets the same application over a new existential and the run never ends.
The halt claims nothing: it asserts neither that the rule applies nor that it does not, so no result, final state or verdict is reported that the step did not justify.

The boundary is concreteness of the whole normalized term on every route, and variable-freeness of the term on execution and search routes.
Concreteness includes Kore constructor-like terms and extends them for the supported anywhere superset, so a variable-free normalized anywhere or overloaded application does not make a ground configuration symbolic.
Such an application is a constructor value, as [Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules) explains.
A variable below such an application keeps the configuration symbolic.
A ground function-headed term narrows only in a configuration with variables, and in proofs: a symbolic configuration holding a ground unevaluated application can still narrow without end (bound it with `--depth`), and symbolic configurations retain fresh rule arguments and their existentially quantified complementary conditions.
Proof routes (`kprove` and the proof library entries) keep the concreteness boundary alone: a claim that writes every cell is a variable-free configuration, and its steps narrow it.
Variable-freeness is not folded into the concreteness classification, which also licenses structural apartness: `bagCat(1, 2)` and `bagCat(2, 1)` are both variable-free and equal under `comm`.
A rule whose left-hand side is ground mints nothing and keeps its match as a branch condition (`h(1 & 2) => a` on `h(2 & 1)` gives two conditional branches).

The halt belongs to the step, not to one rule: a rule that would need an existential leaves its priority group undecided, so the halt also discards a sibling successor of the same step, as for a concrete configuration.
With `go(E) => done(E)` and `go(pair(A, _)) => fst(A)`, `go(lam(3))` under `--strategy all` halts at depth 0 rather than report `done(lam(3))`; the default `--strategy any` takes `done(lam(3))` from the earlier rule.
The halt applies in every priority group the step reaches, so an `owise` rule after a rule whose `requires` is undecided halts the step too.
RPC `execute` follows the execution boundary whatever its client uses it for: an RPC-driven prover's variable-free state receives `Unable to instantiate semantic rule`.
A variable that occurs in a rule but not on its left-hand side, and a right-hand-side `?X` existential, is the rule's own existential and is still minted on every configuration.

This boundary differs from both pinned reference backends: kore-exec 0.1.155 narrows such a configuration (`swap(3)` and `sum(1 & 2 & 3)` do not finish), and the LLVM backend aborts at the first application no equation matches (exit 113).
Anywhere equation evaluation, overload lowering, and covered function-equality matching remain supported before this boundary is applied.
The [rewrite coverage fixture](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/fixtures/rewrite-coverage.kore) and [rewrite tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/rewrite.rs) exercise this boundary, false and binding requirements, equation normalization, and symbolic complements; `execution_and_search_halt_instead_of_narrowing_a_variable_free_configuration` and `execution_still_narrows_a_configuration_with_variables` there, and the `krun_halts_*` tests in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), exercise the variable-free boundary.

## Trivial rule results

A rule whose left-hand side matches and whose `requires` holds has applied even when `ensures false` or a bottom right-hand side makes its result empty.
Its matched region must be removed from the remainder available to lower-priority and `owise` rules.
The K manual fixes both halves: the `requires` clause decides whether a rule applies, while the `ensures` clause is a post-condition that "may cause the entire term to become undefined, but the backend will not stop itself from applying the rule in this case" (`docs/user_manual.md:1153-1164`, "Rule Structure"); an `owise` rule applies "only if all the other rules have been tried and failed", after they have "been shown not to apply" (`docs/user_manual.md:1711-1722`, "`owise` and `priority` attributes").
A rule with an empty result has not failed, so for the matched region the result of the step is empty: `krun` prints `\bottom` and the RPC `execute` answers `vacuous`, never a successor of a lower-priority rule.

This is a recorded divergence from `kore-rpc-booster`, whose Booster rewriter continues with the next priority group when every applicable rule of a group has an empty result (`OnlyTrivial` in `booster/library/Booster/Pattern/Rewrite.hs::rewriteStep`).
The `trivial-result-rpc` differential case compares both answers with the pinned proxy.
Its `execute-trivial` request is krun's depth-0 state, whose initializer functions Booster does not rewrite, so the proxy answers it through its Kore fallback and both sides answer `vacuous`.
Its `execute-trivial-configuration` request sends the evaluated configuration `<k> a </k>`, which Booster rewrites itself: Booster answers `depth-bound` at depth 1 with the `owise` successor `<k> c </k>`, and k-rust answers `vacuous` at depth 0.
That response is the case's `rpc.oracle-exception` row in the [differential manifest](../scripts/reference-differential.toml): the gate fails when k-rust's answer leaves the committed expectation or when the proxy's answer becomes equal to it, and the proxy's measured answer is kept beside the expectation in the [RPC fixtures](../crates/k-rust/tests/fixtures/reference/rpc).
[Rewrite tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/rewrite.rs), including `a_trivial_rule_shadows_lower_priority_rules`, cover concrete and symbolic remainders.

## Conditionals evaluate only the operand they select

`#if C #then T #else E #fi`, `B1 andThenBool B2` and `B1 orElseBool B2` are applications of hooked function symbols (`KEQUAL.ite`, `BOOL.andThen`, `BOOL.orElse`), and an application is undefined wherever one of its arguments is, so the value of a conditional whose condition selects `T` is `T` under the definedness of `E`: `#if true #then 1 #else u #fi` denotes `1 /\ \ceil(u)`. [Conditionals and Boolean connectives are strict](#conditionals-and-boolean-connectives-are-strict) states that value, for these three and for `andBool`, `orBool` and `impliesBool`.
The value keeps that obligation; what the three hooks change is the order of evaluation, which is the exception to "arguments are simplified before the hook" ([Backend scope](#backend-scope), [Resource bounds](#resource-bounds)).
The deciding operand (`C`, `B1`) is simplified first, and then only the operand it selects.
The operand it discards is folded by builtin hooks on ground arguments and not evaluated beyond the bounded steps below: its definedness is a deferred obligation, printed as `\ceil(t)`, and the only equation instances taken on a part of it are the ones that decide that obligation (at most one per application, selected by matching, never a second one on its result).
A simplification lemma the definition states over the conditional itself, over terms (`#if B #then X #else Y #fi +Int Z => #if B #then X +Int Z #else Y +Int Z #fi`) or over predicates (`{#if B #then X #else Y #fi #Equals Z} => {true #Equals B} #And {X #Equals Z}`), still rewrites the conditional as a whole; the definedness of what it binds in such an operand stays deferred, wherever in its left-hand side the conditional stands and whatever term the match builds for the variable (the rest of a map it splits, a value injected into the variable's sort).
What the lemma's right-hand side builds is simplified as any term or predicate is: an operand it keeps inside a conditional stays unevaluated, and one it moves out of the conditional is evaluated there (see the precision list below).
While the deciding operand is not a literal, no other operand is simplified.

A deferred obligation is decided only by:
- pure builtin hooks on ground constructor-like arguments: literals, constructor terms and closed collections of them (`1 /Int 0` and `(1 |-> 2)[5]` are `\bottom`, `(1 |-> 2)[1]` is defined). A hook evaluator reads its arguments only, applies no equation of the definition and refuses a result above its own size bound, which leaves the obligation open. Where the hook's value is one only an execution chooses (`keys_list` or `choice` of two bindings, [Implementation-chosen hook values](#implementation-chosen-hook-values)), every other route takes the application as defined without choosing a value, so a claim is not decided by k-rust's choice. The fold does not enter a collection, so a hook inside one of its items is not folded, and a user function applied to a ground collection is no hook: its obligation stays open unless the steps below decide it;
- a ceil equation whose instantiated condition those hooks decide (`\ceil(X /Int 2)` holds), and, where the operand's terms are only variables, literals and applications of pure `total` hooks, the instance of that ceil equation stated in the obligation's place (`\ceil(W0 /Int W1)` is `W1 =/=Int 0`, which the solver then decides from the path): the instance is an equivalence and holds no part of the operand but variables and literals;
- the distinctness of the literal keys of a map or set without a rest;
- one function equation step selected by matching alone, whose instantiated right-hand side is `\bottom` or needs no obligation (`f(1)` beside `f(1) => 5` is defined; a generated lambda applied to a value its pattern refutes is `\bottom`);
- the value of such a step on literal arguments, when builtin hooks fold its instantiated right-hand side to a literal (`f(-1)` is `0` beside `f(N) => N +Int 1`, so `1 /Int f(-1)` is `1 /Int 0`): one step per application, never a second one on its result;
- a known fact, and the solver, which reads the obligation as the opaque atom `\ceil(t)` reads as.

The equation step is taken only where every higher-priority equation fails to match, and, when a left-hand side has a function symbol below its root (a collection pattern included), only where every argument is a normal form on every instance.
Each of these steps is bounded by the size of the obligation's subject or by one selection scan per application, none repeats on its own result, and none evaluates an `impure` or `IO.` application, so deciding an obligation terminates and performs nothing, whatever the definition's equations do.
The operands the conditional did evaluate are facts of its result, which is taken under the deciding operand's definedness and is the selected operand's value: an occurrence of one of them in the discarded operand is read as its value (`#if X ==K none #then {M[N]}:>Pred #else X and {M[N]}:>Pred #fi` leaves no obligation once `{M[N]}:>Pred` is `p(1)`), and no obligation is stated over one of their subterms, since every subterm of a defined term is defined (the prelude's `findChar` discards an operand that holds the very call its condition evaluates). An operand holding an impure application is no such fact, since two reads are two values.

So recursion guarded by a conditional terminates: with `fact(N) => #if N <=Int 0 #then 1 #else N *Int fact(N -Int 1) #fi`, `fact(3)` is `6` under `\ceil(fact(-1))`, its exact denotation, since the axioms also admit a `fact` that is `\bottom` everywhere.
What a run shows:
- `krun` prints the taken value with the open obligations; `Backend::execute` and RPC `execute` give a leaf under them and a trivial leaf where they fail ([Trivial rule results](#trivial-rule-results)).
- A discarded operand that builtin hooks alone show undefined makes the result `\bottom` (`#if true #then 1 #else 1 /Int 0 #fi` ends with "applied with an undefined result; refuted obligation \ceil(1 /Int 0)"), and so does one a single equation step shows undefined (`1 /Int f(-1)` with `f(N) => N +Int 1`); one whose undefinedness needs more evaluation (`1 /Int g(-1)` with `g(N) => f(N)`) leaves `1` under the open `\ceil(1 /Int g(-1))`, a ground result that prints an obligation where evaluating every operand would have printed `\bottom`.
- A claim through guarded recursion (`go(3) => 6`) reaches the trivial leaf and is proven only with `--allow-vacuous`.
- An open obligation makes the state constrained, so the console is unavailable for the rest of the path: a later `#write` is the unsupported-hook halt, where the `requires` form of the recursion writes.
- In a `requires`, a conditional whose unused operand is undefined is `\bottom`, so the rule does not apply (`requires #if N >Int 0 #then true #else 1 /Int 0 >Int 0 #fi` at `N = 1`).

A discarded console operation is never performed.
Its definedness is shown on literal arguments in the console's domain (`#write`/`#putc` on descriptor 1 or 2 with code points up to 255, `#getc` on 0, `#read` on 0 with a non-negative length), and an application of a symbol declared `total` (`#log`) is defined by the declaration.
A discarded operand whose definedness still depends on an impure application, such as the value of a console read that is not performed (`1 /Int {#getc(0)}:>Int`), or on a function whose equations perform an effect and which is not declared `total`, is the unsupported-hook outcome at the step that discards it, with nothing read or written: no state and no constraint holds an impure application, and wherever such a definedness would have to be stated (a rule's condition, a claim's side, an implication's destination, a resumed state) the request is that outcome or is left undecided, never decided from a fact about the application (`kore-implies` and RPC `implies` answer `unknown`).

Precision lost by not evaluating, each an obligation left open or an operand left unsimplified, never a different value:
- a ceil equation is used only when hooks decide its condition or its operand is hook arithmetic over variables and literals;
- an obligation that only evaluation decides stays open (`\ceil(1 /Int g(-1))` above, `\ceil(fact(-1))`), so ground results print obligations that evaluating every operand would have decided;
- a true claim whose conditional discards (or, under a condition only the solver decides, selects) an operand whose definedness needs evaluation or is not hook arithmetic is no longer proven: `q(X) => 1` beside `q(X) => #if X >Int 5 #then 1 #else 10 /Int h(X) #fi` reaches a trivial leaf under `\not \ceil(10 /Int h(X))` whatever `h`'s equations say, where the same claim over `10 /Int X` is proven;
- with a symbolic deciding operand the other operands stay unsimplified, so `X andThenBool g()` with `g() => false` does not fold by `_ andThenBool false`, and symbolic states keep unnormalised operands;
- the `#if` equations at sort `K` and the discarding equations of `andThenBool` and `orElseBool` state the definedness of an operand they bind as a deferred obligation, even of the operand the path condition later selects;
- a user simplification lemma that moves a conditional's operand out of the conditional (`{#if B #then X #else Y #fi #Equals Z} => ({true #Equals B} #And {X #Equals Z}) #Or ({false #Equals B} #And {Y #Equals Z})`) has its right-hand side simplified without this ordering, so `{Y #Equals Z}` evaluates `Y` whatever `B` is; this affects termination and precision only, since the lemma is the user's axiom;
- a discarded operand whose definedness depends on a hook value only an execution chooses keeps an open obligation on every other route: `{keys_list(1 |-> 10 3 |-> 30)[0]}:>Int` is defined whichever key is listed first, since both are `Int`s, but no order is chosen in a proof, so a valid claim that discards it is not proven, and neither is one that discards `{choice(1 |-> 10 3 |-> 30)}:>Int`, where `choice(1 |-> 10 3 |-> 30)` alone is taken as defined;
- a predicate lemma whose left-hand side is not strict in a variable it binds in such an operand (one under `#Not`, or beside an operand not known to be defined) is not applied while that variable's obligation is open, where evaluating the operand could have decided it.

A `\ceil(t)` in a state given to execution (`Backend::execute`, RPC `execute`, `krust kore-exec`) whose subject holds a function application is read as the deferred obligations of `t`, so a client that resumes from a reported state does not evaluate an operand the run left unevaluated: resuming from `1 /\ \ceil(loopi())` ends at the same leaf.
The input cannot tell a deferred obligation from an ordinary one, so this also captures an ordinary `\ceil`: resumed, `\ceil(f(3))` stays open where a continuous run decides it by evaluating `f(3)`, and a resumed run can report a trivial leaf a continuous run does not.
A `\ceil` over a term without a function application keeps its ordinary reading, and claims, `kore-simplify` and RPC `simplify` and `implies` read every `\ceil` as an ordinary one.

`andBool`, `orBool` and `impliesBool` are unchanged: both operands are simplified, innermost, and their effects performed.
The order reverses `domains.md`'s note that `#if` "does not short-circuit on symbolic backends", and agrees with its note that `andThenBool` and `orElseBool` are short-circuiting.
It differs from both pinned reference backends: LLVM returns the taken operand alone, dropping the obligation, which is false wherever the operand is undefined, and the Haskell backend evaluates the discarded operand in execution.
(`recursion_guarded_by_a_conditional_ends_under_the_definedness_of_the_untaken_call`, `a_discarded_diverging_operand_is_not_evaluated`, `a_discarded_operand_hooks_show_undefined_is_bottom`, `a_discarded_operand_takes_the_value_of_one_ground_equation_step`, `a_lemma_binding_an_operand_below_its_root_does_not_evaluate_it`, `a_lemma_binding_a_term_it_builds_from_an_operand_does_not_evaluate_it`, `a_predicate_lemma_binding_a_discarded_operand_does_not_evaluate_it`, `a_discarded_hook_over_a_ground_collection_is_decided_by_the_hook`, `a_discarded_choice_over_a_ground_map_is_decided_by_the_hook`, `an_implication_through_an_impure_discarded_operand_is_never_valid`, `a_path_decided_conditional_defers_its_discarded_operand`, `a_discarded_operand_reads_an_evaluated_operand_as_its_value`, `resuming_from_a_leaf_with_an_open_obligation_does_not_evaluate_it` and `a_discarded_conditional_operand_performs_no_console_effect` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs); `a_claim_through_a_discarded_operand_never_evaluates_it`, `a_discarded_division_over_variables_states_its_divisor_condition`, `a_claim_through_a_discarded_hook_over_a_ground_collection_chooses_no_value`, `a_claim_through_a_discarded_choice_chooses_no_item` and `a_claim_through_an_impure_discarded_operand_is_never_proven` in [proof_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs); [deferred_definedness.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/deferred_definedness.rs).)

## Set concatenation is nilpotent

k-rust implements one theory of `_Set_`, in concrete and in symbolic evaluation: concatenation is nilpotent.
`S1 S2` is the disjoint union, defined only when `S1` and `S2` share no element; `SetItem(X) SetItem(X)` is `\bottom`.
The union of sets that may overlap is `|Set`, which `domains.md` defines as `S1 (S2 -Set S1)` and which is unchanged; its `SET.union` hook computes that set for two closed sets of constructor-like elements; on any other operands it gives no result, and `domains.md`'s rules for `|Set` (the `[concrete]` equation on ground operands, the `|Set` simplifications) apply as before.
This is the theory the `domains.md` prose states ([`domains.md`](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/blob/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/k-distribution/include/kframework/builtin/domains.md), "Set concatenation": "the concatenation of two sets containing elements in common is `#False`").
It is also the theory AC matching of sets rests on: a pattern `SetItem(X) R` decomposes a set in one way per element because `X` is not in `R`.

So a concrete run that concatenates sets sharing an element ends in an undefined step, like any rule whose right-hand side is undefined ([Undefined steps in proofs](#undefined-steps-in-proofs), [Trivial rule results](#trivial-rule-results)).
`krun` warns that the rule "applied with an undefined result" and prints `\bottom`; a hooked function applied to such a set, such as `size`, is `\bottom` too.
Concatenating disjoint sets and `|Set` give the same results as before.
A cell collection declared with `type="Set"` is such a set too: a step that adds a cell equal to one the collection holds, or makes two of its cells equal, is undefined, where it used to merge them.
The `STAR-CELL-HEATING-SET` fixture shows it: its two tasks complete to the same cell, so `two.pgm` ends in an undefined step at depth 10.

The reason is that concrete and symbolic evaluation are two evaluators of one definition and must agree on every ground instance.
The symbolic side already reads concatenation as nilpotent: the definedness of `S SetItem(I)` is `I` not in `S`.
Before this rule, the concrete side merged the shared element, so `start(1)` with the set `SetItem(1)` reached a successor that the symbolic step, specialised to that instance, says does not exist; a ground claim was proven while its symbolic generalisation failed.
The concrete check costs nothing extra: concatenation inserts every element of one set into the other, which is where a shared element shows.
Internally, `Term::set` keeps a repeated element (sorted, adjacent), as `Term::map` keeps a repeated key, so definedness (`\not(X = X)`), matching (no match), the hooks (`\bottom`) and the simplifier see it.
The simplifier reports a set holding a syntactically repeated element as `\bottom` where it is built, as it reports a builtin's undefined result such as `10 /Int 0`: in a rule's right-hand side, in the result of a function equation, or after a substitution.

This diverges from the reference LLVM backend's concrete execution, which merges overlapping sets: there, `krun` of the `STAR-CELL-HEATING-SET` fixture's `two.pgm` ends with one `<task>` cell, and `domains.md` notes that overlap "may be silently allowed during concrete execution". The pinned Haskell backend does not merge on that program: it reports a disjunction that its own simplifier reduces to `\bottom`, k-rust's result (N41 in the execution gate).
A definition whose concrete runs rely on that merge gets an undefined step in k-rust at the step that concatenates; writing `|Set` there states the union it means.

The `_Set_` production in `domains.md` also carries `idem`, which states `S S = S`.
That contradicts nilpotency for every `S` except `.Set`.
k-rust accepts the attribute and does not honour it: `kcompile` still translates it into its KORE axiom `_Set_(K, K) = K`, as for any `idem` production, so the compiled KORE is unchanged; the backend evaluates and matches by no `assoc`, `comm`, `unit` or `idem` axiom (a hooked collection implements its algebra in its representation; for an unhooked production see [User productions with `assoc`, `comm`, `idem` or `unit`](#user-productions-with-assoc-comm-idem-or-unit)), and the `SET.concat` hook fixes concatenation as nilpotent.
A `total` function whose equation concatenates overlapping sets is not total; k-rust trusts the attribute, as for any `total` function, but a syntactic repeat in the function's result is `\bottom` all the same, because the repeat itself is undefined, whatever the attribute of the function that built it.
So `tAdd(SetItem(1), 1)`, with `tAdd(S, I) => S SetItem(I)` declared `total`, is an undefined step in `krun` and fails a ground claim, as `10 /Int 0` does.
Elements that are not syntactically equal, such as `1` and `I` in `tAdd(SetItem(1), I)`, may denote one element; that stays the set's definedness condition (`\not(1 = I)`), which a trusted `total` application does not raise, so a symbolic claim over it can still be proven.

The `set_concatenation_is_nilpotent_in_krun_and_kprove` CLI test, `spawning_a_duplicate_ground_cell_is_an_undefined_step` and `a_set_holding_an_element_twice_matches_nothing` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), and the unit tests of [set.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/set.rs) cover this.

## User productions with `assoc`, `comm`, `idem` or `unit`

An unhooked production with `assoc`, `comm`, `idem` or `unit`, such as `syntax Bag ::= "nil" | Int | Bag "&" Bag [assoc, comm, unit(nil)]` with the symbol `bagCat`, is compiled with the axioms it declares and executed without using them.
The backend neither matches nor refutes modulo those axioms, so every answer it gives holds in every model of the emitted definition, and it gives fewer answers than the multisets the author means.

`kcompile` emits each declared axiom: `assoc`; `comm` only on a production that is not a function; both unit axioms only with `assoc` or on a function; and `idem` (`algebraic_axioms` in [axioms.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/kompile/module_to_kore/axioms.rs)).
For a production that is not a function, it declares the symbol `functional` and neither `constructor` nor `injective`, and lists it among the alternatives of its sort's no-junk axiom ([roles.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/kompile/module_to_kore/roles.rs), `ProductionKind::Algebraic`).
A production that declares `injective` beside `assoc`, `comm` or `idem` is rejected, since those axioms identify applications with different arguments, and the backend rejects a KORE symbol declared `constructor` with algebraic axioms (`algebraic_facts_refuse_constructor_and_sort_injection_claims` in [definition.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/definition.rs)).
A `function` production with these attributes is a declared function: it is declared `function`, its `assoc`, `unit` and `idem` axioms are emitted and, like every algebraic axiom, are not used by the backend, which evaluates it by its equations, and it is not among its sort's no-junk alternatives; the rest of this section is about productions that are not functions.

In that KORE the equations hold, and nothing distinguishes the symbol's applications from each other or from the sort's other values.
With a unit, the no-junk axiom excludes nothing, since every value `x` is `x & nil`; and no no-confusion axiom names the symbol.
So the definition also denotes algebras in which `1 & 2` is a singleton, or in which `nil = 1 & B` has a solution (the free abelian group on `Int`, `nil` its zero and `B` the inverse of `1`).

The backend drops the algebraic axioms from the executed theory and keeps them as facts on the symbol, whose head role is then `Uninterpreted` (`algebraic_axiom_roots` in [rule.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/rule.rs), `SymbolAttributes::head_role` in [term.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/term.rs)).
A pair rooted at the symbol is decided only when both sides are the same term as the backend receives them; otherwise it is neither decomposed nor refuted, in matching, unification and implication, and stays undecided: a condition of the branch, a blocked equation, or a halted step, as the paragraphs below say.
Such an application is never concrete after normalization, so structural equality does not separate it from another either ([Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules); `an_unhooked_commutative_application_is_not_structurally_distinct_from_its_swap` in [rewrite.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/rewrite.rs)).
A ground left-hand-side pattern applies by identity only when it reaches the backend as written: `kcompile`'s `minimize_term_construction` pass shares a constant that recurs on the right-hand side as `\and(c, _GenN)`, so `rule <k> g(1 & 2) => .K </k> <out> _ => 1 </out>` is compiled to `bagCat(inj(\and(1, _Gen7)), inj(2))`, which holds a variable and does not apply to `g(1 & 2)` by identity, while `rule <k> g(1 & 2) => done </k>` does (`a_ground_algebraic_pattern_with_a_shared_constant_halts_a_variable_free_run` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).

What a run does is decided by its configuration and by whether a rule leaves a left-hand-side variable unbound, not by whether the rule spells the symbol.
On execution and search routes a variable-free configuration is one state ([Concrete rewrite instantiation](#concrete-rewrite-instantiation)): a rule that could apply only with a variable bound modulo the axioms halts the step with `rule … leaves … uninstantiated on a variable-free configuration`, whether the configuration holds an application of the symbol (`sum(I:Int & B:Bag)` on `sum(1 & 2 & 3)`, `h(_:Int & _:Bag)` on `h(1 & 2)`) or not (`h(3)`), and so does the shared-constant rule above on `g(1 & 2)`.
A rule whose left-hand side is ground mints nothing and keeps its match as a branch condition: under `h(1 & 2) => a` and `h(_) => b [owise]`, `h(2 & 1)` gives `a` under `1 & 2 = 2 & 1` and `b` under its negation (`krun_halts_instead_of_narrowing_a_variable_free_configuration_modulo_algebraic_axioms` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).
A configuration with variables still narrows (`execution_still_narrows_a_configuration_with_variables` in [rewrite.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/rewrite.rs)).
A function equation whose left-hand side holds the symbol below its root applies only where its argument is the same term as its compiled pattern: never through a variable below the symbol (`k(I:Int & _:Bag) => I` leaves `k(1 & 2)` unevaluated), and through a ground pattern only when it reaches the backend as written (`f(1 & 2) => 5` gives `f(1 & 2)` the value `5`, while `g(1 & 2) => 1` shares `1` and leaves `g(1 & 2)` unevaluated).
The load reports such an equation as never applied to the value it computes, and an `owise` equation after it is blocked on every other argument of that sort, since none is shown apart from the pattern (`f(2 & 1)`, `f(3)` and `k(nil)` stay unevaluated; `a_function_equation_over_an_unhooked_algebraic_argument_applies_only_to_its_compiled_pattern` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), `an_uninterpreted_commutative_argument_blocks_owise` in [matching.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/matching.rs)).
An `owise` rule applies only under the negated unsolved equation, as `b` above.
Ground terms equal by the axioms are not identified: `(1 & 2) ==K (2 & 1)` stays unevaluated, and `SetItem(1 & 2) SetItem(2 & 1)` and `((1 & 2) |-> 7) ((2 & 1) |-> 8)` carry the condition `\not(1 & 2 = 2 & 1)`, so the map has two entries (`ground_terms_equal_by_unhooked_algebraic_axioms_are_not_identified` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).

`kprove` keeps the concreteness boundary alone.
A claim's unwritten cells are frame variables, so its configuration usually holds variables and narrows.
A claim that writes every cell has none: when it is built from constructors only it meets the instantiation halt and is `indeterminate` (`<k> h(3) => done </k> <generatedCounter> 0 </generatedCounter>`), and when it holds an application of the symbol it narrows like any other (`<k> h(1 & 2) => done </k> <generatedCounter> 0 </generatedCounter>` is `proven` under `h(_:Int & _:Bag) => done` and `h(_) => done [owise]`).
Where `kprove` narrows, a claim whose truth needs two such terms identified or separated is not proven (`go(1 & 2) => done(2 & 1)` by `comm` and `single(1 & nil) => isone` by `unit` are `failed`), a claim that holds on every branch of the unsolved equation can be `proven` (`h(1 & 2) => done` and `h(3) => done` with unwritten cells under the same rules, although `krun` of `h(3)` halts), and no claim valid in every model is `disproved` (`kprove_over_an_unhooked_algebraic_operation_never_disproves_a_claim_valid_in_every_model` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), which pins each verdict above).

Simplification lemmas over such terms are trusted as any lemma is, under the contract that lemmas are theorems of the executable equations ([Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules)): a lemma that contradicts the declared axioms makes the definition inconsistent, as one that contradicts no-confusion does.
Every answer above holds in every model of the emitted KORE, so the behaviour is incomplete, not unsound.
The builtin `List`, `Set` and `Map` sorts implement their algebra in their representation and are the supported way to compute modulo these axioms.

## Map concatenation binds each key once

A map binds each key once, so `_Map_` is defined only on maps whose keys are disjoint: `(K |-> V1) (K |-> V2)` is `\bottom`, whether `V1` and `V2` are equal or not.
Overwriting a binding is `M[K <- V]` (`MAP.update`) or `updateMap`, not concatenation.
k-rust applies this in concrete and symbolic evaluation alike, as for sets ([Set concatenation is nilpotent](#set-concatenation-is-nilpotent)).
`Term::map` keeps a key bound twice, with the same value or not, and does not merge two identical bindings.
Definedness emits `\not(K = K)` for the repeat, matching fails on it, a `MAP` hook with such an argument is `\bottom`, and the simplifier reports the map as `\bottom` where it is built.
So a step whose right-hand side builds such a map, directly or through a function equation (`total` or not), is an undefined step, and a ground claim over it fails.
Keys that are not syntactically equal stay a definedness condition, as for sets.
The condition that a key `K` is not a key of an opaque rest `M` uses the definition's `MAP.in_keys` function at the key and map sorts, as `\not(in_keys(K, M) = true)`.
Without one, it is the pattern `\exists V. \ceil(K |-> V M)`, which states that `K` and `M` are defined and `K` is not a key of `M`, whatever value the entry has; for a set without `SET.in` it is `\ceil(SetItem(K) S)`.
The backend keeps the map and the set form alike as one atom of `K` and `M` (`rule::Absence`), so a step that only changes the value bound to `K` restates the condition its path already holds; responses print it as that pattern, and a request that sends the same pattern back states the same atom.

The reason is the same as for sets: a map value is a finite function, and concrete and symbolic evaluation must agree on every ground instance.
The symbolic side reads `M (K |-> V)` as defined only when `K` is not a key of `M`; merging `(1 |-> 2) (1 |-> 2)` into `1 |-> 2` made a ground instance reach a successor that the symbolic step, specialised to it, says does not exist.
Before this, k-rust merged two identical bindings everywhere, in a rule's right-hand side too, while it kept two bindings of one key to different values (so a `total` function producing them left both in the configuration).

This diverges from reference concrete execution in form, not in outcome: the reference toolchain also treats a repeated key as undefined, but the LLVM backend aborts with an exception ("Duplicate keys in map concatenation") and the Haskell backend's `krun` prints `#Bottom`, or reports a `total` function that evaluated to `\bottom`, where k-rust ends in an undefined step.
The `a_repeated_set_element_or_map_key_is_bottom_where_it_is_built` CLI test, `a_map_binding_a_key_twice_simplifies_to_bottom_where_it_is_built` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend) and `a_map_argument_binding_a_key_twice_makes_the_hook_bottom` in [map.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/map.rs) cover this.

## A collection hook's value is a value of its declared result sort

A hooked symbol `f : S1 ... Sn -> S` denotes a value of `S` whatever computes it, and KORE has no implicit subsorting, so a value of a collection sort `S` is built from `S`'s own unit, element and concatenation symbols and holds elements of exactly `S`'s element (key, value) sorts.
k-rust builds every `LIST`, `SET` and `MAP` hook's collection result in the collection of the applied symbol's declared result sort, as that sort's declaration describes it: `makeListRef(Int, RefVal) : ListRef [hook(LIST.make)]` gives a `ListRef` value, equal to the same value written with `ListRef`'s own symbols, and `keys` of a `ValMap` read as a `Set` holds those keys injected into `KItem`.
An element of a strict subsort of the result's element sort is injected.
A hook that gives one element of a collection argument gives it as a value of the symbol's result sort the same way: `LIST.get`, `MAP.lookup`, `MAP.lookupOrDefault`, `MAP.choice` and `SET.choice` give the element (the value, the default argument, the key, or the set's element) itself when its sort is the result sort and its injection when that sort is a strict subsort, so `Vals "[" Int "]" : KItem [hook(LIST.get)]` on a list of `Val` gives `inj{Val, KItem}` of the element.

A declaration under which the hook gives no value of its result sort in general is rejected at compile time: `kcompile` reports an `InvalidAttribute` error at the production (`Production <label> is hooked to <hook>, but its declaration denotes no value of its result sort <sort>: <what does not fit>`) and writes no KORE.
The check runs for every `--backend`, so `kcompile --backend llvm` rejects such a declaration too, and it reads the declaration, not its uses, so a definition that never applies the symbol is rejected as well: `syntax Int ::= ichoice(Map) [function, hook(MAP.choice)]` is refused with no rule using it, since a key of a `Map` is a `KItem`.
The backend applies the same rule (`builtin::hook_signature`, one implementation over the facts each side states: collection sorts, element sorts and the subsort order) when it loads a definition, so KORE from another producer is rejected there, naming the symbol, its written `path:line:column` and what does not fit (`DefinitionError::CollectionHookMisfit`).
On the compile side the subsort order is the one the emitted subsort axioms state, which has no pair into `K`: a `K` is a `kseq`, and an injection into `K` such as `K ::= KItem` emits no subsort axiom ([Sorts of compiled terms](#sorts-of-compiled-terms)).
So a `KItem` is no `K`, and `K ::= List "<" Int ">" [hook(LIST.get)]`, the same result `K` for `MAP.lookup` and `MAP.lookupOrDefault`, and `LIST.element` or `MAP.keys_list` into a list whose element sort is `K` from a `Foo` or `KItem` argument are rejected.
What does not fit is one of: a result sort that is no collection of the hook's kind, an element argument, or an element, key or value of a collection argument that the hook moves into its result, whose sort is not the result's element (key, value) sort or a subsort of it, a collection argument of another kind than the hook reads, an argument count other than the hook's, or, for the element-returning hooks, an element, map key or value, or default whose sort is not the result sort or a subsort of it (`choice` returns a key of its map and an element of its set).
Such a declaration states that the hook computes a value of the result sort where it computes none: `makeBad(Int, Int) : Vals [hook(LIST.make)]` on a list of `Val` would build a `Vals` holding an `Int`, which no term of `Vals` denotes, and `Int ::= Vals "[" Int "]" [hook(LIST.get)]` would give an `Int` that is a `Val`. The symbol then has no model in which the hook computes it, and the error is in the declaration, so it is reported there rather than at each application.
An argument the hook only reads (an index, a key looked up or removed) is not checked, nor are the hooks k-rust does not evaluate (`LIST.fill`, `LIST.getMInt`, ...); a collection whose items the hook reads without moving them into its result is checked for its kind only: the second operand of `SET.difference`, `SET.intersection` and `MAP.difference`, whose items the hook compares with the first operand's, must be a set (a map), and the second operand of `MAP.removeAll`, whose elements are the keys the hook removes, must be a set, so `Map -Map Int : Map` and `removeAll(Map, Int) : Map` are refused. A declaration that fits but whose arguments the evaluator cannot combine (an opaque rest of another collection sort than the result's, the two sets of `SET.difference`, or the two maps of `MAP.difference`, of different sorts, or a set for `MAP.removeAll` whose element sort is not the map's key sort) still loads, and such an application is left unevaluated, the unsupported-hook outcome of [Backend scope](#backend-scope).
The hooks that read collections into a `Bool` or an `Int` are checked the same way: the collection operand of `LIST.in`, `LIST.size`, `SET.in`, `SET.size` and `MAP.in_keys`, `MAP.size`, and both operands of `SET.inclusion` and `MAP.inclusion`, must be a collection of the hook's kind (of any element sort), and the result sort must be `Bool` (`Int` for the sizes) or a sort above it, so `KItem in Int : Bool [hook(LIST.in)]` and `size(List) : Bool [hook(LIST.size)]` are refused; the sort of the item whose membership is tested is not checked, so `WrappedInt "in" ListInt` on a list of `WrappedInt` is accepted. A result sort strictly above `Bool` (`Int`) loads, and the hook's value is injected there, as every hook's value at a strict subsort of its declared result sort is ([Domain values](#domain-values)).
This is a new rejection: the pinned K accepts `makeBad` and its `krun` rewrites `makeBad(2, 3)` to `ValsItem(3) ValsItem(3)`, a `Vals` whose elements are `Int`s; k-rust's `kcompile` and `krun` refuse the definition at the production, so the backend's load check refuses no collection hook declaration `kcompile` writes.
The `hook_result_sorts` [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend) and the hook model tests of [hook_models](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/hook_models), which run in the builtin sorts' names and in renamed ones, cover the load check; the `a_collection_hook_that_gives_no_value_of_its_result_sort_is_a_located_error`, `collection_hooks_that_give_a_value_of_their_result_sort_compile_and_load` and `collection_reading_hooks_on_collections_of_their_kind_compile_and_load` [compile tests](../crates/k-rust/tests/module_to_kore.rs) cover the compile-time rejection, and the generative compile test classifies it as `collection-hook-misfit` ([expected.toml](../scripts/generative/expected.toml)).

An item or key the hook reads and compares with a collection's elements (keys) may be declared at another sort than the collection's element (key) sort, and it denotes the value it is: `LIST.in`, `SET.in`, `MAP.in_keys` and the key of `MAP.lookup` and `MAP.lookupOrDefault` compare the item with the elements at a common sort.
An item of a strict subsort is compared as its injection, so with `syntax Bool ::= Val "inS" Set [function, total, hook(SET.in)]`, `v() inS SetItem(v())` is `true`: the set holds `inj{Val, KItem}(v())`, which is the element the item denotes. An item of a strict supersort that is the injection of a value of the element sort or of a subsort of it is compared as that value; any other item of a supersort is compared with each element injected into the item's sort, which decides only its absence from a collection without an opaque rest (`false`, `\bottom` for `MAP.lookup`, the default for `MAP.lookupOrDefault`); against a collection with an opaque rest it stays unevaluated.
An item of a sort that is neither below nor above the element (key) sort is related to the elements by no injection, so the application stays unevaluated (for the partial `MAP.lookup` on constructor-like arguments, the unsupported-hook outcome of [Backend scope](#backend-scope)): it is never `false`, a miss or the default. `MAP.remove` and `MAP.update` build a map, and take a key of a strict subsort of the key sort as its injection; a key of a wider sort leaves them unevaluated.
The `collection_item_sorts` [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend) cover every relation, and `krun_reads_a_membership_item_of_a_subsort_as_its_injection` and `kprove_does_not_prove_a_subsort_item_absent_from_a_set_holding_it` in the [cli tests](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs) cover the K route.

## A collection's role symbols have their roles' signatures

A collection sort `C` hooked `LIST.List`, `SET.Set` or `MAP.Map` names its unit, element and concatenation symbols in its `unit`, `element` and `concat` attributes, and k-rust builds every application of one of them as a value of `C`.
The symbol's own declaration also fixes the sort of its applications and of their arguments, so the two must agree, or a role application would have two sorts that no reading of the definition gives it.
k-rust therefore loads a definition only if each role symbol of each collection sort has its role's signature: it has no sort parameters (R1); its result sort is exactly `C`, not a subsort or supersort of it (R2); the unit takes no argument, the element one argument (two for a map: key and value), and the concatenation two arguments, each exactly `C` (R3); and its hook, if it has one, is the role's own, `LIST.unit`, `LIST.element` or `LIST.concat` and the `SET` and `MAP` twins (R4).
A role symbol need not carry a hook: the collection sort's attributes alone fix what it builds.
Another hook asks for a theory k-rust does not evaluate for the role, even a coherent one: `SET.union` on a set's concatenation states an idempotent union, and `MAP.updateAll` on a map's a right-biased update, where k-rust builds the concatenation of [Set concatenation is nilpotent](#set-concatenation-is-nilpotent) and [Map concatenation binds each key once](#map-concatenation-binds-each-key-once).
Without the rule, `syntax LI ::= LI LI [element(mkFoo), ...]` with `mkFoo : Int -> Foo` made `mkFoo(5)` a list where its declaration says a `Foo` stands, so a `Foo` pattern rejected it and a rule the definition never reaches fired.
The backend refuses such a definition when it loads it, before any axiom is internalized, with `DefinitionError::CollectionRoleMisfit`, which names the collection sort, the role, the symbol, its written `path:line:column` and what does not fit (`collection sort SortLI: its element symbol LblmkFoo (path:line:column) has result sort SortFoo, not the collection sort SortLI`); the rule is `builtin::collection_role_misfit`, stated over any sort type so that a frontend can decide its productions with it.
`kcompile` decides the same rule before it writes KORE, over the declarations it emits for each collection sort: the concatenation is the production carrying the `element` attribute that emission writes as the sort's `concat`, the unit and element are the productions its `unit` and `element` attributes name, each symbol's sorts are its production's sorts, and its hook is the hook emission writes, which a `function` carries only when the hook's namespace is builtin or selected with `--hook-namespaces`. So it refuses a definition only when the loader would refuse the emitted KORE (a map element of the wrong arity is refused there as a malformed collection), and it refuses every definition whose emitted KORE the loader would refuse for a role misfit: `syntax LI ::= liItem(Int) [function, hook(MYNS.item)]` as the element of a list is refused with `--hook-namespaces MYNS` and compiles, as an unhooked function, without it, and a second production carrying `element` names no role, since emission does not write its attributes into the sort's declaration.
The error is an `InvalidAttribute` error of the hook namespace checks, at the concatenation production, naming the role, the named production with its own position and signature, what does not fit, and the required signature (`The element attribute of production _LI_ names mkFoo (role.k:6:18) : Int -> Foo, which has result sort Foo, not the collection sort LI; the element of collection sort LI must have result sort LI and one argument, with no hook or LIST.element.`), and no KORE is written; a role production with sort parameters is reported by the parametric checks instead.
The `collection_roles` [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend) and the [facade test](../crates/k-rust/tests/collection_roles.rs) cover the load check, and `a_collection_role_production_without_its_role_signature_is_a_located_error` and `collection_roles_kcompile_does_not_emit_are_not_refused` in the [definition check tests](../crates/k-rust/tests/definition_checks.rs) cover the compile-time rejection.
The rule constrains declarations only: an executable equation rooted at a role symbol, such as `rule ListItem(1) => ListItem(2)`, is not refused; it is indexed at a collection, never applied, and reported as a violated precondition of the value claims ([Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules), `ViolationKind::IndexedAt`).

## A contradicted `total` attribute is named, not repaired

k-rust's backend takes an emitted symbol's `total` (or `functional`) attribute as an axiom: an application of it to defined arguments is defined, so a rule whose right-hand side holds only total applications carries no definedness obligation, and a symbolic claim through such an application can be proven.
The frontend derives value productions' `functional` claims from their equations, including when the source carries deprecated `functional`, as [Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules) describes.
For a source `function, total`, the attribute is the author's claim, and deciding it is undecidable in general, so k-rust neither checks nor derives it, and a false attribute makes the definition inconsistent on the inputs where it is false; any verdict about those inputs then follows from the definition as written.
When evaluation observes the contradiction, an equation of such a symbol whose result, on arguments that were defined when it fired, simplifies to `\bottom` (a builtin's undefined result such as `10 /Int 0`, or a set or map with a repeated element or key), k-rust names it and changes nothing else:
`krun` follows its no-successor warning with ``warning: the `total` attribute of <K label> is contradicted on this input: its equation <label or id> at <file:line:column> reduces <application> to bottom (undefined at <term>); ...``, `kprove` prints the same line under the failed claim's `Vacuous` or `Trivial` leaf; the symbol is named by the K label its KORE name encodes (`tDiv(_)_M_Int_Int`, or the name a `symbol(...)` attribute gives), while the application and the undefined term are printed as KORE, and the backend's execution leaves carry it as `contradictedTotal` (`ExecutionLeaf.contradicted_total`, `HaltReason::Trivial`/`Vacuous`, `ProofLeaf.contradicted_total`).
The innermost such equation is named; an argument that is already `\bottom` before the equation fires names none.
A contradiction that evaluation does not observe, such as the symbolic `tAdd(SetItem(1), I)` of [Set concatenation is nilpotent](#set-concatenation-is-nilpotent), is not reported, and the kore-rpc protocol, which has no field for it, is unchanged.
The `a_contradicted_total_attribute_is_named_without_changing_the_outcome` CLI test and `a_ground_undefined_step_through_a_total_function_names_the_contradicted_attribute` in [undefined_step_coverage.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/undefined_step_coverage.rs) cover this.

## Hook specification exceptions

The hook contract is K's [`k-distribution/include/kframework/builtin/domains.md`](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/blob/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/k-distribution/include/kframework/builtin/domains.md).

- `MAP.inclusion` compares complete entries: each included key must have the same value in the other map. Kore follows this contract; Booster's key-only check does not.
- `MAP.difference` (`M1 -Map M2`) compares complete entries too: it removes a binding of `M1` only where `M2` binds its key to the same value (`domains.md`: "only keys whose value is the same in both maps are removed"), and keeps a key `M2` binds to another value; removing every key of `M2` is `removeAll(M1, keys(M2))`. Values and keys are compared as the values they denote, so two spellings of one `Float`, machine-integer or hooked-`INT.Int` value are one value. The hook is `total`, so where a binding's removal is not decided (a value that may or may not equal the other map's, a key that may or may not be one of `M2`'s, an opaque rest of `M1`, or of `M2` beside a key it does not bind) the application is left unevaluated; `M1 -Map M1`, `M -Map .Map` and `.Map -Map M` are decided whatever the bindings, under the arguments' definedness as for every hook: `f(X) -Map f(X)` is `.Map` only where `f(X)` is defined. (`difference_removes_only_bindings_held_with_the_same_value`, `difference_with_an_undecided_value_or_key_is_not_evaluated`, `difference_of_a_map_with_itself_is_empty` and `difference_compares_builtin_values_not_spellings` in [map.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/map.rs), the `MAP.difference` model in [hook_models](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/hook_models), `map_difference_builds_the_value_of_its_declared_map_sort` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), `krun_evaluates_map_difference_by_binding` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)
- `STRING.find` reports an index in the original haystack. The pinned Kore `kore/src/Kore/Builtin/String.hs` searches the suffix without rebasing the result.
- `BYTES.replaceAt` requires the entire replacement range to fit in the original bytes. The pinned Kore `kore/src/Kore/Builtin/InternalBytes.hs` checks only the start index.

Rust retains the specified behavior for each of these operations.
The [hook fixtures](../crates/k-rust/tests/fixtures/reference/hooks) and `execution.oracle-exception` entries in the differential manifest preserve separate Rust and Kore expectations for the two Kore deviations.
Normalization N18 checks both expectations independently; when a pin fixes the deviation, refresh the reference evidence and remove that exception.

## Conditionals and Boolean connectives are strict

`#if C #then T #else E #fi` is an application of the hooked symbol `ite{S}`, declared `function, functional, total` with the hook `KEQUAL.ite`.
Its meaning at a sort `S` is the hook and the symbol's `functional` axiom: the `domains.md` equations for `#if` are emitted for `ite{SortK{}}` only, and are not its definition at any other sort.
An application in matching logic is undefined wherever one of its arguments is, and `total` promises a value on defined arguments only, so with a decided condition the value is the operand the condition selects under the `\ceil` of the other one: `#if true #then 1 #else undef() #fi` denotes `1 /\ \ceil(undef())` in every model of the definition, and `1` alone is false in each model where `undef()` is undefined.
The same holds while the condition is symbolic: the application stays as written, and where a rule's left-hand side meets it execution splits on the condition, each branch carrying the `\ceil` of the operand it does not select (`mid(#if N >Int 0 #then 1 #else undef() #fi)` under the rule `mid(1) => one()` gives `one()` under `N >Int 0` and `\ceil(undef())`).
A proof checks a destination that holds such an application together with its definedness, as it does for every partial application the destination adds ([Definedness of an implication's destination](#definedness-of-an-implications-destination)).

`andBool`, `orBool`, `andThenBool`, `orElseBool` and `impliesBool` are hooked symbols too, and as strict, also in the operand a decided one makes irrelevant to the value: `false andBool undefb()` and `false andThenBool undefb()` are `false`, and `true orBool undefb()`, `true orElseBool undefb()` and `false impliesBool undefb()` are `true`, each under `\ceil(undefb())`.

What differs between them is the order of evaluation, stated in full in [Conditionals evaluate only the operand they select](#conditionals-evaluate-only-the-operand-they-select):
- `#if`, `andThenBool` and `orElseBool` simplify the deciding operand first and then only the operand it selects. The operand it discards is not evaluated beyond the bounded steps that decide its definedness, which is kept as a deferred obligation: pure builtin hooks on ground constructor-like arguments (literals, constructor terms, closed collections of them), a ceil equation whose condition those hooks decide or whose instance over hook arithmetic is stated in the obligation's place, the distinctness of literal collection keys, one equation step per application selected by matching alone (with its value on literal arguments when hooks fold it to a literal), a known fact, and the solver, which reads the obligation as an opaque atom. No other equation of the definition is run on the operand, and none on the result of such a step, with one exception: a simplification lemma the definition states over the conditional itself is applied to it, and an operand its right-hand side moves out of the conditional is evaluated there.
- A discarded console operation is not performed: an application of a symbol declared `total` is defined by the declaration, a console hook on literal arguments in the console's domain is defined, and a discarded operand whose definedness still depends on an impure application, such as the value of a console read that is not performed (`1 /Int {#getc(0)}:>Int`), is the unsupported-hook outcome, with nothing read or written.
- `andBool`, `orBool` and `impliesBool` simplify both operands, innermost, and perform their effects.
- This reverses `domains.md`'s note that `#if` "does not short-circuit on symbolic backends", and agrees with its note that `andThenBool` and `orElseBool` are short-circuiting.
- Recursion guarded by one of the three terminates: with `fact(N) => #if N <=Int 0 #then 1 #else N *Int fact(N -Int 1) #fi`, `fact(3)` is `6` under `\ceil(fact(-1))`, its exact denotation, since the axioms also admit a `fact` that is `\bottom` everywhere.

What a run shows:
- `krun` prints the selected value with the obligation as a conjunct; `Backend::execute` and RPC `execute` give a leaf under the obligation and, where it fails, a trivial leaf ([Trivial rule results](#trivial-rule-results)).
- A discarded operand that builtin hooks alone show undefined makes the result `\bottom`: `#if true #then 1 #else 1 /Int 0 #fi` ends with "applied with an undefined result; refuted obligation \ceil(1 /Int 0)". So does one that a single equation step shows undefined: with `f(N) => N +Int 1`, the discarded `1 /Int f(-1)` is `1 /Int 0`. One whose undefinedness needs more evaluation (`1 /Int g(-1)` with `g(N) => f(N)`) leaves the selected value under the open `\ceil(1 /Int g(-1))`.
- An open obligation makes the state constrained, so the console is unavailable for the rest of the path: a later `#write` is the unsupported-hook halt.
- A claim through recursion guarded by `#if` (`go(3) => 6`) reaches its trivial leaf and is proven only with `--allow-vacuous`.
- In a `requires`, a conditional whose unused operand is undefined has the value `\bottom`, so the rule does not apply: `requires #if N >Int 0 #then true #else 1 /Int 0 >Int 0 #fi` at `N = 1`.

Both pinned reference backends differ.
The LLVM backend returns the selected operand alone, which is false wherever the unused operand is undefined, and it also short-circuits `andBool` for effects.
The Haskell backend evaluates the unused operand in execution, and its prover reads `#if` lazily ([Definedness of an implication's destination](#definedness-of-an-implications-destination) lists claims it proves and k-rust does not).
k-rust keeps `andBool`, `orBool` and `impliesBool` innermost because they are strict hooked symbols and `domains.md` describes no short-circuit for them, where it does for `andThenBool` and `orElseBool`.

To get a bare value, write the alternatives as rules with complementary `requires`, which never build the operand that is not taken:

```k
rule fact(N) => 1                     requires N <=Int 0
rule fact(N) => N *Int fact(N -Int 1) requires N >Int 0
```

(`krun_keeps_the_definedness_of_an_untaken_conditional_branch`, `a_symbolic_conditional_splits_on_its_condition_under_the_unused_operands_definedness` and `the_strict_connectives_perform_the_effects_of_both_operands` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), `an_untaken_partial_branch_splits_execution_into_a_guarded_leaf_and_a_trivial_one` in [rewrite.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/rewrite.rs); the order's tests are listed in [Conditionals evaluate only the operand they select](#conditionals-evaluate-only-the-operand-they-select).)

## Hook values at the edges of their domains

Where `domains.md` describes a hook's value without settling an edge of its domain, k-rust settles it from the description, the hook's attributes and the K equations `domains.md` gives, and the constant folder computes the backend's outcome through the backend's own evaluation entry ([Constant folding](#constant-folding)).
A partial hook whose description gives no value of its result sort on some arguments is undefined there (`\bottom`).
A `total` hook always has a value, so where its description gives none, the value exists but is not determined by the definition: the backend leaves such an application unevaluated, as a term denoting an unknown value of its sort, without reporting an unsupported hook, and the fold leaves it too; neither `\bottom` nor a chosen value is a consequence of the definition.
A hook whose value on some arguments is one of several the definition admits, such as the order of `keys_list`, is evaluated only in an execution; see [Implementation-chosen hook values](#implementation-chosen-hook-values).

- `substrString(S, START, END)` is the code points of `S` in `[START..END)` on the valid indices `0 <= START <= END <= lengthString(S)`; `domains.md` declares it `total` and says it is "only defined on valid indices", so outside them its value is not determined. Valid indices include `START == END` (the empty string), which the `domains.md` equations of `replaceFirst`, `findChar` and `countAllOccurrences` use on every string. (`substr_is_unspecified_outside_valid_indices` in [string.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/string.rs), `an_unspecified_hook_value_stays_unevaluated_without_an_error` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), and the `string-index-boundaries` oracle exception.)
- `chrChar(N)` is undefined when `N` is not a code point (outside `[0, 0x10FFFF]`): no character has that number, and the hook is partial. For a surrogate code point (U+D800 to U+DFFF) the definition does not settle whether the value exists: K's string syntax writes one (`\uFFFF` "indicates a code point between 0x0000 and 0xffff"), while the KORE language specification makes surrogates illegal in string literals, in which every `String` domain value is written. Both evaluators therefore leave `chrChar` of a surrogate unevaluated, without an error, a term that denotes the one-character string if K strings hold surrogates and is undefined if they do not; `\bottom` would make every claim over such a configuration hold vacuously under the first reading. `ordChar(chrChar(N)) == N` wherever `chrChar(N)` is computed. (`chr_of_a_surrogate_is_unspecified_and_of_a_non_code_point_undefined` in [string.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/string.rs), `krun_leaves_chr_of_a_surrogate_unevaluated` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), `folds_string_chr_only_for_unicode_scalar_values` in [kompile_passes.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/kompile_passes.rs).)
- `rootFloat(F, N)` is "the Nth integer root" of `F`, IEEE 754 rootn, correctly rounded to nearest in `F`'s format for every integer `N`: degree 0 and an even root of a negative value are NaN; the root of a zero is a zero of its sign for an odd positive degree and `+0.0` for an even one, and an infinity of its sign for an odd negative degree and `+Infinity` for an even one; the root of an infinity is an infinity for a positive degree and a zero for a negative one, of its sign for an odd degree (an even root of `-Infinity` is NaN). `sqrtFloat` has no hook; `domains.md` defines it by the rule `sqrtFloat(F) => rootFloat(F, 2)`, so `sqrtFloat(-0.0)` is `+0.0` as well, unlike IEEE 754 squareRoot. The backend computes degrees 1, -1 and 2 with IEEE operations and every other degree by an exact rounding decision (`float/root.rs` in the backend's builtins); the fold writes the backend's value. (`float_root_is_the_ieee_rootn_of_every_degree` in [builtin.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin.rs) and `the_root_lies_between_the_midpoints_around_the_result` in the backend.)
- `countAllOccurrences(S, "")` and `replaceAll(S, "", R)` have no value the definition gives: both are `total`, and `domains.md` defines the count by `countAllOccurrences(S, "") = 1 +Int countAllOccurrences(S, "")` (the empty needle is found at 0 with the whole string after it), which no integer satisfies, and `replaceAll` by `replace(S, "", R, countAllOccurrences(S, ""))`. The fold leaves both unfolded, and the backend's evaluators leave both unevaluated whatever `S` is, a symbolic `S` included. An `Unspecified` verdict reads the hook's documentation only, so the definition's function equations still apply to the application and give it the value they state (a definition may give `substrString` with reversed indices a value by its own rule); an equation is tried as an attempt from its `requires` to the end of the evaluation of its result, during which an occurrence of an application whose own attempt is still open is left as it is. Leaving it is the identity, so the outcome is still equal to the application; but an outcome that still holds such an occurrence states the application through itself, as `domains.md`'s count equation does for an empty needle, or through a value an enclosing attempt is still computing, and gives it no value. An `ensures` the attempt leaves open is part of its result, so one that holds such an occurrence gives no value either. Such an attempt is undone entirely (its result, its budget, its diagnostics and its console reads and writes) and the application's other equations are still tried; an application left unevaluated only because its attempts needed an enclosing attempt's value fails that enclosing attempt in turn, and so does any application, of an `Unspecified` hook or not, whose equations stayed undecided because a condition reached such an occurrence (its verdict may turn on the value that attempt is computing). A `requires` that holds or is refuted, or a result that drops the occurrence, does so whatever the occurrence's value, so the equation gives its value or is passed over. An equation whose result is the application itself (`A = A`) gives no value either. The application stays unevaluated, instead of being rewritten without end, only when none of its equations gives a value. The value such an application received, and an evaluation of one that ended unevaluated after an attempt failed, are kept with what that evaluation read of its surroundings (which applications had an attempt open, and at which depth; which rule conditions were being evaluated; the console and its read position, if it reached a console hook; the assumptions, by content; the options and the solver deciding its conditions) and reused wherever an evaluation would read the same, so retrying an enclosing attempt does not redo it. A rule condition shares them only where its terms are evaluated under the assumptions around it. Its Boolean conjunctions are split and its definedness obligations added as conjuncts, and each conjunct is evaluated with the others not yet decided as assumptions: one decided to hold is no assumption, and after a round that changed a conjunct those that remain are evaluated again, each under the others. So a `requires` that is one predicate, or of which one conjunct is left open (`len(X) > 100 andBool len("ab") > 1`), is decided without redoing what was evaluated around it, while a conjunct evaluated beside another that is still open reuses nothing evaluated outside the `requires`: in `C1 andBool C2` with both left open, or beside a definedness obligation that stays open (an application of a partial function that no equation evaluates). A cycle through a `requires` that keeps two or more conjuncts open can still repeat work a number of times exponential in the depth of the cycle. Reuse never changes a result, except that a reused one does not spend the iteration budget a fresh evaluation would. It can change the diagnostics: a reused evaluation does not emit again the diagnostics its evaluation emitted, and those emitted inside an attempt that was undone were discarded with it, so a warning that the evaluation without reuse reports (the unsupported-hook warning of an application that stays unevaluated) can be missing. The results are the same. Within a priority group the outcome does not depend on the order in which the equations are listed, except where two equations both give a result and the results differ as terms (the definition then states them equal: it has no model when both are values, and otherwise the evaluator leaves that equation between them undecided, as `C = k(0)` beside `C = "c"` for a `k(0)` no equation rewrites, or for a `k(0)` whose `requires` needs the same `requires` of `k(0)` again, which is left undecided) and where the iteration budget runs out. `replaceAll(S, "", R)` is therefore `replace(S, "", R, countAllOccurrences(S, ""))` with the count unevaluated, the value its equation states. A non-empty needle is counted and replaced at the occurrences found from the left, each search after the previous match, as the equations do. `replace(S, "", R, N)` is `N` copies of `R` followed by `S`, and `replaceFirst(S, "", R)` is `R` followed by `S`, by the same equations. (`count_and_replace_all_take_leftmost_disjoint_matches_and_no_empty_needle` in [string.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/string.rs), `a_definition_equation_gives_an_unspecified_hook_application_its_value` and `an_unspecified_application_whose_equation_needs_its_own_value_stays_unevaluated` and `an_equation_that_needs_its_own_application_does_not_discard_another_equations_value`, `an_attempt_that_needs_an_enclosing_application_does_not_discard_its_others`, `an_attempt_that_reaches_its_application_without_needing_it_gives_its_value`, `retrying_after_a_failed_attempt_reuses_the_values_it_computed`, `a_chain_of_unspecified_steps_at_the_root_runs_in_constant_stack`, `an_application_whose_attempts_failed_is_not_derived_again_while_what_they_needed_is_open`, `a_failed_evaluation_is_not_reused_once_the_attempts_it_reached_are_closed`, `an_evaluation_that_ran_out_of_budget_is_not_recorded_as_failed`, `an_application_blocked_by_an_open_attempt_does_not_settle_the_attempt_it_ends`, `an_application_left_for_an_open_attempt_is_not_reported_unevaluated`, `a_failed_attempt_returns_the_budget_it_spent_to_the_other_equations`, `an_attempt_whose_ensures_needs_its_application_gives_no_value`, `an_equation_whose_result_is_its_application_gives_no_value`, `a_failed_evaluation_is_not_reused_where_an_application_it_evaluated_is_open`, `a_cycle_through_a_functions_condition_reuses_what_the_lineage_evaluated`, `an_application_whose_condition_was_refuted_after_reaching_an_open_attempt_is_settled`, `an_evaluation_reused_meets_the_rule_conditions_it_met`, `a_value_computed_under_an_assumption_is_not_reused_without_it` and the randomized `unspecified_evaluation_agrees_with_and_without_reuse_in_every_order` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), `a_value_read_from_the_console_is_not_reused_without_it`, `a_failed_evaluation_that_read_the_console_is_not_reused_after_it_moved_on`, `a_value_computed_with_a_solver_is_not_reused_without_it`, `a_record_is_not_served_where_an_application_it_found_not_open_is_open_below_the_top`, `a_record_is_not_served_under_another_path_condition_with_the_same_predicates`, `a_record_made_under_top_is_served_without_it` and `the_first_record_that_holds_is_served` in [simplify.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/simplify.rs), `krun_leaves_counting_an_empty_needle_unevaluated`, `krun_undoes_the_console_effects_of_a_failed_unspecified_attempt`, `krun_repeats_the_effects_of_a_value_a_failed_attempt_computed` and `krun_decides_a_condition_alike_whether_the_execution_computed_its_value_before` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)
- `String2Token(S)`, a function hooked `STRING.string2token` and declared at the sort it builds (`String2Id`, `String2KVar`, or a user declaration), is the domain value of that sort whose text is the code points of `S`, read by the kind the sort's hook gives in the definition ([Domain values](#domain-values)): `String2MyInt("07")` at a sort hooked `INT.Int` is `7`. Where `S` names no element of the sort (no decimal integer at a sort hooked `INT.Int`, a machine-integer literal of another width, a code point above U+00FF at a sort hooked `BYTES.Bytes`), the description gives no value: the application of a partial declaration is undefined, and that of a `total` one has a value the definition does not determine. Where `S` names an element whose canonical text is beyond a bound of k-rust ([Resource bounds](#resource-bounds)), the element exists and the backend declines to compute it, whatever the declaration's totality; `\bottom` there would assert an undefinedness the definition does not entail, and would make a claim over the application hold vacuously. Without a definition (the library's `builtin::evaluate`) the result sort has no kind, and the application is not evaluated. (`string_to_token_of_a_text_that_names_no_element_has_no_value` and `string_to_token_beyond_a_bound_is_too_large_whatever_its_totality` in the [backend's domain-value tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/domain_values.rs).)
- `String2Bytes(S)` and `Bytes2String(B)`: `domains.md` says only that one can "convert between a `Bytes` and a `String`" and names no encoding, unlike `encodeBytes` and `decodeBytes`, which take one. k-rust reads the pair as mapping each byte to the code point of the same number. This is k-rust's chosen reading, not a statement of `domains.md`. Totality does not choose it: each reading leaves one direction without a value somewhere (`String2Bytes` above 255 under this reading, `Bytes2String` on an invalid sequence under UTF-8 decoding). The module's String section does: it says code points beyond the first 256 are not fully supported, and on exactly the strings within them this reading makes both `total` conversions total and mutually inverse, while UTF-8 decoding leaves `Bytes2String` without a value on invalid sequences whichever strings are supported. Under it, a string holding a code point above 255 is the image of no byte sequence, so the `total` `String2Bytes` has a value there that the definition does not determine. (`string_to_bytes_above_255_is_unspecified` in [bytes.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/bytes.rs).)
- `Int2Bytes(LENGTH, I, E)` is the `LENGTH`-byte two's complement of `I` in byte order `E`; the hook is `total`, and no `Bytes` has a negative length, so its value for a negative `LENGTH` is not determined. (`int_to_bytes_with_a_negative_length_is_unspecified` in [bytes.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/bytes.rs).)
