- `replaceAtBytes(DEST, INDEX, SRC)` replaces the bytes of `DEST` in `[INDEX, INDEX + lengthBytes(SRC))` and is `#False` (undefined) unless that range lies inside `DEST`, that is `0 <= INDEX` and `INDEX + lengthBytes(SRC) <= lengthBytes(DEST)`, as `substrBytes` requires of its range: an empty `SRC` at the end of `DEST` changes nothing, and past the end it is undefined. (`replace_at_past_the_end_is_bottom` in [bytes.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/bytes.rs).)
- `choice(M)` is some key of the map `M` and `choice(S)` some element of the set `S`, the same one for the same collection; both are partial (`function` without `total`). The empty map and the empty set have none, so `choice(.Map)` and `choice(.Set)` are undefined. A map of one binding `K |-> V` has the one key `K` and a set of one element `E` the one element `E`, so every choice gives it, whether or not `K` (`E`) is constructor-like, on every route (execution and proof alike, since no model gives another value); the dropped value `V` still contributes its definedness, so `choice(K |-> f(V))` with a partial `f` is `K` only where `f(V)` is defined. A collection with an opaque rest beside at most one item may have one item under an instance and stays unevaluated until it is known; which of two or more items `choice` gives is not a consequence of the definition: an execution gives the item the collection's listing puts first, and every other evaluation leaves the application unevaluated as a defined value ([Implementation-chosen hook values](#implementation-chosen-hook-values)). (`choice_of_the_empty_collection_is_bottom`, `choice_of_a_one_item_collection_is_its_item_even_symbolic`, `choice_is_the_first_key_keys_list_lists` and `choice_is_the_first_element_set2list_lists` in [map.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/map.rs) and [set.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/set.rs), `choice_gives_its_one_key_or_element_as_a_value_of_its_result_sort` in the `hook_result_sorts` [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), `krun_evaluates_choice_of_one_binding` and `kprove_reads_choice_of_one_binding_and_keeps_the_definedness_of_its_value` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).) A `choice` declaration whose result sort is not the key (element) sort or a supersort of it is refused at its production, whether or not anything applies it ([A collection hook's value is a value of its declared result sort](#a-collection-hooks-value-is-a-value-of-its-declared-result-sort)).
- `F1 /Float F2`, and likewise `+Float`, `-Float`, `*Float`, `minFloat` and `maxFloat`, over a binary32 and a binary64 operand: the operation is the exact result rounded to some destination format, which `domains.md` does not name, so the application is defined and its value is one of several the definition admits. An execution rounds to binary64, k-rust's chosen reading ([Backend scope](#backend-scope)); every other evaluation leaves the application unevaluated as a defined value ([Implementation-chosen hook values](#implementation-chosen-hook-values)); the fold leaves it unfolded. (`float_arithmetic_over_two_formats_rounds_to_the_wider_format` in [builtin.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin.rs).)
- `roundFloat(F, P, E)`, `Int2Float(I, P, E)` and `maxValueFloat(P, E)` round to, or name a value of, "a specified precision and exponent range", and are partial: a precision or exponent width of zero or below names no `pNxM` format (`domains.md` requires positive integers), so the description gives no value and the application is undefined, whatever `F` or `I` is, symbolic or of a format the backend does not compute. A positive pair names a format, which the backend computes for binary32 and binary64 and otherwise reports as the unsupported-hook outcome naming it, a field of 1 included ([Backend scope](#backend-scope)); the fold writes only the backend's values, so it leaves both unfolded. `minValueFloat` has no evaluator in any format, so `minValueFloat(0, 0)` is the unsupported-hook outcome while `maxValueFloat(0, 0)` is undefined. (`float_format_arguments_name_a_format_or_none` in [builtin.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin.rs).)
- `Float2Int(F)` is the integer nearest to `F`; the hook is `total`, and NaN and the infinities have no nearest integer, in any format (one the backend does not compute included, decided from the token), so its value there is not determined. (`float_integer_conversions_are_arbitrary_precision_and_ties_to_even` in [builtin.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin.rs).)
- A `String` is a sequence of code points, and every K string escape names one (`\xFF` "a code point between 0x00 and 0xff"), so `"é"` and `"\xe9"` are one value and `"\xc3\xa9"` is the two code points U+00C3 U+00A9 ([Strings and their literals](#strings-and-their-literals)). `ordChar` of the string a `\xNN` escape writes is the escape's number, and `String2Bytes` maps it to that byte. (`ord_of_an_escaped_code_point_is_its_value` in [string.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/string.rs), `string_to_bytes_above_255_is_unspecified` in [bytes.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/bytes.rs), `krun_reads_and_writes_strings_as_code_points` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)
- `B ^Int E` is "the exponentiation of two integers", of sort `Int`, and the hook is partial. For a negative `E = -n`, `B ^Int E` is the integer `1 / B^n` exactly when `|B| = 1`: `1 ^Int E` is 1 and `-1 ^Int E` is 1 or -1 by the parity of `E`, and for any other base (0, or a magnitude of at least 2) no integer is the power, so the application is undefined. The `smt-hook` `^` is Z3's power, which on two integers is the real power and so has a value (a fraction) where no integer is the power; the backend therefore reads every `^` of an `smt-hook` as itself only where an integer power exists (a nonnegative exponent, or a base of magnitude 1) and as an uninterpreted function of its operands elsewhere, as SMT-LIB leaves a division by zero, so that the solver decides nothing from a value the definition does not give, except a power whose exponent is a numeral, which has an integer value for every base: a positive numeral is read as itself (Z3 reads it as a product), and the exponent 0 as the constant 1, since `X ^Int 0` is 1 for every `X`, `0 ^Int 0` included, while Z3 leaves `(^ 0 0)` uninterpreted (`guard_powers` in [smt.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/smt.rs), `decides_nothing_from_a_power_where_no_integer_is_its_value` in [z3.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/smt/z3.rs), `kprove_proves_nothing_from_a_power_where_no_integer_is_its_value` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)). The guard names each operand several times, and the translation states an `smt-hook` argument that its template names more than once in one `let` (which denotes substitution, so the formula is unchanged), so nested powers, like nested `/Int` and `%Int` whose templates name each argument five times, translate to text linear in their depth (`states_each_hook_argument_once_however_hooks_nest` in [smt.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/smt.rs)). A claim whose proof needs the undefinedness of a power with a symbolic negative exponent is not proven: `domains.md` gives `^Int` no `#Ceil` rule, so that definedness is an atom the solver cannot decide. (`unit_bases_have_a_power_for_every_exponent` and `undefined_integer_operations_are_bottom` in [builtin.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin.rs).)
- `A ^%Int B C` is the Euclidean remainder of `A ^Int B` modulo `C`, in `[0, |C|)`: its `smt-hook` is `(mod (^ #1 #2) #3)`, the form in which the backend states `^%Int` terms to the SMT solver (with the power read as above), and an evaluator that disagreed with it where the hook has a value would let the backend derive two values for one term. The prose's "`(A ^Int B) %Int C`" agrees with it except for a negative power, where `%Int` takes the sign of the dividend. A zero `C` is undefined. For a negative `B`, `A ^Int B` has a value only for `A` = 1 or -1 (above), so `A ^%Int B C` is the remainder of that power (`-1 ^%Int -1 5` is 4) and undefined for every other `A`, even one invertible modulo `C` (`2 ^%Int -1 7`): the modular inverse is not `(A ^Int B) mod C`, and neither the prose nor the `smt-hook` states it. (`powmod_is_the_euclidean_remainder_of_the_power` and `undefined_integer_operations_are_bottom` in [builtin.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin.rs).)
- `String2Base(S, B)` reads the syntax of `String2Int` in radix `B`: an optional sign and one or more digits of `B`, letters in either case. `domains.md` describes the pair as a conversion "between a String and an Int in a particular radix", and `Base2String` of a negative `Int` writes a sign, so `String2Base` reads one. The radix "can be anywhere between 2 and 36", and `domains.md` gives the digits of exactly those radixes; both hooks are partial, so with a radix outside that range there is no conversion the definition describes and the application is undefined. (`integer_conversions_accept_only_signed_digits` and `bases_outside_two_through_thirty_six_are_undefined` in [string.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/string.rs).)
- `findString(S, N, I)` is the first occurrence of `N` in `S` starting at or after code point `I`, and `rfindString(S, N, I)` the last one starting at or before it, -1 when there is none, for every integer `I`: a negative `I` searches `findString` from the start and gives `rfindString` no candidate, and an `I` past the end gives `findString` none and searches `rfindString` from the end. `findChar` and `rfindChar` follow, since their `domains.md` equations pass `I` to these two. (`find_searches_forward_from_any_index` and `rfind_searches_back_from_any_index` in [string.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/string.rs).)
- A machine integer of `MInt{W}` is a `W`-bit pattern without a sign of its own ("machine integers of arbitrary bit width represented in 2's complement"): an operation reads it as its unsigned residue `u` in `[0, 2^W)` (`MInt2Unsigned`), or in two's complement as `s = u - 2^W` when `u >= 2^(W-1)` and `s = u` otherwise (`MInt2Signed`). The `total` operations are therefore arithmetic modulo `2^W`, as their `smt-hook`s (`bvadd`, `bvmul`, `bvnot`, `bvult`, `bvslt`, `bv2int`, `int2bv`, ...) state them to a solver: `Int2MInt(I)` is `I` modulo `2^W` (Euclidean), `+MInt`, `-MInt`, `*MInt`, `--MInt` and `~MInt` wrap, `&MInt`, `|MInt` and `xorMInt` act on the bits of `u`, `==MInt` and `=/=MInt` compare `u`, and the unsigned and signed orderings compare `u` and `s`. `MInt{0}` has the one element `0p0`, whose readings are both 0. `Int2MInt` takes `W` from the sort of its application (a cast or a typed position), and stays unevaluated while that sort's width is a sort variable. A sort `MInt{Foo}` names no width and has no literals, so there `Int2MInt` and `bitwidthMInt`, both `total`, have a value the definition does not determine. That the value exists is the statement of `total` alone, since the hooks' description gives none without a width: an application at such a sort of a user function hooked `MINT.integer` or `MINT.bitwidth` and declared without `total` is left unevaluated too, with the `UnsupportedHookUnevaluated` warning, and is not shown defined, so a claim through `syntax MInt{Foo} ::= partialWord(Int) [function, hook(MINT.integer)]` fails with an `undefined step` leaf where the same claim through a `total` declaration is proven (`a_sort_without_a_numeral_width_leaves_the_total_conversions_unspecified` in [hook_models](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/hook_models), `kprove_takes_a_widthless_mint_conversion_as_defined_only_where_it_is_declared_total` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)). `bitwidthMInt(X)` is `W` for every `X` of sort `MInt{W}`, a variable included, since every element of the sort has that width. A result wider than the bound on the integers the backend computes ([Resource bounds](#resource-bounds)) is not computed: a residue of about `W` bits that a result wraps to at a width above the bound (`--MInt 1pW`, `Int2MInt(-1)`), or a left shift that keeps a set bit beyond it. `/uMInt` and `%uMInt` are the floor quotient and remainder of `u`; `/sMInt` and `%sMInt` truncate the quotient of `s` toward zero and give the remainder the dividend's sign, as `bvsdiv` and `bvsrem` (not `bvsmod`) do. These four are partial and there is no quotient by zero, so a zero divisor is undefined (`\bottom`), and in `MInt{0}`, whose one element is 0, every division is; SMT-LIB gives `bvudiv`, `bvurem`, `bvsdiv` and `bvsrem` values at a zero divisor, a totalization the backend never reads as a K value ([Solver decisions over partial operations](#solver-decisions-over-partial-operations)). `MIN /sMInt -1` is `MIN`, since the quotient `2^(W-1)` has the pattern of `MIN` and the result carries no sign, and `MIN %sMInt -1` is 0. The shifts read their amount as `u`, and `domains.md` gives them a value for every amount, so no shift is undefined although none is declared `total`: an amount of `W` or more gives 0 for `<<MInt` and `>>lMInt`, and 0 or all sign bits (`2^W - 1`) for `>>aMInt` by the sign of `s`. These edges have no reference value: the pinned LLVM interpreter aborts with SIGFPE on a zero divisor (`/uMInt`, `%sMInt`) and on `MIN /sMInt -1`, and does not finish on `MIN %sMInt -1`. (`total_mint_hooks_agree_with_native_fixed_width_arithmetic`, `partial_mint_hooks_agree_with_native_fixed_width_arithmetic` and the tests beside them in [hook_models/mint.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/hook_models/mint.rs), `kore_simplify_of_a_mint_division_by_zero_is_bottom` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)
- `bitRangeInt(I, IDX, LEN)` is the `LEN` bits of `I`'s two's complement starting at bit `IDX`, read as an unsigned number, and `signExtendBitRangeInt(I, IDX, LEN)` the same bits read as a `LEN`-bit two's-complement number, for every integer `I`, negative ones included (their two's complement repeats the sign bit without end). Both hooks are partial, and both are undefined for a negative `IDX` or `LEN`, `signExtendBitRangeInt` also for `LEN = 0`: module `INT` of `domains.md` states them by the equations `bitRangeInt(I, IDX, LEN) = (I >>Int IDX) modInt (1 <<Int LEN)` and `signExtendBitRangeInt(I, IDX, LEN) = (bitRangeInt(I, IDX, LEN) +Int (1 <<Int (LEN -Int 1))) modInt (1 <<Int LEN) -Int (1 <<Int (LEN -Int 1))`, which are axioms of every definition that imports that module, and there their right-hand sides shift by a negative amount, which is `#False`. The backend evaluates both hooks itself on `Int` values with exactly these values and this undefinedness, so in a definition that imports `INT-COMMON`, which declares them, without `INT`, a ground application has the same value or the same undefinedness as with `INT`, and the size bound applies to the result, not to the equations' `1 <<Int LEN` ([Resource bounds](#resource-bounds)). An application with a symbolic argument is left to the equations: without `INT` nothing states its value or its definedness, so, for example, a claim whose step yields `bitRangeInt(X, 0, 8)` is proven with `INT` imported but fails with `INT-COMMON` alone as an undefined step, although the application is defined for every `X`. The pinned backends differ from these equations at the edges of the domain, and from each other: both give `signExtendBitRangeInt(I, IDX, 0) = 0`, the LLVM backend aborts on a negative index or length, and the Haskell backend gives `bitRangeInt(5, -1, 3) = 2`, shifting left by the negative index. (`bit_ranges_are_the_value_of_their_domains_equations` and `bit_ranges_are_bounded_by_their_result_not_by_two_to_the_length` in [builtin.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin.rs), `krun_evaluates_bit_ranges_without_the_int_module_equations` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), and `folded_and_evaluated_applications_agree` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), which runs every hook the fold evaluates on literals and on program arguments.)

## Implementation-chosen hook values

Some hooks have several values the definition admits on the same arguments.
`keys_list(M)`, `values(M)` and `Set2List(S)` are some listing of the items of `M` or `S`, each a function of the collection's value, and `domains.md` fixes no order; `choice` is some item.
With zero or one item every listing is the same, so those cases are consequences of the definition everywhere.
With two or more items, an evaluation either chooses one listing or leaves the application unevaluated, and which one k-rust does depends on whether it is running a program.

An execution runs a program, and any value the definition admits is a correct run, so it lists the items in k-rust's order: the order of their terms, which for `Int` keys and elements is the decimal text order, not the numeric one (`keys_list(10 |-> 1 2 |-> 2 -1 |-> 3)` is `ListItem(-1) ListItem(10) ListItem(2)`).
`values(M)` lists the values in the order `keys_list(M)` lists their keys.
`choice(M)` (`choice(S)`) of a closed map (set) of two or more constructor-like keys (elements) is the item `keys_list(M)` (`Set2List(S)`) lists first, so the hooks share one choice: `choice(3 |-> 30 1 |-> 10 2 |-> 20)` is `1`, `choice(10 |-> a 2 |-> b)` is `10`, and a fold that takes `M[choice(M)]` and removes `choice(M)` until the map is empty visits every binding once. A key (element) that is not constructor-like beside others, or an opaque rest, may put another item first in an instance, so such an application waits on every route.
The order is a function of the value because equal values are one term, `Float` and `MInt` items included, whose domain values are stored in canonical text ([Domain values](#domain-values)).
The reference backends list such items in other orders, which also differ between maps (the pinned LLVM backend lists the map above as `10 -1 2`, and `keys_list(10 |-> 1 2 |-> 2 I |-> 3)` with `I` bound to `-1` by the rule that builds it as `-1 2 10`), so a differential comparison of a listing's order compares two choices, not a fact.

Everything else is not an execution and computes only consequences of the definition: a verdict that read k-rust's order would hold in some models of the definition and not in others.
There `keys_list`, `values` and `choice` of a closed map of two or more bindings whose keys are constructor-like, and `Set2List` and `choice` of a closed set of two or more constructor-like elements, stay unevaluated.
Such an application is defined exactly when its collection is (`UnsupportedHookReason::Unspecified` with `defined: true`): a map with distinct constructor-like keys is defined when its values are, and then has a listing and, being non-empty, a key to choose, so the `\ceil` of the partial `keys_list`, `values` and `choice` is the definedness of the map's values (`\top` for constructor-like values and element variables), and the step that writes one has a successor wherever the map is defined.
It is not an unsupported hook, and no `UnsupportedHookUnevaluated` warning is emitted for it, since the outcome is the intended one.
A symbolic collection, which no evaluator lists in either mode, is unchanged.
So `kprove` proves neither `<k> -1 => ListItem(-1) ListItem(10) ListItem(2) </k>` nor `<k> -1 => ListItem(-1) ListItem(2) ListItem(10) </k>` over `rule I:Int => keys_list(10 |-> 1 2 |-> 2 I |-> 3)`, and disproves neither (a leaf holding the unevaluated listing is not certified, [Proof verdicts](#proof-verdicts)), also when the order is read in a rule's or an equation's `requires`, while `<k> -1 => ?L:List </k>` is proven.
Likewise `kprove` neither proves nor disproves that `choice(1 |-> 10 2 |-> 20)` is `1`, nor that it is `2`, nor which element `choice(SetItem(1) SetItem(2))` is, on a right-hand side or in a rule's or an equation's `requires`. It proves a claim that needs only that `choice`'s result exists, such as `<k> pickr(1 |-> 10 2 |-> 20) => res(?K) </k>` over `rule pickr(M) => res(choice(M))` and its set form, with no `undefined step` leaf; a claim that constrains the chosen item, even one true of every item (`ensures ?K ==K 1 orBool ?K ==K 2`), is not proven. (`choice_is_the_first_key_keys_list_lists` and `choice_is_the_first_element_set2list_lists` in [map.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/map.rs) and [set.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/set.rs), the `choice` models in [hook_models](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/hook_models), `krun_evaluates_choice_as_the_first_listed_key`, `krun_evaluates_choice_in_rule_and_equation_conditions`, `kprove_proves_no_claim_that_depends_on_choice` and `kprove_proves_an_order_free_claim_over_choice` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs).)
The cost is completeness: a claim true in every order but proven only by evaluating a listing is no longer proven, such as `<k> -1 => ?L:List </k> ensures size(?L) ==Int 3`, since `size` of the unevaluated listing is not computed (the size and the membership of an item are determined by the collection, and deciding them without an order is not implemented).
A partial function over an unevaluated listing or `choice` (`keys_list(M)[0]`, `M[choice(M)]`, an `owise` equation of a function that matches on the listing's first item) is neither evaluated nor known to be defined, so a step that writes it has an undefined remainder, and a claim through it fails with an `undefined step` leaf rather than being proven or disproved. So the fold that takes `M[choice(M)]` and removes `choice(M)` until the map is empty is not proven to give the sum of the map's values, although every choice gives that sum; an execution computes it.

A Float operation over a binary32 and a binary64 operand (`+Float`, `-Float`, `*Float`, `/Float`, `minFloat`, `maxFloat`) is the exact result rounded to a destination format `domains.md` does not name, so its value is one of several the definition admits too.
An execution rounds it to binary64 ([Backend scope](#backend-scope)); every other evaluation leaves the ground application unevaluated as a defined value (`Unspecified` with `defined: true`, no warning): every model rounds the exact result to some format, so it exists and only its value is open.
So `kprove` proves `<k> div(0.1f, 2.0) => ?F:Float </k>` over `rule div(X, Y) => X /Float Y`, and neither proves nor disproves `<k> div(0.1f, 2.0) => 5.0000000745058060e-02p53x11 </k>` or a claim that `precisionFloat(0.1f +Float 0.2)` is 53; a claim true in every model that needs the value, such as `isNaN(0.1f +Float 0.2) ==Bool false`, is not proven either.
The operands are read as the evaluator reads them (`float::mixed_destination`), so every spelling of either format (`0.1f`, `0.1p24x8`, `0.1p024x008`; `2.0`, `2.0d`, `2.0p53x11`) is withheld alike, and an operation over one format, or a comparison over two, is evaluated in every mode.

`signFloat(F)` is "the value of the sign bit" of `F`, a value of an IEEE-like format, in which every value has a sign bit and there are NaNs of both signs; `domains.md` does not say which the `NaN` literal has. So `signFloat` of a NaN has a `Bool` value in every model, but which one is open.
An execution answers `false`; every other evaluation leaves the application unevaluated as a defined value (`Unspecified` with `defined: true`, no warning), in every format (`NaN`, `NaNf`, `NaNp113x15`), and the constant folder leaves it unfolded, since its output serves both.
So `kprove` proves neither `<k> sg(NaN) => r(false) </k>` nor `<k> sg(NaN) => r(true) </k>` over `rule sg(X) => r(signFloat(X))`, and proves `<k> sg(NaN) => r(?_B:Bool) </k>` and `<k> sg(-0.0) => r(true) </k>`: the sign of every other value, zeros and infinities included, is its sign bit and is evaluated in every mode (`float::nan_sign`; the [NaN sign tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/nan_sign.rs), `kprove_proves_no_claim_that_depends_on_the_sign_of_a_nan` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).

An application left unevaluated in this way is taken as defined (`Unspecified` with `defined: true`) because the hook's own description gives it a value and leaves open only which one: a defined collection has a listing and, when non-empty, an item; a Float operation has a result in some format; a NaN has a sign bit.
That holds whatever the symbol's declaration says, so a user function hooked `MAP.keys_list`, `FLOAT.add` or `FLOAT.sign` is read the same way with or without `total`.
It is a different case from a hook whose description gives no value on some arguments (`Int2MInt` and `bitwidthMInt` at a sort that names no width, `String2Token` of a text that names no element of its sort, [Hook values at the edges of their domains](#hook-values-at-the-edges-of-their-domains)): there only a `total` declaration states that a value exists, so the application is taken as defined exactly when its symbol is declared `total`, and no proof assumes the definedness of an unevaluated application that neither its hook's description nor its declaration entails.

The mode belongs to the request (`ExecutionOptions::evaluation` and `SearchOptions::evaluation`, `Proof` by default), and every evaluation the request runs sees it, its rule and equation conditions and its `\ceil` obligations included ([`evaluation`](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/evaluation.rs)).
- Execution: `krun` (`--search*` included, with the exit code and the `--pattern` match), `kore-exec`, the library's `Backend::execute*`, `search*`, `search_paths*` and `search_pattern*`, and the Node.js and WebAssembly `execute` and `search` that call them.
- Proof: `kprove`, `kore-simplify`, `kore-implies`, `kore-get-model` and `kore-match-disjunction`; `Backend::prove`, `simplify_kore`, `implies_kore`, `get_model` and their bindings; the RPC `simplify`, `implies` and `get-model`; the RPC `execute` unless its request asks for execution; the backend's own `execute*` and `search*` functions unless their options do; and every analysis at load, whose value claims executions also use (a claim withdrawn because it needed a listing is withdrawn for executions too, which only loses evaluation).
- An effect observer passed to the backend's `execute*` and `search*` functions runs in `Proof`: it is the caller's code, not part of the run.
- The facade's proof routes (`Backend::prove`, the `implies*`, `simplify*` and model functions, `Backend::match_patterns` behind `kore-match-disjunction`, `kprove`, and the RPC `simplify`, `implies` and `get-model`) open a `Proof` scope themselves, and so does every definition load, whose analyses proofs read; they stay proof routes when a library caller runs them inside an `Execution` scope (`k_rust_backend::evaluation::with_evaluation` or `EvaluationScope`, which are meant only for running programs; scopes are dropped innermost first, and the guard is not `Send`).

For library clients this is a source change: `ExecutionOptions` and `SearchOptions` have the new public field `evaluation`, so a struct literal that names every field without `..Default::default()` no longer compiles, and one that takes the rest from `Default` now runs in `Proof` and must set `evaluation: EvaluationMode::Execution` to keep listing collections; `UnsupportedHookReason::Unspecified` has the new field `defined`, so a pattern `Unspecified { detail }` must become `Unspecified { detail, .. }`.
The option structs are not `#[non_exhaustive]`, since that would forbid the struct literals with `..Default::default()` that callers and tests build them with.

The RPC `execute` request takes the k-rust parameter `"evaluation-mode"`: `"proof"` (the default) or `"execution"`.
`execute` is the step primitive of external provers, which decide a claim by `implies` on the states it returns, so by default its states are consequences of the definition; with `"execution"` it lists items in k-rust's order and rounds a Float operation over a binary32 and a binary64 operand to binary64, and its states then must not feed a proof.
The library's `Backend::execute` and `Backend::search` are executions although they expose the same cut-point, terminal-rule, stop-at-branch and depth controls: they run programs, and a prover built on them gets states relative to k-rust's order, from which it must not conclude a claim; `Backend::prove` is the proof route.

The tests are `kprove_proves_no_claim_that_depends_on_the_order_of_keys_list`, `kprove_proves_no_claim_that_depends_on_the_order_in_conditions`, `kprove_proves_no_claim_that_depends_on_the_order_of_set2list`, `kprove_proves_an_order_free_claim_over_keys_list`, `kprove_loses_the_size_of_a_gated_listing`, `kprove_takes_a_listing_of_a_map_with_symbolic_values_as_defined_as_the_map`, `krun_lists_keys_in_the_documented_order` and `krun_keeps_the_order_in_rule_and_equation_conditions` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), `backend_execute_and_search_keep_the_order_in_conditions`, `backend_simplify_stays_a_proof_route_inside_an_execution_scope` and `backend_match_patterns_stays_a_proof_route_inside_an_execution_scope` in [execution_semantics.rs], `a_proof_inside_an_execution_scope_reads_no_order_the_definition_leaves_open` in [proof_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs)(../crates/k-rust/tests/execution_semantics.rs), `execute_evaluates_an_order_the_definition_leaves_open_only_on_request` in [rpc.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/rpc.rs), and the `evaluation_mode` [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend).

## ECDSA recovery on invalid input

`KRYPTO.ecdsaRecover` and `SECP256K1.ecdsaRecover` share one evaluator in [krypto.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/builtin/krypto.rs).
Their hook contract is not in `domains.md`: [`plugin/krypto.md` at blockchain-k-plugin `207ae512`](https://github.com/runtimeverification/blockchain-k-plugin/blob/207ae5121e5178a09742ed746f2d15e34b1750cc/plugin/krypto.md), the revision the [hook capability inventory](../crates/k-rust/tests/fixtures/hook-capabilities.toml) records, declares `ECDSARecover(Bytes, Int, Bytes, Bytes)` as a `function` of sort `Bytes`.
It documents only the successful result, the 64-byte public key that signed a 32-byte message hash with the given `v`, `r` and `s`, and refers to the Ethereum signature form for their meaning; it says nothing about other inputs.

For concrete arguments Rust defines:

- the domain: a 32-byte message hash, `r` and `s` as 32-byte big-endian scalars that form a valid secp256k1 signature, and `v` equal to 27 or 28, which in that signature form encodes the recovery parity as `v - 27`;
- on the domain, the recovered key as its 64 coordinate bytes (the uncompressed SEC1 encoding without its tag byte);
- for every other concrete input, and for an in-domain signature from which no key can be recovered, the empty `Bytes` value.

An application with a non-concrete argument remains unevaluated.

Rust reads the byte-string arguments as fields of fixed width, not as integers of any length.
The hook documentation specifies the message hash as a 32-byte string, and the plugin's own signature form, the result of `ECDSASign`, is 65 bytes in `[r,s,v]` order, so `r` and `s` are 32-byte fields.
A `Bytes` value of another length is a different value that a definition can tell apart (by its length, for one), so Rust does not treat it as that field's 32-byte encoding with zero bytes added or removed.
`v` is restricted to the two values the referenced signature form uses; 29 to 34, which name a recovery with the group order added to `r` or a compressed key, are not part of that form.

The declaration allows either choice outside the documented domain: a `function` without `total` has at most one value ([`docs/user_manual.md`, "`function` and `total` attributes"](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/blob/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/docs/user_manual.md#function-and-total-attributes)), so both `\bottom` and a single `Bytes` value are consistent with it.
Rust returns a value because a failed recovery must stay observable.
Every recovered key has exactly 64 bytes, so the empty value cannot be mistaken for a key, and a definition can branch on it with ordinary `Bytes` operations.
A `\bottom` result would make every configuration that applies the hook to such an input denote no state: that execution path would vanish from execution and search results, and a claim over it would hold vacuously instead of checking how the definition handles a malformed signature.
Ending the run with an error is not a result either: the inputs are ordinary values of the declared argument sorts, and the unsupported-hook error of [Backend scope](#backend-scope) is for hooks without an evaluator.

The fact this diverges from: Booster has no evaluator for the hook, and the pinned Kore evaluator (`evalECDSARecover` in `kore/src/Kore/Builtin/Krypto.hs`) has no failure value and a wider domain than Rust's.
It reads the hash, `r` and `s` as unsigned big-endian integers of any length, accepts `v` from 27 to 34 (the assertion at `:313`), does not check `r` and `s` against the group order, and appends zero bytes to whatever it encodes up to 64 bytes (`:295`, `:300-304`).
On some inputs outside Rust's domain it therefore returns a value where Rust returns empty `Bytes`: a valid signature whose hash, `r` or `s` has the same integer value in fewer or more than 32 bytes recovers the same key, and by the code a `v` of 29 or 30 recovers a key when `r` plus the group order is still the x-coordinate of a curve point.
On other invalid inputs it ends the backend process, through one of its assertions (`:313`, `:327-331`) or, when recovery yields the point at infinity, the `error` at `:414`; a `v` from 31 to 34 always fails the assertion at `:330`, because the recovery index `v - 27` then adds at least twice the group order to `r`, which exceeds the field prime.
The pinned toolchain thus differs from Rust by a value on part of the invalid inputs and supplies no value on the rest, and the differential manifest's `ecdsa-invalid-execution` entry is a `local-gate` exclusion.
The CLI test `krun_executes_invalid_ecdsa_recovery_to_empty_bytes` pins the terminal KORE for [`ecdsa-invalid.crypto`](../crates/k-rust/tests/fixtures/reference/ecdsa-invalid.crypto), and the unit test `invalid_concrete_recoveries_return_empty_bytes` in `krypto.rs` covers each boundary of the domain.

## RPC behavior

A predicate-free `get-model` request returns `Unknown` without a substitution.
`get-model` first simplifies the predicate, retaining any open definedness constraints.
Truth returns `Sat` without a substitution because every valuation satisfies it; an unconstrained variable is absent from the substitution.
False returns `Unsat`.
When the residual abstracts symbols or sorts, `get-model` returns `Unknown` without a substitution for a solver `Sat` answer.
An uninterpreted function keeps congruence but need not satisfy its K definition, so the solver's model need not be a K valuation.
The `bounded-search` model exception records a distinct-constructor result where the oracle reports `Sat`; `start = done` is `Unsat` because distinct constructors cannot denote the same value.
Both `booster/library/Booster/JsonRpc.hs` and `kore/src/Kore/JsonRpc.hs` contain this no-predicate outcome.
A proxy response of `sat` must not override this contract without establishing whether the proxy classified the same input as carrying a predicate or definedness obligation.

A `cancel` request inside a batch returns `-32601`, `Cancel not supported`, following the shipped proxy; an empty batch returns `-32600` with data `[]`.
The older server API document is not authoritative for these measured wire details.
Execution retains an explicit `aborted` reason for incomplete indeterminate, simplification-error and breadth-bound outcomes because Rust has no fallback engine to hide the failure.
[RPC tests](../crates/k-rust/src/rpc.rs) cover predicate-free models, batch cancellation, and error classification; [RPC fixtures](../crates/k-rust/tests/fixtures/reference/rpc) preserve shipped-proxy responses.
An `execute` response lists `next-states` in application order with the remainder last; the order is not part of the contract and the differential gate compares the array as a multiset (N27).
Backend error `data` is compared by class: code, message, and the `error` sentence; context lines are the port's own diagnostics (N28).
A `rule-substitution` binds the applied rule's variables, which k-rust prints with their escaped `Rule#` marker (`Rule-hX`, see [variable names](#variable-names)) so that the predicate keeps them apart from state variables of the same name; the gate compares a rule variable by its name in the definition, whatever marker spelling an engine prints (N40).
Only a request variable spelled like that escape, such as one named `Rule-hX`, prints like a rule variable; when a variable of the request, of the next state or of a bound value does, the rule variable is renamed apart to `Rule#X!apart{N}` (`Rule-hX-bapart0`), whose rule name is still the part before the `!` (`project_rule_substitution` in [rpc.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/rpc.rs)).
A parameter object with a key the method does not declare is rejected with `-32602`, `Invalid params`, and is not executed: the server cannot apply that key, and the result it would return might differ from the one requested, which is the reason for the [CLI-scope](#cli-scope) rule on unknown flags.
An `implies` request is the statement `A -> \exists E. C` under the universal closure of the free variables of `A` and `C`, with `E` the consequent's leading existentials.
A free variable of the consequent that the antecedent does not mention is therefore universal, not an error: the match binding `u := s` of any universal is an obligation `u = s` that the antecedent's condition must entail.
The universal ranges over the values of its sort in the models of the definition, so the answer is `valid` when every such value the antecedent allows satisfies the obligation: when the antecedent is unsatisfiable, when nothing constrains the variable, or when the definition's no-junk axiom for the variable's sort (its `constructor` axiom) leaves `s` as the only value, as for a sort whose one constructor is nullary.
The solver treats a user sort as uninterpreted and does not see the no-junk axiom, so in that last case it reports a counterexample; when the solver does not answer `valid`, the check simplifies `A /\ u =/= s`, and if `u =/= s` excludes every constructor the axiom lists, that conjunction is a contradiction and the answer is `valid`.
Otherwise a value of `u` that violates the obligation may exist; a sort without a no-junk axiom has such values even if the definition declares a single constructor for it.
The answer is `invalid` only when such a value is shown (below): for a universal of sort `Int` or `Bool` whose counterexample the solver finds on an exact query; for a universal of another sort the solver's counterexample ranges over an uninterpreted sort, so the answer is `indeterminate` with the binding as its condition.
An antecedent existential that shares the consequent universal's name is a different variable and is renamed apart.
The RPC differential records this as the `bounded-search` `implies-consequent-universal` oracle exception (N19): with the ground start configuration as antecedent and its `<k>` item replaced by `X:SortState` as consequent, k-rust answers `indeterminate` with the binding `X = start` as its condition, and the pinned proxy answers error code 4, `Implication check error`, "The RHS must not have free variables not present in the LHS".

`invalid` asserts an instance of the antecedent outside the consequent, so the `implies` method, `krust kore-implies` and `Backend::implies` answer it only where that instance is shown ([`refutation_result`](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/implication.rs)).
A refutation of every instance (a term mismatch, a `\bottom` consequent, or obligations the solver answers `Unsat` under the antecedent) needs an instance of the antecedent; a counterexample (the solver answers both the obligations and their negation satisfiable) needs an instance of the antecedent with the negated obligations.
Such an instance is shown when the antecedent's term contains no conjunction of terms, its constraints, the negated obligations where required, and the term's definedness are true syntactically or satisfiable by a solver query whose translation is exact (`smt::translation_exactness`), and every free variable has a sort the definition shows inhabited.
A pattern with a free variable denotes nothing when the variable's sort has no value; the inhabited sorts are the least fixed point of hooked, domain-value and collection sorts, result sorts of constructors and total functions whose argument sorts are inhabited, and supersorts of inhabited sorts.
A `\not C` consequent is decided on the conjunction of both sides: `A -> \not C` is `valid` when the terms of `A` and `C` do not unify or the unified constraints, with the definedness of the unified term and bindings, are unsatisfiable or simplify to `false`, `invalid` when the unified pattern is shown to have an instance as above, and `indeterminate` otherwise, including for `\exists E. \not C`.
A solver `Unsat` refutes the query, whose predicates are definedness-explicit except in the gaps [Solver decisions over partial operations](#solver-decisions-over-partial-operations) names, but a `Sat` for a query with an uninterpreted `smtlib` function, an uninterpreted sort or a partial function made total may come from a model that is no K valuation, and then the antecedent may be empty and the implication vacuously valid.
An antecedent with no instance implies every consequent, whatever its term: when no instance outside the consequent is shown, the check decides whether the antecedent is empty before it answers, from its constraints together with the definedness of its term and of their atoms, simplified and then given to the solver; `false` or `Unsat` answers `valid` with the condition `false`.
A remainder leaf such as `<m> (1 |-> 2) M </m>` under `1 in_keys(M)`, whose term is undefined wherever its constraint holds, is therefore `valid` against any consequent, including one whose term it does not match and `\bottom`.
An emptiness neither the simplifier nor the solver shows is not assumed: an answer with no instance shown and no emptiness shown, and a remainder obligation the solver leaves undecided, is `indeterminate` (`unknown` on the CLI and the bindings) with the condition it would otherwise carry.

An `implies` response reports its antecedent and consequent after simplification, the patterns the verdict was decided on ([`simplified_implication_response_syntax`](../crates/k-rust/src/rpc.rs)); a simplification failure is reported as a `simplify` fault rather than as an unsimplified pattern beside a verdict computed from the simplified one.
Simplification replaces a pattern by an equal one, so the payload denotes the requested implication.
The pinned Booster proxy echoes the request's patterns instead; for the IMP request that is an unevaluated `initGeneratedTopCell` application where k-rust reports the configuration it evaluates to.
The `rpc.imp` `oracle-exception` records that difference with `equivalence = "simplified-implication"`, and N19 checks the claimed equality rather than asserting it: the two responses must be equal outside the payload, and both payloads' antecedents and consequents, simplified by `krust kore-simplify` against the reference definition, must print identically.
Like C8, that evidence depends on the port's simplifier.

The `haskell-logging` parameter selects diagnostic entries; it is not an instruction to the computation.
It decides only whether `haskell-log-entries` is attached (for a non-empty list) and which entries it holds; no other result field depends on it.
A name is matched exactly and case-sensitively against a fixed set of names per entry, not against the entry's `context` array (whose segments are lowercase).
Every method returns one proxy entry, first in the list when selected; it is selected by `Proxy` or by its method name: `Execute`, `Simplify`, `Implies`, `AddModule` or `GetModel`.
For `execute`, each step of the trace yields a success entry selected by `Booster`, `Execute`, `Success`, or its kind: `Rewrite` (a rewrite or claim step), `Simplification` or `Remainder`.
A run that halts stuck, indeterminate or on a simplification error yields one failure entry, selected by `Booster`, `Execute` or `Failure`, and also by `Indeterminate` or `Abort` when the halt is indeterminate.
`Rewrite` does not select the failure entry, even when its `context` names the rule in a `rewrite` element.
Any other name selects no entry, in the same way that `Failure` selects none on a run in which no rewrite failed: an empty selection is the answer to the query, not a dropped request.
The CLI-scope rule that an unknown flag must not be silently ignored therefore does not apply: that rule prevents an operation from returning a result that an unapplied option would have changed, and a `haskell-logging` name changes no result field other than `haskell-log-entries`.
Because the emitted names are listed here, an empty selection for a name outside the list carries no information about the event that name denotes elsewhere.
A `haskell-logging` value that is not an array of strings fails parameter decoding and is rejected with `-32602`, `Invalid params`.

## Definition verification

### Sort injections at load

`inj{A, B}` denotes the embedding of `A` in `B` licensed by a chain of declared subsort axioms; a closed pair outside that order denotes no licensed value.
The backend therefore checks sort injections when it loads a definition or an added module.
An axiom that writes an unlicensed closed injection in a rewrite or equation right-hand side, an `ensures` clause, or a `requires` clause outside a membership's pattern operand is refused with `UnlicensedInjection`, naming the source sort, target sort and axiom.
An unlicensed injection in a matched position makes that alternative dead: rewrite left-hand alternatives, simplification left-hand sides, function argument binder alternatives and ceil arguments containing one are not loaded.
No licensed value can match such an alternative, and dropping it also prevents unification from binding a variable to an unlicensed value.
Within an axiom's condition (outside a predicate simplification's left-hand side, where the axiom is dead), membership `\in(X, P)` is false when its pattern operand `P` contains an unlicensed closed injection, because no licensed value belongs to that pattern.
A sort-variable pair is accepted inside a definition axiom as a schema, including the injection composition axiom whose licensed instances follow from transitivity.

Requests and claims must contain only licensed injections; krun's initial configuration is checked like a request.
`inj{A, B}(t)` is an application whose argument sort is `A`, so its operand must have sort `A` exactly, as every argument has its declared sort: an operand of another sort, a strict subsort of `A` included, is refused with `IncorrectArgumentSort` on every route, verified (definitions, added modules) or not (RPC requests), and the composed `inj{C, B}(t)` is the way to write an operand of a subsort `C` (`internalization_refuses_what_the_verifier_refuses_for_its_sorts` in [sort_injections.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/sort_injections.rs)).
Requests and claims also refuse injection pairs containing sort variables.
An unlicensed alternative of a no-junk axiom prevents that axiom from establishing a finite domain.
A dead axiom remains in the written rule catalog with its executable attribute, but contributes no loaded rule and cannot be selected in an observation allowlist (`UnknownRule`).
These load checks license closed injection pairs in loaded patterns, and rewriting preserves that condition by substituting licensed values and composing licensed embeddings.
Instances of sort-parametric axioms remain the residual: they are not checked closed at load.
Applicable instances arise only from the injection composition schema, whose instances are licensed by transitivity; sort-parametric equations do not match closed subjects.

### Priority conjuncts in rewrite left-hand sides

A rewrite left-hand-side component `\not(A{}())`, with `A` an alias without parameters that denotes a set of configurations, states that the rule applies only outside what `A` denotes.
`A` denotes a set of configurations when its body, read with every application of an alias without parameters replaced by that alias's body (an application denotes what the body denotes), has a term pattern in predicate position or is `\bottom` (nested `\or` of `\bottom`s), the empty set; an application of an alias with parameters counts as a term pattern whatever its body.
An alias without parameters whose body is a predicate other than `\bottom`, directly or through further aliases without parameters, is not such a component: negated, it is a condition like any other and is read as one.
For `\bottom`, the empty set and the false predicate, both readings of the negation are `\top`; it is taken as a component, which is discharged, so the rules an empty priority group precedes are named without it.
In either strategy a rule of priority `p` is offered only subjects to which no executable rule of smaller priority applies, through any of its matches (a symbolic `any` step blocks the lower priorities by every collection candidate and split case of a higher rule, not only the one whose successor it follows first), so the component holds on every subject the rule is offered when everything `A` denotes is a configuration where such a rule applies.
A non-executable rule is offered no subject, so its components are not checked.
The backend shows this at load, from the written definition: each disjunct of `A` (nested `\or` flattened, `\bottom`s discarded) either names an executable, non-sort-parametric rewrite axiom (it is `\exists` of exactly its free variables over that rule's left-hand-side components, priority conjuncts and `\top`s removed, equal up to a renaming of variables) or applies another such alias, and the highest priority reached is strictly below `p`.
A component shown to hold is replaced by `\top` before aliases are unfolded, so `A` is never unfolded and the rule is the rule written without it (`BackendDefinition::axioms()` shows the `\top`).
One that is not shown to hold is refused with `PriorityConjunct`, naming the axiom, the alias and the disjunct path that fails or the rule whose priority is not below the carrier's (`NotStrictlyHigher`).
The backend has no predicate "the subject is not in this set of configurations", so it cannot keep such a component as a condition: read as one, it is an ill-sorted condition that is never decided, which either leaves a stuck branch beside the rule's result or stays, as a negated configuration, in the path condition of every result it reaches.
On a 15 MB EVM definition whose 406 rules carry such components (at priorities 40, 50 and 200, naming three nested aliases), all are discharged; ten steps from a symbolic configuration give byte for byte the result of the same definition with each component written as `\top`, with the same solver checks (15, where reading them as conditions made 646 and left them undecided in both result branches).
For the same reason any other `\not(φ)` component of a rewrite left-hand side with a term pattern in predicate position of `φ` (reached through `\not`, `\and`, `\or`, `\implies`, `\iff`, `\exists`, `\forall`) is refused with `NegatedConfiguration` after alias unfolding; negated predicates such as `\not(\equals(..))` are unaffected.
The check is sufficient, not complete: a definition whose priority aliases are written another way, or name rules by an instance of their left-hand sides, is refused rather than misread ([priority_conjunct.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/priority_conjunct.rs)).

### SMT prelude consistency

The backend builds the SMT prelude when it selects a definition, but it may defer checking whether that prelude is satisfiable until an SMT answer depends on consistency.
Every SMT query includes the whole prelude, so a `sat` answer establishes its consistency without a separate check.
Before using the first `unsat` answer, the backend checks the prelude alone; an `unsat` or `unknown` result from that check fails the operation.
When that check fails, the backend interrupts the active rewrite, search, or proof at its next cancellation point and reports the prelude error; later requests using the same solver report that error at entry.
An `unknown` query answer establishes neither consistency nor inconsistency and does not trigger the prelude check.
This is sound because the prelude constrains SMT answers only: an operation that uses no SMT answer has no result derived from it.

A zero-query run can therefore succeed with an inconsistent prelude.
An RPC server can start with that prelude and report the error on its first dependent request instead of at startup.
With `--io on`, output produced before the first dependent answer can be visible before the error.

The acceptance boundary is the theory of the selected main module, meaning that module and its transitive imports; within it, the boundary includes the sentence, declaration and pattern conditions of KORE validity as the KORE language specification states them, [`docs/kore-syntax.md`, "Validity"](https://github.com/runtimeverification/haskell-backend/blob/ad54c7a55085b726c4d3c2728242a7e0695b0439/docs/kore-syntax.md#validity).
Among its conditions, every sort, symbol and alias an axiom uses is declared, each application agrees with its declaration in sort parameters, arity and argument sorts, and each bound variable agrees in sort with its binder.
It does not include the two module-order conditions on import sentences: an imported module need not appear earlier in the definition (condition 6a), and an import need not precede the other declarations of its module (6b).
Modules are resolved by name, so a definition that violates either condition is accepted and means the same theory as its reordered form.
The reason: imports determine which declarations are in scope, and textual order adds nothing to that scope, as the specification already states for the non-import sentences of a module.
The part of 6a that a topological order implies, that imports form no cycle, is still enforced for the main module's import closure.
Modules outside the main module's import closure are verified only for unique module names and unique sort, symbol and alias names across the whole definition; their sentences are not otherwise checked, so an ill-formed axiom in an unimported module does not stop the definition from loading.
The reason: `-m` selects the theory to execute, and a module outside its import closure contributes no sentence to that theory.
The main module's theory is a matching-logic theory, and every axiom of the modules in its closure belongs to it whether or not the backend later classifies that axiom as a rewrite, an equation or an attribute axiom.
An axiom that is not a valid pattern has no meaning, so a closure containing one does not define a theory, even when execution would never consult that axiom.
Verification therefore checks every sentence of the modules in the closure before classification, including axioms that classification ignores.
This diverges from Booster, which drops some axiom shapes without internalizing them (a `simplification` axiom whose left-hand side is not an application, and a `functional` or `total` existential; `booster/library/Booster/Syntax/ParsedKore/Internalise.hs:629-630,636-641`), so an ill-formed axiom of those shapes does not stop Booster from loading the definition.
Verification also rejects `\dv{S}` on a sort `S` declared without `hasDomainValues`, a condition the specification's Validity list does not state.
Its reason: a `\dv{S}` pattern names a domain value of `S`, and `hasDomainValues` is the declaration that `S` has domain values, so a domain value of a sort declared without it denotes nothing.
The verifier enforces further conditions beyond the specification (among them `BOOL.Bool` literals, subsorts of sorts with domain values, function heads and constructor result sorts, attributes, and claim right-hand-side variables); they are not yet justified in this section.
A subsort axiom `subsort{S, S}` states `S <= S`.
The subsort order is the reflexive-transitive closure of the declared pairs, so it contains `S <= S` for every sort even without a subsort axiom, and the axiom `\exists V:S. V = inj{S, S}(W:S)` is a valid pattern.
A reflexive subsort declaration therefore adds nothing to the order and is not rejected for being reflexive; the other subsort conditions still apply, so `subsort{S, S}` on a sort with `hasDomainValues` is rejected because the verifier does not accept subsorts of a sort with domain values.

The order is over closed sorts: `subsort{SortMInt{Sort8{}}, SortKResult{}}` places `MInt{8}` below `KResult` and says nothing about `MInt{16}`.
No pair is derived from a sort's arguments: `A <= B` does not place `S{A}` below `S{B}`, because no axiom makes a sort constructor monotone.
A sort that mentions a sort variable is below another only if every instance is; the backend answers no when no instance of its head is below the other's head, and otherwise reports the variable.
A collection sort (one with `unit`, `element` and `concat` attributes) has no sort parameters, because those attributes name one symbol each and so describe one sort.
Such a declaration with parameters is rejected at load.

The [definition fixture index](../crates/k-rust/tests/fixtures/reference/definition/index.toml) records pinned verification outcomes and diagnostic fragments.

## Search results

Search output uses deterministic structural KORE order; reproducing Kore's internal `MultiOr` ordering is not a compatibility requirement.
The order is `k-rust-kore`'s `Pattern` order (variant declaration rank, then fields in declaration order, byte-wise strings) and may change between releases.
Differential gates compare disjunctions as multisets so ordering is ignored while multiplicity is still checked.
For `--bound N`, selected results must be distinct members of the same query's unbounded solution set, up to the bound; which members are selected is unspecified.
The CLI test `krun_search_bound_returns_a_subset_of_the_unbounded_solutions` checks this property against the port's own unbounded search.
A state, a solution (a match of the target), and a configuration of a state-set execution among leaves of equal halt meaning (see below) is reported once up to the names of the variables the request does not name.
Such a variable is quantified over its own result (see [Result variables](#result-variables)), so two results that differ only by an injective renaming of those variables, with the request's names fixed, denote the same configurations or the same instances of the target; reporting both would take a `--bound` slot from a different result.
The comparison (`RequestNames::state_key` and `RequestNames::solution_key` in [fresh.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/fresh.rs)) renames those variables by first occurrence and compares the constraints as a set.
Map and set elements and the constraints are visited in the order of a label that sees a renamed variable only through its kind, its sort and the number it already has, so elements that differ only in such names wait until the numbers their variables receive elsewhere (a list, a cell) tell them apart.
Where nothing tells them apart, the comparison tries each variable of the first such group as the next one numbered, skipping one whose exchange with a tried one leaves the result unchanged, and keeps the least renamed result, so every renaming of a result gets one key.
That search stops after 32 complete numberings; the rest of the numbering then follows stored order, which compares names, so two renamings of one such result can both be reported.
Two different results are never merged.
Two leaves of a state-set execution are also one state only when their committed effects and their consoles agree: a console is compared as a process observes it, by the number of input bytes consumed and the bytes written, in order, to each descriptor, not by the hooks or calls that wrote them, so a path that writes `ab` with one `#write` and one that writes it with two `#putc` (or with an extra empty `#write`) end in one leaf, while writes to two descriptors in different orders remain two (`state_set_merges_leaves_whose_console_writes_the_same_bytes_by_different_hooks`, `state_set_merges_leaves_that_differ_only_by_an_empty_write` and `state_set_keeps_leaves_whose_interleaving_differs` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), `ExecutionIoState::observable` in [transition.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/transition.rs)).
A path-set execution reports both paths.
The leaf a state-set execution reports for merged paths is the first of them, with its own trace and halt reason, and carries the diagnostics of every merged path, so a state-set result is without diagnostics exactly when the path-set result of the same request is.
A leaf's halt can mean more than its configuration, so a leaf is merged only into an equal leaf that means everything it means: the leaf of a stop that ended the run (`cancelled`, or the work budget's `work-bound`) also stands for the pending states the run dropped and is never merged; a depth-bound, breadth-bound, timeout or stack-exhaustion leaf, whose configuration's subtree was not explored, is merged only into an earlier leaf whose halt its configuration determines or that is itself such a bound or stop leaf; and a terminal-rule leaf, whose halt records the rule by which its path arrived (the same configuration reached by another rule may have successors), says the configuration is final, so it absorbs and is absorbed by only stuck and terminal-rule leaves, and is kept beside a branching, cut-point, undecided, failed, bound or stop leaf of its configuration. A state-set result can therefore show one configuration more than once, each leaf with a different meaning (`a_state_set_merge_keeps_the_leaf_of_a_stop_that_ended_the_run` and `a_state_set_merge_keeps_bound_and_run_ending_leaves_a_terminal_or_earlier_leaf_cannot_stand_for` in [execute.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/rewrite/execute.rs), `a_terminal_leaf_does_not_absorb_a_bound_leaf` and `a_terminal_leaf_is_absorbed_only_by_a_leaf_final_in_its_own_right` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend)).
A merged diagnostic names variables as the path that met it named them, so a variable the request does not name may not occur in the leaf's state (names minted after a fork are distinct across paths, so it never names one of the leaf's own variables), and diagnostics equal up to such names are each listed.
The pinned reference's LLVM `krun --search` writes the console output of every branch it explores to the one process stream and reports states regardless of what they wrote: on a definition whose two branches write `ab`, one by `#write` and one by two `#putc`, before reaching one configuration, it prints `abab` and one solution.
k-rust keeps the console per path and as part of a state's identity, so a reported state carries the output of a path that reaches it.
A target pattern that matches one state in several ways (a map or set pattern, cell instances included, whose key, element or instance the rest of the pattern does not fix: `<rs> <r> <rid> I </rid> <v> V </v> </r> ... </rs>` on a state with three instances) denotes the disjunction of those ways, since an instance of the state is an instance of the target exactly when it is one under one of the substitutions; each way is therefore a solution of its own, of that one state, and a `--bound` counts them as solutions.
The ways are the collection solver's complete solutions of the pairs the match defers; a state whose collection holds an opaque part that a target entry could lie in, or a symbolic key that a key the target fixes could equal (`1 |-> V M` against `X |-> 10 2 |-> 20`), has no such complete set, and its match stays undecided (`a_search_pattern_that_matches_a_state_in_several_ways_reports_each_way` in [execution_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/execution_semantics.rs), `a_target_that_matches_a_subject_in_several_ways_matches_it_once_per_way` in [several_matchings.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/several_matchings.rs)).
The pinned reference `krun --search-final` reports one of the ways on that probe (`I = 2` among three instances, in a `Map` and a `Set`) and `#Bottom` for a target of two instances, which six ways match; k-rust reports every way, a recorded divergence: the reference's answer omits instances of the target that the final state has.
Which successor `--strategy any` follows among equal-priority rules is likewise unspecified; the differential gate checks that the port's any-strategy result is a member of the reference all-strategy set (N26).
The port follows the first applicable rule in priority order, then `definition.kore` declaration order (main module first, imports depth-first in their written order); which rule the reference follows is engine-internal.
Among several collection matches of that rule, the port follows the first candidate in deterministic structural order; which candidate the reference follows is engine-internal.
The RPC `next-states` array is a set of successors and is compared as a multiset (N27).
A search given no target pattern matches every reached configuration against one variable, spelled `VarResult` as reference krun spells its target, and reports each match as a binding of that variable.
That variable belongs to the request, so when a variable of the initial pattern is already spelled `VarResult` (for example the K variable `Result`), the target is `VarResult0`, or the next `VarResultN` no initial variable spells: sharing the name would identify the two variables in the match, or print one name at two sorts.

A depth-bounded execution result covers every path of every input instance up to the bound, so a configuration reached at the bound is a result whatever other branches did; the port keeps such a leaf when another branch halted, where the reference graph traversal drops it.
Normalization N17 limits the corresponding differential exception to marked depth-bounded cases and requires the reference leaves to remain a sub-multiset of the Rust leaves.
[Execution fixtures](../crates/k-rust/tests/fixtures/reference/execution) and the symbolic differential cover branch sets and depth cuts.

## Execution results

### Variable names

k-rust keeps a variable name read from KORE verbatim, and accepts only a KORE identifier there: `[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9'-]*` for an element variable, `@` followed by one for a set variable.
The textual and JSON readers cannot spell anything else; binary KORE and patterns handed over in process can, and the backend refuses such a name with `DefinitionError::VariableName`.
Every name the backend makes from another one adds a `#` provenance marker (`Rule#`, `Eq#`, `Ex#`) or a `!` counter (`X!0`, `X!apart1`, `X!binder0`), so no name read from a request or a definition is ever a rule variable or a mint of the backend, whatever it is spelled.

A name that is a KORE identifier prints unchanged.
Any other name is escaped, with `-` as the only escape character:

| Internal character | Printed form |
| --- | --- |
| letters, digits, `'`, a leading `@` | unchanged |
| `-` | `--` |
| `#` | `-h` |
| `!` | `-b` |
| `@` after the first position | `-a` |
| any other character | `-u`, its code point in lowercase hexadecimal, and `-` |

For example, `Ex#Frame!0` prints as `Ex-hFrame-b0`, `X!apart1` as `X-bapart1`, and `@Rule#M` as `@Rule-hM`.
The escape is injective, so two names the backend made never print alike, and two names read from KORE never do either.
A name read from KORE and an escaped name print alike only when the former is spelled like the escape of the latter, such as a query variable named `Ex-hFrame-b0` next to the mint `Ex#Frame!0`.
k-rust keeps such a pair out of every response: the names a rewrite mints skip every spelling of the request, and a printed binder whose spelling is the spelling of another variable free in its body is renamed to a `!binder` name (`apart_binders` in [externalize.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/externalize.rs)), so the printed pattern binds what the internal one binds.
An RPC `rule-substitution`, a predicate that equates rule variables with terms over the state's variables, renames a rule variable apart when a variable it can be equated with prints like it (see [RPC behavior](#rpc-behavior)); the host API's observation `bindings` keep each rule variable in a field of its own, apart from the value's variables.

An implication check renames each existential `X` of the consequent to a local name `X!exists{N}`, spelled like no variable of either side.
Its condition prints that name as `X`, the consequent's own binder, unless a free variable of either side or a leading existential of the antecedent is also named `X`: that is another variable the condition can mention, so the binding then keeps the escaped local name, such as `X-bexists0` (`ImplicationSides` in [implication.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/backend/implication.rs)).

Reading a printed result back gives the internal pattern up to a renaming of its escaped names into names of the new request: `Ex-hFrame-b0` is read as a variable of that name, not as the mint `Ex#Frame!0`.
That renaming is a bijection and the identity on the names the request already had, so identity decisions within one response, and across requests on the request's own names, are unaffected.
`term::names::decode_variable_name` inverts the escape for tools that classify printed names.
The contract is versioned by `term::names::VARIABLE_IDENTITY`, which is 3 since names are read verbatim; versions 1 and 2 decoded `-h` and `-b` back into the markers on reading, and its documentation in [names.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/term/names.rs) states what each version promises.

Printing a mint under the spelling of a query variable would identify two variables: map-selection narrowing mints `Ex#Frame!0`, and printed as `ExFrame0` next to a query frame named `ExFrame0`, a leaf stated `ExFrame0 = mapConcat(mapItem(ExKEY1,ExVALUE2), ExFrame0)` about what are two different variables.
The escaped spelling of generated names therefore differs from the spelling the reference engines print (normalizations N4 and C7), and pattern digests, which hash the printed KORE, change with it.

### Result variables

An execution or search result is a disjunction; each disjunct denotes the configurations one branch reaches, for a valuation of the initial pattern's free variables.
Those variables are the only names a result shares with the query.
Any other variable free in a disjunct is read as existentially quantified over that disjunct alone: it names a value the rewrite introduced, such as a `?` variable, which K quantifies existentially at the top of a rewrite rule's right-hand side (`docs/user_manual.md:2443-2444`), a rule variable the match left unbound, or a collection frame.
k-rust prints a disjunct as its term conjoined with its constraints and leaves these binders implicit (`constrained_pattern` in [externalize.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/externalize.rs)).
Every variable a rewrite introduces receives the `Ex#` marker and a `!N` suffix from one counter for the request (`FreshCounter` in [fresh.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/fresh.rs)): a right-hand-side existential `Var'Ques'X` prints as `Ex-hVar'Ques'X-bN`, a rule variable the left-hand side does not bind, `X`, prints as `Ex-hX-bN`, and so does a narrowed rule variable or collection frame.
The counter grows through the whole request and skips every spelling of the request's variables, free or bound, so one name denotes one variable along a path and across the response: reusing a name after its previous variable left the state would identify the values of two rule applications.
A request is an execution or search with its initial patterns and target, or a proof with the proved claim and every circularity and trusted lemma; a claim variable a proof renames to apply a claim (`!claim`) takes its counter from the same request counter.
The spellings are those of the patterns as the client sent them: a search target may apply an alias that ignores an argument, whose variables then do not occur in the internalized target, and the facades, the CLI, and the RPC server pass the spellings of the received patterns to the operation (`RequestNames::of_received`, `ExecutionOptions::request_names`, `SearchOptions::request_names`).

A remainder disjunct, the branch on which a rule does not apply, carries the negated applicability condition `\not(\exists V. C)`, where `V` are the variables of `C` that are free in neither the state's term nor its constraints (`quantify_introduced_variables` in [predicates.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/rewrite/predicates.rs)).
The applicability condition includes a `requires` Boolean term's definedness at instantiation, so an undefined `requires` instance belongs to the remainder; the [symbolic definedness tests](../crates/k-rust/tests/symbolic_definedness.rs) exercise this split with and without Z3.
A carried right-hand-side definedness obligation or an `ensures` can instead exclude an instance without producing a leaf; the same tests cover the obligations retained on surviving leaves.
A rule's variables are universally quantified over the rule (`docs/user_manual.md:2448-2450`), so the remainder must exclude every instance of the rule: a variable of the match that occurs nowhere else in the disjunct is bound inside the negation.
Reading it at the disjunct level instead would say only that some instance does not apply (`\exists x. \not C` rather than `\not \exists x. C`), which is a different set of configurations.

Under this reading three normalizations of [the register](../scripts/reference-normalisations.toml) are equivalences rather than tolerances:

- N4 renames the result variables that are not free in the initial pattern bijectively, sort-preservingly, and per disjunct; that is renaming bound variables, while the initial pattern's variables are compared by name. This holds when the gate supplies the initial pattern (`K_DIFFERENTIAL_INITIAL_PATTERN`, set by the symbolic and MIR execution gates); without it, N4 selects the variables to rename by generated-name shape, compares every other variable by name, and does not check that no initial-pattern variable has such a shape. When the gate instead names the search pattern's `?` variables (`K_DIFFERENTIAL_PATTERN_EXISTENTIALS`, empty for a run without a search pattern; C8 and C10), every other `?` variable is renamed too, whatever name an engine prints it under, except one k-rust prints without a marker or counter: by [Variable names](#variable-names) that is a request variable, compared by name on both sides. A named `?` variable stays fixed whatever its shape, so a search-pattern or request `?X1` is not read as a counted instance of a rule's `?X`; a rule existential printed under that same name then stays fixed too, which can only report a difference.
- N16 drops the outer `\exists` binders of a disjunct that bind generated variables (not free in the initial pattern; by the N4 name shapes when no initial pattern is given), which denotes the same disjunct as its body; it stops at a binder of an initial-pattern variable, which is kept and compared. k-rust prints no such prefix.
- N22 adds, inside a negated existential, the binder of a generated variable that is free there and occurs nowhere else in the disjunct, which is where the reading above places it; k-rust already prints that binder, while the pinned Kore prints the unification's collection frame free in that position.

### Unbound equation variables

An equation (a function, simplification, ceil or predicate equation) is an axiom closed over all its variables: `l = r requires R ensures E` states `l = r` for every value of every variable that satisfies `R` and `E`.
A variable `Y` of `r` or `E` that `l` does not bind is not fixed by matching `l`.
Such an equation can be consistent and meaningful: `f(X) = Y requires Y = X + 1` says `f(X) = X + 1`.
But the evaluator has no witness for `Y`, and the instance of `r` with the equation's own `Y` left free would name no value of the request.
Minting a new variable at each application would not model the equation either, since it would make `f() = f()` false for `f() = Y`.

The definition is loaded, and the backend records these variables per equation.
An instance of such an equation is never used.
Its `requires` is decided with the variables free, as for any equation: refuted, it holds for no value of them, and the equation says nothing about the subject.
Otherwise the attempt is indeterminate (`ConditionIndeterminacy::UnboundResultVariables`, wire kind `unbound-result-variables`, naming the variables as the definition spells them).
When no other equation rewrites the subject, the attempt is reported as an `UndecidedCondition` diagnostic of the equation with that reason and the decided `requires`, so a simplification that leaves the application unevaluated says why; `krust krun` prints it as a warning naming the variables as the K source writes them.
A subject that another equation evaluates (one of the same priority, or a lower-priority simplification) has nothing to explain, and the attempt is not reported.
Nor is one made while the condition of an enclosing equation attempt was simplified, when that attempt was refuted or superseded: the subject was then part of a condition the result does not depend on.
Nor is one whose subject a later part of the same simplification drops, as `drop(_) => 0` drops `f(3)` for a total `f`, or as `X >=Int 0 orBool f(X) ==Int 4` holds at `X = 3` whatever `f(3)` is: the result is what it would be had the equation applied.
That is the same outcome as an equation whose `requires` is open: the subject is kept, and a function's lower-priority and `owise` equations do not apply in its place, while a simplification is passed over.
The static definedness analysis does not use such a ceil equation.

A variable that occurs only in an equation's `requires` never reaches a result.
It is decided with the rest of the `requires`: the `requires` holds only if it holds for every value of the variable, and it is refuted only if it holds for none.
In the predicates of an `UndecidedCondition` diagnostic, such a variable keeps the equation's marker and prints escaped (`Eq-hVarY`, or `Eq-hVarY-bapartN` when it was renamed apart from the subject or from a request variable spelled like it); the wire form lists each one in `ruleVariables` with the name the definition gives it (`definitionName`, here `VarY`).
Such a variable can also reach the subject of another equation while the `requires` is simplified; there it is a variable of that subject, and a diagnostic of the other equation does not list it.
Kompile accepts a function or simplification rule whose right-hand-side variable occurs in its `requires` or is listed in `unboundVariables`; the backend loads it as above.
Rewrite rules are not equations: a variable that the left-hand side of a rewrite rule does not bind is minted per application (see [Result variables](#result-variables)).

### Undefined instances of the initial configuration
