An execution or a search that does not assume its initial configuration defined (`krun`, `krust kore-exec`, an RPC `execute` without `assume-state-defined`, the in-process `Backend::execute`, `search` and `search_pattern`) receives a pattern `t ∧ φ`, which denotes `⌈t⌉ ∧ t ∧ φ`.
An instance of its variables on which a subterm of `t` is undefined, a partial function outside its domain (`X /Int Y` where `Y` is 0) or a map whose keys coincide (`("a" |-> 0) R` where `"a"` is a key of `R`), makes the configuration holding it undefined, so it is no configuration: no rule rewrites it, so it has no successor and is not stuck either.
Before the first step k-rust decides the definedness obligations of `t` under `φ`, each by simplification and then all of them together by the solver.
Established by `φ` (no instance satisfying `φ` fails one, as for `sti(X /Int Y)` under `Y >Int 0`), the configuration has no undefined instance, and nothing is restricted or named.
Refuted (no instance satisfying `φ` meets them all, as under `Y *Int Y ==Int 0`), the configuration has no instance: `krun` warns `the initial configuration simplified to \bottom before any rewrite step; check the configuration variables and the functions it applies` and prints `\bottom`, and a search finds nothing and says why: its result sets `initial_bottom` (wire `initialBottom: true`, omitted when false), which it also sets when the constraints of every initial state simplify to `\bottom`, and `krun`'s search prints the same warning.
Left open, they restrict what the first step leaves over: no remainder, no lower-priority or `owise` rule and no stuck result is given an instance where they fail, and such a result states them (`stz(X /Int Y)` with `\not(Y = 0)` beside `\not(0 = X /Int Y)` under `stz(0) => zero()`).
The rules that apply see the configuration as given, so a successor carries the obligations its own step states, as for any state.
The run reports the open obligations once, as an `UndefinedInitialInstances` diagnostic on the initial configuration's path (wire kind `undefined-initial-instances` with the predicates in `definedness`); `krun` prints `warning: the initial configuration is undefined unless [...] holds; its instances where this fails denote no configuration and have no result`.
A rule's own condition is not an obligation of the configuration: under `stc(X, Y) => pos() requires X /Int Y >Int 0`, `stc(X, Y)` is defined for every `X` and `Y`, and its instances where that `requires` is undefined (`Y` is 0) are configurations no rule applies to, so they stay in its stuck remainder.
The proof route and an RPC request with `assume-state-defined` assume the initial configuration defined and are unchanged.
The constraints `φ` of a request are read with the definedness of each atom's terms stated where the atom stands, an equivalence, and on every route that admits a request (execution, search, `implies`, `simplify`, `get-model`, and RPC and CLI requests alike): a Boolean atom `b` means `true = b`, which is `\bottom` where a term of `b` is undefined, so under `\not` it holds there, and an equality of two terms neither of which is shown defined holds where both are undefined.
So `pos(X, Y)` under `\not(X /Int Y >Int 5) ∧ \not(X /Int Y <=Int 5)` denotes the instances where `Y` is 0, which are stuck under `pos(X, Y) => yes(X /Int Y) requires X /Int Y >Int 0`, and so does `pos(X, Y)` under `X /Int Y = X /Int Y +Int 1`; their leaves carry the atoms as `\not(\not(Y = 0) ∧ X /Int Y >Int 5)` and `(\not(Y = 0) ∧ X /Int Y = X /Int Y +Int 1) ∨ (Y = 0)` (after simplification), so that no solver decision on them, which would read `/Int` as a total operation, answers on the instances where `Y` is 0 ([Solver decisions over partial operations](#solver-decisions-over-partial-operations)).
An equality with a side defined on every instance (a value, a variable, a term of constructors and total functions) is read as an atom over its other side; an equality of two possibly undefined terms is kept as written only where conjuncts beside it state the definedness of both its sides, and is otherwise stated as `(⌈a⌉ ∧ ⌈b⌉ ∧ a = b) ∨ (\not ⌈a⌉ ∧ \not ⌈b⌉)`, so `\not(Z = 0) ∧ \not(X /Int Z *Int 0 = X /Int Y *Int 0)` keeps its instances where `Z` is 1 and `Y` is 0.
A request constraint whose definedness depends on an impure application in an operand that `#if`, `andThenBool` or `orElseBool` does not evaluate, or that has an atom over a term-level `\and` (whose definedness is more than its operands': `Z ∧ W` is empty unless `Z = W`), is refused with `DefinitionError::UnstatedDefinedness`, and so is a request with an `\equals` whose operands are not both terms and that holds a term where a predicate is read (`\equals(B1 ∧ B2, false)` over two Boolean variables, `\equals(B, \top)`): such an equality is read as the `\iff` of its operands taken as predicates, with a term read as `t = true`, which is not its meaning (`B1 ∧ B2` of two values is their intersection, `\bottom` where they differ, and `\equals(B, \top)` is false since `Bool` has two elements). Over JSON-RPC each refusal is error code 2, `Could not verify pattern`, whose detail carries the message.
The `request_constraints` tests in [symbolic_definedness.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/symbolic_definedness.rs) cover each route.

This deviates from the pinned reference's execute mode (`kore-exec` without `--search`, and `krun`), which gives such instances a stuck leaf: `sti(X /Int 0)` under `Y = 0` for `sti(X /Int Y)` and `sti(I:Int) => donei(I)`, and `stm(("a" |-> 0) R)` under `\not(false = "a" in_keys R)` for `stm(("a" |-> V) _) => donem(V)`; its `--depth 2` output of the map counterpart that rewrites the value in place keeps only that leaf, since the reference graph traversal prefers a stuck leaf to one at the bound (N17).
Each such leaf denotes no configuration, so the two results are equal patterns; k-rust omits the empty disjunct and names the condition instead, and the symbolic execution gate drops a reference disjunct that the reference's own search mode, which assumes its initial pattern defined, finds no instance of (N48).
The reference's search mode states the obligation on every state it reports, the initial one included, as assuming the initial configuration defined does; k-rust's search reports the initial configuration as given (the depth-0 state of a `STAR` search) and restricts only what its step leaves over.
For a wholly undefined initial configuration, the reference's execute mode returns it unchanged (`sti(1 /Int 0)`, or `stm(\bottom)` for a map with a repeated key) and its search returns `\bottom`; k-rust's execution and search both return `\bottom` with the warning above.
`an_undefined_instance_of_the_initial_configuration_has_no_result` in [execution_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/execution_semantics.rs) covers execution, search and `krun`'s run driver, and `constraints_that_settle_the_initial_definedness_leave_no_undefined_instance_to_name` the established and refuted cases (execution, state and path search), `krun_warns_when_the_initial_configuration_is_bottom` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs) `krun`'s warning on execution and search; `updating_a_map_value_beside_an_opaque_rest_keeps_the_absence_obligation` and `updating_a_value_through_requires_or_hooks_restates_one_absence` in [rewrite.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/rewrite.rs) cover a consumed map.

### Run coverage

An execute request that sets `collectCoverage` (`ExecuteRequest::collect_coverage`; off by default) receives `coverage`, two sorted lists of `UNIQUE_ID`s (`RunCoverageOutput`); without it the result has no `coverage` key and every other field is unchanged, and collecting changes no other field either.
`execute`, `executeObserved` and their JSON and writer forms accept it; `search`, `prove` and `simplify` do not take the option yet and may adopt it later.
`BackendCapabilities::coverage` advertises it; a server without it rejects `collectCoverage`, since the request rejects unknown fields.
The change is additive and optional, so the backend schema version stays 1.

- `applied` holds every axiom an instance of which the run used:
  - a function, simplification, `anywhere` or overload equation whose instance gave a result: its instantiated right-hand side, or the subject stated `#Bottom` by a refuted binding obligation, `ensures` or `#Bottom` right-hand side; and a `#Top` lemma that dropped an operand of a term-level `#And`. This holds wherever the run applied it: state, constraint and remainder simplification, a rule's `requires`, `ensures`, right-hand side and definedness obligations, and another equation's condition at any depth;
  - a ceil or predicate equation whose instance gave its instantiated right-hand side or refuted its subject;
  - an unconditional `#Ceil` equation whose instance the definedness analysis took as the condition of a partial application in a configuration or a right-hand side;
  - an equation from which the run decided the definedness of an operand that `#if`, `andThenBool` or `orElseBool` does not evaluate, such as `pd(A)` in `#if A >=Int 0 #then A #else pd(A) #fi`: the value of one function-equation step on literal arguments (D1), an unconditional `#Ceil` equation whose instantiated condition refuted or established the operand's definedness, or was stated in its place over builtin arithmetic (D2, D2'), or one function-equation step selected by matching alone that refuted or established it (D4);
  - a rewrite rule for which a step built a successor or an undefined step (a `trivial` leaf or entry), whether or not the step kept it;
  - for every rewrite rule whose `requires` the run began to decide, every equation that the load-time discharge of the rule's definedness applied (unconditional `#Ceil` equations, and the function or simplification equations that decided their conditions): the discharge removed the obligations the run would otherwise decide, and moved the evaluation of the rule's right-hand side out of the step into state simplification.
- `matched` holds `applied`, plus every axiom whose condition the run began to decide for an instance whose left-hand side matched, whatever the verdict (held, refuted, undecided, or cut short by a stop):
  - a rewrite rule whose `requires` phase was entered: its left-hand side matched, or unified with the state under a narrowing condition the solver did not refute;
  - an equation whose condition was evaluated for a matching instance; and, where a lower-priority function equation follows the equation's group, an instance the evaluator does not use but whose condition decides whether that lower equation may apply: an instance that matches only where variables of the subject equal the terms it binds them to, evaluated with those equalities, and an instance a `concrete` or `symbolic` attribute set aside, when its `requires` is not empty. A set-aside instance with an empty `requires` and no such equalities decides nothing and is not counted;
  - an equation instance a definedness decision of an unevaluated operand considered: the function equation selected for one step (D1, D4), whose condition is then checked, and every usable instance of an unconditional `#Ceil` equation (D2), whose condition is then folded;
  - for every rewrite rule whose `requires` the run began to decide, every equation whose condition the rule's load-time discharge began to decide.
  An instance whose attempt failed after its condition held (a disjunctive right-hand side, or an error) is in `matched` only.
- Both lists ignore whether the run kept the result: an application inside an attempt the backend later undid counts.
  An axiom without an id (a compiler-generated one) is listed as `UNKNOWN`.

The lists cover every state the run explored, including the leaves a `state-set` result merges into one: they are equal for `state-set` and `path-set` results and for `execute` and `executeObserved`, whatever the rule filter.
They are a deterministic function of the definition, the request, the solver profile and the k-rust version, and do not depend on earlier requests to the backend: the collection opens inside the execution, after the request's module has been selected, so the work of building a module on the first request that names it (its confluence check and definedness discharge) is never collected, and a discharge's equations are reported by every run of the rule alike.
They depend on the backend's trial order and stopping rules, so a cache of them must be keyed on the k-rust version.
A run that a stop, a timeout, the depth or the breadth bound, or the work budget cut short reports what it collected before it ended, which depends on where it stopped; a refused request has no result.

They never contain a builtin hook (not a sentence; the rule that calls it is covered), an `smt-lemma` axiom (used only by the solver), a macro or alias rule (expanded before execution, [Macro applications that survive expansion](#macro-applications-that-survive-expansion)), a claim, or an equation used only by the load-time confluence check (a use of `anywhere` and overload equations by the definition, outside every run); matching modulo overloads consults the `symbol-overload` relation the compiler generates from production attributes, which is no author sentence and is not listed (an overload equation applied as an equation is).
No list reaches an edit of a rule's or an equation's priority, its `owise`, its `concrete` or `symbolic` attribute, or its left-hand side, where the backend never tried the losing instance: a scan of equations stops at the first that applies, and a rewrite step at the first priority group that applies, so a rule of a lower group whose left-hand side matches the state is in neither list.
Equal sentences share one `UNIQUE_ID` (the catalog marks them `sharedIdentity`), so an id covers each of them.

The leaf `trace` is a diagnostic, not a coverage surface: it holds the rewrite steps of one path and the equations that normalized its states, misses the equations applied inside conditions, right-hand sides and the definedness analysis, and a `state-set` merge keeps the trace of one merged path only.
`crates/k-rust/tests/run_coverage.rs` and [coverage.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/coverage.rs) pin the lists.

### Work budget

An execute request may bound the work of its run: `maxWork` (`ExecuteRequest::max_work`, `ExecutionOptions::max_work` in the backend; absent by default, no bound) is the most states the run may visit across all its paths.
A state is visited when the run takes it from its worklist, whatever becomes of it: an initial state the run takes from its worklist, a state expanded into successors, a state that ends as a leaf, and a state that reaches a configuration another path reached each count once. An initial state that the run turns into a leaf before exploring (a macro or alias that survived expansion, or an unsupported `\ceil` in a resumed state) is not taken from the worklist and does not count.
Every result reports the count as `work`, budgeted or not.
When a state remains after `maxWork` states, it becomes one leaf with the halt reason `work-bound`, the last leaf of the result, and the run ends: the other pending states are neither explored nor reported.
The `work-bound` leaf's state is the state where exploration stopped, as it was taken from the worklist, unexpanded and unnormalised: it is not a reached result, its term may be unnormalised and its constraint unsatisfiable, and the result is incomplete, as a `cancelled` one is.
Under `state-set` it is never merged with another leaf, since it also stands for the states the run dropped.
A leaf finished before the stop carries, under `state-set`, the diagnostics of the paths merged into it before the stop only, so they may differ from the same leaf's in a run with a larger budget; its state, depth, halt reason, effects and console do not.

The run takes states in a fixed order (depth first, a state's successors before its siblings), so the state at which the count reaches the budget, and every leaf finished before it, are a function of the backend's inputs and the request exactly where the result is: the execution determinism contract ([Execute](#execute) under "Determinism and concurrency") covers a budgeted request, and its exclusions apply to the stop point.
In particular, in builds with Z3 a run in which an SMT query can time out (by default after 125 ms, with retries) may explore other branches and so stop at another state, and a reused `Backend` may answer from its cache where a fresh one times out; a run whose token was cancelled, that sets a step timeout, that ran out of native stack, or in which a `Bytes` hook refused a result against the memory allowance is excluded as well, as is a definition whose load met a stop for lack of stack.
A host that caches a budget-stopped result as a reproducible fact therefore keys it on the solver profile, and in a Z3 build only when no query of the run timed out.
Portable and Z3 builds explore different trees, so a count is comparable within one solver profile only.
A budget at least the unbounded run's `work` changes nothing, and a `max_breadth` of zero visits no state (`work` is 0).
A budget of 0 visits no state either: the first initial state becomes the `work-bound` leaf exactly as the request gave it, unevaluated (an initial configuration written as a function application stays that application), and the other initial states are dropped.
At this revision exploration, and so `work`, is the same under `state-set` and `path-set`: the states of converging paths are counted once per path in both, and only the final leaves are merged.

The budget bounds the states, the leaves and the pending states of a run, and with them its time and memory only for a definition whose steps and values are bounded.
It does not bound the work of one state's step (a ground function evaluation runs to its value, bounded only by a step deadline, cancellation and the stack), the size a value reaches, or the length of the leaves' traces, which grows with the depth of each leaf; a host keeps its own time and memory limits for those, and reports their stops as such.
`a_work_budget_stops_a_branching_run_at_the_same_state`, `a_work_budget_at_least_the_run_count_changes_nothing` and `a_work_bound_leaf_survives_the_state_set_merge` in [work_budget.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend/work_budget.rs), and `a_work_budget_stops_a_run_after_that_many_states_with_one_work_bound_leaf` in [execution_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/execution_semantics.rs), pin it.

## Determinism and concurrency

Nothing in what k-rust computes is random: every choice it makes (the order of rules and candidates, generated names, the order of results) is a function of its inputs, except where the paragraphs below name another dependence.
This section states which inputs count, so that a host can cache, compare and parallelise compiles and runs.

### Compile

For one build of k-rust (one revision with its `Cargo.lock`, built by one toolchain for one target, with one feature set and build profile), loading (`load_for_compilation`, `load_with_options`, `load_structured`, `LoadedDefinition::with_definition`) and compiling (`compile_loaded_definition`) equal inputs gives equal results, in any process, on any thread and on every call: the three KORE texts byte for byte, every other field of `CompiledKoreArtifacts` by equality, and a `LoadError` (a definition that fails to load) or a `CompileError` (one that loads and fails to compile) by equality.
The inputs are:

- each source's text and its resolver identity (`ResolvedSource::source`, the name the resolver returns for it): the identity is what `Source` attributes, load diagnostics and `LoadError`s carry and decides whether the text is Markdown, so the same text under `/checkout-a/legacy.k` and under `/checkout-b/legacy.k` gives different KORE. The logical name (`ResolvedSource::logical`, or the identity with `LoadOptions::project_root` stripped) is not an input of the artifacts or errors: it reaches only the loaded definition's source table (`LoadedDefinition::source_table()`, and the provenance of its definition JSON). So `project_root` does not change the `Source` attributes, and artifacts that must not depend on the checkout need resolver identities that do not (resolution from a fixed root);
- the resolver's answers;
- for `load_structured`, the `Definition` value it is given, and for `LoadedDefinition::with_definition`, the receiver and the `Definition` value;
- `LoadOptions` and `CompileOptions`;
- the environment variable `KRUST_PARSE_DIAGNOSTIC_WORK`, read once per process, which bounds the work spent explaining a failed parse (`DiagnosticWork`; it changes which diagnostic a failed load reports, never whether a load succeeds).

The `*_timed` and `*_with_timings` variants return the same artifacts or errors; the timings they also return are wall-clock and are not covered.

Builds with Z3 type inference also take `KRUST_TYPE_INFERENCE_MODE` and the Z3 library as inputs; the diagnostic reported when every candidate typing of a rule fails names the first failure in Z3's enumeration order, so in those builds the promise covers accepted compiles and not the text of that diagnostic.

An input deep enough that a compile pass needs a mapped stack segment is refused (a `TermTooDeep` error, citing `LargestSegment` or `MappingRefused`) or not depending also on the calling thread's remaining stack and on whether the host maps the segment, so such refusals are outside the promise; a host out of memory may also panic in a guarded recursion, which is no result at all.

The order and representation of the artifacts may change between builds; a host that caches them keys the cache on the build (or on the binary's identity).
[compile_determinism.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/compile_determinism.rs) compiles representative definitions twice in one process, on a small-stack thread, on concurrent threads and in two child processes, whose hash seeds and address layouts differ, and requires equal artifacts and identical bytes, an identical failure for a definition that fails to load and for one that fails to compile, different `Source` attributes for two resolver identities of one text, and equal artifacts for two logical names under one identity.

### Concurrency

`LoadedDefinition`, `Definition`, `CompileOptions` and `CompiledKoreArtifacts` are `Send + Sync`; `LoadError` and `CompileError` are `Send`; `Backend` is `Send`.
Relinking (`LoadedDefinition::with_definition`) and compiling (`compile_loaded_definition`) read only their arguments and the process-wide inputs of the Compile paragraph, so several threads may call them at once on one shared `LoadedDefinition` (an `Arc<LoadedDefinition>` is the shared baseline) or on distinct ones, and each call returns what the same call returns alone on one thread: equal artifacts or an equal `CompileError`.
The tables a `LoadedDefinition` computes lazily (those of `ResolvedDefinition`) are filled idempotently from the definition, so which thread fills one does not change what it holds.
Of the Compile paragraph's inputs, `KRUST_PARSE_DIAGNOSTIC_WORK` and, in Z3 builds, `KRUST_TYPE_INFERENCE_MODE` are process-wide, and both are read only by loading; relinking and compiling read no process-wide input, and no compile reads the locale or any other state a host thread may change. Apart from these, no state shared between threads reaches the artifacts (the process-wide counters of the `measure` feature only count).
Every compile stage runs on a stack sized from the definition's deepest term, so the artifacts do not depend on the calling thread's stack size; refusals for lack of stack are as stated for compiles above.
Wall time and peak memory under concurrency are not promised.
Building `Backend`s on several threads at once is covered by the Execute paragraph below, including the load-time critical-pair check that `Backend::new` runs.
[compile_concurrency.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/compile_concurrency.rs) pins the bounds and compares concurrent relinks and compiles of one baseline, and of a mutant that fails to compile, with the sequential ones.

### Execute

For one build of k-rust (as for compiles), the result of `Backend::execute`, `execute_observed` or `execute_observed_to_writer` (its leaves and their order, states, predicates, halt reasons, traces, branch identities, observations and diagnostics) is a function of the backend's inputs and the request: it is the same on a fresh `Backend` and on one that ran other requests before, in any process and on any thread.
The backend's inputs are the KORE definition and the module, `BackendOptions`, and, where the route of construction or a later call takes them: the `execution_rewrite_order` given to `Backend::internalize_source_definition` (it orders the rewrite rules, and so the leaves); the SMT prelude given to `from_definition_with_prelude` or `from_internalized_with_prelude`; the modules added with `add_module` that a request names; and the stack budget set with `set_simplification_stack_budget`. Builds with Z3 also take the Z3 library as an input.
The promise excludes:

- a request whose cancellation token was cancelled before the call returned, whether or not a leaf reports `cancelled` (a token cancelled while the last leaf is normalised leaves that leaf unsimplified and reports no `cancelled` leaf);
- a request that sets `step_timeout_ms` or `moving_average_timeout`, whose stops depend on the clock;
- a run whose result reports a nonzero `stackStops` (`ExecutionResult::stack_stops`): the stops for lack of native stack the run met, those that ended a state (a `simplification-error` leaf whose `cause` is `{"kind": "stack-exhausted"}`; in state-set modality such a leaf may be merged with a leaf of the same configuration that another path ended otherwise, for example as `stuck`, so the result may show no such leaf while `stackStops` still counts the stop) and those met while a result was only refined (normalising a leaf's state, simplifying a definedness condition, folding a hook, deciding an undefined instance; the refutation of a list update evaluates one builtin hook, which takes no simplifier stack, and so cannot meet one), which leave the sound but less simplified result and no other trace. Whether such a stop happens depends on the stack, not on the inputs: the simplifier's per-thread stack budget (`set_simplification_stack_budget`) is counted beyond the calling thread's own stack, so the thread's stack size matters; its process-wide budget is shared with every other thread of the process; and the stack a level of the simplifier takes, with the red zone it keeps, is fixed by the build ([`docs/backend-port.md`](backend-port.md#simplification-stack-depth), "Simplification stack depth"). A request whose state is too deep for the backend can likewise fail with `Err` (a refused stack segment, `StackSegmentRefused`) depending on the calling thread's stack and on whether the host maps the segment, and a guarded recursion on a host out of memory may panic, which is no result at all. Search and proofs do not count the stack stops they dismiss: a search reports a state a stack stop ended as a `simplification` entry whose failure is `stack-exhausted`, and a proof records it as a `stack-exhausted` leaf and goes on with its other states, but a stop met while one of their results was only refined leaves no trace, so their results carry no such guarantee;
- a run in which a `Bytes` hook bounded by the memory allowance (`padRightBytes`, `padLeftBytes`, `Int2Bytes`) refused a result, which shows as an `unsupported-hook-unevaluated` diagnostic for `BYTES.padRight`, `BYTES.padLeft` or `BYTES.int2bytes`: the refusal's text names the allowance and where it was read (physical memory, `RLIMIT_AS`, a cgroup's `memory.max` with its path), which differ between hosts and between processes on one host ([Resource bounds](#resource-bounds)). Whether a hook refuses also depends on the allowance, and the value it refuses may be an intermediate one that no result shows, so a host records the process's allowance (`k_rust_backend::allowance::Allowance::of_process()`) with each result and compares only results computed under equal allowances;
- a definition whose load met a stop for lack of stack, in its critical-pair check or in its discharge of rewrite-rule definedness: its value claims, or the rewrite rules whose definedness it discharged, which execution reads, depend on the loading thread's stack and on the stack other threads hold. Read the counts of the definition the backend executes with, `backend.select_definition(module)?`, for every module a request names, after the first call that selects it (the backend internalizes the default module when it is built, on that thread, and another module inside the first call that selects it, a request naming it, `select_definition`, `rule_catalog` or a search, on that call's thread), or build the backend with `Backend::from_internalized` from the definition whose counts were read: a nonzero `value_claims().counts().condition_stack_stops` or `discharge_counts().stack_stops` is excluded, and a stop of the poll budget of either (`condition_budget_stops`, `discharge_counts().budget_stops`) is a function of the definition and is not.

In builds with Z3 the promise does not hold when an SMT query can time out (by default after 125 ms, with retries), and a `Backend` keeps a cache of satisfiability answers and a failed prelude across requests, so a reused `Backend` may answer where a fresh one times out.
Search output keeps the order stated in [Search results](#search-results).
[execute_determinism.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/execute_determinism.rs) runs a definition that explores several paths, with paths that converge in state-set modality and a symbolic branch, in path-set and state-set modality, through `execute`, `execute_observed` and `execute_observed_to_writer`, each request on a fresh backend and in rounds on one reused backend, on four threads and in two child processes, and requires identical serialized results.

## Observation events

Observed execution and search (`executeObserved` and the `*Observed` search methods of the host API) report three event kinds, a closed union tagged by `kind`.
A `transition` event is a committed transition of the leaf's `branch` (or a witness's `id`); under an allowlist only the admitted ones are reported, always in branch order.
An `evaluation` event is an equation, simplification, or builtin application that normalized a branch state; its `anchor` is the number of branch entries before it, whether or not the allowlist reports them.
An `uncommitted` event is an attempted transition that no surviving branch retains.
A cut-point rule is proposed, not committed: the leaf stays at the state before it, and neither the rule nor its successor's normalization appears in the leaf's events, unless the leaf reports the successor's pattern because its normalization failed or is bottom.
With branch stopping, each candidate successor is normalized after its transition on its own branch: a candidate that continues as the only successor keeps those evaluations, and a candidate reported by a branch or cut-point halt carries them with it (in the backend's `AppliedRule::observations` and `RemainderBranch::observations`; the host wire does not expose halt candidates structurally).
Evaluation events are diagnostics; which ones occur and in which order depends on the simplifier's strategy.

This shape replaced an earlier one at backend schema version 1, without a version change, in line with the other closed-variant changes of that schema:
equation, simplification, and builtin applications were `transition` events with a `TransitionId` and the classes `function-equation`, `simplification`, and `builtin`; `transition.class` is now one of `rewrite`, `remainder`, and `claim`, and those three classes moved to `evaluation.class`.
The event fields are camelCase (`ruleLabel`, `introducedPredicates`), as the TypeScript declarations always stated; the JSON previously spelled them `rule_label` and `introduced_predicates`.
Builtin failure fields now use the camelCase names in the napi and wasm declarations (`thenSort`, `elseSort`, `exponentBits`, `leftPrecision`, `leftExponentBits`, `rightPrecision`, `rightExponentBits`); search instantiation failures use `missingVariables`.
These backend schema version 1 JSON fields previously used snake_case; clients reading serialized failures must accept the camelCase names.
The builtin failure kind `mismatched-float-formats` (with `leftPrecision`, `leftExponentBits`, `rightPrecision` and `rightExponentBits`) is removed at backend schema version 1, without a version change: a Float operation over a binary32 and a binary64 operand is no failure ([Backend scope](#backend-scope)).
This is a breaking change for a client that names the kind: a TypeScript `case` or type guard on `'mismatched-float-formats'` no longer compiles against the napi and wasm `BuiltinFailure` unions, stored JSON holding the kind no longer deserializes as a `BuiltinFailureOutput` (whose kinds are closed), and the public Rust variants `k_rust_backend::builtin::BuiltinError::MismatchedFloatFormats` and `k_rust::backend::wire::BuiltinFailureOutput::MismatchedFloatFormats` are removed, so code that names them no longer compiles.
The builtin failure kinds `unsupported-float-format` (with `hook`, `precision` and `exponentBits`) and `unsupported-float-format-parameters` (with `hook`, `precision` and `exponentBits`) are removed the same way, with the public Rust variants `BuiltinError::UnsupportedFloatFormat`, `BuiltinError::UnsupportedFloatFormatParameters` and the `BuiltinFailureOutput` variants of the same names: a Float of a format the backend does not compute is no malformed input ([Backend scope](#backend-scope)).
Together with `mismatched-float-formats`, no builtin failure kind carries a Float format any more (the fields `precision`, `exponentBits`, `leftPrecision`, `leftExponentBits`, `rightPrecision` and `rightExponentBits` are gone); `invalid-float-token` remains, for a text that names no Float. The halt statuses move accordingly: a run that reached a Float of another format halted with `simplification-error` and now halts with `unsupported-hook` (a `total` hook over it only where the run needs the value); a format argument of zero or below, which was a `simplification-error`, is now undefined (`#Bottom`), so the path is removed from execution and search; and an operation over a binary32 and a binary64 operand, which was a `simplification-error`, is evaluated in an execution and stays unevaluated as a defined value elsewhere.
Clients must drop the member, the case or the match arm (no request produces the failure any more) and discard or rewrite stored failures of that kind.

## CLI scope

`krust` retains its source-plus-flags interface.
For source-driven `krun`, the syntax or configuration parser module supplies the concrete grammar and the selected main module supplies the executable production catalog and visible macro sentences.
Parsed applications must rebase into the main catalog before macro expansion, sort injection, and KORE conversion; an absent source-catalog production index may be discarded only from a self-describing token, whose lexical hook is read from the parser module before falling back to the main module.
Macro expansion uses the frontend's KAST-domain expander after rebasing and before conversion to executable KORE; `kast` retains its separate unparsing-module scope.
A macro- or alias-headed term that survives expansion is invalid executable input and must be rejected before the first rewrite step rather than narrowed as an ordinary function application ([Macro applications that survive expansion](#macro-applications-that-survive-expansion)).
The backend also rejects such a head defensively when a direct caller bypasses the CLI validation.
The project provides its own versioned compiled-directory runtime contract rather than K's file format; it does not implement K-derived module defaults, every K flag alias, or K's pretty-output and proof-verdict framing solely for tool interchangeability; its own verdict words are defined under [Proof verdicts](#proof-verdicts).
K tools and pyk serve as differential oracles; the conformance driver translates recipes into supported Rust operations.
An unknown or untranslatable flag must remain explicitly unsupported rather than being silently ignored.
The `declined-capability` category records steps requiring an interface with no Rust equivalent.
Semantic input selection, warning handling, execution status, and configuration initialization remain testable contracts; this policy does not exclude them.
The source-plus-flags interface includes standalone Bison parser generation and executable or shared-library parser artifacts, while relying on the system Bison, Flex, and C compiler rather than implementing those tools in Rust.
A generated parser accepts exactly the programs of the start sort in the syntax module; when that sort has no programs there (no production, or every production needs a sort without programs, as for a sort declared bare whose program syntax lives in a module the syntax module does not import), there is no Bison grammar for it, and generation fails before Bison with an error naming the sort and those productions.

`krun --output captured` is an explicit ordinary-execution mode for definition-computed console output.
It uses buffered `--io off` stream semantics with pre-buffered standard input, requires exactly one complete unconstrained terminal execution leaf and exactly one structurally identified stdout stream buffer, writes that buffer to process stdout once, and suppresses KORE rendering.
Bottom, constrained or multiple leaves, incomplete execution, malformed stream state, search, surface result matching, and an explicit `--io on` are errors.
`krun --io on --output none` is the corresponding committed live mode with pre-buffered input and byte-exact descriptor 1/2 delivery.
Default KORE output remains unchanged.

## Macro applications that survive expansion

A `macro`, `macro-rec`, `alias` or `alias-rec` rule defines an abbreviation: its left side is replaced by its right side in rules and claims before compilation, and in a program, a configuration value or a search pattern before execution.
Expansion rewrites innermost first; a `macro` or `alias` rule applies at most once on a path, neither again to the term it produced nor below it, and a `macro-rec` or `alias-rec` rule applies again until no rule applies, within the recursion bounds (`kompile/passes/expand_macros.rs`).
The macro rule is not an axiom of the executed definition: `kcompile` writes it to `macros.kore`, not to `definition.kore`.
Nor is the macro-like production a constructor of its sort there: `definition.kore` declares its symbol `function`, `macro` and `value-production` with no equation, its sort's no-junk axiom does not list it among the sort's alternatives, and the backend loads it as a `Residual` head, a `Function(Partial)` symbol with definedness obligations ([Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules)).
So an application of it left after expansion has no value the executed definition determines.
By the declaration (`value-production`) it is a defined value of its sort, which by the no-junk axiom equals some value the sort's listed alternatives build, but the definition does not say which; the backend reads the head more weakly, as possibly undefined.
Either way no rule whose left side has a constructor where such an application stands can be decided against it, so every route refuses it rather than executing it.

The refusal is enforced at three points.
A rule or claim that still holds a macro-like application after expansion is rejected at compile time with `InvalidMacroExpansion` ("Rule contains macro symbol that was not expanded"); a context is exempt.
A program, a configuration value, the initial state given to any library execution or search entry point or to RPC `execute`, and an implication pattern are refused before internalization (`DefinitionError::MacroOrAliasInExecutablePattern`, and `MacroOrAliasInImplication` for an implication pattern); `krun` prints `invalid executable input: macro or alias symbol '…' survived expansion` and exits 1.
A rewrite successor that holds one ends its path as an indeterminate entry (`IndeterminateReason::SurvivingMacroOrAlias`): the state is not a result, and a search that meets it is incomplete.

Under `syntax Stmt ::= "var" Id ";" | "decl" Ids ";" [macro] | Stmt Stmt`, three situations leave such an application.
A non-recursive rule whose right side keeps a macro-like application: with `rule decl X , Y , Rest ; => var X ; decl Y , Rest ;`, `decl x , y , z ;` expands to `var x ; decl y , z ;`, and the rule does not apply again to `decl y , z ;`.
An application no rule's left side covers: `decl x ;` matches that rule nowhere.
An application that another use of an already applied non-recursive rule would expand: with the base clause `rule decl X:Id ; => var X ;` added, `decl x ;` and `decl x , y ;` expand completely and run to `<k> .K </k>`, while `decl x , y , z ;` is still refused, because the first clause cannot apply again on its path.
The refusal is the cost of the one-step strategy in the third case: read as equations, the two clauses rewrite every remaining application to `var` statements, and the refusal claims nothing about the program.

A surviving application is not read as a constructor, because that would add a value to its sort that the no-junk axiom excludes, and would contradict the macro rule read as the equation it states: `decl x , y , z ; = var x ; decl y , z ;` against the no-confusion of a constructor `decl _ ;` with `_ _`.
Executing it as it is gives no answer either.
Its value is not determined, so a rule whose left side has a constructor where it stands neither applies nor is refuted there.
On execution and search routes a variable-free state then halts as an incomplete outcome where such a rule leaves a left-hand-side variable unbound ([Concrete rewrite instantiation](#concrete-rewrite-instantiation)), and branches under an undecided equality with the application where such a rule binds every variable, as one with a ground left-hand side does; a rule that rebuilds the state then repeats that without end, as [Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules) states for a withdrawn anywhere head.
A symbolic state, or any state on a proof route, would narrow it against every rule whose left side can hold its sort.
The refusal reports that incompleteness where it arises: before the first step for input, and at the successor for a state a step builds.

This differs from the pinned reference, whose two backends also differ from each other.
The LLVM backend keeps a surviving application as an ordinary constructor, so `decl x , y , z ;` ends there at `<k> decl y , z , .Ids ; ~> .K </k>`.
The Haskell backend (`kore-exec` 0.1.155) fails with `DecidePredicateUnknown` while applying an equation, and `krun` reports `Backend crashed during rewriting`, so that backend returns no result for it.

A definition makes its expansion complete, or does not use a macro.
With `macro-rec` on the production and a clause for every argument shape (the two clauses above), `decl x ;` and `decl x , y , z ;` both expand completely and run to `<k> .K </k>`.
Where the one-step unfolding should be observable during execution, it is an ordinary rewrite rule or function equation instead of a macro.
`krun_rejects_a_macro_symbol_that_survives_expansion` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs), `simplification_rules_do_not_inherit_macro_kinds_during_expansion` and `reports_a_macro_symbol_when_repeated_variable_matching_fails` in [kompile_passes.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/kompile_passes.rs), `implication_patterns_reject_aliases_and_macros` in [definition.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/definition.rs) and `a_successor_with_a_surviving_macro_is_an_indeterminate_entry` in [search_boundary.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/tests/search_boundary.rs) pin the refusal.

## Proof verdicts

`krust kprove` prints one verdict word per selected claim: `proven`, `disproved`, `failed`, `indeterminate`, `depth bound` or `breadth bound`.
The Node.js and WebAssembly `status` field uses the same words, with `depth-bound` and `breadth-bound`.
The [README kprove section](../README.md) lists them with the leaf listing each word prints.
A word is a statement about the claim; the process exit status is only a summary of several claims: 0 exactly when every selected claim is `proven`, 1 otherwise with `one or more reachability claims were not proven`.

This differs from the pinned `kprove`, which reports only whether the backend proved every claim.
It prints `backend terminated because the configuration cannot be rewritten further` whenever the backend exits with status 1 (`k-frontend/src/main/java/org/kframework/kprove/KProve.java`), and the pinned `kore-exec` exits with status 1 whenever its result lists any claim it did not prove (`kore/app/exec/Main.hs`, `koreProve`).
The message therefore states that a claim was not proven; it does not state that the claim is false.
k-rust separates the two because a consumer acts on the word: a `disproved` claim cannot be proven by any strategy and needs a changed claim or definition, while a `failed` claim may be true, for example a vacuous claim or a one-path claim whose search took a rule that leads nowhere.
Reporting a true claim as false is a wrong result, not a presentation difference.

A claim `φ => ψ` is refuted, under the manual's [one-path](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/blob/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/docs/user_manual.md#one-path-interpretation) and [all-path](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/blob/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/docs/user_manual.md#all-path-interpretation) readings ("there exists a path", "all paths ... will reach"), by a configuration of `φ` that, for an all-path claim, has a path ending in a configuration with no successor without passing through `ψ`, or, for a one-path claim, has no path to `ψ`.
`disproved` requires a leaf that shows this, a certified stuck leaf (`stuck (certified)` in the listing), which must meet all of the following conditions:

- (a) No successor: the rewrite step on the leaf is stuck.
  A state the search stopped without rewriting, such as a stuck-check stop, is stepped once and is certified only if that step is stuck, because a configuration that can still move may reach `ψ` later.
- (b) Every path followed: the claim is all-path, or its one-path trace kept every successor.
  A one-path claim is false only if no path reaches `ψ`, and a trace that took one of several applicable rules says nothing about the others.
  The sequential rewriter does not report whether a step dropped an applicable alternative, so every one-path rewrite step counts as one that may have, and a one-path leaf is certified only when its trace has no rewrite step.
- (c) No claim step: no circularity or trusted claim on the trace.
  Such a step replaces paths by an assumed claim instead of following them, so the leaf shows at most that the assumption and the claim cannot both hold.
- (d) Non-empty outside the destination: the leaf term applies no function symbol (it may hold constructors, domain values and variables) and is not a conjunction of terms at its top, and the leaf constraints together with the definedness of its term hold syntactically, or are satisfiable by an SMT query that approximates nothing (only `Int` and `Bool` variables, no abstracted subterm, no partial function).
  A leaf denoting the empty set refutes nothing, an unevaluated function application may denote no value or a value the destination accepts, and a satisfiable abstraction may be spurious.
  The leaf constraints carry the complement of every destination condition checked on the trace, including the uncovered part of a state that the destination condition covers only in part; the complement places the leaf outside `ψ` only if every destination check on the trace ran and was decided.

Every other stuck leaf, and every empty leaf the vacuity policy rejects (`Trivial`, `Vacuous`), makes the claim `failed`: the search stopped there without establishing that the claim is false.
A `Trivial` leaf of a step is the set of instances on which the step is undefined: some rule applies, its result is empty there because its `ensures` or the definedness of its right-hand side fails, and no applied rule of the same priority gives them a defined result.
This covers a condition the step could not decide as well as a refuted one, so a symbolic claim is never proven while one of its instances fails: `apart(X, Y) => halt` under `rule apart(A, B) => halt ensures A =/=Int B` fails like its instance `apart(0, 0) => halt`.
The leaf is dropped only when its constraint is refuted, for example when `ensures ?X >Int 0` always has a witness.
When leaves disagree, the first of `disproved`, `failed`, `indeterminate`, `depth bound`, `breadth bound` applies.
A verdict never reads a value the definition leaves to the implementation, such as the order of `keys_list`, `values` and `Set2List` of two or more items or the item `choice` gives of them: a proof leaves such an application unevaluated ([Implementation-chosen hook values](#implementation-chosen-hook-values)).
The conditions are sufficient, not necessary: a false claim whose refutation k-rust cannot certify is reported `failed`, never `disproved` without evidence.
The conformance driver compares kprove recipes only as proven, not proven or error (`kprove_verdicts` in `scripts/conformance/run.py`), so `disproved` and `failed` are the same outcome there.

[reference-proof-differential.sh](../scripts/reference-proof-differential.sh) runs each `[[proof]]` entry's `failure-claim` through both toolchains.
It requires the reference `kprove` to exit with the message above and k-rust to print `claim <failure-claim>: disproved`; N12 omits the counterexample framing from that comparison.
The reference observation supplies only the fact that the claim is not proven.
The `disproved` expectation is k-rust's own stronger statement: it is valid for an entry only when the failure claim is false and k-rust's leaf for it meets (a)-(d), and the gate passing shows the leaf was certified, not that the reference refuted the claim.
`mini-proof`'s `claim-refuted`, the all-path claim `<k> start => stuck </k>` over a definition whose only rules are `start => middle` and `middle => done`, meets them: its leaf is `<k> done </k>` with no constraint, reached by the only path and without a successor.
A failure claim that is not false cannot carry the `disproved` expectation, and its entry must record its own expectation with the reason.

## Driver scope

The conformance driver translates each upstream `ktest` recipe into krust operations and compares their outcomes.
A plain `krun --output none` recipe with a non-empty expected console output runs under `--io off` and compares the stdout stream buffer of its single unconstrained execution leaf with that output under C9; it does not require host console effects from the backend.
An explicit `--io on` recipe compares the committed console stdout bytes directly with its expected output.
When C9 detects that buffered stdin cannot reproduce an implicit recipe's tokenization ([Comparison contract](#comparison-contract) states the one detected symptom), the driver retains the attributed C9 result and re-runs that recipe under pre-buffered `--io on --output none`; only the live bytes decide that step.
A recipe that defines no translatable step supplies no oracle: a `ktest-kdep.mak` or sub-make-only Makefile, a Makefile whose `ktest.mak` include is disabled upstream, a recipe that discards the output it would compare, or an expected kompile failure that leaves no definition for a later step.
The `undriven-recipe` category records such cases with the concrete recipe feature; the skip is not evidence of a Rust pass and must be reconsidered when the driver learns to translate the feature.

Every case whose accepted verdict is not `match` carries a disposition: exclusions, or a pending reason.
A non-empty `exclusion`, or the `exclusion` of a step exclusion, names the category whose section here justifies leaving the difference, and the inline `reason` states the concrete feature or decision for that case or step; the justification is complete in this repository.
Where no exclusion covers a divergent step, the non-empty `reason` records a measured port or driver gap that is pending work; the work itself is tracked outside this repository, and [testing.md](testing.md#manual-conformance-acceptance) describes how a local backlog is audited against the measurements.
A step exclusion (`step_exclusions`) accepts the divergence of the steps its selectors name and nothing else: the ratchet ranks the case as the driver would have ranked it had those steps not run, so any other step keeps the case ranked whatever its verdict, and a case whose every step is excluded ranks as `match`.
A case `exclusion` leaves every step of the case unranked, including steps a later driver adds, so it also needs a `whole_case_reason` stating why no step of the case can be ranked; a difference confined to some steps is a step exclusion instead.
A `kore-verify` step is never excluded: the verifier reports one rejected sentence at a time, so accepting one diagnostic would hide every later one. An accepted verifier divergence is a normalisation of the verification copy, which leaves the verifier to judge the rest of the definition.

## Comparison contract

[reference-normalisations.toml](../scripts/reference-normalisations.toml) is the authority for each permitted equivalence and exclusion.
Normalization identifiers such as N3 are durable names defined in that register, not work-item identifiers.

N3 permits only the counted multi-alias freezer family exclusion.
K's generated suffix assignment depends on unordered Scala context iteration, including `Source` and `Location` attributes; Rust retains deterministic declaration order.
The frontend orders catalogs, emitted sentences, and checks by dependency-first declaration order; no Scala `Ordering` is reproduced.
Sentence identity for deduplication (`sentence_equivalent`) keeps K's `Sentence` equality.
Single-alias identities remain compared.
N23 applies the same limited policy to generated lambda families: `kernel/src/main/java/org/kframework/compile/ResolveFun.java` assigns suffixes in `localSentences` iteration order, whose sentence hashes include the source path.
The pinned `issue-1528`, `let-test` and `record-llvm` cases exhibit different suffix assignments from different checkout paths.
The comparator collapses multi-suffix names consistently in declarations and uses, ignores only the affected UNIQUE_ID attributes, compares the remaining sentence multiset, and prints the collapsed axiom count.
The tradeoff is explicit: a suffix-to-body association is outside this comparison, while signatures and rule bodies remain checked.
N32 applies only to the reference side of the semantic KORE compile comparison.
It removes a generated sort-predicate true equation that places `X:S` at `K` through `inj{S,KItem}` (or `inj{S,K}`) when the KORE definition has no `subsort{S,KItem}` axiom, and removes only that equation's negated applicability disjunct from its matching `owise` false equation.
The predicate symbol, other conditions, and all equations with a declared `subsort{S,KItem}` remain compared; [the sort-placement contract](#sorts-of-compiled-terms) explains why the omitted equation cannot apply.
Variable numbering inside a generated owise competitor disjunction is the port's own; N4 renames it on both sides.
N33 applies only to the reference side of the KORE compile comparisons: it drops `injective` from a reference symbol that carries `anywhere` or a macro-like attribute when the port declares that symbol without it, the divergence [Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules) states; the port can never gain the attribute through it, and every other attribute stays compared.
N34 applies only to the reference side of the semantic KORE compile comparison: it drops the injectivity and pairwise no-confusion axioms that mention an overload-greater the port declares `anywhere` and states none of those axioms for, the two conditions [Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules) states; the no-junk axiom and every axiom that mentions no such symbol stay compared, and a port that states one of those axioms for the symbol leaves the reference untouched.
N36 applies to semantic and syntax KORE compile comparisons: it drops the reference's `functional` and the symbol's functional axiom when both declarations carry the same anywhere or macro-like attribute and the port withholds both claims, the evidence-based totality contract [Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules) states.
For those matching value productions it drops `value-production{}()` and any extra `function{}()` on the k-rust side, accepting both the Rust target's marked function and the LLVM target's marked value declaration and printing separate counts for both attributes and the withheld functional declarations.
A source function's `function{}()` and every unrelated attribute or axiom stay compared.
Every other attribute and axiom stays compared, and a declaration without the matching equation-head marker is ineligible.
N38 applies only to reference-tool verification of the k-rust LLVM-target definition (the kore/llvm compile pairing, and the conformance driver's `kore-verify` step of a `--backend llvm` recipe): it adds `function{}()` to exactly the declarations carrying `value-production{}()` in a separate verification copy, counts the changed declarations, and refuses any marked declaration that already carries `function` or `functional`.
The verifier requires a function or functional equation head; the external LLVM backend must instead receive the original declaration so that an irreducible value-production application remains a value, the per-target contract [Anywhere rules](#anywhere-rules) states.
The Rust/Haskell pairing verifies the original definition without this normalization.
N35 applies only to the reference side of the semantic and syntax KORE compile comparisons, after N32: it drops the sort predicate of a sort without a `subsort{S,KItem}` axiom, with its functional axiom and its then unconditional `owise` false equation, when the port declares no symbol of that name and no other sentence or attribute mentions it, the divergence [the sort-placement contract](#sorts-of-compiled-terms) states.
A predicate the port declares, a remaining true equation, and any use of the predicate stay compared; the comparison prints the number of predicates dropped.
The pinned WASM semantics, whose main closure declares `syntax #Layout [token]`, is the case it covers.
N39 applies only to reference parsed definitions: it removes `total` from configuration-generated symbols whose evidence is established during compilation.
A cell-map key projection is identified through the local `MAP.concat` cell collection, its `wrapElement` cell production, the cell's first key field, and the projection's exact generated signature.
A nullary initializer is identified through the configuration that generates it: a local cell production (with `cell` and `cellName` attributes, label `<name>`, the cell sort, and opening and closing cell terminals) determines the initializer label `init<CellSort>` and its result sort, which is the cell sort, or for `multiplicity="*"` the local cell-collection sort whose `cellCollection` production wraps that cell with the matching `concat` hook; the production must also carry the `initializer` marker, the `function` attribute, and the exact nullary signature.
The parsed artifact precedes compilation's `SoleGenerator` judgement for projections and its judgement of all initializer equations with the complete main-module equations and a least fixed point over child initializers.
The normalization requires each corresponding k-rust parsed production to differ only by lacking `total` and the sibling `definition.kore` to declare exactly one matching symbol with the same argument/result signature and `total{}()`.
It refuses per symbol if the parsed claim is already present, another parsed difference exists, or the final totality claim is absent; a genuinely partial initializer therefore refuses normalization, and an already present parsed claim on a projection requires removing N39.
A source-written nullary `[initializer, function, total]` production that no local cell generates is not recognised and stays compared unchanged; a recognised initializer whose k-rust parsed counterpart already carries `total` refuses with a message saying that it may be source-written. After rewriting, each module's sentences are sorted and deduplicated, so a module that held a production both with and without `total` keeps one copy.
It prints the number of removed attributes; every other parsed attribute and production remains compared, and the semantic KORE comparison still compares the final totality claim.
N43 and N44 apply only to the reference side of the semantic KORE compile comparison, and compare a generated symbol's totality claim as the reference's own axioms support it.
N43 gives a field projection `project:S:f` the claim (`functional{}()`, `total{}()` after `function{}()`, and its functional axiom) exactly when the reference states the no-junk axiom of `S` with the single alternative `\exists X0 ... . C(X0, ...)` of a constructor `C` and the unconditional equation `project:S:f(C(Y0, ...)) = Yi` over distinct variables: every value of `S` is then an application of `C`, which the equation gives the value `Yi`, so the claim is a theorem of the reference definition, and stating it does not change the definition's models.
The port claims exactly that totality (its `SoleGenerator` evidence); a projection whose derivation lacks a premise is compared unchanged.
N44 withdraws `total`, `functional` and the functional axiom from a generated `#lambda` symbol that the reference's own equations do not support: a lambda is the function of one `#fun`/`#let` rule, and nothing gives a value to an argument that rule does not match.
A claim is supported when the lambda has exactly one equation, unconditional, whose every argument pattern is a variable of the declared parameter sort, and whose right-hand side applies no lambda whose claim N44 withdraws (a least fixed point).
A definition that states no equation for the lambda (a syntax definition) gives no evidence either way, and its claim stays compared as written.
`let-test`'s `#lambdaF__` (pattern `inj{Int,Exp}(BAR)` on an `Exp` parameter) and `fun-llvm`'s `#lambdaF2__` (pattern `F3:FooT ~> .K` on a `K` parameter) and the lambda that applies it to an arbitrary `K` are withdrawn; the port withholds the same claims ([Configuration value transport](#configuration-value-transport)), and states such a lambda `\bottom` off its pattern ([Refuted local-function patterns](#refuted-local-function-patterns)), which N49 handles.
A supported claim stays, so a port that withholds it, or claims a projection the reference cannot derive, still differs; both print the number of declarations they change.
N49 applies only to the k-rust side of the semantic KORE compile comparison: it drops each `owise` equation of a generated `#lambda` symbol (name prefix `Lbl'Hash'lambda`) whose right-hand side is `\bottom`, the equation [Refuted local-function patterns](#refuted-local-function-patterns) adds for a lambda whose pattern does not cover its parameter sort.
The reference states no value for such a lambda off its pattern, so it has no axiom to compare that equation with; a user cannot write an equation on a generated label, so the shape identifies exactly the added equations.
The lambda's own equations, a match predicate's `owise` `false` equation, an `owise` `\bottom` equation of a written symbol and every other axiom stay compared; the comparison prints the number dropped (`let-list-binder`: its `SUB:Chunk .Chunks` binder on a `Chunks` parameter).
N45 applies only to the k-rust side of the semantic KORE compile comparison, and only to the axioms a compile case lists in its `lifted-cells` manifest key, each entry naming a rule label and a cell, with a written `lifted-cells-reason` that `scripts/reference-manifest.py` requires.
It covers the divergence [Cells written inside another cell](#cells-written-inside-another-cell) states: the port compiles a cell the rule writes inside the wrong parent, the reference discards it, and the reference therefore has nothing to compare the cell against.
The labelled axiom of each side is walked in parallel with the other through equal heads; where the port applies the listed cell's symbol and the reference has a variable of that cell's sort that its own completion generated, the application is replaced by one fresh generated variable per reference variable, so N4 numbers it as the reference's.
The whole axiom is then compared as before, so a port axiom that differs anywhere else, or that breaks the reference's sharing of the variable between the two sides of the rewrite, still differs.
An entry whose labelled axiom is missing or repeated on either side, whose reference position holds a named variable, or that replaces nothing (the reference states the cell there, so it no longer discards it) fails the comparison; it prints the number of applications replaced and of listed axioms (`mir`: 4 in 2).
Which cells a rule writes in the wrong parent is stated by the manifest entry and its reason, checked against the rule's source, not by the port's own completion, whose decision the comparison is meant to test.
The conformance driver's surface-text comparison (`scripts/conformance/run.py` `execution_text_diff`) applies the same N4 reading to kprint's `?Name:Sort` tokens as C7: the rule existentials both engines instantiate through a fresh counter are compared modulo a bijective, sort-preserving renaming by first occurrence per disjunct, applied before the C1 sort.
Every other variable, including the search pattern's own variables and any `?` variable the recipe's `--pattern` text names, and every string literal are compared literally, so lost sharing, a changed sort, or a renamed pattern variable remains a mismatch.
When that text comparison fails, C8 (`compare_simplified_kore`) re-runs the reference recipe with `--output kore`, simplifies that result and the krust result with `krust kore-simplify` against the reference kompiled definition and main module, and compares the two simplified patterns with the structural execution comparator without `K_DIFFERENTIAL_DEFINITION`, so N4 renaming applies and N15 does not.
The pinned reference prints a rewrite result before its own simplification is complete: `no-junk-macro` retains a constraint that the definition's own `smt-lemma` makes valid, and `concrete-function` leaves `foo(inc(sym2(?X)))` unevaluated because the argument's definedness is open, while the port discharges the constraint and applies the equation under a `\ceil` obligation that the surrounding constructor context already entails.
Both pairs are equal patterns; simplifying both with one simplifier makes them comparable.
A step that matches only this way records the C8 comparison label and the text difference, never plain match text; the checked-in `.out` stays the oracle, so the re-run's result must still print as the `.out`.
A simplification or comparison that fails or is unavailable leaves the text mismatch in place; a reference re-run that produces no result records `reference-error` with the reference's stderr, which is an oracle change and not evidence for either side.
C8 is supplementary evidence in the sense of [testing.md](testing.md#comparator-evidence): it depends on the port's simplifier, like N15.

C10 (`kore_output_comparison_environment`) covers a krun or search recipe whose checked-in output is KORE (`--output kore`): the driver compares it with the krust result by the structural execution comparator, which C8 also uses, and passes the `?` variables of the recipe's `--pattern`, none for a plain run, in `K_DIFFERENTIAL_PATTERN_EXISTENTIALS`.
N4 then renames every other `?` variable of either result bijectively, sort-preservingly, and per disjunct, which is the reading C7 gives kprint's `?` tokens.
Such a variable instantiates a rule's `?` variable, which K quantifies existentially over the rule's right-hand side, so it denotes some value and its name is not observable ([Result variables](#result-variables)): `issue-1682-korePrettyPrint`'s `rule <k> a => ?_:Int </k>` leaves `Var'QuesUnds'Gen0:SortInt{}` in the reference's result and `Ex-hVar'QuesUnds'Gen0-b0:SortInt{}` in k-rust's, and the two results denote the same configurations.
Identifying two such variables, splitting one into two, or changing its sort changes what the result denotes and stays a mismatch.
The variables a result shares with the query are observable: the search pattern's `?` variables are named by the recipe, and a `?` variable k-rust prints without a marker or counter is a request variable ([Variable names](#variable-names)); both are compared by name, whatever their shape.
A request variable that k-rust loses is not detected this way, because the driver has no initial pattern to fix it by; that is the shape-mode limitation [Result variables](#result-variables) states for N4.

C9 compares the bytes a tutorial definition accumulates in its stdout stream buffer under `--io off` with the checked-in output of the corresponding `--output none` recipe.
The comparison requires exactly one execution leaf in total, that leaf to be unconstrained, and exactly one structurally identified `#ostream(1)`, `"off"`, `#buffer(S)` stream.
Any residual leaf, multiple terminal leaves, and malformed stream configurations are mismatches and remain reported.
The tutorial stream rules append the same strings in both IO modes and make the `on` mode's `IO.write` hook only a transport for those bytes; K itself selects `off` for search and debug executions.
For input programs, C9 applies only where krust's buffered stdin is the piped input and K's stream rules tokenize those bytes as they tokenize the recipe's interactive stream; [Backend scope](#backend-scope) states where the two tokenizations differ.
When krust attributes an undefined result to a `STDIN-STREAM` rule and the input begins with a parse delimiter or contains adjacent parse delimiters, the driver records that C9 precondition failure and drives the implicit recipe under committed pre-buffered `--io on`.
This detection is a sufficient test for one symptom, not a decision procedure: the precondition depends on the sort each `#parseInput` requests and on the input suffix it reads, which the input bytes alone do not determine.
Any other failure of the precondition, for example `stdinParseString` taking the whole remaining buffer under `--io off` where the interactive stream supplies one token, is not detected; it runs to completion and is reported as a C9 stdout-buffer mismatch, to be diagnosed against [Backend scope](#backend-scope), never excluded.
This comparison remains independent of the captured output mode: C9 extracts the KORE result through its own structural helper and does not invoke `krun --output captured`.
C9 also remains independent of live delivery: the normal tutorial measurements continue to use its definition-computed buffer, while only a proved C9 input-precondition failure selects the separate committed transcript path.

A text difference that neither C8 nor N15 can compare stays a mismatch with a measured reason; no prose exclusion category exists for it.
Normalization N15 may prove residual constraints equivalent by checking both implications, subject to the independence limitations in [testing.md](testing.md#comparator-evidence).
Different text or a successful Rust implication check alone must not establish implication correctness.

## Reference evidence

`reference-crash` means the reference supplied no expected behavior; `stale-oracle` means the pinned toolchain did not reproduce an upstream checked-in output.
Both remain visible and must be reconsidered when the reference pin changes.
Neither is evidence of a Rust pass, and locally regenerating an upstream output does not independently establish the contract.
The driver records a reference tool that fails to produce a result as `reference-error` with its stderr, and a reference result that differs from the checked-in output as a stale oracle; the two are never merged.
`both-reject` permits different diagnostic presentation only after both toolchains reject the input; diagnostic classes remain separately recorded to expose rejection for the wrong reason.

The [conformance expectations](../scripts/conformance/expectations.toml) preserve measured revisions, result digests, accepted ranks and stages, and individual case or step reasons.
Historical digests identify measurements; they do not imply that their full temporary logs are distributed with this repository.
A new checkout can seed the versioned floor and measure selected cases using the commands in [testing.md](testing.md#manual-conformance-acceptance).

## Proof gate verdicts

The kprove verdict words are defined in the [README kprove section](../README.md): `disproved` is reserved for a certified refutation, and `failed` covers a failing leaf that does not show the claim false, including an empty leaf that the vacuity policy rejects.
[reference-proof-differential.sh](../scripts/reference-proof-differential.sh) requires every case's failure claim to be rejected by both toolchains, and requires k-rust's verdict word to be the case's `failure-verdict` in [reference-differential.toml](../scripts/reference-differential.toml): `disproved` unless the entry says otherwise, in which case `failure-verdict-reason` states why the claim is not false.

`trivial-proof` expects `failed` for `TRIVIAL-SPEC.ct2`, the all-path claim `<k> t1 => t3 </k>`.
The definition's only rule for `t1` is `t1 => t2 ensures false`; its result is empty, so it states that `t1` has a successor in the empty set, which holds of no configuration.
The claim's left-hand side is therefore empty, and the claim holds vacuously; nothing refutes it.
k-rust reaches a `Trivial` leaf, which the vacuity policy rejects as `failed`, and `--allow-vacuous` proves the claim.
The reference toolchain's rejection of the claim remains checked as the case's oracle observation; k-rust's `failed` verdict records that this rejection is not a refutation.

## Solver decisions over partial operations

An `smt-hook` states its symbol's operation to the solver, and for a partial symbol that statement is exact only where the symbol is defined: an SMT operation has a value at every argument, and an `smt-hook` such as `(div #1 2)` on a symbol whose equations define it only at even arguments gives the solver a value where the definition gives none.
A solver answer about a condition over a partial term `t` is therefore a K answer only under `\ceil(t)`, which the backend states beside `t` wherever it introduces `t` (a rule's or equation's conditions, a hook's arguments, a request's constraints, a pattern's facts), as described below.
In a conjunction, a `\ceil(t)` conjunct is simplified under the equalities of the known path condition but not under its sibling conjuncts': a sibling `t = c` would make it `\ceil(c)`, which holds, and leave the definedness of `t` to an equality that the solver may then decide valid with `t`'s SMT reading and drop.
So `\ceil(t)` stays beside `t = c`, and every solver decision over `t` is taken under it; the path condition is kept by the step, so its own equalities may still discharge it.
The path condition discharges it in one more way: every application is strict, so a term that denotes an element has defined subterms at every depth, and a known predicate that states a term defined (a Boolean condition read as `true = P`, an equality with a defined side, a `\ceil`) states the definedness of each of its subterms; a `\ceil(t)` conjunct over such a subterm holds.
A claim that requires `half(X) <=Int 0 andBool half(X) >=Int 0` thus has `half(X)` defined, and a rule conditioned on `half(X) ==Int 0` applies; a negated or disjunctive known predicate states no term defined, since `\not(t = 0)` holds where `t` is undefined (`a_ceil_conjunct_is_not_discharged_through_a_sibling_equality` and `a_ceil_conjunct_is_discharged_by_a_known_predicate_that_states_its_term_defined` in the [backend tests](../crates/k-rust-backend/tests/backend), `kprove_decides_a_partial_smt_hook_only_under_its_definedness` in [cli.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/cli.rs)).
A definition is not checked for `smt-hook`s whose out-of-domain values are determined: whether a template leaves them open cannot be read from the template, and `domains.md`'s own `MInt` division hooks have SMT-LIB values at zero, where the hooks themselves are undefined ([Hook values at the edges of their domains](#hook-values-at-the-edges-of-their-domains)).

A predicate is definedness-faithful when every K valuation that satisfies it extends to a model of its SMT translation; a solver `Unsat` then refutes it: no K valuation satisfies it.
That rests on the definition's SMT reading, which is trusted and not checked: each `smt-hook` is exact where its symbol is defined (`X /Int Y` is SMT-LIB `div`), each `smt-lemma` axiom holds of the definition, and so does the user's SMT prelude; a client that cites a solver refutation inherits that trust.
Under it, abstraction keeps the property: a term or predicate without a translation becomes a fresh constant or atom (an opaque `\ceil`, `\in` or `\floor` among them), a variable of a sort other than `Int` or `Bool` ranges over an uninterpreted sort, and an uninterpreted `smtlib` function is constrained only by congruence and the trusted lemmas.
A partial hook translated as a total operation does not: an atom over `X /Int Y` denotes `\bottom` in K where `Y` is 0 and has a value in SMT, so `\not(X /Int Y >Int 5) ∧ \not(X /Int Y <=Int 5)`, which holds in K exactly where `Y` is 0, has no SMT model.
A `Sat` shows a K valuation only when the translation is exact (`smt::translation_exactness`).

The backend states predicates in a syntactic form that implies faithfulness, called definedness-explicit here.
A predicate (or a list of predicates, read as a conjunction) is definedness-explicit when each atom over a partial term either is a conjunct of a conjunction enclosing it that also holds that term's definedness (`\not(\not(Y = 0) ∧ X /Int Y >Int 5)`), or stands at the top level and shows its term defined itself (`true = X /Int Y >Int 0`, which holds only where `X /Int Y` is defined), and when each equality of two possibly undefined terms whose sides are not both shown defined that way takes the form `(⌈a⌉ ∧ ⌈b⌉ ∧ a = b) ∨ (\not ⌈a⌉ ∧ \not ⌈b⌉)`, since such an equality holds where both sides are undefined (an equality with a side defined on every instance is an atom over the other side).
A guard counts only as such a conjunct: where it is false it falsifies its conjunction in K and in SMT alike, whatever the atom's reading, while beside the atom under a disjunction it does not (`\not(\not(Y = 0) ∨ X /Int Y >Int 5)` is not definedness-explicit).
