The form is closed under conjunction, so a solver `Unsat` on the conjunction of several definedness-explicit predicates, such as several leaves' constraints, a leaf's constraints with admitted predicates, or the unifier's equalities between subterms of terms whose definedness is a conjunct, also shows that the conjunction has no instance.

These are definedness-explicit: rule and equation conditions as they are instantiated (`definedness::condition_definedness`), the definedness a step carries for its bindings and right-hand side, a remainder that negates an applicability built from those conditions, a pattern's facts with the definedness of its term (`pattern_knowledge`), and every predicate of a request, stated so when the request is admitted (`Admitted`, `definedness::request_definedness`) on every route that reads one: execution and search with their targets, both sides of `implies` (the argument of a `\not` consequent included), `simplify`, `get-model`, and the CLI and RPC commands over them; the request means what it meant ([Undefined instances of the initial configuration](#undefined-instances-of-the-initial-configuration)).
A request predicate that has no such form is refused with `DefinitionError::UnstatedDefinedness`: its definedness depends on an impure application in an operand that `#if`, `andThenBool` or `orElseBool` does not evaluate, which deciding it would perform, or it has an atom over a term-level `\and`, whose definedness is more than its operands' (`Y ∧ Z` of two variables is empty unless `Y = Z`); so is a request with an `\equals` that holds a term where a predicate is read, the third gap below.
Admission's result is a fixed point: admitting it again leaves it unchanged.

Three gaps remain, and they are gaps of k-rust's own answers, not obligations of the client:
- An `\equals` between two possibly undefined terms written in a rule, equation or claim condition of the definition (in K source, `{ A #Equals B }` in a `requires`) is kept as written; its translation `(= a b)` has no model where both sides are undefined although the equality holds there, so `execute`, `search`, `simplify`, `implies` and `prove` answers over such a definition may wrongly be empty, `valid` or `proven`. How the evaluator should read such a condition is itself open: k-rust leaves `meq(X, 0)` stuck under `meq(X, Y) => a() requires { X /Int Y #Equals X /Int Y +Int 1 }`.
- The prove route does not state the definedness of a claim's own constraints: a claim's left-hand side is read from the definition, not admitted as a request, and the proof adds only the definedness of its term. A claim written in KORE with a negated or disjunctive partial atom (`pos(X, Y) ∧ \not(true = X /Int Y >Int 5) ∧ \not(true = X /Int Y <=Int 5)`) can be proven vacuously, and a K-source claim whose Boolean `requires` the proof splits into ML connectives (`requires notBool (X /Int Y <=Int 0)`) can fail on instances the `requires` excludes.
- In a definition's axioms, an `\equals` whose operands are not both terms and that holds a term where a predicate is read (`\equals(\and(B1, B2), false)` over two Boolean variables, `\equals(B, \top)`) is read as the `\iff` of its operands taken as predicates, with a term read as `t = true`, which is not its ML meaning (`B1 ∧ B2` of two values is their intersection, `\bottom` where they differ; `\equals(B, \top)` is false); a Boolean term-level `\and` inside a term is an opaque constant to the solver, which is unfaithful under a negation. Answers over such a definition may wrongly be empty, `valid` or `proven`, in both builds, since the simplifier decides the `\iff` reading without a solver. K source emits neither shape; a request with the first is refused, and one with the second, an atom over a term-level `\and`, too.

The leaf contract (docs/backend-port.md, "Final search depth cuts", and the rustdoc of `ExecutionLeaf`) states what this gives a client: the constraints of every execution leaf and search state are definedness-explicit, so a solver `Unsat` on them, alone or conjoined with other leaves' constraints, shows that there is no instance; proof leaves are outside it, by the second gap.
For a state a client writes itself, `Backend::implies(state, \bottom)` (RPC `implies` with a `\bottom` consequent) decides whether it has an instance: `valid` when k-rust shows it empty from its constraints, their atoms' definedness and its term's definedness, `invalid` when it shows an instance, and `unknown` otherwise.
`request_constraints` and `every_leaf_constraint_is_satisfiable_when_the_leaf_has_an_instance` in [symbolic_definedness.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/symbolic_definedness.rs) cover requests and leaves.

## Definedness of an implication's destination

A destination `t /\ φ` denotes `\ceil(t) /\ t /\ φ`: a term is `\bottom` where it is undefined, so the destination holds of a state only where its term is defined there.
The implication check matches `t` against the state's term, and each part `l` of `t` that the match leaves equal to a part `r` of the state becomes the equation `l = r`, which is checked together with `\ceil(l)` ([`branch_obligations`](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/implication/consequent.rs)).
`r` is defined wherever the state's term is, so `\ceil(l)` is a consequence of the equation and stating it adds nothing the equation does not imply; it is stated because the solver reads a partial application as total, and because an `#if` equation is checked through its condition and branches, and both would otherwise drop it.
A claim whose right-hand side applies a partial function that its left-hand side does not already contain is therefore proven only where its condition makes that application defined.
With `undef`, `undefb` and `undefi` functions without equations, `<k> a(B) => #if B #then 1 #else undef() #fi </k> requires B`, `B andThenBool undefb()` and `B andBool undefb()` under `notBool B`, and `0 *Int undefi()` are not proven; `0 *Int (1 /Int Y)` is proven under `Y =/=Int 0` and not without it (`a_claim_is_proven_only_where_its_right_hand_side_is_defined` and `a_claim_condition_that_defines_a_right_hand_side_operand_keeps_it_proven` in [proof_semantics.rs](../crates/k-rust/tests/proof_semantics.rs)).
A destination existential that occurs only under such an application needs a witness at which the application is defined, as one in an `ensures` does: `<k> z(X) => 0 *Int w(?W) </k>` with only `rule w(5) => 5` is not proven, because the witness search does not find `W = 5`.
Likewise, an application whose definedness the simplifier cannot show on a symbolic argument is not proven defined, even when the function's equations define it everywhere, as in an `ensures` or a rule's right-hand side today.
For example, `<k> z(X) => 0 *Int fo(X) </k>` with `fo(X) => 1 requires X >Int 0` and `fo(_) => 2 [owise]`, and `<k> zb(B) => 0 *Int gb(B) </k>` with `gb(true) => 1` and `gb(false) => 2`, are valid but not proven.
Declaring such a function `total` states its definedness, and the claim is then proven.
The same check decides RPC `implies`, `krust kore-implies` and `Backend::implies`: antecedent `0` and consequent `0 *Int undefi()` is `indeterminate` (`unknown`) with `\ceil(undefi())` in its condition (`a_remainder_equation_is_checked_under_the_definedness_of_its_destination_side` in [implication.rs](../crates/k-rust-backend/src/implication.rs)).
`implies` ignores the request's `assume-defined` ([rpc.rs](../crates/k-rust/src/rpc.rs)), so a client that sets it to skip definedness gets `unknown`, not `valid`, where definedness is not shown.
Before `\ceil(l)` was stated, k-rust reported such claims `proven` and such implications `valid`.

This diverges from the pinned Haskell prover on some of these claims.
It proves `<k> a(B) => #if B #then undef() #else 1 #fi </k> requires notBool B`, and a claim whose state reaches `#if B #then 1 #else undef() #fi` under `B`, both of which k-rust reports `failed`; it refuses the `#if` destinations whose condition is partial and `0 *Int undefi()`, as k-rust does; and it stops with an internal error on the `andThenBool`, `orElseBool` and `andBool` destinations.

## Undefined steps in proofs

A step is undefined on a configuration when a rule applies to it but has an empty result there, and no rule of the same priority gives it a defined result.
The rule applies: it matches and its `requires` holds.
The result is empty because its `ensures` fails, or because its right-hand side is undefined, for example a partial function such as `/Int` by zero, or a `Set` or `Map` concatenation whose operands share an element or key ([Set concatenation is nilpotent](#set-concatenation-is-nilpotent), [Map concatenation binds each key once](#map-concatenation-binds-each-key-once)).
Such a configuration is not stuck, because the applying rule also shuts out the lower priorities.
It is not empty either: it exists, and the path to it is real.
It simply has no successor.

A claim `φ => ψ` states something about every configuration of `φ`.
A configuration of `φ` whose path reaches an undefined step outside `ψ` never reaches `ψ`, so under both the one-path and the all-path reading the claim is false for that configuration.
k-rust therefore reports the step's undefined instances as a `Trivial` leaf.
This holds whether the step decided the condition or only carried it symbolically.
The leaf's constraint is the condition under which the step is undefined, restricted by every applied sibling rule of the same priority.
The leaf is dropped only when that constraint is shown unsatisfiable, and otherwise it makes the claim `failed`.
`--allow-vacuous` accepts such leaves, like the other empty branches.

The other reading takes the rule as an axiom: `σ(l ∧ R) → •(r ∧ E)`, with an empty result for `σ`, makes `σ` itself impossible.
The branch then counts as vacuous, and its configurations are dropped as if they did not exist.
For a functional left-hand side that axiom is false: the configuration can be written down, and it reaches the rule by defined steps.
So pruning it proves claims that have false instances, and a rule's wrong `ensures` then vanishes silently instead of showing up as a failing configuration.
It also makes a symbolic claim hold while one of its ground instances fails.
The `reference-prunes-undefined-step` category records conformance cases where the reference toolchain proves a claim only in that way.

In `set_unification` (`a-spec.k`, claim #1), the claim is `<k> start(I) => end ...</k> <set>... (.Set => SetItem(I +Int 1) ?_:Set) ...</set>`.
Its only step is `<set>... .Set => SetItem(I) ...</set>`, which rewrites the set cell `S` to `S SetItem(I)`.
`Set` concatenation of sets with a common element is undefined ([`domains.md`](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/blob/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/k-distribution/include/kframework/builtin/domains.md), "Set concatenation").
So on every configuration whose set already contains `I`, the step is undefined, for example `start(1)` with the set `SetItem(1)`.
Such a configuration never reaches `end`, so the claim does not hold for it.
k-rust reports `failed` with a `Trivial` leaf under `I in S`, and proves the claim with `--allow-vacuous`.
The ground instance fails in the same way: the claim `<k> start(1) => end ...</k> <set> SetItem(1) (.Set => SetItem(2) ?_:Set) </set>` has the undefined step `SetItem(1) SetItem(1)` and is `failed`, because concrete concatenation is nilpotent too.

## Proof oracle incompleteness

A `reference-incomplete-port-proves` exclusion needs a soundness argument for the particular claim: a Rust `proven` result alone cannot justify departing from a reference refutation.
The pinned K cases are under [`k-distribution/tests/regression-new/spec-rule-application`](https://github.com/runtimeverification/k/tree/4a46d1231473b599c699160132fd6e76a5c46406/k-distribution/tests/regression-new/spec-rule-application).
Their `def.k` defines `incPos(X) = X + 1` when `X >= 0`, and the only ordinary transition is `start X => mid X`.

For `def61-spec.k`, after that transition choose trusted-claim variables `Y1 = X1` and `Y2 = X2 - 1`.
The subject requires `X1 >= 0` and `X2 - 1 >= 0`, so `incPos(Y1) - Y1 - 1 = 0` and `incPos(Y2) = X2`.
Both components of the trusted claim's `binop` therefore match the subject, and its right-hand side is exactly the destination `end X1` with the same variable cell.

For `def81-spec.k`, choose `Y1 = X + 1` and `Y2 = X + 2` after the initial transition.
The subject requires `X >= 0`, hence `incPos(Y1) - 1 = X + 1`; also `Y1 - 1 = X`, `Y2 - 1 = X + 1`, and the trusted claim's condition `Y2 = Y1 + 1` holds.
The trusted claim reaches exactly the specified destination without an uncovered remainder.
The upstream test plan's parts 6 and 8 explain evaluation under constraints; the plan also says part 8 passed an older simplification algorithm, so it does not by itself establish the current pinned backend's outcome.
The individual step exclusions retain the observed oracle mismatch separately from these substitution arguments.
